[1] The Hacker News - Attackers Compromise Microsoft's Agent Framework PyPI Package to Steal Cloud Credentials
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 12:15:26 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/attackers-compromise-microsofts-agent.html

[TAM METİN - 657 kelime]
Threat actors have compromised Microsoft's agent-framework package to push a credential stealer, according to reports from Aikido, Chainguard, Endor Labs, OX Security, SafeDep, Socket, StepSecurity, and Google-owned Wiz.

The malicious versions, 1.26.1 and 1.26.2, were published 33 minutes apart on November 17, 2026, and are designed to run a credential stealer on every Python start. As of writing, both the release versions have been quarantined on the Python Package Index (PyPI) repository. It's unclear how the attacker managed to obtain publishing access.

Agent-framework is the meta-package for Microsoft Agent Framework, an open-source framework for building and orchestrating artificial intelligence (AI) agents using Python.

"Both versions install Python startup hooks that silently collect credentials, environment data, and cloud identity tokens, then exfiltrate them to a webhook[.]site endpoint," Socket said in a report.

Once either of the versions is installed, the package drops a .pth file in the site-packages root directory, which then gets executed automatically on every interpreter start, causing the credential stealer to run whenever Python is started in an environment, even if the package is not explicitly imported.

"Each wheel adds a .pth file to site-packages," Endor Labs said. "Python executes import lines in .pth files on every interpreter start, so the stealer runs on any Python launch in an affected environment, including pip itself, whether or not anything imports Agent Framework."

The names of the .pth files are below -

agent_framework_meta.pth in version 1.26.1

af_runtime_diag.pth in version 1.26.2

The "agent_framework_meta.pth" file contains a single import statement referencing "agent_framework_meta," which then launches the stealer payload. It's capable of capturing the following data -

Environment variables based on substring-based matching

Credential files associated with Azure CLI (~/.azure/*.json), .npmrc, pip.conf, .netrc, .git-credentials, .docker/config.json, Hugging Face, Microsoft Azure DevOps agents, and .env and JSON files

Azure DevOps OIDC tokens

Azure managed-identity tokens

Azure instance metadata and retains up to 8,000 characters of the response

"Besides reading credentials already on disk, the code asks the platform to issue new ones," SafeDep said. "Rotating stored secrets does not cover those."

"In an Azure DevOps pipeline, SYSTEM_ACCESSTOKEN and SYSTEM_OIDCREQUESTURI are enough to request a federated token for api://AzureADTokenExchange. On Azure compute, it asks the managed identity endpoint for an access token to each of these resources. It uses IDENTITY_ENDPOINT or MSI_ENDPOINT on App Service and Functions, and 169.254.169[.]254 elsewhere. It also pulls instance metadata."

It's worth noting that the Azure instance metadata service (IMDS) is a RESTful web service that runs on a local IP address ("169.254.169[.]254"), and provides information about the virtual machine, its region, tags, and the identity token.

All the collected information is saved into a JSON document, zlib-compressed, Base64-encoded, and sent via an HTTPS POST request to a hard-coded URL ("webhook[.]site/d381fd80-ec62-42ad-8f5c-d1b15103c9bb").

Version 1.26.2, on the other hand, imports a new top-level module, af_runtime_diag.py, collects GitHub Actions OIDC tokens, and expands the search patterns used for harvesting credential-related environment variables.

Given that the package attracts about 190,000 downloads a month, users are advised to perform the following steps -

Isolate affected hosts and runners

Pin the package to the most recent safe version (1.26.0) and restore from a clean environment

Check for exposure to the malicious versions between 8:35 and roughly 9:20 p.m. UTC on November 17, 2026

Search proxy and DNS logs for requests to webhook[.]site/d381fd80-ec62-42ad-8f5c-d1b15103c9bb

Rotate exposed credentials from a clean system

Audit Azure identities and review sign-in and activity logs

Audit GitHub Actions workflows that deploy to Azure

"The malicious agent-framework shows how attackers are targeting the AI developer ecosystem and the CI pipelines behind it," OX Security said. "While directly targeting an official Microsoft package, it seems that attackers found a way to hijack the wheel creation flow to add malicious logic inside."

Agent-framework is the second Microsoft-related PyPI package to be compromised this year. In May 2026, the threat actors behind TeamPCP pushed three malicious versions of durabletask, Microsoft's Python client for the Durable Task workflow execution framework, to distribute the Mini Shai-Hulud worm.

[2] The Hacker News - Russian COLD RELIC Hackers Use Stolen Google Workspace Data to Breach Second U.S. Organization
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 11:28:04 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/russian-cold-relic-hackers-use-stolen.html

[TAM METİN - 793 kelime]
The Russia-linked cyber espionage group known as Cold Relic (formerly COLDRIVER and UNC4057) has been observed weaponizing a single cloud compromise into a launchpad for a series of linked intrusions resulting in third-party compromise.

Google's Mandiant and Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) said it investigated a 2026 compromise at a U.S.-based organization in which the adversary leveraged data, the corporate staff directory, and identities harvested from an earlier attack on another U.S.-based entity in late 2025.

Cold Relic, also tracked as Callisto, Gossamer Bear, and Star Blizzard, has a track record of orchestrating sophisticated phishing and malware campaigns to gain access information that may be of strategic importance to the Russian government.

The access is abused to register rogue multi-factor authentication (MFA) devices, passkeys, and MFA backup codes to establish long-term persistence and then conduct aggressive data collection and exfiltration operations targeting email, chat, and cloud storage repositories. In addition, the compromised access is used to target the victim's social and professional networks.

Attacks mounted by the threat actor have successfully compromised high-value targets, including government officials, defense contractors, and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) across Europe and North America.

"Once Cold Relic compromises a target, it uses stolen emails and data to establish authentic pretexts and impersonate contacts when reaching out to subsequent targets," Google said.

According to the tech giant's cybersecurity and incident response teams, Cold Relic compromised the cloud productivity environment of an unspecified U.S.-based organization in late 2025. Initial access to the network was achieved by means of a residential proxy network located in close geographical proximity to the victim.

Using the stolen credentials of a Super Admin account and combining it with an MFA push notification fatigue attack, the threat actor is said to have gained access to the account after the victim accepted an MFA request. This was followed by the attacker registering their own device within the tenant to retain access. The threat actor was also observed using passkeys and MFA backup codes as alternate access mechanisms.

"Cold Relic used the privileges of the compromised Super Admin account to download the contents of the entire organization's cloud storage drive by both mass sharing files to a Cold Relic-operated email account and downloading files directly," Google explained.

"While operating inside the tenant of the victim organization, Cold Relic accessed the Admin Console, queried Admin security investigation and email audit logs to check defensive visibility, and viewed, modified, and deleted Alert Center notifications to remove forensic evidence of the initial intrusion."

These actions ultimately enabled the threat actor to export full employee lists and domain-wide data via the Google Takeout service using the Super Admin privileges. Following mass data theft, the attackers continued to maintain access over several months and perform additional activities -

Sync email inbox via OAuth to external email clients.

Re-authenticate using unrevoked attacker-enrolled passkeys that persisted across password and session resets and bypassed device registration controls.

Target cloud drive document access related to credentials, email archives, personnel information, and other documents of interest.

Even after the attackers were ousted from the victim organization, Google said it observed Cold Relic using email accounts impersonating employees of the victim organization to target both internal employees and associates within the same professional circles as part of a renewed social engineering campaign.

The attack then moved to the next phase when the threat actor compromised a Microsoft Entra ID user account of a second U.S.-based organization that used Entra ID as its federated single sign-on (SSO) identity provider for Google Workspace.

By impersonating an employee at the first organization, the threat actor established communications with the target over a secure messaging app and then shared phishing links designed to harvest credentials and authentication tokens for Entra ID under the pretext of sharing a document containing details about a technical issue.

"Upon successful authentication using the stolen credentials, Cold Relic registered and used a new MFA device in the SSO solution to retain access to the user account throughout the intrusion," Google said.

"By impersonating someone known in the same professional circles as the new target, Cold Relic increased their chance of phishing success. Ultimately, in this case, Cold Relic was able to obtain credentials to the organization and perform bulk download of files accessible in the compromised user's cloud drive."

Upon successfully compromising the second organization, the messaging app account used to initiate the conversation had its profile name changed to a different persona. It's suspected that the new persona was likely used to target other organizations in the same professional network.

"Cold Relic's campaigns demonstrate the convergence of human-centric social engineering and cloud identity abuse," Google said. "By obtaining access to strategic personnel rosters and operational documents, Russian intelligence services turn legitimate business communications into attack vectors against trusted partners."

[3] The Hacker News - Java4Shell Flaw (CVE-2026-114938) Enables Pre-Auth RCE in Jira, Jenkins, Keycloak and More
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 09:57:41 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/java4shell-cve-2026-114938-enables-pre.html

[TAM METİN - 1442 kelime]
A Java flaw called Java4Shell can let attackers without an account run their own code on Jira, Jenkins, Keycloak, and three other widely used applications, says Calif, the security research firm that disclosed it this week.

The flaw is present in Java 17.0.6 and later, and the vulnerable code runs only on x86-64 Intel and AMD chips that support the AVX-512 VBMI feature.

Tracked as CVE-2026-114938, the fix was released today by Oracle and the OpenJDK project, whose open-source code underpins Java builds from many vendors. Amazon has fixed its Corretto Java builds, but Red Hat is still preparing fixes for its own.

The researchers are holding back their attack code for the six applications, and other labs have so far reproduced crashes and a memory leak but not the code execution. Canada's Cyber Center said this week that it was not aware of any attacks exploiting the flaw.

Servers often decode Base64-encoded data, such as HTTP Basic login headers, SAML artifacts, and CSRF tokens, before checking whether a user is logged in. That is how an attacker without an account can exploit the flaw.

The flaw is in a faster version of Java's Base64 decoder that runs on chips with AVX-512 VBMI. Java switches to it after compiling the decoder for speed, which it does for code that runs often.

A malformed string that ends with an extra = makes this decoder write 62 or 63 bytes past the end of its output. Java rejects the string, but only after the write.

The bytes written past the end are fixed, so an attacker controls only where they land. For that reason, early readings of the source code had pointed to a crash rather than code execution, as The Hacker News noted in an earlier report yesterday.

The attacks worked without a login under the default settings of Keycloak, Atlassian Jira, Atlassian Confluence, JFrog Artifactory, Jenkins, and PingFederate, and with a login on Apache Kafka Broker, Apache Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit, and Spinnaker, Calif says in its technical write-up. The researchers' lists are incomplete, and they rate the flaw as 9.2 out of 10 under CVSS 4.0.

They get past the fixed bytes by arranging the server's memory so that the overflow expands a neighboring array. They then use that array to write bytes of their choosing and a second array to read back from memory, a step they call JavaBleed. From there, the researchers say, the attack reaches the parts of Java that run code.

There is no single exploit that works everywhere. Each target requires its own memory layout and timing, and some attacks are sensitive to network delays.

A proxy that waits for complete requests, as nginx does by default, blocks some attacks but not all. A failed attempt can crash Java, and an attacker can often try again.

Security company Upwind reproduced the memory corruption on an AWS server with an Intel Ice Lake chip, where it damaged the next object in memory and eventually crashed Java, according to its lab results.

Blackpoint Cyber's Adversary Pursuit Group crashed a test Spring Boot application by sending about 28,300 malformed login requests over roughly 20 seconds, without ever logging in.

The group also used the JavaBleed step in a local test to read a secret stored next to the overflow, which worked in all 30 runs. It did not reproduce remote code execution, its test results show.

Oracle scores the flaw 8.1 out of 10 under CVSS 3.1, rating it hard to exploit but requiring no login. The description in its CVE record says, "Successful attacks of this vulnerability can result in takeover of Oracle Java SE."

Red Hat also rates it as requiring no login, with a preliminary score of 9.8 out of 10. Amazon scores it 8.8 out of 10 and describes the risk in its Amazon Linux entry as "a JVM crash for unauthenticated users or, in certain application environments, code execution by an authenticated low-privileged user."

Canada's Cyber Centre also describes the flaw as exploitable remotely without authentication in its government alert.

The flaw was not in the KEV catalog of exploited vulnerabilities, kept by the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA), when The Hacker News checked it today.

The vulnerable code is in Java 17.0.6 and later on the Java 17 line, and in Java 21, 25, and 27. Standard Java 8 and Java 11 do not contain it, but the Java SE 8 Performance Pack build 8u511b33-perf is affected. GraalVM for JDK 17.0.21.0.3, GraalVM for JDK 21.0.13.0.3, and GraalVM Enterprise Edition 21.3.20.0.3 are also affected.

It runs only on x86-64 chips that support AVX-512 VBMI. These include Intel Xeon Ice Lake, Sapphire Rapids, Emerald Rapids, and Granite Rapids chips, as well as AMD Zen 4 or newer chips. ARM64 chips do not run it.

Because the vulnerable code runs only on these chips, the same container image can run it on one server and not on another. On Linux, this command shows whether a server's chip has the four features the vulnerable code needs, and all four must appear in its output:

grep -m1 '^flags' /proc/cpuinfo | tr ' ' '\n' | grep -E '^(avx512f|avx512bw|avx512vbmi|bmi2)$'

The attacker also needs malformed Base64 to reach Java's standard decoder, through paths such as the login headers and tokens described earlier.

Calif also says a Java program that opens a TLS connection to a malicious server can be compromised, even if the program runs no service of its own. The exploit for this came from another researcher, samczsun, and the researchers have not published how it reaches the decoder.

Fixes and Workarounds

The OpenJDK project's vulnerability advisory lists the flaw as fixed in this week's releases, and Oracle's security advisory lists the affected versions of its builds.

For Oracle Java, the fixed builds are the new .1 releases in the table, all released this week. The release notes for 17.0.21.1 say it includes the update's security fixes, and the notes for 21.0.13.1, 25.0.5.1 and 27.0.1.1 say the same.

For the Java 8 Performance Pack, the latest build is 8u513, available in both standard and Performance Pack versions. For GraalVM, the November security releases are GraalVM for JDK 17.0.21.1, GraalVM for JDK 21.0.13.1, and GraalVM Enterprise Edition 21.3.20.1.

The Corretto changelog for 17.0.21.1.2 lists the flaw as fixed, as do the changelogs for the new 21, 25, and 27 builds. Amazon Linux also marks its Corretto 17, 21, 25 and 27 packages as fixed.

Azul's November update fixes the flaw in Zulu 17, 21, 25 and 27, according to its Zulu release notes.

Red Hat has not yet released fixes for Red Hat Enterprise Linux 8, 9 and 10, or for its OpenJDK 17, 21 and 25 builds, according to its security bulletin. Containers based on its images need to be rebuilt once updated images are out.

SAP released SapMachine 17.0.21.1, 21.0.13.1, 25.0.5.1, and 27.0.1.1 this week, but their release pages do not specify what they fix. Eclipse Temurin, the Microsoft Build of OpenJDK, and BellSoft Liberica had not listed fixed builds when The Hacker News checked today.

Products that bundle their own Java runtime need an update from the product's maker. After updating, restart Java services, and rebuild and redeploy container images so the running process uses the new build.

If you cannot update yet, start the JVM with one of these flags, and remove it once the fixed build is installed:

-XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics turns off only the faster Base64 code, which removes the vulnerable path and leaves other AVX-512 speedups in place.

-XX:UseAVX=2 turns off AVX-512 entirely. Use it only on x86-64 servers, because Java on ARM64 does not recognize it and will fail to start.

In Upwind's lab, both flags stopped the overflow on every affected version it tested. The company warns that another flag, -XX:DisableIntrinsic=_base64_decodeBlock, stops Java from starting when it is used on its own.

To spot attempts, it suggests checking for the signs below, while warning that none of them proves a compromise and that their absence does not prove a server is safe:

Base64 tokens of 32 characters or more that end in = and whose length is not a multiple of 4, especially in Authorization headers

Bursts of IllegalArgumentException errors from java.util.Base64$Decoder

Unexplained hs_err_pid crash logs or bursts of restarts

A malformed input that triggers the overflow was posted publicly on September 9, in a proposed fix submitted to OpenJDK's code repository as a pull request. The proposal described an overflow in the same AVX-512 Base64 decoder code.

Its author closed it without merging it in early November, after an automated notice that it had been inactive for more than eight weeks.

[4] The Hacker News - Researchers Claim Two RCE Exploits That Bypass Telegram’s New Android Defenses
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 01:04:20 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/researchers-claim-two-rce-exploits-that.html

[TAM METİN - 773 kelime]
A malicious widget can bypass the security filter that Telegram added to its Android app last week and run code outside it, according to NebuSec, a security company.

Widgets are small interactive blocks that bots send inside chat messages, and their JavaScript runs on the user's phone. The researchers ran their demonstration on a Telegram test server, because only approved bots can send widgets on the live service now.

Bots could start sending widgets last week. Two days later, an account called V12 posted a video on X claiming that any Telegram message could now be used to execute remote code.

Telegram then put QuickJS, the small engine that runs widget JavaScript, into its own process on Android. It also added a seccomp filter, which can block or record the system calls that the process makes. Both changes appear in the source code it published for Telegram for Android 13.2.2 (7303).

NebuSec said in a technical analysis published earlier this week that its researchers got code running outside that filter in two separate ways. One uses a widget bug the filter does not cover, and the other chains a widget bug the filter does cover with a kernel bug.

In their video, a bot's Telegram widget on Android 13.2.2 (7303) bypasses the filter and opens the phone's calculator app, the researchers say.

Their write-up and their demonstration post on X do not say what the code could reach beyond that, such as the app's messages or the rest of the phone. They also do not say whether the user had to start the bot or tap the widget first.

The researchers are holding back the details for now. Their write-up names no CVE identifier and does not say whether the bugs were reported to the app's developers.

The widget documentation says widgets run in a sandbox, with rules such as no network access. Those rules live in the engine's own code, the researchers say. A single use-after-free or out-of-bounds write bug in QuickJS lets a script read and write the process's memory, and then the rules no longer protect anything.

The new filter names a few dozen system calls, such as ptrace, bpf, and mount, and lets every other call through. The filter's source code leaves ioctl unfiltered. The filter sits on top of Android's own limits on the engine process.

One process also runs every widget, so a hijacked widget from one bot shares memory with the widgets of every other bot, the researchers say.

In the published source code for Telegram for Android 13.2.2 (7303), that filter is set to an audit mode, The Hacker News found. In audit mode, it still blocks attempts to start new programs or processes, but it only records the other listed calls and lets them run.

A comment above the setting says how long that will last: "Audit until the logs from the devices say the engine never makes them." The published code does not show whether the version people install uses the same setting.

"Yes, currently, general bots aren't allowed to use widgets even in private chats," a contributor to the telegram-bot-api project wrote late last week in a bug report thread on GitHub. As of this week, no later message in the thread says which bots are still allowed or that the limit has been lifted.

The researchers' write-up lists no fixed version, and the published Android source code is no newer than 13.2.2 (7303) as of this week.

"No one could be hacked just by receiving a message," Pavel Durov, Telegram's chief executive, wrote late last week in a channel post. His post addressed reports of code execution through widgets in the Android and Windows apps, and came before the researchers' demonstration. He added that a user first had to start a third-party service, and that even that was fully patched on the server side.

The company had earlier said that the problem in V12's first demonstration was fixed and that the fix had reached all users.

Separately, the security vendor Kaspersky said late last week that it had detected suspected exploitation of a flaw in Telegram widgets when people visited some Telegram channels.

In that activity, a PowerShell script ran without the user noticing and downloaded and launched WebRAT, also known as Salat Stealer. The malware allows attackers to control the infected system and steal data from crypto wallets and Telegram accounts.

The vendor's malware warning does not identify the flaw or the Telegram version, does not report the number of victims, and does not link the activity to either demonstration. It advises not running widgets until a fix is released.

[5] The Hacker News - Oracle to Patch 11 Java Flaws Today as Critical OpenJDK RCE (CVE-2026-114938) Report Emerges
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 20:47:36 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/oracle-to-patch-11-java-flaws-today-as.html

[TAM METİN - 907 kelime]
Oracle is preparing to release security patches for 11 vulnerabilities in Java SE on Tuesday, November 17, 2026, all of which may be exploited remotely without authentication, as separate reports warn of a potentially critical remote code execution (RCE) flaw affecting OpenJDK.

The 11 vulnerabilities carry a maximum CVSS 3.1 severity score of 8.1, according to Oracle's advance security notice. The company has not disclosed their CVE identifiers, affected components, or the individual consequences of exploitation.

The separate OpenJDK vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-114938, has reportedly been assigned a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2 and affects OpenJDK 17.0.6 and later. However, those details remain unconfirmed, and there is no evidence yet that the reported flaw is among the 11 vulnerabilities Oracle plans to patch.

Security firm Calif said in a post on X that it discovered and reported the flaw, which it calls Java4Shell, and that the vulnerability had leaked. Calif said it would publish technical details by 1 p.m. Pacific time on November 17, after Oracle releases a patch.

UPDATE: Java4Shell (CVE-2026-114938) What’s Affected, What’s at Risk, and How to Fix It

Security company watchTowr acknowledged the reports in a post on X, but cautioned that the technical details were still unconfirmed.

Separate analyses of the OpenJDK source code by security companies Upwind and Equixly point to a flaw in Java's Base64 decoder whose likely result is a crash of the Java virtual machine (JVM) rather than code execution.

The vulnerability's CVE record remains reserved without a public description. No official advisory has specified the vulnerable component, precise exploitation requirements, affected version range, or the availability of a fix.

Oracle's ops.java site now lists the replacement builds below as its current releases. Oracle's advance notice, release notes and download page still showed the earlier builds as of 16:59 UTC.

Oracle Prepares Java Security Updates

Oracle advises customers to stop using its current builds of Java 8, 11, 17, 21, 25, and 27 after the November 17 security update and move to the corresponding replacement releases.

The company's Java release notes contain the update guidance, while ops.java lists the following planned builds:

The November security notice identifies the following affected Oracle products and versions:

Oracle Java SE: 8u511, 8u511-perf, 11.0.33, 17.0.21, 21.0.13, 25.0.5, and 27.0.1.

Oracle GraalVM for JDK: 17.0.21 and 21.0.13.

Oracle GraalVM Enterprise Edition: 21.3.20.0.3.

Oracle JavaFX: 21.0.13, 25.0.5, and 27.0.1.

Oracle says all 11 Java SE vulnerabilities may be exploited over a network without requiring credentials. That does not necessarily mean they all allow remote code execution or that every application running an affected Java version can be attacked directly.

The advance notice does not identify the vulnerable Java components or specify what an attacker could accomplish by exploiting each flaw.

Oracle also warns that the information may change before the complete security advisory is published.

Critical OpenJDK RCE Report Remains Unverified

The reported CVE-2026-114938 vulnerability introduces a separate concern for organizations operating Java applications, particularly those exposed to the internet.

However, the public information does not establish whether the flaw affects the Java runtime itself, a particular library, or functionality that applications must expose for exploitation to succeed.

However, Oracle's notice and the OpenJDK advisories do not establish whether the flaw affects the Java runtime itself, a particular library, or functionality that applications must expose for exploitation to succeed.

Upwind said in a technical analysis that its reading of the OpenJDK source points to a flaw in Java's AVX-512 Base64 decoder, and Equixly reached the same finding in a separate analysis. The flawed code runs only on x86-64 processors with AVX-512 VBMI support, when untrusted data reaches the basic or URL decoder of java.util.Base64. ARM servers do not run it.

The code is in Java 17.0.6 and later and in Java 21, 25 and 27, but not in standard Java 8 or 11. A crash is the likely result because the bytes written past the buffer are fixed and not chosen by the attacker. Neither firm reported reproducing the overflow on AVX-512 hardware.

Oracle's 8u371-perf release notes show that its Java 8 Enterprise Performance Pack added a "Base64 Decoding optimization for x86 using AVX-512". Neither firm said whether the Performance Pack is affected.

Both firms named two JVM options as stopgaps to keep the flawed code from running: -XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics, which turns off the Base64 intrinsics, and -XX:UseAVX=2, which turns off all AVX-512 code paths and can slow other work. Neither option has been confirmed by a vendor for this flaw, the firms said.

Oracle's November notice covers Oracle products and does not establish the patch status of third-party OpenJDK distributions.

The OpenJDK Vulnerability Group, which coordinates security disclosures affecting OpenJDK source code, had not published an advisory newer than October 24 at the time of the earlier review.

Until official details are available, organizations cannot determine from the reported CVE alone whether their Java deployments are vulnerable or which specific update addresses the issue.

Oracle began a monthly security patch cycle on May 28, introducing targeted security releases between its quarterly Critical Patch Updates.

Its Java security update plan, last updated September 10, identified November 17 as the next scheduled monthly release.

Organizations should inventory deployed Java runtimes, identify internet-facing applications, and prepare to test and install the relevant vendor updates once they become available.

This is a developing story. We will update this article as Oracle publishes its full vulnerability advisory and additional verified details about CVE-2026-114938, affected versions, and available patches emerge.

[6] The Hacker News - Trigram Campaign Impersonates About 50 AI and Blockchain Brands to Target Devs with Infostealers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 20:47:28 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/trigram-campaign-impersonates-about-50.html

[TAM METİN - 1824 kelime]
Artificial intelligence (AI), blockchain, and logistics sectors across Europe and the U.S. have emerged as the target of a large-scale, industrialized campaign dubbed Trigram.

"Trigram is a large-scale campaign that impersonates about 50 brands in the blockchain and AI sectors, to trick developers into executing an infostealer themselves," Stormshield said in a deep-dive technical report published Monday.

"Three social engineering scenarios (recruitment, conference, and package tracking), a software supply-chain compromise, and trojanized code repositories all converge on the same infrastructure and the same payload."

The activity is assessed to share similarities with intrusion sets linked to North Korea, particularly Kimsuky and UNK_DeadDrop. Some aspects of this campaign have been previously documented by Proofpoint, SafeDep, Jamf, and Genians Security Center.

It's worth noting that the cluster tracked as UNK_DeadDrop exhibits similarities with a long-running North Korea-aligned campaign referred to as Contagious Interview. First observed between April and May 2026, it employs phishing emails using developer role recruitment or code review themes to target developers across finance, cryptocurrency, education, and technology sectors to deploy an information stealer.

On Linux and macOS systems, the attack chains lead to the deployment of Overlord, an open-source remote access trojan (RAT) framework to facilitate data theft.

Then, in August 2026, Jamf Threat Labs disclosed details of a macOS campaign using a fake Zoom installer to deploy Overlord RAT, although the Apple device management company said at the time that it found no "direct overlap" between the activity and UNK_DeadDrop.

"The Overlord variant observed here shares a LaunchAgent label (com.zoom) and plist name (com.zoom.plist) with FlexibleFerret, a DPRK-attributed macOS malware family associated with the Contagious Interview campaign," it added.

Overlord has also been distributed as recently as late October 2026 via another channel: a forged commit in the antd-img-crop GitHub repository that installs the malware by means of a prebuild hook ("prebuild.js") as soon as a developer runs the "npm run build" command. The npm package in itself was not impacted.

The JavaScript payload is designed to download and run a fake installer that sets up Node.js and fetches an encrypted JavaScript next-stage that runs entirely in memory and locates its command-and-control (C2) server via a GitLab dead-drop. The third-stage malware is responsible for harvesting credentials and installing the OverLord framework.

On macOS and Linux systems, it also displays a fake dialog asking users to enter their system password so as to "apply system updates and verify drivers and security settings." Interestingly, the same payload is delivered via two other initial access vectors -

A fake careers portal impersonating Amber Group, a legitimate cryptocurrency firm

A sideloaded Microsoft Visual Studio Code (VS Code) or Cursor extension named "google-dev-tools.google-update-support" that's likely launched via a coding assessment and deleted after it's run

"The fake recruiter, the paste-a-command 'verification' step and the focus on developers and cryptocurrency holders match the publicly documented Contagious Interview campaign," SafeDep said. "The match rests on technique and does not prove who the operators are."

The third branch of the campaign revolves around another cluster tracked by Genians as Operation GitPower. Attributed to the Kimsuky group, the activity involves operating local large language model (LLM) environments using Ollama and GPT4All to generate decoy documents and use them to target professionals and organizations in the fields of policy, academia, international cooperation, diplomacy, and security research.

Present within the phishing email is a Windows Shortcut (LNK) that, once launched, runs PowerShell code designed to retrieve the lure document via GitHub's raw content service and open it, while stealthily downloading additional payloads like AsyncRAT in the background.

The campaign has since evolved to target Ukrainian experts in diplomacy, security, and international policy, utilizing international conference invitations and requests for analytical collaboration to elicit responses from targets and eventually distribute malware in the form of ZIP archives containing malicious LNK files.

In at least one case, the phishing emails have been found to trick recipients into clicking on bogus links to harvest their Google account credentials under the pretext of joining a Zoom meeting via Gmail. Another method involves distributing a counterfeit version of a Zoom installer that, in addition to installing the legitimate app,  runs malware designed to run arbitrary PowerShell scripts and send the results back.

"This attack is particularly noteworthy as it indicates that Kimsuky has expanded its targeting scope beyond its traditional focus on South Korea to include key individuals in Ukraine," Genians noted last month, adding these efforts were mostly unsuccessful.

"Kimsuky's attacks against Ukraine may be cyber espionage operations aimed at collecting information on the war, the extent of North Korean troop losses, developments in the questioning and handling of prisoners of war, and the details of consultations between South Korea and Ukraine."

The latest findings from Stormshield merge together all of these separate findings into a single, cohesive picture -- a toolset comprising a management platform, victim-tracking consoles, and email-delivery infrastructure. The attack sequence is a single infection chain that's adapted for three environments -

Send the target an email advertising a job opportunity, entice them into clicking a link, and initiate the "recruitment" process.

The website masquerades as a recruitment platform where visitors can view job listings and submit job applications.

In the background, the web page fingerprints the visitor and uses an IP address-based geofencing mechanism to lock out users from specific regions (possibly China). If access is restricted, users are served a stripped-down version of the home page without any ability to interact with it.

If the "applicant" satisfies the filtering criteria, they are served an application form to enter their personal details.

The user is then prompted to enter a referral (or invitation) code, indicating that the campaign is aimed at pre-identified targets rather than a broad audience. If the code is rejected, the site displays one of three messages: "Already used," "Locked to another IP," and "Invalid code."

If the code is accepted, the candidate is served a confirmation message and is instructed to copy and run a command on the Linux terminal as part of a technical check.

Once the command is run, it sends the "nonce" information displayed on the web page to a remote server via an HTTP POST request. No file or script is downloaded or executed on the victim machine at this stage.

The web page, which is waiting for confirmation from the server, responds with an error message, stating: "Operation timeout. Please try again. Nonce Refreshed."

The attack moves to the second stage when the web page displays the same verification screen but with a modified "Copy" button behavior that injects the malicious command directly into the victim's clipboard. The same pattern is observed even when the target uses the "Ctrl+C" keyboard shortcut to copy the command from the web page.

The actual command that gets executed depends on the operating system -
    
On Windows, it launches PowerShell to download and run a file named "v.cmd"
On Linux, it launches bash shell to download and run a file named "u"
On macOS, it launches bash shell to download and run a file named "verify_m”

On Windows, it launches PowerShell to download and run a file named "v.cmd"

On Linux, it launches bash shell to download and run a file named "u"

On macOS, it launches bash shell to download and run a file named "verify_m”

"These scripts act as loaders: they prepare the execution environment, retrieve the necessary components, and launch a JavaScript payload, install_guard.js, fetched from a remote server," the French cybersecurity company explained.

This preparatory stage also involves extracting a Base64-encoded script that configures the working directory, establishes a locking mechanism to prevent concurrent installations, and checks for an existing Node.js installation.

If a compatible version (minimum version 18) is not found, it proceeds to download and install Node.js version 20.18.1 based on the infected host's operating system and its architecture. It then downloads "install_guard.js," which deploys an infostealer tasked with collecting system metadata, browser credentials, cryptocurrency wallets, and sensitive files present on the machine.

The entire operation is powered by a multi-layered architecture comprising fake recruitment (like Amber Group), conference invitation,  and package delivery sites; C2 servers that host the JavaScript and are fetched by means of a GitLab dead-drop; and the back office hosted on "170.205.30[.]27:8000" that enables campaign management and victim tracking via a platform called "rms-platform."

The tracking console records every action undertaken by the user, while simultaneously keeping tabs on bot visitors and lookalike domains that have been taken down. Each "real visitor" is then rated "important," "moderate," or "useless," suggesting some sort of shortlisting based on their value.

Further analysis of the API routes associated with "rms-platform" show that it's capable of identifying potential targets by scraping GitHub profiles, sending phishing emails, managing decoys, issuing access codes, viewing and exporting collected data, and cryptocurrency wallet monitoring.

"These three levels rely on abused legitimate services: GitHub and GitLab for code and configuration hosting; Brevo, Resend, Mailgun, and Smartlead for email delivery; and FullStory for visitor session recording," Stormshield explained.

"The tracking consoles are not standalone applications: they are delivered within the same JavaScript code as the decoys. The same bundle is served to all visitors, and a check on the hostname or URL path determines which interface is displayed."

The technical indicators that point in the direction of UNK_DeadDrop and Kimsuky are listed below -

eros1030109@gmail[.]com, a super-administrator account for the consoles that matches with an email address used in Operation GitPower.

0xapp123, a Medium account that discusses Web3 trends and lists "eros1030109@gmail.com" as the contact email address as far back as June 2022.

silviattech1129_db_user, an identifier that appears as a username for a MongoDB database associated with "rms-platform" and in the history of the GitLab dead-drop file used in the "antd-img-crop" repository hijack as "silviattech1129."

alexlopez-g/rms-platform, a backup GitHub repository hosting "rms-platform," with the user having contributed to a separate project named "Foxtopia-NFT/foxtopia-frontend" that includes a ".vscode/tasks.json" file mirroring tactics outlined in UNK_DeadDrop. The commit is signed with an email address "harper.danie95@gmail[.]com," which has been used to target designers on Contra with fake job offers.

asteara[.]org, a domain flagged as "related infrastructure" for UNK_DeadDrop that has been used to host or serve the Trigram back office.

pichi_finance_hr, a Telegram account that impersonates the human resources department of Pichi Finance, a decentralized finance (DeFi) platform that went offline in January 2025, and is likely used as a way to approach targets by posing as recruiters.

"Based on these infrastructure and tooling overlaps, we assess with high confidence that Trigram represents a continuation of the UNK_DeadDrop cluster, while illustrating an evolution in its delivery tactics," Stormshield concluded.

"Trigram stands out through the diversification of its initial access vectors and the remote retrieval of its final payload, rather than embedding it directly within the trojanized repository."

"This system automates the targeting of users on GitHub, orchestrates massive phishing campaigns through four email providers, monitors the compromise of each victim in real time, and launders the stolen funds through cryptocurrency wallets."

[7] The Hacker News - Hackers Found a Way Around GitHub SHA Pinning. This Webinar Explains How to Close the Gap
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 17:55:06 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/hackers-found-way-around-github-sha.html

[TAM METİN - 618 kelime]
Pinning GitHub Actions to an exact version of their code, known as SHA pinning, has become the go-to defense against software supply chain attacks. Attackers have already found a way around it.

In March 2026, the hackers who hijacked Trivy, a widely used open-source security scanner, planted malicious code that looked like it came from GitHub's official checkout tool. They even labeled it a security fix. Teams pinned to it would have passed every check.

The trick works because GitHub lets copies of a project, called forks, share code with the original. In May, the same technique hit the popular actions-cool/issues-helper tool, stealing credentials from the pipelines that ran it.

The lesson: pinning guarantees the code won't change, but not who wrote it.

That gap is the focus of an upcoming webinar, GitHub Actions Security Beyond SHA Pinning. If your team treats pinning as the finish line, save your seat to learn what it actually protects you from, and what it doesn't.

Immutability Is Not Provenance

Pinning earned its reputation. When the tj-actions/changed-files compromise repointed tags to a malicious commit across more than 23,000 repositories in March 2025, teams that had pinned to SHAs were unaffected.

GitHub now lets administrators enforce pinning as an organization-wide policy, and few security teams argue against it.

📅 Save Your Spot: Learn Why “Best Practice” Won’t Stop the Next GitHub Actions Attack

But a pinned SHA leaves three questions unanswered:

Authorship: A SHA doesn't tell you who wrote the commit. Any fork commit can be pinned.

Project membership: Org-level enforcement checks that a reference is a 40-character SHA, not that the commit actually lives in the upstream project.

Runtime: A Docker-based action pinned to a SHA can still build its image at runtime, pulling in dependencies that were never frozen.

With agentic development pushing more code through CI than ever, each of those gaps gets exercised more often and reviewed less closely.

GitHub's Lockfiles Help. They Don't Finish the Job.

GitHub's 2026 Actions security roadmap, published March 26, put deterministic dependency locking front and center. Workflow lockfiles entered public preview on October 14 and became generally available on October 28, 2026. They tie every run to recorded commits for actions and their transitive dependencies, even if a tag moves later.

That's real progress. Locked commits must exist on a branch of the source repository, which blunts fork-only impostors. But GitHub's own changelog is clear that locking "does not determine whether the dependency itself is safe." Commit authorship and runtime builds remain open; immutable releases only help when maintainers opt in, and lockfiles aren't currently supported on GitHub Enterprise Server.

Not sure where your pipelines fall in that gap? Register for the webinar for a clear-eyed breakdown of what GitHub's roadmap fixes and what it still leaves to you.

Led by Erika Heidi, Staff Developer Relations Engineer at Chainguard, the session draws a hard line between immutability and provenance, and covers:

What org-level pin enforcement actually verifies, and the false confidence it can create

How impostor commits work, and why GitHub's repository network makes them possible

What GitHub's Actions roadmap addresses, and what it doesn't

Other ways to close the trust gap, including a curated catalog of vetted actions

Platform and DevOps engineers who own CI/CD, AppSec and DevSecOps teams writing pipeline policy, and security leaders who signed off on SHA pinning as "done." If your workflows pull in third-party actions, this session is for you.

Attackers have already shown they can disguise a malicious commit as a hardening fix. The next one won't announce itself either.

Register now for GitHub Actions Security Beyond SHA Pinning, pick the session time that works for you, and find out what your pins aren't telling you.

[8] The Hacker News - Researchers Detail iPhone Flaw That Let Apps Record Audio Despite Denied Microphone Permission
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 17:44:36 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/researchers-detail-iphone-flaw-that-let.html

[TAM METİN - 816 kelime]
A now-patched flaw in Apple's audio service let apps on iPhones and iPads record audio even after the user tapped "Don't Allow" on the microphone prompt.

The first security updates with the fix came out on August 17. Neither Apple nor the two researchers who found the flaw report that it was used in attacks.

Nik Tsytsarkin of Meta Red Team X published the technical details last week. Mustafa Calap, a security engineer who reported the same flaw separately, published his own analysis the same day.

On iPhones, an orange indicator shows when an app is using the microphone. On one of the researchers' earlier test builds, that indicator stayed off while the app recorded.

In Tsytsarkin's tests, the prompt appeared the first time the app opened. Declining it did not stop the recording, and the prompt did not reappear on subsequent launches.

In the earlier build of his technical analysis, the system logs showed no record of the app using the microphone. They named the audio service itself as the recorder.

His demo video shows the indicator while the test app records with no microphone permission. He wrote that the video "was filmed on a later build that appears to have restored the orange microphone indicator."

Calap's test app recorded while its permission still read denied, according to his detailed write-up. No orange indicator appeared in the Dynamic Island or in Control Center. He does not say which of his two test builds that was.

Neither researcher describes a way for users to check whether an app recorded them before the fix. Calap reproduced the bypass on iOS 26.6, released July 27, and on an iPadOS 27 beta.

The bypass needed an app already installed on the device. His two test apps were sandboxed apps with no added entitlements. Their only privacy key was NSMicrophoneUsageDescription, which makes the microphone prompt appear.

In his tests, recording had to start while the app was in the foreground. Once started, it continued after the app went to the background and after the device was locked. The background version also set UIBackgroundModes = audio.

Both apps were installed from Xcode and never went through App Review. So the tests do not show whether App Review would have stopped such an app.

The Meta analysis compared two iOS 27 test builds. Neither researcher reports testing macOS, tvOS, watchOS, or visionOS, though fixes are listed for those systems too.

The bypass covered microphone capture through the audio service only. It did not give the app other privacy permissions.

These releases fix the flaw, which is tracked as CVE-2026-65339:

iOS 26.6.1 and iPadOS 26.6.1 (August 17), for iPhone 11 and later, iPad Pro 12.9-inch 3rd generation and later, iPad Pro 11-inch 1st generation and later, iPad Air 3rd generation and later, iPad 8th generation and later, and iPad mini 5th generation and later

iOS 18.7.11 and iPadOS 18.7.11 (October 26), for iPhone XS, iPhone XS Max, iPhone XR, and iPad 7th generation

macOS Tahoe 26.6.2 (August 17)

macOS Sequoia 15.8 (September 14)

tvOS 27 (September 14), for Apple TV 4K 2nd generation and later

watchOS 27 (September 14), for Apple Watch Series 9 and later

visionOS 27 (September 14), for Apple Vision Pro (all models)

The researchers describe no setting that blocks the bypass, so installing these updates is the fix.

The iOS 27 notes do not list the flaw, nor do those for later iOS 27 updates as of today. Meta's timeline says the fix shipped in iOS 27.0 beta 5 on August 10.

CISA gives the flaw a CVSS score of 5.0 out of 10. The agency's catalog of known exploited vulnerabilities does not list it, as of the catalog's update late last week.

Apple's notes for the August 17 updates describe the flaw only as one that may let an app leak sensitive user information.

Tsytsarkin wrote: "That description leaves out the most striking part: the app could record after permission was denied, without the orange indicator appearing."

In his own tests, the indicator was missing only on the earlier build. An app does not decide for itself whether it may record. That decision goes through the Transparency, Consent, and Control (TCC) system, which checks which process is asking.

The audio service, called audiomxd, accepted the app's own word about which process was asking. An audit token identifies a process. An app could send the service a token that named audiomxd itself.

The service then treated the app as itself. It took a shortcut meant for its own internal audio and allowed recording without asking TCC. The kernel itself provided the app with the details it needed to impersonate the service.

The fix makes the service check the real caller's identity, which the kernel supplies. Outside callers now need the private entitlement com.apple.private.audio.client-audit-token-override to replace the client identity the service relies on. An app cannot grant itself that entitlement.

[9] The Hacker News - New GitDoor and GraphDoor Backdoors Turn GitHub and SharePoint Into Covert C2 Channels
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 16:50:18 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/new-gitdoor-and-graphdoor-backdoors.html

[TAM METİN - 1088 kelime]
Government sectors in Asia and Africa have been targeted by an active cyber intrusion campaign that delivers to previously undocumented backdoors that leverage legitimate services like Microsoft SharePoint and GitHub for command‑and‑control (C2).

The activity has been codenamed Operation ShadowCloud by Kaspersky. The Russian cybersecurity company said it identified the campaign in early 2026.

"The threat actor behind it is abusing the legitimate MeshAgent utility to drop two brand-new backdoors," Kaspersky researchers Saurabh Sharma and Noushin Shabab said in a two-part analysis. "These implants hide their command‑and‑control (C2) traffic behind cloud services Microsoft SharePoint, GitHub, and Ably, allowing the implants to blend in with normal traffic, effectively evading network-based detections."

The modular backdoors, dubbed GraphDoor and GitDoor for their choice of the cloud platform used for C2, enable the download of additional plugins on demand and the execution of shellcode directly in memory to extend their functionality. Traffic between infected systems and the C2 servers is proxied by deploying a modified Erfrp client and Ably messaging service-based plugins.

The starting point of the attack has been traced back to a MeshCentral Agent, a legitimate remote access tool that's used for remote desktop control, command‑line access, and file management. The attack is said to have targeted an unspecified Taiwanese organization.

The exact method by which the utility is installed on the host remains unknown. However, signs point to the "reuse of a prior infection already present on the network," Kaspersky added.

The MeshCentral Agent is configured to contact a hard-coded C2 server ("wss://27.102.66[.]162:443/agent.ashx") and then fetch three payloads staged on the endpoint -

A legitimate binary used for DLL sideloading (start.exe, pythonw.exe, and zoomit.exe)

A rogue loader DLL used to unpack and execute the next-stage malware (jli.dll, SbieDll.dll, python39.dll, WINMM.dll, and libcef.dll)

An encrypted file containing the core payload

At least two different DLL loaders have been identified to date, each employing its own method to decode and launch the encrypted backdoor payload by injecting it into the current process's memory.

"Loader (Type 1) employs a hybrid scheme: first running the data through an RC4 stream cipher and then applying an XOR mask, while Loader (Type 2) simplifies the process by using a straight XOR operation alone," Kaspersky noted.

The end goal is to deploy backdoors like GitDoor and GraphDoor, which employ a number of encryption and obfuscation techniques like control flow flattening and nested helper functions to complicate analysis efforts and make it harder to trace genuine execution flow.

GitDoor, as the name implies, uses GitHub for C2. It relies on two distinct repositories for receiving command payloads ("/repos/LatonyCharnleyaW/Havo") and uploading the results of the execution ("/repos/LatonyCharnleyaW/Trix"). While the Havo repository is public, the Trix repository is private.

The creation date of these repositories suggests that preparations for the campaign have been underway since at least the end of 2024. GitDoor supports 26 commands that allow the backdoor to -

Perform file management

Launch a proxy, including using Ably

Load shellcode to Base64-encoded Common Object File Format (COFF) binaries and terminate the backdoor

The custom shellcode feature is used to download and run different modules that facilitate the following functions -

Conduct process injection using methods like Asynchronous Procedure Call (APC) injection

Enumerate and manage  Windows services

List files and directories, wipe file contents, and recursively delete a directory

Conduct network-related activity by displaying active TCP connections and modify firewall rules

Manage processes, including dumping the memory of the process with a specified process ID into a file and terminating specific processes

Execute commands using "cmd.exe"

Enumerate, create, and run scheduled tasks

Enumeration Windows Registry keys and values recursively and set a value in the Registry

Extract user information, along with local groups and its members

Add of a new Windows user account or add a user to a global or a local group

Record keystrokes to a file ("%APPDATA%\Microsoft\SystemCertificates\Cache\logs.dat")

Use Mimikatz to dump credentials from Windows LSASS

Capture screenshots of the entire screen or a specific window identified by its process ID

Extract WhatsApp's database decryption key

Initiate NTLM authentication to an attacker-controlled destination

Calculate system idle time and display the last time a user was active on the host using mouse movement and keyboard input

GraphDoor, on the other hand, opens a named pipe, creates a directory named "Intel_Log" inside the system's Application Data folder, and gathers system information to send back to the attacker.

To establish communications with the C2 server, it authenticates with a malicious application using "login.microsoftonline.com" on port 443 and uses the access token to communicate with the Microsoft Graph API and ultimately stage the data on the SharePoint drive.

The malware then fetches three plugin modules from the server and launches them in parallel to carry out screenshot capture, process listing, and keylogging. It also proceeds to reestablish contact with the SharePoint website to retrieve the commands to be executed on the machine.

The complete list of plugins supported by GraphDoor is as follows -

kl.ts, to log keystrokes at "%APPDATA%\Microsoft\SystemCertificates\Cache\logs.dat"

pr.ts, to list running processes

scr.ts, to take screenshots of the window that matches a supplied process ID and save them as JPEG images

moveDir.ts, to move the legitimate executable, the malicious loader DLL, and the encrypted backdoor file to specific target path

channel.ts, to launch the proxy

abchannel.ts, to use the Ably messaging service to proxy traffic

ab2channel.ts, a covert SOCKS5 proxy that tunnels TCP traffic through Ably's Pusher‑compatible WebSocket service

In addition, GraphDoor's custom shellcode capability expands on GitDoor to support two additional commands that allow it to run commands using "cmd.exe," as well as MZ executables.

The identity of the threat actor behind the campaign is unknown, but Kaspersky said it shares some degree of overlap with a China-nexus activity that Sophos tracks under the moniker Bronze Sandcastle. Tactical similarities have also been observed with prior campaigns undertaken by FamousSparrow and CopperTurtle (aka BlackTech).

"On several victim systems, these groups shared the same staging directories as the GitDoor backdoor, with their implants deployed within a one-day window of GitDoor deployment," the company concluded. "While this does not prove a direct link, it suggests that a Chinese-speaking group may be developing and/or utilizing the GitDoor and GraphDoor backdoors."

"By using cloud-based storage and messaging services, the actors blend in with normal traffic (as cloud API calls to Microsoft Graph, GitHub, and Ably are indistinguishable from legitimate enterprise usage, evading many network-based detections), and enable dynamic payload delivery, where on-demand plug-ins and shellcode are retrieved from cloud repositories, allowing for rapid updates and functional extensions without modifying host files."

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/new-gitdoor-and-graphdoor-backdoors.html, thehackernews.com, 17.11.2026)

[10] The Hacker News - Apple Tightens macOS App Permissions to Limit Data Access Amid AI Agent Security Risks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 16:37:31 +0530
Link: https://thehackernews.com/2026/11/apple-tightens-macos-app-permissions-to.html

[TAM METİN - 370 kelime]
Apple on Monday introduced new controls that allow Mac users to give more granular access to data they give to apps, including those that use artificial intelligence (AI) agents.

The changes, currently previewed in macOS 27.3 beta 2, relate to increased transparency and more options for two of its APIs: Device Control and Data Access, and Full Disk Access.

"Before granting an app such extraordinary access, users should understand that it puts all of the data on their Mac at risk," Apple said in a statement. "These changes are designed to help them do that and to empower them to make informed decisions about their own data and privacy."

To that end, Device Control and Data Access is being split into two settings: System & App Protection Override and Accessibility.

Other changes include -

A single method for granting permissions by selecting the requesting app from a list

Encouraging users to grant access to just the specific apps (for System & App Protection Override) or folders (for Full Disk Access) that are required for their workflows

Increased visibility into the range of data that apps will receive access to or have control over when users grant full permissions

An option for users to configure apps to ask every time they require access

A transparency indicator when one app is controlling another app using System & App Protection Override

Apple stressed that app permissions previously granted by users will remain intact, adding that developers should be prepared to make changes to their onboarding workflows to ensure their apps can handle scenarios where access is granted to only a limited set of folders or apps via these APIs.

In addition, apps that use Full Disk Access for disk backup or the new Accessibility settings for assistive technologies can request entitlements to offer the same experience for new installations that they offered before macOS 27.3.

"Currently, known apps aren't affected and will keep the same experience without an entitlement," the tech giant added. "Additional entitlement details will be available soon."

The development comes a little over a month after Apple began taking steps to tighten controls around the Full Disk Access macOS setting due to security risks posed by artificial intelligence (AI) agents.

[11] BleepingComputer - British Scattered Spider hacker sentenced to 6 years in prison
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 03:24:18 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/british-scattered-spider-hacker-sentenced-to-6-years-in-prison/

[TAM METİN - 564 kelime]
A leader of the Scattered Spider cybercrime collective has been sentenced to six years in prison in the United States for stealing millions in cryptocurrency and conspiring to hack a dozen companies.

US prosecutors charged 24-year-old British man Tyler Robert Buchanan and four other suspects in November with wire fraud and aggravated identity theft in relation to a series of phishing attacks between September 2021 and April 2023.

According to court documents, their victims include companies across a wide range of industries, such as telecommunications, technology, business process outsourcing (BPO), entertainment, and information technology (IT) suppliers, as well as virtual currency providers, cloud communications providers, and individuals.

Buchanan and his accomplices sent hundreds of SMS phishing messages to victim companies' employees' phones (while impersonating the companies or a contracted IT or BPO supplier) to trick them into entering sensitive information (including credentials and personally identifiable information) into phishing pages that looked like their employers' legitimate websites.

"Those thefts had severe, life-altering consequences for many victims and underscore both the seriousness and far-reaching impact of this conspiracy," the prosecutors argued in a sentencing memorandum.

"In April 2023, on a digital device found at Buchanan's Scotland residence, he possessed the names and addresses of numerous individual victims, including a text file that contained cryptocurrency seed phrases and login information for one victim's account," the US Justice Department added.

Buchanan was arrested in Palma de Mallorca, Spain, in June 2024, has been in US federal custody since April 2025, and pleaded guilty to crypto theft charges in April.

Three of his accomplices (Evans Onyeaka Osiebo, Ahmed Hossam Eldin Elbadawy, and Joel Martin Evans) were also charged in November 2024 with wire fraud, wire fraud conspiracy, and aggravated identity theft and are now serving multi-year prison sentences after pleading guilty.

Noah Michael Urban (also known online as Sosa and Elijah), a fourth conspirator and another Scattered Spider key member, has been sentenced to 10 years in prison after pleading guilty to wire fraud and conspiracy charges in April 2025.

Scattered Spider hacking collective

The Scattered Spider gang (also tracked as 0ktapus, Scatter Swine, Octo Tempest, Starfraud, UNC3944, and Muddled Libra) is a loose-knit group of young, English-speaking threat actors that orchestrates attacks through Telegram channels, Discord servers, and hacker forums.

Scattered Spider has also partnered with several Russian ransomware operations since early 2023, including BlackCat/AlphV, Qilin, and RansomHub.

This cybercrime group was previously linked to breaches at multiple high-profile companies and organizations, including Caesars, Riot Games, MailChimp, Twilio, DoorDash, and Reddit.

According to the FBI, they're using a wide range of tactics to breach corporate victims' networks, including social engineering, multi-factor authentication (MFA) bombing (targeted MFA fatigue), phishing, and SIM swapping.

Some Scattered Spider members have also previously been linked to "The Com," another hacking collective behind violent incidents and cyberattacks.

[12] BleepingComputer - Critical Java4Shell flaw allows RCE attacks on major Java applications
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 18:04:21 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/critical-java4shell-flaw-allows-rce-attacks-on-major-java-applications/

[TAM METİN - 685 kelime]
A critical vulnerability dubbed Java4Shell in OpenJDK, the open-source implementation of Java, can allow unauthenticated attackers to remotely execute code on popular enterprise applications, including Jenkins, Atlassian Jira, Confluence, and Keycloak.

Java is one of the most widely used programming platforms in the world, powering enterprise applications, web services, financial systems, and critical business infrastructure.

Because many applications use the same Java runtime, vulnerabilities in its core components can affect a wide range of different and unrelated applications.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-114938, is a memory-corruption flaw in Java's hardware-accelerated Base64 decoding functionality that can be exploited to disclose sensitive information from memory or achieve remote code execution (RCE).

Security researchers at Calif, who disclosed the flaw on Tuesday, rated it critical with a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2, while Oracle assigned it a lower severity rating of 8.1 (High) using CVSS 3.1.

Java4Shell affects OpenJDK 17.0.6 and later, as well as Java distributions built from the affected code, including Oracle Java SE, Amazon Corretto, Eclipse Temurin, Microsoft Build of OpenJDK, Azul Zulu, and Red Hat OpenJDK.

The vulnerability stems from a bug in Java's Base64 decoder, which processes encoded data commonly found in authentication, file uploads, and network communications.

According to Calif, specially crafted Base64 data can cause the decoder to overwrite portions of Java's memory, potentially allowing attackers to steal sensitive information or execute malicious code.

The researchers say that the memory corruption can be triggered with just 64 "A" characters followed by a single equals sign (=):

Older OpenJDK 17 versions require 128 "A" characters followed by the equals sign.

The flaw only affects a hardware-accelerated decoding function used on certain x86-64 processors that support AVX-512 instructions.

The researchers have been able to exploit this bug to perform remote code execution against a variety of widely used Java applications running with their default configurations, including:

Unauthenticated RCE: Keycloak, Atlassian Jira, Atlassian Confluence, JFrog Artifactory, Jenkins, and PingFederate.

Authenticated RCE: Apache Kafka Broker, Apache Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit, and Spinnaker.

Calif was also able to exploit the flaw in sample applications built using the Apache Struts and Spring Boot frameworks.

However, the researchers said Java4Shell does not have a universal exploit, and attackers must find an application-dependent way to deliver malicious Base64 data to the vulnerable decoder to achieve code execution.

The vulnerability also requires the affected hardware-accelerated AVX-512 decoding function to be used, which depends on supported x86-64 processors with AVX-512 VBMI capabilities.

Calif says researcher Quang Luong discovered the flaw using OpenAI's Codex AI coding assistant, which helped identify the vulnerability during an audit of OpenJDK's internals.

According to the researchers, Luong discovered the flaw in approximately one hour and developed an initial working exploit in another two hours, at a total AI usage cost of less than $200.

The company says AI agents also helped develop additional application-specific exploitation chains.

Calif has published a proof-of-concept demonstrating the memory corruption but has not released the weaponized exploits used against the affected enterprise applications.

Oracle released patches for CVE-2026-114938 on November 17 as part of its November Critical Security Patch Update, which addresses 655 security vulnerabilities across its product portfolio.

The company recommends that customers apply the available security patches as soon as possible.

For those who cannot immediately update their Java implementations, Calif recommends disabling the vulnerable hardware-accelerated Base64 functionality by launching OpenJDK with the -XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics option.

Alternatively, administrators running Java on x86-64 systems can use -XX:UseAVX=2 to disable AVX-512 instructions.

However, these workarounds are temporary mitigations, and all developers should update the Java runtimes to the latest version.

[13] BleepingComputer - Cloud keys leaked in Docker Hub images are stolen within seconds
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 16:27:17 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/cloud-keys-leaked-in-docker-hub-images-are-stolen-within-seconds/

[TAM METİN - 552 kelime]
Credentials and access secrets exposed in public Docker Hub images are being extracted and exploited by attackers within as little as eight seconds of publication.

Two researchers at Stony Brook University, Billy Tsouvalas and Nick Nikiforakis, measured the extraction speed by publishing a total of 300 honeypot images across eight public container registries.

Over a period of four months, the researchers exposed AWS, OpenAI, and Anthropic API keys, along with decoy SSH and database credentials.

They then monitored the use of these keys and placed detection tools to notify them when the images were built, ran, or had a shell opened.

The two researchers presented the experiment and the resulting findings yesterday at the ACM Conference on Computer and Communications Security (CCS).

They noted that their images were pulled more than 75,000 times, and the planted AWS keys received over 140,000 API calls from 5,000 unique IP addresses.

The AWS traffic showed a broad pattern, from checking whether the keys worked to listing available resources, and sometimes proceeding to consume them.

The fastest observed use of a planted AWS key came 7.86 seconds after the image was published on Docker Hub. The median time for the first API call was 51.19 seconds, while all 100 AWS keys were used within 15 minutes.

The exposed OpenAI and Anthropic keys were quickly hijacked to exhaust the test-account balances.

Docker Hub accounted for nearly all AWS activity, while Harbor, one of the other registries in the experiment, also saw some AWS key abuse.

The researchers dubbed the automated scanners that interacted with their test images “ContainerBots,” though this is more of a loose umbrella term, as the scans showed no evidence of being coordinated by the same operators.

Monitoring rarely detected anyone actually running the honeypot images, which led the authors to conclude that scanners generally extracted credentials by inspecting image layers directly.

“The scanners interacting with our images overwhelmingly do so through static analysis, e.g., unpacking image layers or querying the registry API to extract filesystem contents, without ever instantiating the container,” explain the researchers.

Exposing secrets this way is common in the real world [1, 2, 3], and revoking the leaked keys requires significant work and coordination.

The researchers recommend that Docker Hub scans images for secrets before pushing them live, and warn publishers if they appear to embed any secrets.

They also suggest restricting or rate-limiting access to Docker Hub’s public feed of recently published or updated images, to thwart mass-scanning tools.

Tsouvalas told BleepingComputer that they reported their findings to Docker in May, along with the full paper, but besides an acknowledgment, they received no follow-up replies.

A small-scale experiment they ran in August to determine whether any defenses had been implemented on the platform showed no changes.

[14] BleepingComputer - Japan takes Fugaku supercomputer offlline after cyberattack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 15:51:21 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/japan-takes-fugaku-supercomputer-offlline-after-cyberattack/

[TAM METİN - 436 kelime]
The Fugaku supercomputer operated by Japan's RIKEN Center for Computational Science has been taken offline following a security breach.

The organization says that between November 10 and 13, an unauthorized party exploited the public dashboard displaying Fugaku's operational status to gain access to multiple internal servers.

Although the research center has yet to determine the access method, RIKEN’s investigation confirmed that the intruder accessed some servers to plant malware that targeted user data.

Specifically, the organization says that the following details have been compromised:

Names, email addresses, and user IDs of 4,222 Fugaku users

Names and email addresses of 375 Rikyu users

SSH private keys and access keys stored on users’ computers

Research data files stored on users’ computers

RIKEN notes that the attacker used some of the access keys in attempts to connect to additional systems, but the investigation did not determine further unauthorized access or theft of research results.

The organization has not attributed the attack, and it’s still working to determine the exact intrusion method.

RIKEN’s Center for Computational Science operates the two supercomputers, making them available to researchers in academia and industry sectors.

Fugaku, which entered shared use in March 2021, is a 537 petaflops supercomputer used in drug discovery, severe-weather forecasting, and simulations of airborne virus transmission. Rikyu is a 64 petaflops supercomputer used in AI scientific research.

In response to the incident, RIKEN shut down Fugaku on November 14, disabled users’ login keys, and asked users to revoke and replace any credentials they had stored on the supercomputer.

RIKEN said Fugaku operations will resume only after it has ensured that it’s safe to do so.

The remediation effort includes addressing the exploited software vulnerability, rebuilding compromised servers, reviewing the network configuration, and implementing advanced AI-driven security measures.

Users are advised to revoke any SSH keys and other credentials stored on Fugaku, and watch out for suspicious emails impersonating RIKEN.

A RIKEN spokesperson told BleepingComputer that they have no more details to share about the incident at the time, but promised to publish updates when the investigation uncovers new findings.
