[16] BleepingComputer - Researchers hijack Hugging Face account for popular Kokoro AI model
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 13:21:24 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/researchers-hijack-hugging-face-account-for-popular-kokoro-ai-model/

[TAM METİN - 696 kelime]
A Lack of ownership verification on the Hugging Face platform allowed researchers to take control of Kokoro, a text-to-speech model used in projects from Google, NVIDIA, AMD, Intel, and Amazon Web Services.

An attacker hijacking the account could use it as a distribution channel in supply-chain attacks, potentially leading to data theft or code execution.

However, the researchers demonstrated a stealthier attack: they modified the model's weights so it produced audio that differed from the input text, without changing the underlying application code.

Because only the weights change, there is no malicious code or signature for malware scanners to detect, the researchers say.

"We changed what this model says by fine-tuning its neural weights. Same input text. Same voice file. Same configuration. Same inference code — not a single line touched in the application that runs it," said security researcher Alon Hertz.

Kokoro is an open-weight model that converts text into speech. It supports multiple languages and has more than 50 voice presets.

Although its developer, Hexgrad, has not pushed an update since April 2025, the project continues to be popular, with nearly 13 million monthly downloads on Hugging Face. The GitHub repository and the Python package on PyPI also went silent around the same time.

Kokoro is supported by developer tools from Google, NVIDIA, and Intel, including software for voice agents and on-device speech synthesis. AMD and Amazon Web Services have also published guides demonstrating its use in AI-agent workflows.

Hertz and his team took control of the Kokoro account on Hugging Face by simply registering the domain used by the original owner and resetting the login password.

Over the past weekend, the researchers registered the “hexgrad.com” domain listed on Kokoro’s public DONATE.md file and used it to perform a password reset on the publisher’s account.

They then ran a controlled test in which they modified Kokoro’s model weights in 87 seconds using a gaming GPU, causing the model to produce poisoned output.

Hertz says the team tested the modified Kokoro model locally using its normal inference pipeline, but never uploaded the modified weights to Hugging Face, as that would likely put at risk many production systems depending on the model.

The researcher underlines that an update would still carry all the hallmarks of a legitimate release, as tests from another account showed that Hugging Face would not flag anything as suspicious.

Despite the takeover, the publisher appeared the same, just like the file delivery route, the format, and the architecture. File sizes would be similar, and the release would contain no malware.

A broader trust problem

The researcher notes that the supply-chain risk from compromised publisher accounts distributing altered weights via routine updates to systems that automatically fetch new revisions is significant.

Kokoro was just a high-profile example the team used to highlight the issue.

Hertz says even non-hijacked accounts could pose a risk, as anyone can curate an ostensibly legitimate image for a period of time, then push a malicious update at some point.

“Hugging Face does not vet publishers. It doesn’t verify whether an account is still held by its original, legitimate owner — or by whoever quietly took it over last week. It doesn’t vet the models or datasets those accounts upload,” Hertz says.

“The same unvetted weights and datasets flow through mirrors and converted versions, GitHub releases, package registries, deployment guides, and direct downloads across the entire open-model ecosystem,” added the researcher.

Hertz reported the issue to Hugging Face, hoping for quick action, but received no response.

BleepingComputer has also contacted Hugging Face for comments, but we have not received a response yet.

The project says this update includes a security fix to improve relay defenses against denial-of-service attacks.

"One fix in particular is especially important for relay DoS defenses related to IPv6 handling," explains the project.

Tor is working on additional defenses and expects to issue further releases in the near future.

Relay operators are urged to upgrade to version 0.4.9.17 as soon as possible. The project said no additional action is required after installing the update.

The announcement did not provide any other details as to who is behind the DDoS attacks or when they began.

The Tor Project thanked relay operators for helping respond to the attack, pointing to active discussions on its tor-relays mailing list and asking operators to continue providing support.

"Thank you to all… except our attacker(s)."

[18] BleepingComputer - Flipper Devices launches $399 ‘Flipper One’ Linux-based multi-tool

Flipper Devices also relied on Kickstarter to launch its iconic Flipper Zero in 2020, since then selling over 1 million devices.

[19] BleepingComputer - Inside Modern Phishing Kits Built to Steal Microsoft 365 Sessions
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 10:01:11 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/inside-modern-phishing-kits-built-to-steal-microsoft-365-sessions/

[TAM METİN - 888 kelime]
It’s never been easier to steal credentials, thanks to a growing number of phishing kits that give aspiring attackers everything they need in one convenient interface. Some now come with admin dashboards and round-the-clock support, like a SaaS business, and they’re designed specifically to steal Microsoft 365 session tokens.

Even entry-level attackers who replay a stolen token can end up in an active session where the login and MFA are already complete, with instant access to the victim’s cloud resources and email.

Huntress researchers analyzed three of these modern phishing kits. This article breaks down how they capture and replay tokens and what defenders can do about it.

What’s inside a phishing kit

A phishing kit is a pre-packaged set of code and templates, with instructions for launching a phishing campaign and using the data it steals for malicious activity. Basic kits are often simple HTML and script files that spin up a single fake login page.

More advanced kits feature admin dashboards that attackers use to track victims, modify phishing page content, and relay credentials in real time.

To understand why these kits are so dangerous, it helps to look at how they turn phishing lures into usable sessions.

2026 Cyber Threat Report: State of Phishing

Phishing has been around forever, but it’s not slowing down. It’s just getting sneakier.

The Huntress State of Phishing report walks through the top phishing trends over the last year, from the lure themes and impersonated brands to the psychological tricks designed to slip past your defenses. And it shows you how to turn your people from a vulnerability into your strongest line of defense.

When attackers pivot from passwords to sessions

Phishing kits now give attackers usable authenticated sessions through three tradecraft techniques.

In a device code phishing attack, the victim authenticates directly with the identity provider while the attacker quietly captures the resulting authorization and uses it. The flow reduces friction for attackers because the victim never types credentials into an unfamiliar page, so the entire experience feels like a normal sign-in.

AitM attacks reveal the limitations of MFA; the threat actor relays the authentication flow in real time, either with a fake login page or by intercepting an exchange within the legitimate app. The victim completes the MFA prompt while the attacker captures the resulting session information.

This is why successfully completed MFA does not make the session trustworthy on its own.

Token theft is far more valuable to attackers than password theft. After an attacker gets their hands on a sign-in token, a phishing kit like Kali365 (which we’ll dig into below) can help them replay it into an active session, where they can access cloud resources and the victim’s email accounts.

If you suspect replay, look past the original phishing page and investigate how the token was used.

These techniques don’t live in isolation. In the last few months, Huntress has analyzed several advanced phishing kits targeting Microsoft 365 tokens, including EvilTokens, Knight Office, and Kali365.

Three phishing kits targeting Microsoft 365 tokens

In early 2026, Huntress discovered EvilTokens as part of a device code phishing campaign that abused the Railway Platform-as-a-Service (PaaS) platform as a token-harvesting and replay engine.

A joint effort between Microsoft’s Digital Crimes Unit and the FBI later in the year led to the arrest of one of its alleged operators.

Knight Office is an adversary-in-the-middle (AiTM) phishing kit that steals valid Microsoft 365 session tokens and feeds them into an operator console. Attackers can replay those sessions and set up persistence across tenant accounts.

The third kit, Kali365, bundles a full Phishing as a Service (PhaaS) operation into a single control panel so operators can capture tokens through device code flows and reverse-proxy lures, replay them into live Microsoft 365 sessions, maintain access, and scale activity with built-in AI features.

Kits like these help attackers walk away with persistent access, not just usernames and passwords.

What every defender can do about token theft

A single signal is only one piece of the attack. Follow the full sequence from the lure to authentication, token issuance or theft, device registration, and mailbox activity to understand identity compromise in your environment.

Defenders can look for these signs of a stolen session:

Start by checking for successful sign-ins from unfamiliar cloud infrastructure.

Start by checking for successful sign-ins from unfamiliar cloud infrastructure.

Then hunt for device code authentication and automated token refresh.

Then hunt for device code authentication and automated token refresh.

Review new devices, authentication methods, and mailbox activity.

Review new devices, authentication methods, and mailbox activity.

On the response side, keep these priorities in mind:

Containment must address identity artifacts, not just the original lure.

Containment must address identity artifacts, not just the original lure.

Revoke refresh tokens, then remove unauthorized devices and Windows Hello credentials.

Revoke refresh tokens, then remove unauthorized devices and Windows Hello credentials.

Restrict or block device code authentication where you can, and investigate mailbox access, forwarding rules, and outbound phishing.

Restrict or block device code authentication where you can, and investigate mailbox access, forwarding rules, and outbound phishing.

Keep going after containment. Monitor for replay or re-entry, and keep hunting after the original phishing domain disappears.

Join us for Tradecraft Tuesday to dig into more attacker tradecraft like this with Huntress researchers.

[20] BleepingComputer - Court clears brothers of $25 million Ethereum crypto heist charges
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 04:44:22 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/court-clears-brothers-of-25-million-ethereum-crypto-heist-charges/

[TAM METİN - 519 kelime]
US District Judge Jessica Clarke has acquitted two brothers accused of stealing $25 million in cryptocurrency in about 12 seconds through a scheme that exploited a bug in the Ethereum blockchain.

Clarke's decision comes one year after a mistrial in the same case, after jurors failed to agree on whether to convict or acquit former Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT) students Anton Peraire-Bueno and James Peraire-Bueno in this first-of-its-kind scheme, as Reuters first reported.

"The question before the Court is not whether the Peraire-Buenos' conduct was right or moral, or even whether it violated uncharged crimes. It is simply whether the evidence at trial, construed in a light most favorable to the Government, could support wire fraud and related convictions in this case," the federal judge said.

"Because the Government failed to prove that the Peraire-Buenos made any material misrepresentations in the execution of their scheme, the motion is granted, and a judgment of acquittal is entered for Anton Peraire-Bueno and James Peraire-Bueno."

The Peraire-Bueno brothers were arrested in Boston and New York in May 2024 on wire fraud and money laundering charges and were facing 20 years in prison for each count if convicted.

According to prosecutors, the brothers allegedly manipulated blockchain transaction validation processes by accessing pending private transactions, altering them, obtaining victims' cryptocurrency, and rejecting requests to return the stolen funds.

The indictment claimed they learned their victims' trading behaviors while preparing the attack (starting in December 2022) and took measures to hide the stolen proceeds and their identities.

They were also accused of using multiple cryptocurrency addresses and foreign exchanges and setting up shell companies. After the attack, they also allegedly moved the stolen crypto assets through a series of transactions to obscure their source and ownership.

The prosecutors also said that, while planning and executing the attack, the brothers deployed a series of "bait transactions" designed to identify "victim traders," exploited a vulnerability in the MEV-Boost relay code, causing the relay to release the full content of a proposed block prematurely, and reordered the proposed block to their advantage.

In the next stage of the scheme, they allegedly reordered the block on the Ethereum blockchain, which resulted in the theft of approximately $25 million in cryptocurrency from the victim traders.

Throughout the process, the brothers also allegedly searched online for information on carrying out the attack, concealing their involvement in the Ethereum exploit, and laundering the criminal proceeds through cryptocurrency exchanges with lax verification procedures.

Prosecutors also said they hired attorneys with expertise in cryptocurrency, extradition procedures, and the crimes outlined in the indictment.

[21] Krebs on Security - Attackers Used Optimizely to A/B Test Malware on Top Websites
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 02:52:04 +0000
Link: https://krebsonsecurity.com/2026/11/attackers-used-optimizely-to-a-b-test-malware-on-top-websites/

[TAM METİN - 1095 kelime]
At least 178 top web destinations unwittingly served a variety of malware exploits against visitors over the past 24 hours thanks to a security vulnerability involving Optimizely, a company that helps websites customize user experiences and test out new features. Experts say the incident, which saw data-stealing malicious software silently injected into sites like the BBC, CNN, Equifax, Ledger and Zoom, was exacerbated by the reality that few popular websites enforce strict rules on the sources of third-party content.

The Zoom.com homepage loading the malicious JavaScript file from Optimizely. Image: blog.report-uri.com. Click to enlarge.

Helme noted the affected websites also served exploit code custom made to attack known vulnerabilities in older Apple iPhone and iPad devices. Known by such names as “DarkSword” and “Coruna,” these are zero-click exploit kits that chain together a half-dozen security holes to seize complete control over outdated devices and extract data.

“The important lesson here is that trusted JavaScript doesn’t always remain trustworthy,” Helme wrote. “A website can be compromised through a third-party dependency without its own code or infrastructure ever being breached.”

Contacted for comment, Optimizely said that on November 16, 2026 it “identified a security incident in which an unauthorized party exploited a vulnerability in the Optimizely Experimentation to add malicious JavaScript to some customer projects.”

“We acted swiftly to contain the incident and have fully remediated the identified vulnerability,” reads a written statement shared by Optimizely. “These measures included blocking access to the vulnerable path, deploying a fix, removing the injected code from affected projects, ending active sessions, revoking tokens, and resetting access for affected user accounts. We have also implemented additional protections to prevent malicious code from being delivered in Cloudflare. While the incident has been fully remediated, we are working with third-party experts on a comprehensive investigation.”

Many top web properties no longer fully display content unless visitors agree to let often dozens of third parties serve JavaScript, a powerful web scripting language that enables interactive content. JavaScript code is run on the user’s device, and it can read information displayed in the user’s browser and even modify a website’s appearance or content.

One reason JavaScript is so heavily used is that it enables fairly precise browser fingerprinting and other ways of tracking visitors and clicks. According to the 2025 Web Almanac by HTTP Archive, the median homepage loads at least 22 JavaScript files. But as the incident with Optimizely shows, a compromise at a third-party vendor’s website can quickly snowball into a supply chain attack against downstream websites that end up serving visitors tampered and malicious JavaScript files.

Zach Edwards is a researcher at Infoblox and the co-founder of the ad-tracking service DecryptAds. Edwards said Optimizely is a vendor which often collects data outside of consent flows, with their JavaScript snippets being embedded as close to the top of the page as possible to ensure that it’s one of the first things to render, which is important since the product is primarily built for A/B testing.

“So to make the product work as expected, this code is setup to fire immediately on-page load, and not restrict rendering behind a consent dialog box,” he said. “This means that this supply chain attack didn’t require any clicking or consent to trigger — most of the impacted clients would immediately serve up the payload as soon as someone loaded the page, further increasing risks from this type of JavaScript snippet being compromised.”

Websites can minimize the chances that a breach at a third-party content provider spirals into a supply chain attack by taking full advantage of “Content Security Policies” or CSPs, which specify the domain names that are allowed to serve third-party content. But Edwards said most websites — apart from perhaps the largest e-commerce, social media and financial platforms — don’t even bother with CSPs, or else tend to leave them overly permissive for marketing reasons.

“With literally all websites which embed 3rd party JavaScript resources, if you don’t have a content security policy then if any of those partners get compromised, new domains can be injected into your website via JavaScript which won’t be stopped,” he said. “We’re in the age of JavaScript supply chain attacks and yet almost no one is talking about why content security policies were invented and how this is largely the solution for operators, even though it’s not always simple to get it initially deployed.”

How broadly deployed were CSPs at the websites impacted by the Optimizely attack? According to Report URI, 68 of the sites had no CSP at all, while 50 of them had a CSP that still allowed the malware payload and the data theft. The company found only 24 of the affected sites (15 percent) had CSPs that fully blocked the malicious payload.

Image: blog.report-uri.com.

The overall deployment of CSPs across the Web’s busiest sites is somewhat lower — about 10 percent — according to Helme’s site crawler.ninja, which scans the top million websites daily for security issues. Helme said he hopes this incident will encourage more sites to adopt effective CSPs, noting that it could have been far worse: By Report URI’s estimates, the purveyors of this malware attack managed to steal only around $7,500 worth of virtual currency from affected users, as well as an unknown number of payment card records.

Helme said he often compares the current widespread lack of CSPs to the Wild West.

“Organizations are running all this code, but they don’t know what it’s doing,” Helme said in an interview with KrebsOnSecurity. “This is why we keep seeing such large scale breaches in places [where] there are no checks and rules in place on the code we’re running, so this was kind of inevitable. Once an attacker gets to the point of running code on your site, the question is what do they want to do with that and can they write the script to do it. Now with AI that’s not a problem anymore.”

Internet users can control the JavaScript elements allowed to run on webpages by using simple script-blocking plugins like NoScript. But these tools have always only been effective for diehard security and privacy nerds who are willing to put up with sifting through dozens of JavaScript requests on each new website.

Happily, modern ad blocking software can handily block most third-party JavaScript requests. Ten years ago, the standard security advice for end users was to install antivirus and enable a firewall. These days, your ad/script blocker is your firewall. We explored several options and approaches to blocking ads in our August 2026 story, Who’s Tracking You?

[22] Security Affairs - Apple Services Disrupted as NSA Network Goes Down and Equinor Faces Cyberattack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 20:52:24 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203354/security/apple-outages-add-to-growing-concerns-over-technology-service-disruptions.html

[TAM METİN - 644 kelime]
Apple Services Disrupted as NSA Network Goes Down and Equinor Faces Cyberattack

Apple and NSA services faced outages as Equinor reported a cyberattack, highlighting the risks facing increasingly connected infrastructure.

Apple customers faced widespread service problems on November 17, with issues affecting iCloud, the App Store, Apple Music and AppleCare on Device.

The disruption came after a week of technical failures involving major technology companies, banking services and US government networks. At the same time, Norwegian energy company Equinor disclosed a cyberattack against some of its IT systems.

Apple’s System Status page reported problems with iCloud Account and Sign In, affecting all users. The issue prevented customers from signing in to their Apple accounts or caused repeated requests for their Apple ID passwords. Some users also reported difficulties activating new iPhones, suggesting that the disruption was affecting essential account services, not just individual apps.

According to TechCrunch, the problems also affected the App Store, Apple Music and AppleCare on Device. Apple had not issued a detailed public explanation at the time of publication, beyond listing the incidents on its status page. The company said in a statement that the issue was caused by an internal update.

“Many of our systems are back up and running after an internal update caused some outages this morning,” reads the statement. “We’re working to get the remaining systems online as soon as possible.”

The Apple outage attracted attention because it followed several other major disruptions. Microsoft had experienced a widespread failure affecting Outlook and Teams, while JPMorgan Chase customers had reported problems with online banking. A separate outage also affected the National Security Agency’s classified internal communications network, known as NSANet.

The NSA incident raised particular concerns because the network supports classified communications. However, the cause and full extent of the disruption remained unclear. Reports cited by Forbes Middle East said there was no evidence that the NSA outage resulted from a cyberattack. A system patch had reportedly been underway before the disruption.

The growing number of outages does not mean they share the same cause or are part of a coordinated cyber campaign. Technical problems, software bugs, configuration errors and infrastructure failures can affect major services without any cyberattack. Claims about AI-powered attacks should not be made without evidence.

On the same day, Norway’s energy company Equinor reported a cyberattack against some of its IT systems. The company said the attack had not affected its operations or production, and it had found no evidence of data theft. Equinor said it had activated its response teams and notified the relevant authorities. The company stated that “there were no signs data had been stolen.”

The Equinor incident added a confirmed cyberattack to a day already marked by several service outages. However, these events should not be confused. Equinor confirmed a cyberattack, while there was no evidence that the outages affecting Apple and the NSA were caused by malicious activity.

Cyberattacks can compromise systems and data, while ordinary technical failures can disrupt services that businesses, public institutions and millions of people depend on. Both require effective incident response, clear communication and the ability to maintain essential operations when technology fails.

For now, there is no evidence that the recent outages affecting Apple, Microsoft, the NSA, banks and aviation systems are connected. Nor should every major service disruption be treated as evidence of a cyberattack. As Forbes reported about the NSA incident, “there was no evidence the disruption was the result of a cyberattack.”

The distinction matters. In a period of heightened cyber threats and growing dependence on digital services, organizations need to investigate incidents carefully, communicate what they know and avoid confusing coincidence with attribution. A technical outage is not necessarily a cyberattack, but it can still expose serious weaknesses in the systems on which society depends.

[23] Security Affairs - Fireworks AI Breach Exposes Uber Data and Highlights Supply Chain Risks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 19:11:20 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203339/data-breach/fireworks-ai-breach-exposes-uber-data-and-highlights-supply-chain-risks.html

[TAM METİN - 1001 kelime]
Fireworks AI Breach Exposes Uber Data and Highlights Supply Chain Risks

Hackers used compromised Fireworks AI credentials to steal personal data and driver’s license information belonging to nearly 150,000 Uber users.

Uber is notifying nearly 150,000 people after attackers used compromised credentials belonging to one of its technology providers to access personal information stored in the company’s external environment.

“On October 9, 2026, a service provider notified Uber that an unauthorized third party had compromised the service provider’s internal credentials and used them to access Uber’s account with the service provider. After being notified of this incident, Uber immediately severed data connections with the service provider and launched an investigation. On October 13, 2026, our investigation determined that the unauthorized party had used the compromised credentials to access Uber data connected to the service provider.” reads the data breach notification shared with the California Attorney General’s Office. “We are notifying you about this incident because our investigation concluded that your personal information was among the information accessed.”

The stolen data includes images of drivers’ licenses and other identity details, turning a security incident at an AI infrastructure supplier into a significant privacy issue for the ride-hailing company.

The incident dates back to October, when Uber learned that an unauthorized party had compromised the internal credentials of a third-party provider. According to notifications filed with U.S. state authorities, the provider informed Uber on October 9 that its credentials had been stolen and used to access the company’s data.

Uber found that attackers had accessed the data on October 7, two days before the company was notified. Uber then cut off the affected provider and began investigating the incident. The company said the provider’s stolen login details did not give attackers access to other Uber systems. This suggests that the breach was limited to the supplier’s environment.

The provider was identified as Fireworks AI, a cloud platform that gives developers access to open AI models. It allows companies to run and fine-tune these models without managing the required infrastructure themselves.

The stolen data includes drivers’ names, home addresses, dates of birth and driver’s license numbers. Attackers also accessed images of driver’s licenses and information from other government-issued IDs, according to Uber’s disclosures to regulators.

The scale of the incident became clearer after Uber filed a notification with Oregon authorities, reporting that 149,346 individuals had been affected. The company has also submitted breach notifications in several other states, including California, Iowa, Massachusetts, South Carolina, Texas and Vermont. However, Uber hasn’t disclosed how many of those affected are drivers, nor whether the total number could increase.

TechCrunch reports that Uber said it already had security alerts enabled to detect unauthorized access to its environment at Fireworks. They didn’t work because the attackers used legitimate internal credentials belonging to the compromised provider.

“In Uber’s case, the company said it had alerts enabled to detect unauthorized access to its Fireworks environment, but because the hackers used Fireworks’ own internal credentials, “no alerts were triggered.”” states TechCrunch.

This is a clear example of how access controls and monitoring systems can fail to identify malicious activity when an attacker operates through an account that the environment already trusts.

The breach is part of a wider security incident at Fireworks AI. In a November 9 update, the company said attackers had accessed customer account and billing details, as well as secrets, datasets and unfinished AI models belonging to a small number of customers. Fireworks hired Palo Alto Networks Unit 42 to investigate the incident and notified law enforcement.

The company linked the attack to a financially motivated cybercriminal group that wanted to sell stolen AI access tokens through unauthorized services. This type of attack is known as LLMjacking. It allows criminals to use stolen credentials or tokens to access AI services and run models at the victim’s expense. No known cybercriminal group has publicly claimed responsibility for the Uber data theft.

Uber was not the only Fireworks customer affected. Notion and Cursor also confirmed that attackers had accessed some of their data during the incident, according to TechCrunch. The case shows how a security breach at one technology provider can expose several customers, even when they have no direct connection with one another.

Fireworks has taken steps to contain the incident with the help of Palo Alto Networks Unit 42. The company strengthened its security by replacing compromised credentials, limiting privileged access, isolating affected systems, restricting access to stored data, and improving logging and monitoring to detect further suspicious activity.

Fireworks pointed out it had found no evidence of further successful unauthorized access to the customer-facing Fireworks platform after October 25.

Uber is offering affected individuals free credit monitoring and fraud assistance through Cyberscout, a TransUnion company. Recipients have 90 days from the date of their notification letter to enroll. Reuters reported that Uber had not identified any misuse of the exposed information at the time of its November 13 report.

For affected drivers, the exposure of license images and personal details creates risks that go beyond the immediate breach. Criminals could use this information to support identity theft, impersonation attempts, fraudulent applications or highly targeted phishing campaigns. Combining a person’s identity details with knowledge of their professional relationship with Uber could also make fraudulent communications more convincing.

This is also Uber’s second publicly known data breach in 2026. In August, the company was reportedly investigating a separate incident involving its Uber Freight trucking business, after an extortion group claimed to have stolen data.

The latest incident raises a broader question about how organizations monitor the access they grant to external technology providers. Uber had alerts in place, but those controls didn’t identify the activity because the attackers used credentials that the system considered valid. The company said it had not identified any misuse of the data to date. That is encouraging, but it doesn’t remove the need for affected individuals to remain alert to identity fraud and targeted social engineering.

[24] Security Affairs - Fridge Controller Attacks: Researchers Find Six Zero-Days in an Hour
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:41:36 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203324/security/fridge-controller-attacks-researchers-find-six-zero-days-in-an-hour.html

[TAM METİN - 1089 kelime]
Fridge Controller Attacks: Researchers Find Six Zero-Days in an Hour

Midnight Blue found six unpatched remote takeover flaws in Danfoss AK-SM800A controllers in under an hour. Agencies agree: take them off the internet.

Since 11 October, attackers have been hitting internet-exposed fridge controllers from Danfoss and Copeland across Europe, starting in France. They triggered defrost cycles, spoiled food worth tens of millions of euros and caused fires in some cases. SentinelOne warns that attribution is open, and analysts shouldn’t assume one actor behind every incident.

Now cybersecurity firm Midnight Blue says one of those controllers is in worse shape than anyone assumed.

“Midnight Blue researcher Carlo Meijer found several pre-authentication remote code execution 0-day vulnerabilities in the Danfoss AK-SM 800A in less than an hour with casual manual inspection.” reads the analysis pubòished by Midnight .

Researcher Carlo Meijer found six pre-authentication remote code execution zero-days in the Danfoss AK-SM800A in less than an hour of manual review, using firmware anyone can download from Danfoss’s site. Every version up to and including 5.3.2 is affected, and there are no CVE numbers yet because coordinated disclosure with Danfoss is still running.

All six flaws are stack-based buffer overflows. Five can be exploited remotely without logging in because they affect web services on the controller. The main application also runs with root privileges and lacks basic security protections, making exploitation more dangerous.

Danfoss Strict mode adds HTTPS and session checks, blocking most unauthenticated attacks. However, the sixth flaw affects the component that enforces this protection. Researchers found that it can crash the service and may allow code execution with further work.

An attacker who gains root access could change controller settings, trigger defrost cycles, hide activity from logs, or install malware that survives factory resets and firmware updates. They could even erase the device’s memory and make it unusable. This could leave refrigeration systems offline for days.

All six are stack-based buffer overflows. Five sit in web request handlers that need no login, inside sm_app, a large program that handles almost everything on the device. It uses hundreds of unchecked strcpy calls, has no stack canaries and no PIE.

“Of course, remotely triggering a crash is bad in itself, but being able to leverage the buffer overflow to obtain remote code execution is much worse. Any reasonably experienced exploit developer will agree exploiting straightforward stack-based buffer overflows on ARM Linux without exploit mitigations is very straightforward. And if they don’t, there’s probably a frontier or open weight model out there willing to help them agree.” continues the report. “The only challenge (if you can call it that) left to overcome is the fact that strcpy() stops copying bytes when it hits a null byte. If we wish to gain remote code execution, we inevitably need to write several addresses containing null bytes. Thus, we need to somehow overcome this inherent limitation of strcpy() and while we won’t share our approach for now it’s not exactly rocket surgery. Also, the exploited application runs as root (because of course it does).”

Danfoss’s Strict mode, the default since version 4.0, requires HTTPS and session tokens and closes most of the unauthenticated surface. That’s why CERT-FR recommended it. The sixth flaw sits in sm_fcgi_app, the gateway that enforces Strict mode, so in an emulator with Strict mode on it crashed, and with more work the researchers say it can become code execution.

The damage could go beyond another defrost cycle. The controllers don’t use secure boot, so an attacker with root could change settings, start defrosts, hide it from the logs and plant code that survives a factory reset and a firmware update. They could also wipe the flash and make a controller unusable until someone replaces it. That could leave freezers unavailable for days.

Nobody has shown this in the wild. Germany’s BSI says they are no aware of attacks in the wild exploiting one of these six flaws, and neither agencies nor researchers link them to the autumn incidents. Earlier AK-SM800A bugs were already documented in 2023 and 2025, and public exploits for them appeared within 48 hours of the French attacks, before the second wave on 2 November.

There’s no patch. The researchers say version 5.4.0 doesn’t fix the flaws, since the firmware was likely built on 29 October, before the second wave and before their reports. The BSI first said 5.4.0 wasn’t affected, then corrected itself and said it can’t rule that out. Danfoss told trade press on 12 October that most units hit in France were older models or ran outdated firmware.

Exposure is still large. Censys shows hundreds of Danfoss systems online, though some are surely honeypots, plus over 1,000 Copeland and Dixell controllers and hundreds from similar vendors like Eliwell that haven’t been targeted yet. Danfoss business units hold IEC 62443-4-1 certification for secure development, and Midnight Blue says asset owners should see how little such certificates say about a product. The certificate says secure development, while the compiler flags say otherwise.

Midnight Blue says that applying individual patches and relying on Strict mode are not enough. It recommends reducing internet exposure instead. Owners should identify every controller accessible online, including those connected through an integrator’s 4G modem, and restrict remote access to VPNs, private networks or strict firewall rules. Broad geographic restrictions and blacklists are not sufficient.

Belgian and Irish agencies also advise checking controller settings for unauthorized changes, including temperature levels, defrost schedules, alarms, user accounts and remote access. If a compromise is suspected, the BSI recommends changing all passwords and checking that connected equipment still works properly.

However, owners should not simply disconnect their refrigeration service providers. They should first review contracts and insurance requirements and establish secure remote access so that monitoring and maintenance can continue.

The report conludes with the following mitigations:

“Instead, we strongly recommend asset owners and system integrators to take the following course of action for all Danfoss, Copeland, and Dixell controllers and preferably for similar controllers by other vendors as well:

Ensure controller management interfaces are only accessible through either a VPN, a private APN, or highly restrictive firewall-enforced IP whitelist (broad IP geofencing or blacklisting is not sufficient). As per CERT-FR recommendations, IP whitelisting is an intermediate mitigation and not long-term sustainable due to the risk of configuration drift and change management challenges.”

Inventarise which controllers are internet exposed, not just on asset owner public IP ranges but also through system integrator 4G modems

Follow me on Twitter: @securityaffairs and Facebook and Mastodon

(SecurityAffairs – hacking, Fridge Controller)

[25] Security Affairs - Infoblox Confirms Supply Chain Breach After Attackers Access Sensitive Data
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 10:54:35 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203309/data-breach/infoblox-confirms-supply-chain-breach-after-attackers-access-sensitive-data.html

[TAM METİN - 818 kelime]
Infoblox Confirms Supply Chain Breach After Attackers Access Sensitive Data

A breach at an Infoblox supplier exposed customer network snapshots and employee data, highlighting the risks of trusted third-party access.

Infoblox, one of the major players in cybersecurity and network infrastructure, is investigating a data breach after attackers gained access to internal company information through a compromised third-party provider. The incident is more serious than the initial disclosure suggested, while subsequent reporting indicates that the stolen information includes customer network configuration data and sensitive employee records.

The company confirmed the incident on November 16, explaining that unauthorized access involved an external provider responsible for data migration.

In a statement, a spokesperson for Infoblox told TechCrunch that the company is “investigating a security event involving unauthorized access to certain company information through a compromised third-party data migration vendor.”

“We immediately activated our incident response procedures, took steps to contain the incident and are working with leading outside cybersecurity experts to investigate the facts, understand the scope and address any confirmed risks.”

The compromised supplier reportedly provided SharePoint migration services and had legitimate access to Infoblox’s Microsoft environment. According to sources cited by The Register, attackers abused the vendor’s accounts to extract information directly from SharePoint.

“According to sources, the compromised third-party data migration vendor had trusted access to Infoblox’s SharePoint system, and the intruders used this vendor’s accounts and access to exfiltrate data directly from Microsoft SharePoint.” reported The Register. “This data included configuration information related to some customer’s NIOS (Network Identity Operating System) deployments. These are saved snapshots of customers’ network data, such as DNS, DHCP configurations, Grid databases, and reporting logs, that can be used to fully recover the appliance or grid in case of a system failure or faulty upgrade.”

Infoblox hasn’t identified the supplier or explained how its systems were initially compromised.

The stolen material reportedly includes snapshots from customer deployments of NIOS (Network Identity Operating System), Infoblox’s platform for managing core network services. These files contain DNS and DHCP configurations, Grid databases and reporting logs. Administrators use this information to restore network appliances and infrastructure after failures or unsuccessful upgrades.

This is an important difference. The stolen data does not include passwords or login details, but it gives hackers a clear map of a company’s internal network, system names, and setup. Depending on what is in the leaked files, attackers could use this information to plan more specific attacks against affected customers.

The data breach also exposed personal information about Infoblox’s employees. According to an email from CEO Scott Harrell seen by The Register, the stolen files included bank deposit details, contact information, birth dates, and Social Security numbers for staff members and their family dependents.

Infoblox has begun informing the people affected and offering help. The Register reported that impacted employees received $100 to buy a password manager, along with two years of free credit monitoring and identity protection for themselves and their families. The company also warned staff to be on the lookout for scam emails, fake phone calls, fraud, and other suspicious messages.

Infoblox pointed out that the incident hasn’t affected its commercial technology, at least according to the evidence collected so far.

“We have sent notifications about this security event and are providing support and guidance as appropriate,” the Infoblox spokesperson told The Register. “Based on our investigation to date, we have no indication that any Infoblox products, cloud services, software updates or build-and-release processes were involved. Our priority is to fully investigate and resolve this issue.”

The statement is relevant because the company provides DNS, network automation and security services used by organizations to manage critical parts of their IT infrastructure.

However, the distinction between a breach of corporate information and a compromise of customer-facing products doesn’t eliminate the potential consequences for customers. Network configuration snapshots can contain operational details that organizations would normally restrict to trusted administrators and support personnel.

The incident also raises questions about the level of access granted to external service providers. Data migration projects often require suppliers to handle large volumes of internal information, sometimes across systems connected to corporate identity platforms. If a supplier’s credentials are compromised, the attacker’s access can extend well beyond the supplier’s own environment.

For security teams, this case reinforces the need to treat third-party access as part of the organization’s attack surface. Supplier accounts should have limited permissions, access should be monitored, and temporary privileges should be revoked when migration or maintenance work ends. Organizations should also review what information they share with external providers and whether exported backups or configuration files contain credentials and other sensitive details.

Infoblox hasn’t disclosed how many customers or employees were affected, the full volume of stolen information, or whether the attackers contacted the company with demands. At this time, the identity of the compromised supplier also remains unknown.

[26] Security Affairs - OPTIMJACK: BBC, CNN and Zoom Among Targets of Optimizely Script Hijacking
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 09:38:44 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203289/malware/optimjack-bbc-cnn-and-zoom-among-targets-of-optimizely-script-hijacking.html

[TAM METİN - 1089 kelime]
OPTIMJACK: BBC, CNN and Zoom Among Targets of Optimizely Script Hijacking

OPTIMJACK: Attackers hijacked Optimizely scripts on 178 organizations’ websites, spreading malware to steal crypto, payment card data and target iPhones.

Visitors to 178 organizations received malicious JavaScript through Optimizely Web Experimentation, a service sites use to test page changes. Report URI researchers found it in 188 of the 1,611 snippets they checked, on sites including Zoom, the BBC, CNN, Salesforce, and Equifax. Optimizely’s own main site and login page carried it too, and some researchers call the campaign OPTIMJACK.

In an incident notice posted at 01:02 UTC on 17 November, Optimizely published a notice confirming the incident and saying that attackers exploited a vulnerability in Experimentation to add the malicious JavaScript to some customer projects.

“Earlier today, Optimizely identified a security incident in which an unauthorized party exploited a vulnerability in Experimentation and used it to add malicious JavaScript to some customer projects.” read the company’s notice. “We’ve fixed the vulnerability and removed the injected code from affected projects.”

The vendor says it fixed the flaw, removed the code and added protections. It hasn’t said how the flaw worked, how many projects were affected, when the fix went in, whether the attacker still has access or what customers should do.

Websites using Optimizely load a small piece of code from the company’s CDN. This code includes a section called Project JavaScript, where customers can add their own code to run on every page. The attacker added a malicious script to this section without changing the rest of the code. Because the file still came from Optimizely’s official domain and used the company’s certificate, browsers treated it as trusted and ran the malicious code.

“The attacker wrote their own code into the Project JavaScript of customer projects, and the rest of each snippet was untouched. The file was still served from cdn.optimizely.com, with Optimizely’s certificate, from a host that every affected site already trusted.” continues the report.

Even websites that hosted the snippet on their own domains were affected because the malicious code was added to the same Optimizely project. Report URI says this was not a cache poisoning attack. The CDN simply delivered the modified code it had received. Investigators still don’t know how the attacker gained permission to modify projects belonging to so many different customers.

The researchers found three versions of the injected loader. The first was a normal script tag pointing to 1688360[.]cc/track.js. The second hid the same address using Base64 encoding, making it harder to find during a simple text search. The third included the entire malicious code directly in the page, allowing it to run even if the external domain was blocked. Smartsheet’s code was cleaned up but later infected again with the hidden version, suggesting the attacker still had access.

The main malicious script, called track.js, carried out several activities. It replaced legitimate cryptocurrency wallet addresses with the attacker’s addresses in webpage text, forms, links, clipboard content and outgoing requests. It targeted Bitcoin, Ethereum, TRON, Solana, TON, Litecoin, Bitcoin Cash and Dogecoin.

The script checked that wallet addresses were valid before replacing them. According to StepSecurity, it also looked for payment-related words such as “wallet” or “deposit” near the address. This means users copying cryptocurrency addresses from affected websites could unknowingly send their money to the attackers.

On iPhones and iPads, the code loaded a script from two .icu domains that picked an exploit chain by iOS version. Coruna handled iOS 13 to 17, DarkSword handled iOS 18, and a separate chain for iOS 26 only collected device details. Visitors on iOS 18.6 or earlier saw their page reload every 60 seconds as the kit retried, which is hard to miss. The kit’s own comments mark iOS 18.7.3 and later, and 26.3 and later, as patched.

Nobody reports a real iPhone taken over. Cynet retrieved the second stage with an emulated iPhone browser and describes conditional delivery, not a compromise of a physical device.

The money side is small so far. Report URI counted about $7,040 in the attacker’s wallets by 19:25 UTC on the 16th, all within roughly nine hours. StepSecurity puts it near $6,600, notes that two of the addresses were in use before the injections, and says the chain doesn’t show which transfers came from victims.

The first injection appeared at 04:21 UTC in the BBC’s snippet, loading webshell[.]lol. Report URI ties that injector to the same actor, who had also planted it through Kissmetrics, StorifyMe and Minute Media scripts since late the previous week and on Turkish sites since 17 September. The payload was rebuilt 26 times that day, and new wallets were added but never removed.

Cleanup began around 20:40 UTC, and by 21:52 only TalkTalk’s snippet still carried the code. A clean snippet today may still have served the code on Monday, so check the history.

Report URI also checked the content security policies of 256 affected hosts. 68 had none and 50 let the payload and the data theft through. Another 17 blocked data theft with a connect-src rule while address swapping still worked, and 24, or 15%, blocked the payload.

Allowing cdn.optimizely.com in a website’s security policy does not protect it, because the malicious code came from that same trusted domain. The security rules called ‘strict-dynamic’ and ‘unsafe-eval’ can also allow the malicious code to run. Optimizely’s own documentation recommends allowing ‘unsafe-eval’. HSBC’s security policy blocked the main malicious script and prevented data theft, but the attackers’ backup code still ran directly on the page. Websites that allowed only the external sources they actually needed were better protected.

StepSecurity also recommends asking Optimizely which project IDs were modified, checking logs from 08:00 UTC on Monday and avoiding third-party scripts on checkout pages.

If you visited an affected website that day, update your iPhone or iPad to iOS 18.7.3 or later, or version 26.3 or later. Before sending cryptocurrency, always check that the wallet address is correct. When possible, verify it on your hardware wallet rather than relying on copied text. If you entered payment card details, monitor your bank statements for suspicious transactions. Report URI has saved copies of all infected snippets, including their hashes and timestamps, and can share them with affected organizations and CERTs on request.

“The important lesson here is that trusted JavaScript doesn’t always remain trustworthy.” concludes the report. “A website can be compromised through a third-party dependency without its own code or infrastructure ever being breached.”

[27] Security Affairs - Germany: Greiz Hospital Reopens Emergency Room After Ransom Attack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 08:20:03 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203279/cyber-crime/germany-greiz-hospital-reopens-emergency-room-after-ransom-attack.html

[TAM METİN - 574 kelime]
Germany: Greiz Hospital Reopens Emergency Room After Ransom Attack

Greiz hospital reopens its emergency room after an extortion attack. Investigators are probing the case, but no state links have emerged.

The Greiz-Ronneburg district hospital in Thuringia reported its system failure on Saturday evening, 14 November. By Sunday morning the emergency room was out of service. On Monday evening, 16 November, it started to reopen gradually.

“The hacking attack on the Greiz-Ronneburg district hospital was an extortion attempt, according to district spokesperson Uwe Müller. The extortion message reached the hospital on Sunday.” reports the website MDR. “Müller did not provide further details.”

District spokesman Uwe Müller says the attack was an extortion attempt, and the message reached the hospital on Sunday. He gave no details. Earlier, the district wouldn’t say whether anyone had demanded a ransom, and called that a matter for the state criminal police.

The hospital continued to care for patients during the outage. Managing director Tommy Jehmlich said there were enough staff on site and all patients received treatment. Planned treatments also went ahead, while doctors decided what other care they could provide on a case-by-case basis. People needing emergency care were told to call 112, and the emergency control center in Gera decided which hospitals should receive them.

The hospital is now accepting emergency patients again, working with local emergency services. However, problems with digital treatment plans remain, and several systems were affected. The hospital has not commented on whether patient data was compromised, citing the ongoing investigation. It is also still unclear how the attackers got into the network or how much damage they caused.

The prosecutor’s office and the state criminal police are investigating. According to the district, police are following a specific lead, and MDR reports they’re investigating a suspect. No name or group has been made public.

Interior Minister Georg Maier, of the SPD, talked about the case on Monday while presenting the state’s constitutional protection report in Erfurt. He didn’t comment on the attack’s background, and said the case shows how tense the security situation is in Thuringia and Germany.

“On Monday, Interior Minister Georg Maier (SPD) presented the new report on the protection of the constitution.” continues MDR. “Hybrid attacks like the one on the Greiz clinic have become commonplace in Thuringia – the state is increasingly under scrutiny.”

Keep two things apart. A ransom demand is how criminal gangs work, and nobody has tied this attack to a state or to any group. A hostile climate and an ordinary extortion crew can both exist at once, and the evidence will show which one hit Greiz.

Calling this a hybrid attack before investigators confirm it does not help. It only risks spreading the wrong message. It makes a hospital attack look like an act of war when, so far, the evidence points to a criminal extortion attempt. We should stick to the facts: the hospital’s systems were disrupted, they received a ransom message, and the emergency room is gradually reopening.

This offers an important lesson for other hospitals. Redirecting emergency patients worked, but digital treatment plans remain a problem. Hospitals need tested paper-based backups and clear plans for sending patients to other facilities when their systems go down. These practical measures matter more than labels. Until investigators provide more details, the hospital director remarks that patient safety is their top priority.

[28] Security Affairs - From Report to Tested Detection in Hours: The ARTEX Signature Pack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 16 Nov 2026 11:50:46 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203230/hacking/from-report-to-tested-detection-in-hours-the-artex-signature-pack.html

[TAM METİN - 824 kelime]
From Report to Tested Detection in Hours: The ARTEX Signature Pack

The researcher MalwareMustDie released an open ARTEX detection pack in seven formats, built on behavior and a repeatable playbook, because attackers now turn weeks into hours.

The researcher MalwareMustDie released a public detection pack for ARTEX, the open-source AI pentest framework that criminals now run against real targets. He published it quickly because campaigns built on this framework are compressing intrusion timelines from weeks into hours.

The pack comes in seven formats: Sigma, including five behavioral correlation rules, Sentinel KQL, Suricata 7, Snort 3, osquery, YARA and a STIX 2.1 bundle. The IOCs expire after a year by design, and the behavioral rules don’t expire. If you read this as an IOC list, you’ve missed the point.

“The real release is the workflow: how to turn the next ARTEX-style attack into detections as fast as the attacker moves.” reads the report. “ARTEX is an LLM harness. Swap the model, prompts or infra and every IOC changes. What changes much less is how an agent behaves: machine-speed recon chains, ~1/s Password Sprays with rotating UAs, self-correcting API retries, systematic record enumeration. So the pack is behavior-first.”

ARTEX is a framework that connects AI models with tools. If attackers change the AI model, prompts or servers, the signs of an attack can also change. Many ARTEX indicators found on compromised systems are created by the AI model or the tools it uses, rather than being built directly into the framework.

For this reason, the researcher focused on sequences of suspicious actions rather than individual strings. These include automated reconnaissance at high speed, password attacks with about one attempt per second and changing user agents, API requests that automatically retry with corrections, and systematic searches through database records.

Every indicator in our detection plan goes through eight checks. These cover its source, reliability, classification, format and testing. Confirmed ARTEX indicators trigger alerts first. More generic indicators come with a warning and are never used alone to raise an alert. For example, the use of a curl user agent is common in legitimate traffic, so it should not trigger a security alert by itself.

Every indicator in the playbook passes eight gates, from source check to tier, format and testing. Confirmed ARTEX indicators alert first, and generic strings carry a warning and never fire alone. A curl user agent appears in plenty of harmless traffic, so nobody should get paged for it.

“Act on what the identity does, not where it comes from.” continues the report. “The Global Administrator grant came from Microsoft-internal private IPv6, so the ConnectWise app-ID rule now fires on role self-assignment or credential addition regardless of source IP. Source IP is enrichment only.”

MalwareMustDie tested the playbook on four campaigns, with an LLM helping and humans signing off every verification step. The ConnectWise Cloud Backup case from October 2026 shows why. A vendor’s app credential was compromised, and service principals then gave themselves directory roles, with one becoming Global Administrator from a Microsoft-internal private address. Microsoft said its own systems weren’t compromised, and attackers planted secrets that expire in 2028. A rule that trusts the source address would miss this.

The Korean financial case taught a different lesson. Attackers pulled about 25,000 records over roughly 30 hours, around 14 a minute, from peripheral systems like a broker loan inquiry service and an employee app. Volume thresholds miss that, so we count distinct records read per identity over time. The Japanese wave, reported by JPCERT/CC and GMO, exploited known bugs and misconfigurations, with no zero-days.

The expert also left things out on purpose. No backdoor trigger values, no private individuals and no victim names appear in the pack. Indicators he couldn’t verify wait in a hold queue until someone confirms them.

For network teams, the researcher added an experimental Zeek approach. One layer checks who you’re talking to, and another tracks what’s said and how often. Zeek can’t see sign-ins to Entra ID, though, because that traffic never crosses your perimeter.

The pack has moved since release. He updated it to v1.1.3 after traffic tests, added data from the FBI flash report that came out the same day, and confirmed Sophos X-Ops indicators that had been on hold. A chronology of public ARTEX reporting from October and November is in Appendix F. The quick Shodan scan on release day wasn’t comforting.

The full report includes the detection playbook, rules and indicators of compromise (IoCs). The expert encourages other researchers to test them, identify weaknesses and report both false positives and true positives.

Attackers use LLMs to complete in hours what once took weeks. Defenders must keep up with this speed while ensuring that humans remain involved in the process.

Defenders can use LLMs to compress “new report → tested detection” into hours too, as long as humans keep the verification gates.

Follow me on Twitter: @securityaffairs and Facebook and Mastodon

(SecurityAffairs – hacking, ARTEX)

[29] SANS ISC - A phishing page interested in MX records, (Tue, Nov 17th)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 10:56:38 GMT
Link: https://isc.sans.edu/diary/rss/33526

[TAM METİN - 646 kelime]
A phishing page interested in MX records

I’ve mentioned before in some of my diaries that it can be instructive to look at what appear to be “generic” phishing messages in detail from time to time, since some of them might prove not to be as generic as they initially appear. I came across a good example of this while going over phishing messages delivered to our handler inbox over the past few weeks, when a message that, at first glance, appeared to be a fairly ordinary attempt at stealing e-mail credentials turned out to lead to a page interested in collecting a little more information than just the victim's password.

As you can see, the original message was not especially noteworthy on its own, being a spin on the usual “your password will expire today” approach…

The link pointed to an HTML page hosted on Google Cloud Storage, with the recipient's e-mail address included in the URL fragment:

hxxps[:]//storage[.]googleapis[.]com/alx2/03/1a/we.html#[recipient_e-mail_address]

After opening the link, the recipient would be presented with a generic login form, personalized using the domain part of their e-mail address. The page also attempted to use a screenshot of the legitimate website of the recipient's organization as its background, which it retrieved using the thum.io service. This didn't work quite as intended for isc.sans.edu, since the screenshot service returned an error message instead of the requested image. However, when I modified the URL to contain an e-mail address from another domain, the resulting page looked considerably more convincing.

Looking at the source code revealed a couple of layers of URL-encoding and additional JavaScript obfuscation. Once these were removed, the page turned out to contain a fairly typical credential-harvesting kit, which supported multiple languages and attempted to collect not just the submitted password, but also the visitor's public IP address, geographic location, browser information and language. This information, along with a timestamp, was supposed to be sent to the attacker using the Telegram Bot API.

Such extensive collection of information is not particularly unusual. In fact, another phishing page that I examined recently collected a similarly extensive set of information and also sent it to the attacker using Telegram. In the present case, however, one additional field included in the information sent to the attacker caught my attention – it was labeled "MX Record".

A closer look at the corresponding code revealed that the JavaScript actually performed a DNS-over-HTTPS query for MX records associated with the victim's e-mail domain, using Google's public DNS service. The relevant parts of the code (slightly simplified and reformatted for readability) looked as follows:

The retrieved records were then included in the message containing the stolen credentials:

The use of MX records in phishing is certainly not new. For example, Infoblox documented a phishing-as-a-service operation called Morphing Meerkat in March 2025, which used DNS-over-HTTPS queries for MX records to identify victims' e-mail service providers and dynamically select appropriate phishing templates[1].

In the present case, however, the MX records weren't used to customize the phishing page. Instead, the lookup was performed as part of the credential collection process, and its results were included in the information sent to the attacker.

Of course, MX records are publicly available, and there is nothing preventing an attacker from looking them up independently. Nevertheless, including them with each set of stolen credentials would help the attacker automatically identify the e-mail infrastructure used by the targeted organization, which could be useful for further attacks or for prioritizing the collected credentials.

Whether the threat actor behind this particular phishing actually used the collected MX records in this (or any) way is impossible to say. Still, having the phishing page perform a little additional reconnaissance on the victim's organization, and including the results with the stolen credentials, seems like a noteworthy detail in an otherwise fairly ordinary phishing kit…

[1] https://www.infoblox.com/blog/threat-intelligence/a-phishing-tale-of-doh-and-dns-mx-abuse/

Jan Kopriva

Your board doesn't ask how many alerts your team closed. It wants to know what your security investments are worth in terms it already uses: revenue protected, losses avoided, and costs reduced.

[31] Cyberscoop - A vulnpocalypse bug worthy of attention: Researchers disclose critical flaw in Java with help of AI
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 22:24:39 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/java4shell-critical-java-flaw-openjdk/

[TAM METİN - 894 kelime]
A vulnpocalypse bug worthy of attention: Researchers disclose critical flaw in Java with help of AI

Security researchers on Tuesday published details of a critical flaw in Java, one of the world’s most used programming languages, that could let an attacker take over servers without a password.

The group behind the find, Calif Research, said AI coding tools did much of the work. Oracle Corp. shipped a fix the same day it released a November update covering 652 flaws across its product line.

The flaw is tracked as CVE-2026-114938 and nicknamed Java4Shell. It is a memory-corruption bug: code that writes data where it does not belong, which can crash a program or, at worst, let an attacker run their own commands. The buggy component is the Base64 decoder in OpenJDK, the free Java runtime that underpins software across banking, aviation and telecommunications, and that, by Calif’s count, runs at 90% of Fortune 500 companies.

Calif rated the flaw 9.2 out of 10 on the CVSS scale, where anything above 9 counts as critical. The group said an unauthenticated attacker, one who needs no username or password, could corrupt a server’s memory, read nearby data in a manner similar to the 2014 Heartbleed bug, and escalate that access into full control.

The bug lives in an unusual place: hand-written processor instructions that OpenJDK uses to make Base64 decoding faster. The optimization runs only on chips that support a feature set called AVX-512; ARM64 systems are unaffected. Calif found the decoder breaks on a specific input: 64 ‘A’ characters followed by a single equal sign, the padding mark Base64 uses to round out incomplete data.

The code then miscalculates a length and writes 64 bytes into a buffer, a small block of memory meant to hold as few as 50, overwriting whatever sits next to it. The program rejects the bad input afterward, but the surrounding memory is already damaged.

On its own, that corruption usually crashes the program. Calif showed that an attacker who carefully arranges what sits in the surrounding memory, a technique known as heap grooming, can turn the corruption into control of the server.

The group reported unauthenticated code execution in widely used products including Keycloak, Jira, Confluence, Jenkins and PingFederate, and demonstrated a working break-in against Keycloak, a widely used open-source login system. Authenticated execution, requiring a valid account, was shown in Kafka, Solr and Elasticsearch. Researcher samczsun showed Java programs can also be compromised simply by connecting to a malicious server.

OpenJDK published a security advisory Tuesday, and Oracle shipped patched Java builds Tuesday afternoon.

The find adds to a fast-growing catalog of vulnerabilities surfaced with AI. Calif said researcher Quang Luong found the bug in about an hour auditing OpenJDK with OpenAI’s Codex, for under $200 in computing tokens, and that AI agents produced most of the group’s exploit chains nearly on their own. It was the group’s second major AI-assisted find in recent weeks, after last month’s disclosure of php2shell.

In its write-up, the group called this the era of “the centaur hacker, part human, part AI,” and said the hunt for the bug felt like “looking for copper and found gold.”

Calif also framed the bug as a byproduct of software’s shared complexity: the AVX-512 speedup shipped with every copy of Java though few programs need it, so an optimization became a critical risk for all. The group said there is no universal exploit and each target requires tailored steps, and it is withholding product-specific exploit details until users have had time to upgrade.

No exploitation in the wild had been reported as of this article’s publication, and none of the CVEs related to the issue have been added to CISA’s Known Exploited Vulnerability list. However, the agency published a notice calling for a patch late Tuesday.With Calif researchers already proving that unauthenticated RCE is possible in widely deployed apps, researchers tell CyberScoop the likelihood of real-world exploitation is high, especially given how fast attackers can leverage artificial intelligence.

“We’re talking about a vulnerability in one of the most widely used software runtimes in the world, with initial exploit development taking just a few hours,” said Gal Elbaz, co-founder and CTO of Oligo Security. “That doesn’t mean every Java application is vulnerable or that exploitation will be straightforward. Attackers still need to account for specific hardware, runtime configurations, and application behavior. But the technical barriers are clearly surmountable, and organizations should treat this as a serious exposure that requires compensating controls capable of blocking exploitation while patches are rolled out.”

Update, 11/17/26, 6:00 p.m.: This story was updated with information about a CISA notice and comment from Gal Elbaz.

[32] Cyberscoop - GSA says post-quantum work on PIV cards shows new encryption can work on old tech
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 21:19:45 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/gsa-post-quantum-encryption-piv-cards/

[TAM METİN - 564 kelime]
GSA says post-quantum work on PIV cards shows new encryption can work on old tech

As federal agencies revise their strategies for implementing post-quantum encryption by President Donald Trump’s new 2030 deadline, agency leaders and federal contractors are mulling over how to adapt the new protections to older, legacy technology and address gaps in visibility over federal encryption protocols.

Dan Pomeroy, deputy associate administrator at the Office of Technology Policy at the General Services Administration, said his agency focused first on the smart Personal Identity Verification (PIV) cards that grant employees access to GSA facilities, computers and the authority to sign official documents.

Pomeroy said agency technologists ran new post-quantum encryption algorithms directly onto the card chip, issued new credentials, and built the surrounding policies, certifications and hardware security modules to support the transition.

Post-quantum cryptography tends to require larger keys and signatures, along with a fast, reliable network connection to process larger amounts of data. That could be a problem for assets like satellites, which have weaker connections. But in many cases, agencies can switch to newer algorithms using the same systems, data and devices already in use throughout the government.

“That work demonstrated that quantum-resistant credentials are not theoretical, they are achievable with the cards we have today, the technology we have today,” said Pomeroy Tuesday at FedTalks presented by FedScoop.

Pomeroy also said the government constantly needs to test for bugs and interoperability issues. His agency works with contractors to make sure they use post-quantum algorithms “from the start” and bring the necessary infrastructure to support the agency’s encryption efforts.

An executive order issued earlier this year moved the mandatory federal deadline for completing the post-quantum transition from 2035 to 2030. Major private-sector organizations like Google have set similar internal timelines of 2029 for their own encryption switchovers, citing similar concerns.

The order cut by more than half the time agencies were originally given to transition their public key encryption. While national security officials have cited threats like “harvest now, decrypt later” in pushing for faster adoption, experts say that encryption switchovers are often measured in decades.

Vanessa Hunt, general manager for federal markets at IBM, said many federal agencies have “blind spots” in their network, particularly in areas where “the sensitivity of the data outlives the approach protecting it.”

Additionally, encryption protocols are often spread across different datasets, identities, infrastructure and networks, making it difficult to map everything that needs to be upgraded.

“I think the biggest blind spot right now is just the visibility,” said Hunt. “I think it’s easier to know where our sensitive data lives, it’s less easy to know where the cryptography that protects it is.”
