[33] Cyberscoop - How CISA is prioritizing companies for talks about security improvements
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 20:52:59 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/cisa-100-critical-infrastructure-companies-fed-talks/

[TAM METİN - 617 kelime]
How CISA is prioritizing companies for talks about security improvements

The Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency has identified approximately 100 companies that own critical infrastructure for talks on hardening their systems because of how vital they are to the nation, CISA acting director Nick Andersen said Tuesday.

That figure does not cover everyone CISA wants to speak with, because those companies have so many interdependencies with other firms, he said. And the conversations are going to vary wildly from company to company, he told CyberScoop.

“For the initial workstream, we’re probably talking about around 100 companies,” Andersen said after speaking at FedTalks, produced by FedScoop. “We want to be focused on being able to gather what are those lessons learned that are most appropriate coming out of it, what works and what doesn’t, and the problem with each one of those different companies.”

To generate the list, CISA went through an intergovernmental Homeland Defense Working Group that identified companies that provide functions deemed most important to the economy, public health and safety, national security and defense critical infrastructure.

Then, Andersen said, the agency overlaid  five to six years of intelligence about what cyber adversaries appeared to value most, such as where they were pre-positioning or where their research and development budgets were going. Companies that appeared on both lists became the targets for “initial engagement,” which also meant involving the right sector risk management agencies.

So hypothetically, if the decision is that a particular port is especially important, CISA would talk to the port operator. “But I also need to have a conversation with their energy provider, with their telecommunications provider, et cetera, et cetera, et cetera,” he said.

In his FedTalks speech Andersen said the ideal is to have conversations that include IT and operational technology providers, along with cybersecurity providers. He said the goal is to advance the agency’s “CISA 2030” strategy, which focuses on resilience, minimum viability and related priorities.

“It’s going to continue to result in several significant, important conversations over the coming weeks and months about, what does it mean to appropriately invest?” Andersen said. “What does it mean to collaborate with one another on that longer-term resilience as we move forward? That’s going to have to be a collective effort across our communities.“

So far, he said, the conversations have been productive.

“A lot of this has also included things like CI-Fortify assessments with those companies, which have been really really good conversations for us to be able to have around, what does it mean to be able to plan for isolation and recovery?” Andersen said.

Andersen, and other Trump administration officials, have spoken in recent months about the need to be selective in how they prioritize vulnerabilities and hacking targets.

[34] Cyberscoop - Cyberattack or no, parade of tech outages highlight need for resilient systems, CISA acting director says
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 19:58:05 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/cisa-nick-andersen-cyber-resilience-outages/

[TAM METİN - 654 kelime]
Cyberattack or no, parade of tech outages highlight need for resilient systems, CISA acting director says

A cavalcade of tech outages is underscoring the need to build systems with resilience and outcomes in mind — regardless of whether it’s to withstand a cyberattack or something else, the head of the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency said Tuesday.

Acting director Nick Andersen said “it’s been a highly disruptive last couple of days” marked by flight ground stops, the Microsoft Outlook global outage and banking slowdowns, but that “I do think it’s a it’s a good illustration of the types of things we’ve been talking about over the course of the last year or so.”

“Ultimately being able to prevail by being able to demonstrate resilience against disruptions, whether they occur because of things like an IT outage or a bad patch, or a modernization update gone awry, or because of a malicious malicious cyber incident — we want to make sure we’re engineering in the resilience that is going to be able to anticipate for and ultimately overcome those outages and those impacts,” Andersen said, speaking at FedTalks, presented by FedScoop.

The cause matters less than trying to think about what the final picture ought to be, he said.

“At the end of the day, people just care about the outcomes,” Andersen said. “They care about clean drinking water continuing to flow into their houses. … They care about groceries being on grocery store shelves. They care about just being able to live their lives without having to plan for consistent, significant disruptions.”

On social media, a lot of discussion is about the “vulnpocalypse” brought about by artificial intelligence’s rapid vulnerability discovery, he said. As a result, “perhaps forgotten” are concepts like building and testing for resilience, he said.

That’s a point of emphasis in the “CISA 2030” strategy, he noted.

After his speech, Andersen referred a reporter to comments from Department of Homeland Security Secretary MarkWayne Mullin and Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy about last week’s Federal Aviation Administration ground stop. But Andersen added that “there’s nothing we found that indicates that there was any malicious cyber activity that went into that.”

He also referred a question about the National Security Agency’s internal communications system going down to the Defense Department.

This week, the Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Committee is scheduled to hold a hearing for Michael LaSapio to serve as the permanent director of the agency. Should LaSapio win confirmation, Andersen said he would go where the administration wants him.

“I’m going to continue to continue to serve wherever people give me the opportunity to serve,” he said. “I think we’re all looking forward to our nominee getting his day with the Senate and being able to have the opportunity to continue to talk about CISA and why its mission is so important. And I know that’s something that people recognize across you know both the executive and the legislative branch, but it’ll be nice to be able to see him have that opportunity to talk about it with some of the senior most decision makers there in the Senate Homeland Security Committee.”

[35] Cyberscoop - Pakistani hackers are spying on defense workers with fake job sites
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:00:00 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/pakistani-hackers-defense-workers-fake-job-sites/

[TAM METİN - 478 kelime]
Pakistani hackers are spying on defense workers with fake job sites

Hackers possibly aligned with the Pakistani government have targeted Indian defense contractors and military personnel in a cyberespionage campaign to install spyware on victim devices  — but their tactics could also hit the defense sector elsewhere around the world, according to research out Tuesday from Lookout.

The mobile security firm, in research first shared with CyberScoop, said it observed activity dating back 12 months that remains active using the GravityRAT malware commonly deployed by the Pakistani-aligned group known as SpaceCobra or Cosmic Leopard.

What stands out is how far hackers went to  make it believable, with fake job recruiters building realistic, detailed websites to trick victims into downloading the malware.

One website featured an “our clients” section with logos of defense firms such as Airbus, BAE Systems, Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, Rheinmetall, Rolls-Royce, Saab, Safran and Thales.

Some of the websites’ social engineering was specific to India, some of it was not. In at least one case, a website made no reference to India. “Nothing like that,” said Parth Bhatt, senior manager for security intelligence research at Lookout.  That’s why it also shows that it could be also broadly targeted towards defense personnel anywhere.”

Furthermore, he said, “one interesting thing that we have noted is that they also have a button to download the app from Google Play Store, but as soon as someone clicks, it just pops up that ‘Hey, our app is under review at Play Store, so for now, please just download it from here.’”

GravityRAT, active since 2015, is designed to steal data. It can collect contact directories, call logs, texts, WhatsApp messages and more, Lookout noted.

[36] Cyberscoop - Alleged ransomware group member extradited from Italy to face US charges in hospital, school extortion case
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 13:03:59 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/roman-khlynovskiy-ransomware-extradition/

[TAM METİN - 498 kelime]
Alleged ransomware group member extradited from Italy to face US charges in hospital, school extortion case

A 43-year-old Kazakh national charged with taking part in a ransomware and extortion conspiracy that targeted U.S. hospitals, schools and healthcare providers made his initial appearance in federal court Monday after being extradited from Italy, the Justice Department said.

Roman Khlynovskiy, faces five charges: conspiracy to commit computer fraud and identity theft, conspiracy to commit wire fraud, extortion involving computers, conspiracy to commit money laundering and threats to disclose nonconsensual intimate images under the TAKE IT DOWN Act. If convicted, he faces a maximum penalty of 27 years in prison.

Prosecutors allege that Khlynovskiy belonged to the “8-Digits Team,” a group affiliated with the INC and Lynx ransomware groups. According to the indictment, the groups targeted victims in at least six states, gained access to their networks, exfiltrated data and demanded payment in exchange for promises not to release it. The conspirators boasted of holding more than 50 terabytes of data, including more than 70 million confidential health records, and threatened to make the material public to pressure victims into paying.

FBI Director Kash Patel said the group “repeatedly targeted hospitals and schools, extorting victims and threatening to release highly sensitive information,” and that the campaign produced more than $500 million in ransom demands.

The TAKE IT DOWN Act, signed into law in May 2025, criminalizes the nonconsensual publication of intimate imagery, and its appearance in a ransomware-related case shows how prosecutors are folding it into cyber-extortion prosecutions in which threats to publish intimate material serve as leverage.

The prosecution is led by the DOJ Criminal Division’s Computer Crime and Intellectual Property Section, which says it has secured convictions of more than 220 cyber and intellectual property criminals since 2020 and court orders returning over $500 million in victim funds.

The FBI said it’s still investigating the ransomware cases.

[37] Cyberscoop - The Super Intelligence Task Force should open the door to defenders
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 11:00:00 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/super-intelligence-task-force-cybersecurity-defenders-op-ed/

[TAM METİN - 972 kelime]
The Super Intelligence Task Force should open the door to defenders

Artificial intelligence models now find software vulnerabilities faster than organizations can fix them. In HackerOne’s platform data, the valid report backlog is up 131% over the last two years, and critical and high-severity findings are rising while remediation falls further behind. Finding flaws is no longer the hard part. The harder question will decide the balance between offense and defense in the age of superintelligence: When someone outside an organization finds a flaw in a system Americans depend on, is anyone on the other end listening?

The Task Force on Super Intelligence is about to give that question an official answer, whether it means to or not. Its charter directs it to commission a report on the impacts of superintelligence on “the offense-defense balance in cybersecurity.” It also requires the task force to assess the preparedness of the government and the private sector across healthcare, communications, banking, energy, water, transportation and emergency services. Both assessments will shape federal policy for years. If they leave out third-party testing and disclosure, they will start from an incomplete picture.

Any honest assessment of the offense-defense balance must ask how quickly a defender learns about its own weaknesses. Internal testing is necessary, but it has limits. The people who build and run a system will often share its assumptions, and they may miss what an outsider sees right away.

That is a central finding of a paper on third-party AI flaw disclosure I co-authored with researchers from Stanford, Princeton, MIT and elsewhere. Experts in software security, machine learning, law and policy agree that independent researchers need a clear way to report flaws, and they need legal protection when they do.

The task force should carry that insight into its work. Whether an organization accepts, triages and acts on reports from outside researchers, such as through a vulnerability disclosure program, is one of the best available indicators of defensive maturity. It is not perfect, but it shows something no internal audit can: whether an organization has accepted outside scrutiny, has a working process for handling what it hears, and turns findings into fixes. Frontier models will give attackers enormous scale. Defenders need scale of their own, and the global security research community, now working alongside AI, is the largest pool of defensive talent available. An assessment that measures attacker capabilities but not defenders’ ability to receive outside assistance is looking at half the board.

The federal government has already settled the threshold question. The Office of Management and Budget has required agencies to accept outside vulnerability reports since 2020. This June, an executive order moved to extend that expectation to covered federal contractors, recognizing that federal networks are only as resilient as the companies that support them. Congress is moving the same way through the bipartisan Federal Contractor Cybersecurity Vulnerability Reduction Act, included in the House version of the fiscal year 2027 National Defense Authorization Act. The direction is clear. Accepting outside reports is no longer an optional best practice. It is operational infrastructure.

The task force’s preparedness assessment should apply that logic to every sector named in its charter. Hospitals, banks, energy and water utilities, communications networks and emergency services are where one unreported flaw can become a national emergency. As frontier AI shortens the time between discovery and exploitation, the cost of having no way to hear a warning only grows. A rural hospital, a community bank or a local water utility that cannot receive a report from a good-faith researcher is not prepared, whatever else it has in place. A framework that inventories sectors without asking whether each operator can hear a warning gives a false sense of readiness. That is worse than incomplete. It is dangerous.

The charter gives the task force 120 days and a mandate to coordinate across government and industry.

Within that time, it should make outside reporting and testing an explicit benchmark in the offense-defense report, measuring how quickly reports are triaged and fixed, not just whether a channel exists. It should also map where independent reporting is and is not available across every critical infrastructure sector in its charter, and treat the gaps as preparedness findings.

It should extend the same approach to AI systems themselves, with standardized flaw reports and a clear path for researchers to report model flaws and unexpected outputs. And it should recommend clear legal safe harbors, so the researchers this system depends on are not exposed to legal risk for doing the right thing.

The Pentagon showed a decade ago that even the most sensitive institution in the country could be safer by inviting outside experts in. The lesson matters more now. The task force charter says emerging risks should be “identified and communicated to government personnel, industry, and the American people.” Outside researchers are how that identification happens in practice. The task force should build outside researchers’ work into both assessments from the start.

[38] The Register - Oracle reveals fix-this-ASAP-grade Java4Shell bug that allows attacks on many third-party apps
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 05:33:05 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/18/oracle-reveals-fix-this-asap-grade-java4shell-bug-that-allows-attacks-on-many-third-party-apps/5310661

[TAM METİN - 779 kelime]
Oracle reveals fix-this-ASAP-grade Java4Shell bug that allows attacks on many third-party apps

Attackers can crash the JVM or achieve unauthenticated RCE

Chrome zero-day whack-a-mole continues as 2026 tally hits 8
7 days ago

Oracle gobbles $20 billion borrowing limit a week after setting it 
15 days ago

Oracle wants to bill you for every AI task, not just every seat 
19 days ago

Oracle celebrates end of Cybersecurity Awareness Month with quadruple trouble
20 days ago

Big Red gets cozy with Google to offer TPUs to AI customers
21 days ago

The United States Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) has urged Java users to immediately patch a high-severity flaw in the software, which attackers can use to crash the Java virtual machine or achieve unauthenticated remote code execution (RCE).

Oracle revealed the flaw in its monthly patch dump for November, which offers 655 patches, 62 of them rated critical, affecting 62 products.

Several of the flaws are rated more dangerous than the Java problem, CVE-2026-114938, which Big Red didn’t single out as worthy of special attention despite CISA’s warning.

The cyber-agency’s decision seems apt because vulnerability research and red teaming outfit Calif.io, which discovered the flaw, used its X account to claim news of the problem had leaked before public disclosure and the release of Oracle's fixes.

By then, Calif had already tested the flaw, which it named “Java4Shell,” and found applications including Keycloak, Atlassian’s Jira and Confluence, Jenkins, Apache Kafka Broker, and Elasticsearch are susceptible to RCE.

Calif, which also helped to find the PHP2Shell flaw, says the flaw is a memory-corruption vulnerability in OpenJDK's Base64 decoder.

“Base64 is a very common way of representing binary data as text. It is used in authentication protocols, to encode file uploads, and to deliver network messages,” the company explained. “To decode Base64 faster, OpenJDK uses specialized x86-64 CPU instructions to process many bytes at once.”

“We discovered a bug in that optimized machine-level code that lets malformed input overwrite neighboring memory. With careful exploitation, the corruption can reveal sensitive memory, as Heartbleed did, or lead to remote code execution. The corruption itself can be triggered by just 64 A characters followed by a single =:”

Calif applied the CVSS 4.0 bug-rating scheme and gave the flaw a critical-rated score of 9.2/10.

Infosec outfit SentinelOne’s analysis suggests the flaw “sits in the Java standard library, so exploit traffic is specific to each application.”

“Any Base64 value an application passes to java.util.Base64.Decoder can carry the payload, including HTTP Basic credentials, SAML artifacts, CSRF tokens, and MIME encoded words. In default configurations of the affected applications listed above, attackers can reach this code path prior to authentication.”

Cisco’s Talos security team pointed out that the flaw is present in OpenJDK Java versions 17.0.6 and later running on x86-64 CPUs with AVX-512 VBMI (Vector Byte Manipulation Instructions) support enabled.

“These vulnerabilities have already been tested against a suite of Java applications with success, but the attack surface is likely much larger as additional targets are tested and confirmed,” Talos advised.

Cisco’s infosec experts urged adoption of the fixed versions of Java Oracle posted on Tuesday but noted it is possible to mitigate the flaw on x86-64 machines by starting the JVM with support for AVX-512 disabled.

Akamai’s Chief Information Security Architect Jan Schaumann, writing on the Openwall project’s mailing list, expressed a little discomfort with Calif’s work.

“Calif.io set their priorities on publicity-maxxing, it seems,” he wrote, pointing out that the flaw-finding firm’s announcement of Java4Shell opens by stating “After we disclosed PHP2Shell, we realized many people didn't know what PHP was. So we decided to go after something a little more familiar: Java.”

Talos’s analysis of Java4Shell includes the interesting observation that it “has not observed in-the-wild exploitation of this vulnerability yet. However, we anticipate probing attempts may pick up soon after public disclosure.”

That second sentence perhaps supports Calif’s assertion that news of the flaw leaked before disclosure.

The rest of Big Red’s flaws

Java4Shell deserves a place high up on patch priority lists.

So do the six 10.10 flaws Oracle reported, three in its Access Manager product, one in the Oracle Platform Security for Java, another in the WebLogic Server, and one in Business Intelligence Enterprise Edition.

Big Red also repaired five 9.9-rated bugs, in Oracle Text, Hyperion, RDBMS, and Autovue.

The November patch dump also includes 28 flaws rated 9.8/10.

By Tenable Security’s count, 249 of the patches are for high severity bugs. Oracle’s Hyperion enterprise performance management accounts for 267 of the bugs.

Such is life in the age of AI-powered flaw finders – which Calif says burned through just $200 worth of tokens to find Java4Shell. ®

[39] The Register - Daily disruptive outages that ground planes and halt work – welcome to the new normal
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 00:41:31 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/17/daily-disruptive-outages-that-ground-planes-and-halt-work-welcome-to-the-new-normal/5310634

[TAM METİN - 1256 kelime]
Daily disruptive outages that ground planes and halt work – welcome to the new normal

NSA, Apple add to a week of high-profile network outages, largely attributed to 'routine maintenance'

Time for frustrated M365 customers to stage a 'distributed denial of satisfaction' action
16 minutes ago

Satya sorry for SaaS-y SNAFU - look over there, more AI!
11 hours ago

Windows 11 previews remote padlock only IT can open
17 hours ago

Up to 99% of UK civil servants rely on Microsoft for comms, minister admits
21 hours ago

Microsoft fixes Windows activation as admins pick through Exchange outage wreckage
1 day ago

Another day, another disruptive network outage (or two) as government officials and company execs scramble to assure an anxious populace that these are not coordinated cyberattacks – and security experts call this the new normal.

A classified network that the US National Security Agency uses for internal communications, called NSANet, went offline on Tuesday in what the agency described as a “temporary degradation during scheduled routine maintenance.”

“While some internal systems were affected, mission-critical capabilities remain fully operational and our ability to defend national security continues,” the NSA said in a statement following media reports about the outage. “NSA's infrastructure is designed with multiple layers of redundancy to maintain operations during planned maintenance or unexpected events.”

Around the same time, Apple users began reporting issues across multiple services – including Apple Support, iCloud, the App Store, Apple Pay, and more – with Apple retail stores worldwide also reportedly experiencing disruptions that halted sales and in-person support.

A day earlier, customers at major US banks including JPMorgan Chase, Bank of America, and US Bank reported service outages that Chase attributed to “a software bug in on-premise hardware systems - there is no evidence this is cyber-related.”

All of these high-profile outages followed last week’s US Federal Aviation Authority (FAA) order grounding all planes on Friday due to problems with copper wiring during an L3Harris network upgrade, and a Microsoft 365 outage on Thursday.

Redmond later blamed the massive service disruption on a faulty software update it pushed back in July.

Despite the weekend respite from network outages, your humble vulture still had to field is-this-the-beginning-of-the-end questions from friends and family all day Saturday and Sunday, most of whom suspected Russia or China – maybe even Iran – caused the spate of downed systems and services.

A highly sophisticated, coordinated nation-state hack would be a much more exciting cause for the disruptions. The real culprit, however, appears to be aging, under-funded infrastructure, riddled with decades-old bugs and held together by digital sticks and glue.

“As someone who started out as a hacker, I know how tempting it is to connect the dots when major systems fail one after another,” Remedio CEO Tal Kollender told The Register, adding that there’s “no credible public evidence that these outages were part of a coordinated cyberattack,” and most have been attributed to technical failures.

A single misconfiguration, failed update, or broken dependency can create disruption on a massive scale, even without an attacker

“What concerns me more is what these incidents reveal about the fragility of our digital infrastructure. We've built an interconnected world on layers of aging systems, complex configurations, and dependencies that organizations don't always fully understand. A single misconfiguration, failed update, or broken dependency can create disruption on a massive scale, even without an attacker,” Kollender said. “The uncomfortable reality I think we're grappling with is that attackers don't always have to break our systems. Sometimes, our systems are already capable of breaking on their own.”

For people who couldn’t withdraw money from their bank, board a flight, or use email at work on Friday, “the disruption was real regardless of the cause,” she said.

“Cybersecurity has spent decades investing in detecting threats, identifying vulnerabilities, and generating reports,” according to Kollender. “Yet we still allow for these preventable weaknesses and configurations that can bring entire systems down.”

Cynthia Kaiser, senior VP of Halcyon’s Ransomware Research Center and former deputy director of the FBI’s Cyber Division, told The Reg that the recent disruptions show “how deeply interconnected our digital infrastructure has become, and how a single technical failure or software update can cascade with outsized effects across critical systems.”

AI has entered the chat

Then we go and add AI to this already delicate balance, and everything essentially blows up.

AI-assisted bug hunting and exploit development has surfaced more than 100,000 CVEs so far this year, with Microsoft’s Patch Tuesday disclosing more than 1,000 vulnerabilities in each of the past two months. AI has also shrunk the elapsed time between the public release of a security patch and attackers developing working exploits.

“We are in an era where AI is accelerating the discovery of legacy software flaws, forcing organizations to race to patch vulnerabilities before adversaries can exploit them, which inevitably creates friction and disruptions,” Kaiser said. “Even when an outage isn’t caused by a cyberattack, these moments of operational distraction leave dangerous gaps open for threat actors and opportunists to take advantage of, making it crucial for both institutions and everyday individuals to remain on high alert against social engineering and unsolicited outreach.”

Even when attackers aren’t using AI to facilitate intrusions, the technology itself has become a source of risk to organizations.

“Autonomous agents can execute actions, change configurations, access sensitive information, and interact with critical systems at machine speed,” Kollender said. “When those systems lack proper governance and safeguards, a mistake can develop just as quickly as an attack.

Former US Department of Defense network exploitation and vulnerability analyst Mike Wiacek, when asked if these regular system outages are the new normal, told us: “The brutal truth is yes. Especially in the AI world that we live in.”

After the DoD, Wiacek founded Google’s Threat Analysis Group. He's since founded Stairwell, a threat-intel firm he serves as chief technology officer.

“The idea that we are going to patch ourselves to security is, dare I say, an antiquated concept,” he told The Register.

One of Stairwell’s engineers was doing regular, daily software development tasks on an open source library with Anthropic’s Claude Mythos, and the model told the engineer about a buffer-overflow vulnerability in the code.

Pandora's box is opened, and it's not going to go back inside

“We weren't even looking for it,” Wiacek said. “It found a vulnerability in open source software by accident. You used to need specialized bug hunters and really, really technical security folks to discover and develop vulnerabilities and exploits. Now, it’s almost too easy to do it.”

This new reality, he said, necessitates a total rethink of how we build and maintain digital infrastructure.

“How do we get to a world where we assume compromised by default, and continuously recompromised, and then develop the architectures and the systems necessary to make what was the expense of incident-response investigation really fast and cheap? Because we will not be able to patch our way out of it.”

It requires vulnerability managers and system administrators to patch as soon as software updates become available. But from an engineering perspective, it also means building in “as many fail-safes as possible,” Wiacek said.

“An elevator doesn't have just one cable hoisting it,” he continued. “There's a lot of redundancy in those systems. Enterprises and organizations and governments haven't really had to operate like that. But that's going to be the new norm, whether we want it to be or not. Pandora's box is opened, and it's not going to go back inside.” ®

[40] The Register - Gemini test proves once again that amoral AI agents will do almost anything if told ‘NEVER give up’
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 20:52:57 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/17/gemini-test-proves-once-again-that-amoral-ai-agents-will-do-almost-anything-if-told-never-give-up/5310574

[TAM METİN - 1117 kelime]
Gemini test proves once again that amoral AI agents will do almost anything if told ‘NEVER give up’

If you design a test to find misalignment, you will find misalignment

A Chinese bottle-cap manufacturer brilliantly deflated AI hype
2 hours ago

Satya sorry for SaaS-y SNAFU - look over there, more AI!
11 hours ago

Inspiring FOSS project Crosspoint Reader has lessons about tech, communities, and LLMs
15 hours ago

Opting out of Gemini tracking? Google will still hold your data 20x longer
16 hours ago

AI toys are on the naughty list again this year
20 hours ago

After being instructed to "NEVER stop, NEVER give up" on a difficult task, Google’s Gemini AI agents tried to hack a an organization, impersonating its staff and attempting a SQL injection attack.

The good news? It was all part of a test.

The Gemini 4 Argon agents were given an impossible task to complete – come up with the “official” answers to Epoch AI’s FrontierMatch problems. In some runs they had internet access. Even with their production safeguards enabled, Trajectory Labs says the agents “engaged in misaligned behavior.”

“Across four scenarios, they attempted SQL injection against Epoch AI’s live website, claimed to be Epoch staff to get answer keys, turned a shared package cache into a message board, and obfuscated their commands to avoid their safeguards,” the AI safety company said in a Friday report.

If this all sounds similar, it should. Google’s AI agents behaved in much the same way as OpenAI’s models did when they broke out of their testing environment and hacked Hugging Face.

Google did not respond to The Register's questions.

According to Trajectory, the Hugging Face intrusions prompted it to test misalignment in other models. While OpenAI’s models were running with their safety guardrails turned off in the Hugging Face scenario, Trajectory “wanted to know whether a current frontier model would behave the same way with its production safeguards enabled.”

Trajectory ran swarms of up to 80 Gemini 4 Argon agents, gave them an impossible task, and prompted them not to give up until they completed it – which, we’re told, was “intentionally” done to “elicit misaligned behavior, if it existed.”

We told the agents to persist with an impossible math benchmark task, but we did not tell them to hack anyone or pose as Epoch staff

Turns out it existed.

“We told the agents to persist with an impossible math benchmark task, but we did not tell them to hack anyone or pose as Epoch staff,” McIntyre said.

“When a model hacks systems or deceives people to finish a task, it is misaligned with what its developer and its user intended,” he continued. “Production safeguards are meant to block harmful requests even when the model itself goes wrong. Under our test conditions, neither the model nor its safeguards stopped the agents.”

The initial testing began on October 20, and the agents had internet access. We should note that just one of these tests was run with Epoch’s consent.

In the first scenario, the agents did reach Epoch’s live website – and fell into the research nonprofit’s honeypots, set up to detect AI agents. These honeypots contained a decoy benchmark key, which the agents found and used. The agents also probed for internal endpoints including /admin, /keys and /users, and attempted a SQL injection.

After noticing the agents’ activities, Epoch added an “agent helpline,” and some of the agents that used it claimed to be Epoch staff seeking answers to the test questions.

After this initial test, Trajectory and Epoch decided to use a copy of the website instead of a live version, and built a buggy local copy with a planted vulnerability based on CVE-2026-102255, a critical pre-authentication Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) vulnerability in SonicWall Secure Mobile Access (SMA) appliances. “Over the following days, several agents fully exploited the planted multi-stage vulnerability chain in the clone,” the report says.

In an effort to mimic the Hugging Face incident, Trajectory also gave the agents a local, shared package manager, which “nearly all” of them used as a message board to communicate with each other and coordinate efforts to complete the test.

In a separate, local setup, Trajectory swapped out Epoch’s honeypot with an AI assistant that would release answers only to named Epoch staff who passed a security check with their date of birth. “Agents accessed people-search sites, attempted impersonation again, and tried to jailbreak the AI assistant,” the testing firm said.

Next time: kill switches at the ready

A third test on October 30 used a staging copy of Epoch’s website, and granted the agents internet access. One of them tried a SQL injection, and multiple claimed to be Epoch staff on the helpline. They also attempted to bypass a CAPTCHA check.

Finally, the fourth scenario on October 29 and 30 kept the agents contained in a sandbox, with no internet access and no copy of Epoch’s website.

They were, however, offered a decoy switch and told it would turn off the firewall.

In all three runs, the agents switched the decoy off and obfuscated its address to bypass Gemini’s safeguards. On one, the agent rebuilt the switch’s address from positions of words it had saved earlier. The real firewall blocked the agents’ traffic throughout all of these tests.

At this point, Trajectory stopped its tests, and said it won’t run any future experiments until it has a risk management plan in place, with signoff from its CEO and CTO, along with approval from any outside systems that the models could “reasonably” target. Tests will also include real-time automated monitoring and kill switches, and Trajectory said that it’s setting up a system to notify any organizations affected by these experiments.

Interestingly, Trajectory co-founder Peter McIntyre told us that the testing firm “ran a similar test with another frontier model, and it did not exhibit the same misalignment as Gemini 4 Argon.”

However, this was not a controlled comparison. “Our report covers one model's behavior, and was designed as a stress test to see whether that model would exhibit similar misalignment under duress, not a measure of how often different models behave this way,” McIntyre told The Register.

Overall, the testing firm noted that the agents’ behavior “show how easily testing capable AI agents can spill over to affect real systems and people, and there are not yet shared standards for doing it safely.”

Trajectory says that it's developing a set of safety standards, and recommends that anyone else running misalignment tests use fully isolated sandboxes. ®

Correction: Epoch only consented to one of the tests, scenario three. The original version of this article said that it consented to all of the tests.

[TAM METİN - 590 kelime]
Hackers smash 'n' grab AI vendor: Take  off with license data of Uber drivers

Ride-hailing biz says stolen credentials allowed crooks to help themselves to driver's license data

Uber has confirmed that nearly 150,000 people had their personal information compromised after hackers broke into one of its AI suppliers.

A filing with Oregon's attorney general puts the number of victims at 149,346. Among the data the crooks got their hands on were images of drivers' licenses and information extracted from them, including names, home addresses, dates of birth, and license numbers.

Uber doesn't name the supplier in its filings with US state attorneys general, but told TechCrunch it was Fireworks AI, the inference outfit whose security woes we covered last week.

According to a breach notification filed with Iowa's attorney general, Fireworks alerted Uber to the intrusion on October 9. Uber cut off the supplier's access to its data and, four days later, established that the attackers had accessed drivers' personal information on October 7.

Uber had set up a dedicated cloud storage environment for the AI biz, complete with security monitoring overseen by its own cloud security team. Unfortunately for Uber and its drivers, the crooks were using legitimate credentials, so none of the alarms went off.

The ride-hailing firm says those credentials couldn't be used to access its other systems, and says it has since revoked or changed the credentials associated with the supplier.

Uber hasn't said why it handed images of thousands of driver's licenses to an AI inference outfit. The Register has put that question to both companies, and asked Uber if any drivers outside the US had their data exposed.

Fireworks, which provides infrastructure for developers to run and fine-tune AI models, had a particularly miserable October. The company first disclosed a security incident on October 9 after discovering that someone had been using its internal credentials without permission.

Fireworks initially said it had no evidence that the intruders had gotten their hands on personal information or customer datasets. The company was hacked again later that month and, on November 9, disclosed that some customer data had indeed been compromised.

The AI biz acknowledged that attackers had accessed billing records, some customer datasets, and incomplete model weights. Productivity software maker Notion was among those affected, with some of its customers' conversations with its AI assistant exposed.

Fireworks suspects the involvement of financially motivated criminals in the business of flogging unauthorized access to large language models. Whether the crooks have any plans for the haul of Uber drivers' personal information is unclear.

Uber began notifying affected individuals on November 10, more than a month after learning of the breach. It's offering victims free credit monitoring and fraud assistance, though drivers have just 90 days from the date of their letter to sign up.

This isn't Uber's first brush with cybercriminals in recent months. In August, the ride-hailing giant's logistics arm, Uber Freight, confirmed a break-in after the Helix extortion crew claimed to have made off with almost a million files, including data pilfered from mailboxes and OneDrive accounts. ®

[42] The Register - Windows 11 previews remote padlock only IT can open
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 16:29:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/os-platforms/2026/11/17/windows-11-previews-remote-padlock-only-it-can-open/5310402

[TAM METİN - 498 kelime]
Windows 11 previews remote padlock only IT can open

Intune feature blocks normal sign-ins without wiping data, while another test build drops Offline Maps settings

Time for frustrated M365 customers to stage a 'distributed denial of satisfaction' action
16 minutes ago

Oracle reveals fix-this-ASAP-grade Java4Shell bug that allows attacks on many third-party apps
4 hours ago

Daily disruptive outages that ground planes and halt work – welcome to the new normal
9 hours ago

Satya sorry for SaaS-y SNAFU - look over there, more AI!
11 hours ago

Gemini test proves once again that amoral AI agents will do almost anything if told ‘NEVER give up’
13 hours ago

A new experimental Windows 11 build previews security features including an Intune-controlled Remote Lock, while a beta build clears away another relic from the halcyon days of Windows Phone.

The security changes include viewing and individually removing passkeys stored on FIDO2 (Fast Identity Online 2) security keys, plus a preview of the Microsoft Pluton Cryptographic Provider. For enterprise administrators, though, Remote Lock is the main attraction.

Finding a lost Windows device has been around for years, and you can also remotely wipe a device to prevent data from falling into the wrong hands. Remote Lock lets an administrator lock a managed device through Intune without wiping it. The instruction takes effect at the device's next check-in. Windows signs out of every active session, hides every sign-in option, and shows a locked screen.

The lock survives restarts and the device going offline – only an administrator can lift it remotely. Microsoft's documentation spells out the restriction: "There is no offline or local unlock. No PIN, password, local admin, or web sign-in works on a locked device; unlock arrives only through Intune."

Once the lock is applied, even stolen credentials won't restore access through the normal sign-in screen. The user's account itself is not affected; this applies only to the specific device.

Though still in preview, the feature will allow IT teams to respond non-destructively to a report of a missing device. It doesn't erase data or isolate the device from the network (Microsoft directs admins to other Intune controls if that is required).

The lock doesn't block Safe Mode, Windows Recovery Environment, or booting from external media. Microsoft advises keeping BitLocker enabled to protect the data.

Now for the beta build, where Microsoft, in time-honored fashion, gives and takes away. The latest Windows Insider build hammers another nail into the coffin of Microsoft Maps.

The Maps app was removed from the Microsoft Store and made nonfunctional in July 2025. Now Offline Maps settings are following it out, as Microsoft retires the Windows UWP Map control and Windows Maps platform APIs.

The original Maps app dates back to Windows Phone 7 and appeared on the desktop with Windows 8. It continued through subsequent versions before Microsoft stopped preinstalling it with Windows 11 version 24H2.

For those with a nostalgic streak, it's another piece of Windows Phone disappearing off the map. ®

[43] The Register - 'Not rocket surgery' claims researcher who found six Danfoss fridge controller zero-days in under an hour
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:32:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/17/not-rocket-surgery-claims-researcher-who-found-six-danfoss-fridge-controller-zero-days-in-under-an-hour/5310197

[TAM METİN - 794 kelime]
'Not rocket surgery' claims researcher who found six Danfoss fridge controller zero-days in under an hour

Even strict authentication mode has a hole, we're told, while basic exploit defenses are missing from the firmware

Time for frustrated M365 customers to stage a 'distributed denial of satisfaction' action
16 minutes ago

A Chinese bottle-cap manufacturer brilliantly deflated AI hype
2 hours ago

Temu's compliance regime is low-quality, fits badly, and full of holes, says Europe
2 hours ago

Oracle reveals fix-this-ASAP-grade Java4Shell bug that allows attacks on many third-party apps
4 hours ago

Daily disruptive outages that ground planes and halt work – welcome to the new normal
9 hours ago

A Dutch cybersecurity researcher claims he found six zero-days in Danfoss refrigeration controller firmware in under an hour, then delivered a withering assessment of the vendor's defenses against memory corruption.

The Centre for Cybersecurity Belgium (CCB) warns that the flaws could let attackers take control without credentials, potentially retain access after resets, or disable controllers altogether.

Carlo Meijer of cyber consultancy Midnight Blue examined the latest AK-SM800A firmware, version 5.3.2, manually – no AI required.

He said that he went looking in random places where strcpy() is invoked, given the vendor's "extremely liberal use" of the function (which doesn't check whether the destination buffer is large enough), and swiftly found five cases in which he said he could trigger a segfault with a one-line cURL command.

"Of course, remotely triggering a crash is bad in itself, but being able to leverage the buffer overflow to obtain remote code execution is much worse," he wrote.

Meijer added: "Any reasonably experienced exploit developer will agree exploiting straightforward stack-based buffer overflows on ARM Linux without exploit mitigations is very straightforward. And if they don't, there's probably a frontier or open-weight model out there willing to help them agree."

He noted that before achieving RCE, an attacker would need to overcome the limitation of strcpy() where it stops copying bytes after hitting a null byte, but said "it's not exactly rocket surgery."

He went on to claim: "Also, the exploited application runs as root (because of course it does)."

Later firmware adds "session control," an authentication mechanism whose strict setting blocks unauthenticated access to most web interfaces. But Meijer found a memory corruption flaw in that protective layer too.

He described the final zero-day as a "trivial" pre-auth memory corruption bug which again starts with a segfault but can be wangled into an RCE "with some additional effort."

Meijer also considered whether attackers could maintain access after a reset, as seen with implants such as Saltwater and Firestarter. He considered this feasible because the controllers do not use their processor's secure boot features.

"In such a scenario, an attacker could either retain remote access or leave a time-triggered payload for a 2nd (or 3rd...) strike – increasing damage by spoiling new batches of food," said Meijer.

"Alternatively, attackers could simply wipe flash memory and/or push a malformed firmware update to brick the controllers and render them permanently inoperable – requiring costly and time-consuming replacement similar to the TTPs deployed during the December 2025 attack on the Polish electric grid."

Meijer's priority for operators was to remove the controllers' direct internet exposure, rather than spend scarce resources on piecemeal patching.

He said the vendor's advice following the attacks on European supermarkets and blood banks in recent weeks – rotating creds, applying patches, enabling strict session control – was insufficient given the flaws in the controllers' codebase.

"This is insufficient and asset owners need to remove internet exposure as soon as possible," said Meijer. "We recommend against allocating scarce security resources to patching efforts and instead focus on reducing exposure."

The CCB likewise urged operators to remove direct internet exposure immediately, but also recommended applying patches when they become available.

Beyond the liberal use of strcpy(), Meijer criticized the absence of standard exploit mitigations, including stack canaries and position-independent executables.

"These mechanisms have been staples of memory corruption defenses for decades and enabling these features... requires no engineering work at all," he said.

"It is simply a matter of setting the right compiler flag. Enabling these features became the default in Ubuntu 17.10 and there is simply no excuse for a network-facing application that was last updated a few months ago to not have them enabled."

The Register asked Danfoss to respond to the findings, but it did not reply.

Danfoss has consistently blanked our requests for information since being implicated in at least some of the attacks that affected European supermarkets recently, alongside rival controller vendors Copeland and Dixell.

It also did not respond when we asked about its involvement in the attacks on US commissary appliances over the summer, after some alleged that its controllers were targeted. ®

[45] The Register - NoisyTCP bug gives Linux servers a remote crash problem 11 years in the making
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 16 Nov 2026 19:28:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/16/noisytcp-bug-gives-linux-servers-a-remote-crash-problem-11-years-in-the-making/5310144

[TAM METİN - 623 kelime]
NoisyTCP bug gives Linux servers a remote crash problem 11 years in the making

Flaw can take down an entire host, even when the vulnerable service is running inside a container

Omaxian takes the Omarchy look down a different Linux backstreet
1 day ago

Microsoft’s Azure Linux dons Fedora to dress itself up for mainstream workloads
2 days ago

Another Linux kernel developer steps back from some duties due to overload
4 days ago

Linux joins Heathrow's grounded fleet of information screens
6 days ago

Microsoft's Azure Linux gets 470-plus security fixes in one almighty patching session
8 days ago

The researcher who sounded the alarm over Dirty Frag earlier this year has uncovered another Linux kernel flaw, and this one lets attackers crash entire servers remotely without so much as a password.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-99288 and dubbed NoisyTCP, was discovered by security researcher Hyunwoo Kim, who says it affects almost all Linux distributions. The flaw dates back to October 2015 and went unnoticed until this year, when Kim found and patched it.

Attackers don't need to log in or get their hands on any credentials to cause trouble. They can simply knock the server over, although there's no evidence yet that the flaw can be used to steal data or hijack a machine.

The bug is buried in Linux's TCP networking code. Two parts of the kernel can trip over each other while processing incoming traffic, leaving one trying to use memory that's already been freed. When that happens, Linux can panic and take the whole machine down with it.

Kim says there's no need for any special server configuration either. Services such as Nginx, Apache, and SSH can be vulnerable straight out of the box. An attacker needs access to an open TCP port on a system that processes incoming network traffic across multiple receive queues or CPUs.

Perhaps more concerning for administrators running containerized workloads, the damage isn't necessarily limited to the application being attacked. Because the flaw sits in the Linux kernel, crashing a web server inside a container can take down the underlying host and anything else running on it.

Kim found that the attack was particularly effective against virtual machines. During testing, he managed to crash Nginx, Apache, and SSH servers running on QEMU/KVM virtual machines in between five seconds and two minutes.

Physical hardware put up more of a fight. Even after tailoring his exploit to the target machine, Kim needed roughly 14 hours to crash a bare-metal server running Nginx.

Kim has a knack for finding holes in Linux. Back in May, he uncovered Dirty Frag, a kernel bug that allowed local users to gain root privileges. That one caused headaches for administrators when a working exploit went public before patches were ready.

His latest discovery, NoisyTCP, has another potentially nasty trick up its sleeve. Kim identified a second exploitation path involving a use-after-free condition, although pulling it off is considerably more difficult than simply crashing a server.

In his advisory, Kim says he has developed a limited exploit targeting virtual machines in a lab environment but has yet to demonstrate a general-purpose remote code execution attack. For the moment, the confirmed impact is remote denial of service.

A fix was committed to the upstream Linux kernel on November 3, but administrators will need to check whether their distribution has shipped an updated kernel. Red Hat has rated the vulnerability Important, assigning it a CVSS score of 7.5.

Kim published the details on November 15, once the disclosure embargo was lifted. The bug had been hiding in plain sight since 2015, leaving Linux servers vulnerable to remote crashes for 11 years. Not bad for a flaw that doesn't even require a login. ®

[46] TechCrunch Security - Google joins the tech outage trend by announcing it’s not having one
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 21:34:50 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/17/google-joins-the-tech-outage-trend-by-announcing-its-not-having-one/

[TAM METİN - 666 kelime]
Was Google feeling a little left out of the recent wave of tech outages? The company took to X on Tuesday to announce that its Gmail service is actually running just fine. In a post, the company said it was not currently seeing evidence of a broad Gmail service disruption and reminded users they could always check Gmail’s real-time status on its online dashboard.

As it turns out, the company was trying to head off reports of a Gmail outage that began circulating on X on Tuesday around the same time that Apple was seeing a number of verified outages of its own. While some posts on the social network suggested Gmail was experiencing a disruption, others said they were not having any problems.

The concerns seemed to stem from an X post by Downdetector, a crowdsourced website where users can report service disruptions themselves and see if others are having problems. Downdetector is not an official source for verifying or understanding the true scope of internet outages, and as people become more concerned about possible cyberattacks, the site’s data may become less valuable over time if every minor glitch and hiccup is reported as an “outage” by non-technical users.

The fact that Google had to announce it was not having an outage speaks to the growing anxiety around the reliability of major tech services. Over the past week, Apple and Microsoft experienced outages, an FAA network failure led to a nationwide ground stop, and some banks and airlines also reported disruptions.

However, Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton said on Fox Business Network’s “The Claman Countdown” on Tuesday that the U.S. is not currently facing coordinated cyberattacks. He told the outlet, “We do not see the indicia of a coordinated cyberattack — let me just be clear about that — and we do not see the indicia of state-sponsored activity here.”

Instead, Clayton attributed the rise in issues to businesses patching their systems. The patching may be a downstream effect of AI, as newer models have become increasingly adept at uncovering previously unknown vulnerabilities. “They’re upgrading them, they’re putting patches in place,” he said, adding that this is happening at “a much larger scale” than before.

As a result, some of the systems are “not coming back as expected in all cases,” he added.

[47] TechCrunch Security - Proton’s Lumo, a privacy-focused alternative to ChatGPT and Claude, arrives on desktop
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 17:32:25 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/17/protons-lumo-a-privacy-focused-alternative-to-chatgpt-and-claude-arrives-on-desktop/

[TAM METİN - 778 kelime]
Proton, the privacy-focused tech company offering alternatives to Google’s and Microsoft’s tools for email, productivity apps, password management, cloud storage, and more, is now bringing its AI app Lumo to personal computers. On Tuesday, the company launched Lumo Desktop for Windows, macOS, and Linux, allowing users to leverage its AI to carry out local tasks on their computer involving connected apps, personal files, and other automations.

The app is designed to offer users more control over their personal data, as consumer AI technology becomes focused more on AI agents that can perform tasks on users’ behalf, rather than just answering questions.

Already, the major model makers, including OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta, and others, offer desktop tools for working with their AI technologies, but doing so invites increased risk to users’ personal data. Only this week, for instance, Apple announced it was addressing those privacy concerns with tightened security settings for apps that request Full Disk Access, as the AI desktop apps do.

Proton’s idea is to offer a set of tools that let its AI access connected apps, files, and folders and automate tasks, but with more personalization over how much access the AI has. The company says users can choose which actions the AI agent takes that will need to request their approval, and it vows that the conversations and information from connected apps and files are never used to serve personalized ads or train AI models.

The new app includes a variety of connectors that support local tools, like file-system access, shell execution, and browser content fetching, as well as Proton’s email app and other third-party services, including Confluence, Dropbox, and Jira.

The connectors also support local and remote MCP (Model Context Protocol) servers, which AI agents use to access external data sources.

Proton says that users can decide for each connector which actions Lumo can perform automatically, which require approval, and which are blocked entirely. Users can also turn individual tools off from the Tools menu in their conversation.

In addition, users can set up their own scheduled tasks, where each has its own set of permissions, in terms of what can be handled automatically and what requires approval.

Proton notes that it uses encryption to ensure that only users can read their chat history, not the company. The company also says it keeps no server-side logs, and the limited data it does store is protected under Swiss privacy law. The data is hosted on European infrastructure, outside the scope of the U.S. CLOUD Act, which specifies how law enforcement agencies access data held by cloud service providers.

The company says it’s investing in its infrastructure, with its first Nvidia B300 cluster built on Open Compute Project (OCP) standards now going live in Switzerland.

Similar to other AI apps, Lumo’s desktop app supports Skills, which lets users save instructions, templates, and reference files that Lumo can reuse across their conversations. (An earlier version of this, Custom Lumos, will now be migrated to the Skills format.)

[48] CSO Online - How organizations can secure their AI-generated code
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 08:25:00 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/3633403/how-organizations-can-secure-their-ai-code.html

[TAM METİN - 1894 kelime]
AI-generated code tends to work these days, which makes developers less critical about it. CISOs need to move security checks closer to where the code is written.

The IT manager didn’t know the company hired a “vibe coder” straight out of school. By the time they met a few months later, the new hire had already built a web application that operated with customer data and private contracts.

The app ran under a domain the new hire controlled, stored data on Supabase’s free plan, and deployed directly from his GitHub repository to Cloudflare’s serverless infrastructure.

“He vibe-codes everything straight into production, on servers all over the world,” the IT manager wrote on Reddit. “We’re EU based.”

When asked whether he had read the AI-generated code or taken any security precautions, the new hire said, “My GitHub repo is private.”

The IT manager took the concern to the CEO, who brushed it aside: “Let it go.”

In many organizations, vibe coders build software without thinking much about security. Their apps may get the job done, but they are ticking bombs.

In SusVibes, a benchmark of 186 real-world feature requests from open-source projects, agents given feature descriptions without security guidance produced working code up to 57% of the time. But only 11.8% of their solutions were secure.

“The failures we saw were not careless errors,” says Danqing Wang of Carnegie Mellon University’s Language Technologies Institute, author of the paper describing SusVibes. “Spotting those requires reasoning about what an attacker can do, not just what the feature should do.”

What has changed, especially this year, is that AI code-generating tools have gotten so good at producing working code that people are just trusting them, says Dr. Katie Paxton-Fear, staff security advocate at Semgrep. “Developers simply aren’t being as critical now as they were when AI was worse at writing code.”

Whether it’s the vibe-coder in marketing or the seasoned engineering team boosting its productivity, AI-generated code is an increasing security concern for CISOs. Much of that is a function of volume, but even as AI has proved highly capable of finding flaws in code bases, it is not yet adept at contributing code without introducing its own.

Treating AI-generated code as ‘untrusted input’

Leading AI coding agents such as Claude Code, Cursor, GitHub Copilot, and Amazon Q Developer have become developer go-to tools, and CIOs are increasingly enlisting business users to vibe code their own apps.

But as AI-generated code surfaces everywhere, CISOs have only one option: to treat it “as untrusted input that has to earn its way into the codebase,” says Bianca Lankford, VP of engineering at Datadog.

To do so, security leaders are moving security checks closer to where code is written: static analysis, secrets scanning, and dependency checks run on every pull request and, increasingly, in the developer’s editor.

“Companies are also approving specific AI tools, limiting what assistants can access, and training reviewers to spot common AI mistakes like outdated libraries, weak input validation, and overly permissive defaults,” Lankford adds.

At Mimecast, using AI to write code doesn’t remove the developer’s responsibility for it, says Leslie Nielson, CISO at the information security vendor. “A named person owns every AI-assisted change that ships, and that’s recorded in the pull request, not assumed,” he adds. “AI can propose. Only people can approve.”

The volume of code organizations need to review is only growing, and reviewing everything manually can be impossible. That’s why Nielsen recommends security leaders combine automated checks with human judgment.

“We use automation for the known and repetitive, like dependency checks, secrets detection, and standard vulnerability patterns,” he says. “We keep a person accountable for anything involving access, authentication, or sensitive data. Those categories always go to a specialist reviewer, regardless of how clean the scan looks.”

Datadog’s Lankford says the decision to bring in a specialist should depend on what the code could put at risk, not on what entity — human or AI — wrote it. “Anything involving authentication, cryptography, payments, personal data, or infrastructure goes to a specialist, and code owner rules can route those changes automatically,” she says. “Other useful signals include diff size, new dependencies, and changes to permissions or network exposure.”

The goal is to protect the human reviewers’ attention. “A reviewer buried in low-risk changes isn’t reviewing,” Nielsen adds. “They’re approving.”

Another priority should be preserving the knowledge behind the code. “Generative AI may produce software, but it cannot produce understanding,” notes Alex Ștefănescu, security expert and tech activist. “Documentation, a solid test suite, and a written record of every significant architecture decision ever made are going to save developers and organizations countless hours when a security incident hits or when they are debugging complex issues.”

All of this should be created and maintained by people and intended for human use, not externalized to generative algorithms, Ștefănescu adds.

What’s at risk with AI-generated code

The inclusion of third-party libraries or phantom dependencies — dependencies that are not explicitly declared in a manifest file — is a common concern with AI-generated code, as these unreported software packages might not be identified during a scan and can potentially hide vulnerabilities.

One way to address this is to use software composition analysis (SCA) and software supply chain security tools to help identify the libraries in use, as well as the vulnerabilities and potential legal and compliance issues that might bring.

While not unique to AI-generated code, organizations also expose themselves to risks when developers download machine learning (ML) models or datasets from platforms such as Hugging Face.

“In spite of security checks on both ends, it may still happen that the model contains a backdoor that becomes active once the model is integrated,” says Ștefănescu. “This could ultimately lead to data being leaked from the company that used the malicious models.”

When it comes to coding agents and code completion functionality in tools like GitHub Copilot, Ștefănescu worries about hallucinations. “An LLM will always generate the most statistically probable continuation of a given prompt, so there are no real guarantees in place that it will generate a real package from PyPI, for example, after the word ‘import’,” they say. “Some attackers are aware of this and register package names on platforms like npm and PyPI, filling in some functionality that code completion tools suggest in order to make the packages seem legitimate.”

If these packages are imported into real applications, they can do real damage.

Sensitive information inadvertently leaking into codebases because of developers using AI-powered coding tools is another key concern.

To address these risks, CISOs can establish protocols for downloading and integrating ML models or datasets from external platforms such as Hugging Face and ensure those protocols are incorporated in AI code agents’ instruction files where applicable. Such protocols can include implementing automated scanning tools to detect malicious code or backdoors, having a policy that only allows the use of models from verified publishers, or conducting internal testing in isolated environments.

Not all AI-based code comes from teams full of software engineers. “We see a lot of adoption being driven by data analysts, marketing teams, researchers, etc., within organizations,” says Darren Meyer, staff research engineer at Endor Labs.

These teams aren’t traditionally developing their own software but are increasingly writing simple tools that adopt AI libraries and models, so they’re often not aware of the risks involved. “This combination of shadow engineering with lower-than-average application security awareness can be a breeding ground for risk,” he adds.

To make sure these teams are working safely, CISOs must consider forming relationships with these teams early in the process. Cybersecurity leaders might also want to set up training programs tailored to non-traditional development teams to educate data analysts, marketing professionals, and researchers on the potential risks associated with AI-based tools and libraries.

“Security budgets don’t generally grow at the same pace that software development accelerates, and AI adoption is only widening that gap,” Meyer says. Application security is often underfunded in most organizations, yet allocating sufficient time and resources to it is essential, as AI adoption and AI-assisted coding accelerate the pace of software development.

“A portfolio of high-quality security tools that can help address this gap is no longer optional,” Meyer says. “And while tools are critical to closing the gap, so are AppSec and ProdSec staff that can effectively partner with developers — even non-traditional developers — and understand the technical, compliance, and security implications of AI.”

When it comes to securing enough resources to protect AI systems, some stakeholders might hesitate, viewing it as an optional expense rather than a critical investment. “AI adoption is a divisive topic in many organizations, with some leaders and teams being ‘all-in’ on adoption and some being strongly resistant,” Meyer says. “This tension can present challenges for insightful CISOs and business information security officers (BISOs).”

CISOs that are aware of both the advantages and disadvantages of this might try to set controls to manage risks effectively, but this might create the perception of holding the organization back from innovating if they don’t explain what they do properly. “Organizations need to develop comprehensive strategies that balance the productivity benefits of AI tools with robust security practices,” says Thomas Segura, senior technical writer at GitGuardian.

Cost-effective changes CISOs should implement

But if a security leader has 90 days and no budget to spare, which changes would have the biggest impact?

Datadog’s Lankford says a short, plain-language AI tool policy can make expectations clear. At a minimum, such a policy should spell out which tools employees can use, what code and data they can share, and which changes need specialist review. The aim is to give people answers they can use while they work, with a clear point of contact when they’re unsure.

It’s also important to understand that employees don’t want to circumvent policies. They just want to get their work done without friction and without waiting weeks for approval. Giving them the right tools makes the safer route easier to take.

By contrast, banning tools doesn’t work, because employees will find a way to still use them. A ban can push AI use out of sight, leaving security teams with less visibility into the code being written and the data being shared, Mimecast’s Nielsen says.

CISOs should look across the development process to identify where they can intervene and which risks they can address at each stage. “Understand what layers you can influence and what vulnerabilities you can influence at each layer: in the design phase, in the CLAUDE.md file, as the agent is writing, during a pull request, as part of an AI pentest,” Semgrep’s Paxton-Fead says.

It’s also important to explain to employees who are using AI code-generating tools that security lives in an implicit context: the threat model, the trust boundaries, what the attacker controls. “Agents cannot reliably plan and reason on these, so they optimize for the only signal they have, which is to satisfy the given functionality description,” Carnegie Mellon’s Wang says.

When things go wrong, people in the organization need to feel safe telling security what happened, including which unapproved tools they used and what data they shared. Fear of punishment can delay that conversation, leaving the team trying to contain a problem without the full picture.

“Offer amnesty instead of a crackdown, so people tell you the truth about what they’re using,” Nielsen advises.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/3633403/how-organizations-can-secure-their-ai-code.html, csoonline.com, 18.11.2026)
