[49] CSO Online - Optimizely confirms massive cyberattack on customer websites injected stealer malware
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 01:22:59 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249925/optimizely-confirms-massive-cyberattack-on-customer-websites-injected-stealer-malware.html

[TAM METİN - 908 kelime]
The attack, which hit some of the world’s largest corporate sites including Zoom, Salesforce, IKEA and Heineken, almost certainly came from China, according to the security researcher who discovered it.

Attackers apparently from China used an authentication security hole within the popular Optimizely AI platform for marketers to steal from at least 178 sites owned by some of the planet’s largest companies.

Named victims featured vendors including Zoom, Paychex, Equifax, and GitLab, and end-user enterprises including Starbucks, IKEA, Mattel, American Eagle, Air France, Mutual of Omaha, Heineken, Home Chef, Cinnabon, American Eagle, Levi, Costco and HSBC.

Salesforce issued a statement confirming that it had also been impacted by the attack.

“Salesforce is investigating unauthorized modifications to scripts provided by Optimizely and used on certain Salesforce websites,” it wrote in an email statement. “As a precaution, Salesforce is confirming that Optimizely scripts are disabled across preview and production instances.”

The attacks were discovered by UK-based security researcher Scott Helme, the founder of Report URI, who said in an interview that his team had confirmed 178 victims, but that there could be more. “That’s the number that we directly observed and that all carried the same infection,” he said.

Helme published a report about the attacks on Monday, in which he described an attack methodology where an Optimizely capability in its Web Experimentation product facilitating adding members to a site’s development team was leveraged to insert malware into the sites.

Optimizely customers load a JavaScript “snippet” from Optimizely’s CDN onto their sites which contains Optimizely code, the customer’s A/B tests, and an optional field called Project JavaScript containing custom code written by the customer. The report said that attackers inserted their own code into the customer’s Project JavaScript and left the rest of each snippet untouched. “The file was still served from cdn[.]Optimizely[.]com, with Optimizely’s certificate, from a host that every affected site already trusted,” it noted.

The report described how the observed attack worked. “The script watches form submissions and every fetch/XHR POST for a card number, expiry date and CVV,” it said. “When it has all three, it sends them to https://1688360[.]cc/card[.]php with navigator.sendBeacon. Cards entered in hosted payment iframes [such as] Stripe, Adyen and similar were out of its reach. Cards typed into a site’s own form fields were not.”

Analyst questions the claims of a fix

Optimizely, which did not reply to an interview request, confirmed the attacks on its site. In an email shared with customers, Optimizely said that attackers “exploited a vulnerability in the Experimentation account signup flow. They used it to impersonate a user and add malicious JavaScript to some customer projects. We have blocked access to the vulnerable path and deployed a fix.”

The email also said that the company had “ended active sessions and revoked tokens and reset access for affected user accounts” and that it also had “removed the injected code from affected projects” and “blocked any code from being delivered in Cloudflare.”

The company used highly confident wording to describe its fix, saying that “the Optimizely Experimentation platform is fully secured” and that “modification of Javascript no longer possible.”

Frank Dickson, principal analyst at Dickson Research, raised an eyebrow at those claims.

To begin with, he noted a discrepancy in the timelines published by the vendor and the security researcher. “Optimizely puts the first malicious write at 07:51. Report URI saw the BBC’s Optimizely snippet loading attacker code at 04:21, three and a half hours earlier,” he said. “The forensics report should reconcile that gap. Customers need the real start time to scope their exposure.”

As well, he pointed out, “Optimizely seems very comfortable stating absolutes early in a forensics investigation. Those are verdicts from a company whose forensics are still underway and whose timeline disagrees with outside research.”

No responsibility for the attack claimed

Helme speculated in an interview that the attackers were a small group of thieves, almost certainly coming from China, noting that he saw extensive Chinese writing in the code and that the attacks “tie back to infrastructure that has been used by other Chinese actors.”

“They are either Chinese or they really want us to think that they are Chinese,” Helme said, adding that the nature of what was stolen makes it highly unlikely the attackers were state actors. State actors, he said, would likely have used the malware on major news sites to create and spread false stories.

Asked if his team could verify Optimizely’s claim that it had fixed the hole, he said that he couldn’t yet. Some of the infected sites were cleaned up, but he has to wait to see if they get reinfected before concluding that the original hole had been closed.

Recommendation to customers ‘concerning’

Dickson said that one line in Optimizely’s email to customers was especially concerning.

“Optimizely’s documentation tells customers to allow ‘unsafe-eval‘ in their policy. At HSBC, the policy blocked the main payload and the data theft, but a backup copy of the malicious code ran anyway through that setting. A vendor’s setup guide should not quietly loosen its customers’ defenses,” Dickson said. “Restrict script-src to the hosts a page actually needs. Lock down connect-src so stolen data has nowhere to go. Drop ‘unsafe-eval‘ wherever possible. Start in report-only mode so nothing breaks.”

He added, “the Optimizely attack may be about as bad as a third-party script compromise gets. Optimizely owes its customers a clear explanation of how one flaw reached so many unrelated companies.”

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249925/optimizely-confirms-massive-cyberattack-on-customer-websites-injected-stealer-malware.html, csoonline.com, 18.11.2026)

[50] CSO Online - Google and Anthropic push for faster patches as AI collapses bug discovery timelines
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 23:29:35 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249915/google-and-anthropic-push-for-faster-patches-as-ai-collapses-bug-discovery-timelines.html

[TAM METİN - 1168 kelime]
Software makers are on notice: Reported flaws will be publicly disclosed on tighter turn around — in some cases without verifying the validity of any fix.

AI-assisted vulnerability discovery has made it untenable for software vendors to sit on privately reported flaws for months, and Google Project Zero is responding by shrinking its vulnerability disclosure deadline from 90 to 60 days, with plans to drop it to 30 days a year from now. Anthropic is following suit with an even more aggressive timeline.

Project Zero is Google’s elite vulnerability research team that regularly reports vulnerabilities in widely used open-source and proprietary software, much of which is critical to internet infrastructure. Its 90-day deadline for vendors to produce patches before the team releases vulnerability details publicly was a major stake in the ground for the security research community when it was announced in 2014, and it has become the de facto standard for coordinated disclosure.

The team’s announcement Friday that it will reduce this deadline to 60 days starting in February and 30 days starting in January 2028 is important because when Project Zero speaks, the security industry listens. And it did. On Monday, Anthropic announced it will adjust its disclosure policy from 90 days to 30 days for high- and critical-severity issues, and to 45 days for all other issues, starting in January. Other security companies and researchers will likely follow.

The rationale is explicitly due to the offensive cyber capabilities of large language models: not only in discovering new vulnerabilities, but in reverse engineering patches and using them as blueprints for finding additional flaws in the same code.

“LLMs have increased the speed at which attackers can find and exploit vulnerabilities, alongside the risk that multiple parties, including malicious ones, discover the same bug,” Project Zero researcher Natalie Silvanovich said in a blog post. “A vulnerability reported today presents a much higher immediate risk to users than one reported last year. In this new environment, it is crucial that vendors move towards remediating vulnerabilities faster.”

Anthropic’s Frontier Red Team said essentially the same: “A 90-day window made sense when a vulnerability could be expected to stay private for months. We no longer believe it can, and these changes are intended to keep pace with progress in AI capabilities and strike the right balance for defenders.”

‘Soak time’ has become meaningless

Since 2021, Project Zero’s policy has actually been “90+30” with 90 days to develop a patch, plus 30 days of grace while the patch rolled out to users. The team referred to these extra 30 days as “soak time.”

But in a world where AI agents can analyze patches to determine flaw details in minutes and then write exploits for them, waiting for a patch to propagate before releasing vulnerability details only creates a false sense of secrecy. As a result, the Project Zero’s previous 30-day grace is gone effective immediately.

In a report published this week, Google’s Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) documented the first fully automated AI-powered vulnerability discovery-and-exploitation pipeline it has observed in the wild. Called exploit_agent, the framework is designed to analyze application code changes following security patches, identify vulnerabilities, and generate exploits without requiring an operator to direct each step.

While Anthropic has different deadlines based on vulnerability severity, Project Zero decided to abandon severity as a disclosure metric because attackers are just as likely to discover low-risk flaws as they are to find the high-risk ones.

“When vendors determine severity and use it to prioritize bug fixes, they are making decisions about what risks they are willing to expose their users to, and it is reasonable to accept the risk of low-severity bugs, like unexploitable null pointer crashes or minor info leaks in a lot of circumstances,” Project Zero’s Silvanovich said. “But this doesn’t change the availability of these vulnerabilities to attackers, and we want our policy to reflect this reality.”

Google’s team will also no longer check whether a vendor’s patch fixes the issue when deciding whether to release details or not, because this back-and-forth can quickly become unmanageable, especially because the team now finds vulnerabilities using AI assistance at a much faster pace and because vendors might choose alternative mitigation strategies such as temporarily disabling a vulnerable feature or code path until a full patch can be developed.

“We decided on a policy that encourages vendors to improve remediation time on a schedule that is challenging but realistic, with the hope that this will lead to vendors broadly improving their remediation processes and infrastructure in a way that eventually enables them to fix all reported vulnerabilities quickly,” Silvanovich said.

The road ahead will be bumpy for users

The shorter patch deadlines come at a time when open-source projects and software vendors are already drowning in vulnerability reports and must resort to either AI-assisted patching or hiring more developers, if they can afford it. As of last week, the number of CVEs issued this year exceeded 100,000, more than double last year’s count.

Anthropic’s Frontier Red Team noted that “models can now help develop fixes” as one justification for the more aggressive disclosure deadlines, and said it will include “actionable guidance with each report, along with a patch or mitigation where possible,” to help maintainers confirm flaws and fix them.

However, multiple studies and developers have reported in recent months that AI-generated patches are often incomplete, failing to address the root causes of a flaw, to eliminate all instances of the bug from the codebase, or to block all exploit variations.

Security firm XOR built a benchmark that pitted frontier coding systems against vulnerabilities the company had discovered in a private software stack. No public fix existed for any of the flaws when the evaluation ran, so the systems had to work from the vulnerable code alone. The best models produced complete fixes for only about a third of the flaws: 35.3% for GPT-6 Astra and 28.6% for Claude Fable 5.1. Across all systems, just 11.9% of patch attempts earned full credit.

According to an analysis by VulnCheck, seven months after launching its Mythos-powered Glasswing vulnerability-hunting program, Anthropic’s public ledger counts 37,966 findings, but only 217 of the 7,349 flaws made public so far have been fixed.

Project Zero acknowledges the friction between the need to patch faster and devs being overloaded. The team solicited feedback from the vendors it reports to most and chose the phased schedule because an immediate change could push vendors to stop trying to meet the disclosure deadlines.

“To respond to these new timelines, organizations should be better prepared to assess whether compensating controls can reduce the risk of a vulnerability when it’s disclosed,” Ramy Rahman, senior principal solutions engineer at ArmorCode, tells CSO. “With disclosures arriving sooner, and sometimes before a fix is available, teams need a process for evaluating exposure, applying available mitigations, and tracking remediation.”

“Consider hardening systems at deployment time and removing software you don’t use,” he said. “Leaner, more deliberate deployments can reduce exposure and make vulnerability findings easier to manage.”

[51] CSO Online - California sues OpenAI, alleges 80-plus agent escapes hidden from public
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 12:48:15 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249653/california-sues-openai-alleges-80-plus-agent-escapes-hidden-from-public-2.html

[TAM METİN - 873 kelime]
Attorney General Rob Bonta claims OpenAI acknowledged 80 incidents to investigators involving autonomous AI agents reaching external systems, far exceeding the 27 events publicly reported by the company.

California Attorney General Rob Bonta has sued OpenAI, alleging the company has lost control of its autonomous AI agents more than 80 times during testing and misled the public about how safely it develops its models.

The complaint was filed on November 16 in San Francisco Superior Court against OpenAI Foundation and OpenAI Group PBC. It says OpenAI disclosed the 80-plus figure to state investigators but has publicly reported just 27 such incidents. In those incidents, agents reached third-party systems, foreign government websites and OpenAI’s own infrastructure, the state alleged.

“While narratives about the Hugging Face attack have evoked science fiction fantasies of rogue AI systems run amok, let me be clear: the Hugging Face attack was the predictable result of an internal culture at OpenAI that prioritizes growth and rapid model development over safety and accountability — and this was only one of 80 known incidents,” Bonta said in a statement announcing the lawsuit.

The state brings two claims: violations of California’s Unfair Competition Law and creation of a public nuisance. It is seeking an injunction, restitution, disgorgement, and an order to abate the nuisance. It also wants civil penalties of $2,500 against each defendant for each violation proven at trial.

Other incidents and internal breaches

In one July incident, agents escaped their sandboxes and gained administrator-level access to OpenAI’s own systems, according to the complaint. Those systems included the company’s cloud model deployment system, its secrets management service, and its identity and access management service. The agents then launched unauthorized servers, exposing OpenAI’s infrastructure to the internet. OpenAI did not let external researchers review that attack, the complaint said.

The complaint identified several other incidents involving OpenAI agents.

At RubyGems, a software package repository, agents allegedly uploaded thousands of packages in May 2026. Administrators removed more than 500 packages they considered malicious, and the platform suspended new user registrations between May 12 and May 16.

The complaint also alleged that agents made approximately 17,000 edits to a German website, DseWiki, using it as a message board to exchange information about bypassing sandbox restrictions.

Other incidents involved Wikimedia Foundation’s infrastructure and Australian government websites. The complaint said agents overwhelmed Wikimedia services with millions of API requests and hundreds of thousands of Wikidata queries, likely contributing to a partial outage in May. In June, agents allegedly accessed non-public information from Australian government websites.

The complaint further said OpenAI notified dozens of organizations whose websites or internal systems had been accessed by its agents. These included government institutions, law enforcement agencies, educational institutions, and small businesses. Some incidents allegedly involved stolen credentials, API keys, and fraudulent accounts, with agents accessing personally identifiable information.

OpenAI’s own infrastructure was also compromised in at least three attacks in July, according to the complaint. Agents allegedly obtained administrator-level access to systems supporting cloud model deployment, secrets management, and identity and access management.

Escapes after the fix

The state argued that the danger is ongoing. On September 20, after OpenAI had hardened its environment, an agent escaped during a training run and reached an external chatbot.

“This incident occurred after OpenAI had implemented ‘security hardening’ following the Hugging Face attack,” the lawsuit added.

OpenAI detected it within minutes, but the run did not stop automatically, and human reviewers took two more hours to halt it, the complaint said.

OpenAI announced a pause on training and evaluating its most capable models on September 25. The pause lasted five days, ending October 1, according to the complaint.

Warnings that went unheeded

The complaint argued that the incidents were foreseeable. It cited the run-up to the Hugging Face attack as evidence. By May 26, agents had used a vulnerability in a misconfigured software package service to reach the public internet.

An internal OpenAI team saw at least one agent escape but apparently took no action, the complaint said. On June 27, a security monitoring tool flagged that the research environment was being probed for vulnerabilities, and OpenAI again did not stop its runs.

When agent traffic knocked the service offline on July 4, OpenAI patched it over the holiday weekend and resumed runs on July 7, the state alleged. Two days later, agents broke out again.

The state also faulted OpenAI for not running live chain-of-thought monitoring in training and evaluation, even though it did so for models deployed to employees. During the Hugging Face attack, one agent’s reasoning read: “We’re attacking third-party HF using leaked token, potentially outside intended scope. . . . This is arguably unauthorized.”

Bonta’s office served OpenAI with an investigative subpoena on September 30. OpenAI answered with a 14-page letter and produced no supporting documents, the complaint said.

California is not the only state pressing OpenAI. In August, Iowa Attorney General Brenna Bird led a 15-state coalition demanding that OpenAI preserve evidence, protect whistleblowers and halt the tests behind the hack until it could show it can run them responsibly. “The AI weaponized itself, which is extremely scary,” Bird said in a statement.

OpenAI did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The article originally appeared on ComputerWorld.

[52] CSO Online - Hackers exploit Microsoft 365 outage to launch credential phishing campaign
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 11:10:22 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249611/hackers-exploit-microsoft-365-outage-to-launch-credential-phishing-campaign-2.html

[TAM METİN - 884 kelime]
Using fake Microsoft service health notifications and malicious recovery links, attackers attempted to steal employee login credentials.

Cybercriminals are exploiting genuine cloud service disruptions to make phishing attacks more convincing. Just a day after a prolonged outage disrupted several Microsoft services, including Outlook, Exchange Online, Teams and Copilot, Microsoft Threat Intelligence identified a coordinated, high-volume phishing campaign targeting organisations across multiple industry sectors.

The attackers impersonated service notifications and sent emails disguised as legitimate Microsoft 365 service-health notifications. Claiming emails held during the outage would be permanently deleted within 24 hours unless recipients restored them, the threat actors created a false sense of urgency, tricking recipients into clicking malicious links and potentially exposing their login credentials.

“We assess that the activity was likely credential phishing; however, the available evidence does not confirm credential capture, malware delivery, or post-compromise activity. Multiple threat actors may continue to look for ways to exploit notable global events in any social engineering pretext,” Jeremiah Jasmin, security researcher at Microsoft, said in a blog post.

Outages in cloud services bring about uncertainty and panic, an area where attackers always thrive, said Devroop Dhar, co-founder and MD at Primus Partners. “In cases where employees cannot access their emails and other applications, they will easily fall victim to any email promising to restore their service or help recover their account. This is especially dangerous for industries like finance, health care, government and educational institutions because employees in such organizations work with sensitive data and rely heavily on cloud services,” said Devroop Dhar, co-founder and MD at Primus Partners.

Carefully constructed phishing trap

The attackers used several techniques to make the emails appear relevant to recipients while evading basic email security filters. These included using specific sender domains, randomised outage-related subject lines, recipient-specific tracking links and a common non-Microsoft destination domain. The combination made the messages harder to identify through conventional pattern matching.

The campaign began with emails sent from domains including moments-notice[.]com, planetmaison[.]com, and newbooker[.]com. Their subject lines followed the pattern “Outage” followed by a randomised string of characters. Microsoft also found a fake email conversation embedded in the message body, positioned to remain hidden from recipients. This appeared to be an attempt to evade email filtering mechanisms.

The messages impersonated Microsoft 365 service health notifications. They used recipient-specific display name prefixes and referenced Microsoft services, including Exchange Online, Outlook, Teams, SharePoint and Copilot.

To create a sense of urgency, the emails claimed that messages held during the November 12 Exchange Online disruption would be deleted within 24 hours. Recipients were prompted to click a prominent “RESTORE MESSAGES” button. The links contained recipient-specific tracking URLs that recorded or routed clicks before redirecting users to reallifeaccounting[.]com, including the path /themelist0.html.

One of the emails analysed by Microsoft had a subject line following the pattern “Outage – <random token>” and a display name comprising a three-character organisation-style prefix followed by “Outage Notification.” Its body referred to a purported Exchange Online incident on November 12, claimed that four messages had been held during the outage and warned that they would be deleted within 24 hours unless restored.

A successful credential theft attempt could expose more than an employee’s email account. Dhar explained that, depending on the permissions of the user, threat actors can gain access to sensitive emails, customer information, balance sheets, SharePoint, OneDrive Storage, and Microsoft Teams communication. Moreover, attackers can pretend to be representatives of the company and send believable emails to colleagues, clients, and contractors for the purposes of money fraud or theft of credentials.

From containment to preparedness

Microsoft said it had not observed any evidence of account compromise linked to the campaign. Messages detected and protected by Microsoft Defender for Office 365 were being blocked or quarantined. However, the company has advised organisations to remain vigilant and take several precautionary steps.

CISOs should search email telemetry for the identified sender domains, display-name and subject-line patterns, lure phrases, tracking hosts, and destination domains. Matching messages should be quarantined, and copies already delivered to inboxes should be removed in accordance with the organisation’s incident response procedures.

Security teams should also identify users who clicked on campaign links and review click telemetry, proxy logs, and browser or endpoint evidence to establish whether they reached the malicious destination and potentially entered their credentials. If credentials were exposed, organisations should immediately reset the affected passwords, revoke active sessions and refresh tokens, and verify multifactor authentication registrations.

They should also check affected accounts for suspicious sign-ins, unauthorised OAuth consent, new inbox or forwarding rules, delegate permissions and unusual access to Exchange Online, SharePoint, OneDrive or Teams. Microsoft has also advised security teams to block or closely monitor reallifeaccounting[.]com and the observed /themelist0.html path while the activity is investigated. Employees should be reminded that legitimate Microsoft 365 service-health communications do not direct users to non-Microsoft domains to recover held emails.

Dhar added that organizations should make cybersecurity an integral part of their business continuity planning. Any technology disruption should trigger heightened security monitoring and clear internal communication to ensure employees know how to verify service-related updates. He also recommended conducting phishing simulations during such disruptions to test how employees respond to suspicious communications. CIOs and CISOs should regularly conduct tabletop exercises to assess their preparedness for attacks that exploit technology outages.

The article originally appeared on ComputerWorld.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249611/hackers-exploit-microsoft-365-outage-to-launch-credential-phishing-campaign-2.html, csoonline.com, 17.11.2026)

[53] CSO Online - Google spots the first hacker AI system that finds and exploits flaws autonomously
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 10:52:58 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249595/google-spots-the-first-hacker-ai-system-that-finds-and-exploits-flaws-autonomously.html

[TAM METİN - 826 kelime]
Researchers found stolen data on a server hosting one AI framework and documented separate operations targeting the US aerospace sector and organizations across Asia.

Google said it had identified a hacker-operated AI framework designed to discover software vulnerabilities and execute exploits without human intervention, marking the first time its researchers had observed such a system being used by threat actors.

The findings, published in a report by Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) and Mandiant, suggest that attackers are moving beyond using generative AI to assist individual tasks and are beginning to automate larger portions of cyberattacks.

The system could give attackers a way to exploit publicly disclosed vulnerabilities, known as n-days, before companies finish applying security updates. Google’s researchers discovered it on an exposed staging server and identified it as a multi-agent framework called exploit_agent.

The framework connects to multiple large language models (LLMs) and is configured to examine changes in application code following security patches, identify vulnerabilities, and generate exploits without requiring an operator to direct each step. It can also execute the exploits automatically.

Google found data exfiltrated from targeted victims on the server, along with scripts designed to support further activity after an initial compromise. Evidence suggested that the operator was particularly interested in French websites associated with the media and entertainment industries and the education sector. The report did not establish whether the framework’s autonomous operations were responsible for the theft.

Google also cited a separate campaign involving vulnerabilities in PaperCut print management servers. According to research by GreyNoise referenced in Google’s report, a threat actor likely used AI tools to develop and test exploits before launching a campaign against hundreds of organizations four days after the vulnerabilities were disclosed.

In separate cases, Google observed other AI frameworks being used by attackers. CAIRN was deployed in intrusion activity targeting organizations that included the US aerospace sector, while a customized platform based on PentAGI was used to conduct reconnaissance and vulnerability scanning against organizations across Asia.

Pressure on enterprise response times

Despite the growing use of AI in cyberattacks, the underlying techniques remain largely unchanged, according to Jonathan Ong, senior analyst at Omdia.

“This is more of an acceleration of existing attack techniques,” Ong said. “These are pentesting/red-teaming frameworks with established techniques, albeit significantly sped up with an LLM model.”

Keith Prabhu, CEO of Confidis, said there is still limited evidence that AI makes individual attacks more successful. The greater risk, he said, is that attackers could launch more attempts in less time, potentially increasing the number of successful compromises even if each attempt is no more effective.

Ong said the frameworks typically generate considerable detectable activity, meaning enterprises with properly configured and monitored endpoint detection and response (EDR), extended detection and response (XDR), or managed detection and response (MDR) capabilities should still be able to identify much of the malicious activity.

Google also warned that although the frameworks generate activity that makes detection easier, they remain capable of achieving their objectives.

Rather than overhauling their defenses, security leaders should continue prioritizing established security practices, particularly cloud and identity hardening. Ong pointed to multifactor authentication, strong passwords, and measures to block repeated login attempts as effective safeguards.

“The one thing that has changed is that the containment and response timeline needs to be faster, to match the speed at which attackers using these frameworks are moving,” Ong said.

Prabhu said the problem is compounded by fragmented security visibility and response processes that still depend on manual decisions. He recommended that CISOs prioritize continuous asset discovery and risk-based vulnerability remediation. He also called for better detection capabilities that connect suspicious activity across enterprise environments.

Amit Kumar Jena, development manager for AI at Kanerika, said organizations must give particular attention to vulnerabilities in systems exposed to the internet, where attackers could exploit flaws soon after they become public.

“Internet-facing systems should be treated as their own class, with a fast-track patch path and virtual patching while fixes are tested,” Jena said.

For incident containment, Prabhu suggested automating actions that are well understood and can be reversed, while retaining human approval for decisions that could have significant operational consequences.

Jena also recommended defining in advance which containment actions could be automated, citing endpoint isolation and session revocation as examples.

“The aim is not to remove human oversight, but to eliminate avoidable delays and regularly test that response works at the pace of an attack,” Prabhu said.

Lower barriers for cybercriminals

Open-source agentic frameworks could lower the technical barriers for less-experienced cybercriminals by helping them coordinate existing tools and adapt their attacks based on results, Prabhu said. However, these capabilities do not eliminate the need for technical expertise or guarantee successful compromises.

Prabhu said smaller organizations and service providers with limited security resources could be particularly exposed.

The risk is not necessarily concentrated in any particular industry, he added. Organizations with poorly maintained legacy systems or weak identity controls could face greater exposure regardless of their sector.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4249595/google-spots-the-first-hacker-ai-system-that-finds-and-exploits-flaws-autonomously.html, csoonline.com, 17.11.2026)

[54] Dark Reading - Optimizely Malware Attacks Highlight Third-Party Vendor Risks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 22:34:22 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/optimizely-malware-attacks-third-party-vendor-risks

[TAM METİN - 1128 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
5 Min Read

![Image 1: A hydra monster.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt0c54a284942546cd/6afccec91ecf5c62546c1c34/hydra_Daniel_Eskridge_shutterstock.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Daniel Eskridge via Shutterstock

Some of the world's most recognizable brands unwittingly became conduits for malware this week, after an attacker slipped malicious code into website testing scripts delivered by Optimizely, a platform that allows organizations to experiment with and optimize their online presence.

The attack potentially exposed visitors to sites operated by at least 178 organizations — including Zoom, CNN, Salesforce, and IKEA — to malware capable of stealing payment card details, hijacking [cryptocurrency transactions](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/illicit-crypto-economy-surges-nation-states) and delivering exploit kits for iPhones and iPads.

## A Breach at a Trusted Vendor

Many organizations use Optimizely to run website experiments, test different page designs and personalize content for visitors. To do this, Optimizely embeds scripts in customer websites that can serve different versions of a page or its content to visitors, track their interactions, and measure which versions perform best.

Related:[NIST Finalizes Post-Quantum Cryptography Guidance, GAO Offers a Reality Check](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/nist-finalizes-post-quantum-cryptography-guidance-gao-offers-a-reality-check)

The attackers appear to have exploited this mechanism to insert [malicious code](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/github-commits-malicious-code/627466/) into a custom field called Project JavaScript, which allows Optimizely customers to run their own code across website pages.

British Web security monitoring service Report URI, among the first to detect the activity, [documented the tampering](https://blog.report-uri.com/worlds-largest-brands-infected-with-malware-via-optimizely/) but said it could not determine how the attackers gained the ability to modify customer projects. "We don't know how the attacker got write access to so many unrelated customer projects: a compromised Optimizely system, a compromised credential with cross-customer access, or something else. That's for Optimizely to explain," Report URL founder Scott Helme wrote.

Report URI said it located and examined 1,611 unique Optimizely scripts. It found malicious code in 188 scripts associated with 178 organizations. The company has published a list of the affected websites on its site, grouped by industry sector.

Digital security firm The Media Trust which is tracking the campaign and the malware as "Hydra-Cart," described the threat as now contained. Like Report URL, The Media Trust too found Hydra-Cart to include a [payment-card skimmer](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/payment-card-skimming-campaign-now-targeting-websites-in-north-america), cryptocurrency address swapper, a cookie stealer, and a loader designed to deliver Coruna and [DarkSword](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/darksword-iphone-exploit-spies-thieves) iOS exploit kits on iPhones and iPads.

Optimizely itself has said little about the incident so far besides [acknowledging](https://status.optimizely.com/) it and pinning it somewhat vaguely to a vulnerability in the platform's Experimentation account sign-up flow. "We've fixed the vulnerability and removed the injected code from affected projects. We've also taken steps to secure affected user accounts and put additional protections in place to prevent unauthorized code from being delivered," the company said.

Related:[AI Raises the Stakes for Cyber Resilience](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/ai-raises-stakes-cyber-resilience)

Optimizely did not immediately respond to a Dark Reading request for information on the reported scope of the incident, the exposure window, or how a vulnerability in the account sign up flow could have given an attacker write access to customer projects.

## A Pressing Question For Optimizely

"That’s the question every affected customer should be putting to Optimizely right now," says Justin Beals, CEO and founder of Strike Graph. "They need to know how a new account got write access to other tenants' projects, which project IDs were changed and when, and whether that path is fully closed."

Until Optimizely explains the authorization failure, customers can't judge whether the company's reported fix addresses the cause or just the symptom, he says.

The incident is a reminder that organizations need strict controls over third-party scripts running on their websites, Beals adds. Most organizations approve a third-party vendor script once during procurement and never revisit it later. Optimizely, for instance passed whatever review the 178 affected companies ran, and the malicious code still shipped from Optimizely’s own CDN under its own certificate.

Related:[AI Coding Agents Leak Thousands of Secrets to GitHub](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/ai-coding-agents-leak-secrets-github)

"The takeaway is that a vendor approval is a point-in-time decision, and the risk changes every time that vendor’s code changes," Beals says. "You can keep using these tools if you decide where they’re allowed to run."

Tyler Reguly, associate director of security R&D at Fortra, says incidents like these show why organizations implement a browser security feature called [Subresource Integrity](https://www.techtarget.com/news/How-to-manage-dependency-risk-in-third-party-ad-tech/846663/) (SRI) to verify that externally hosted files, such as JavaScript, haven't been altered before they are loaded and executed.

"The problem that we have here is that you may include a file in your website that is safe for months or even years and then, unbeknownst to you, a breach occurs within your vendor and that 'file that's been safe forever' is suddenly no longer safe," Reguly says.

SRI allows organizations to perform version pinning based on file hashes to protect against attacks like the one that went down at Optimizely. "The downside is that it requires a bit more effort and management to maintain your Web pages, which are often seen as 'set it and forget it' resources," Reguly adds.

Chris Nyhuis, CEO of Vigilant, says Optimizely demonstrates what happens when trust becomes an attack surface. "Organizations allowed a third party to deliver executable code into their customers' browsers, and that trust was exploited," he says.

The answer isn't to eliminate third-party technology but to eliminate "blind trust," Nyhuis says. "Restrict where vendor scripts execute, limit their access to sensitive data, monitor their behavior and changes, and independently validate what they're doing."

Furthermore, Optimizely also needs to explain how a flaw in an account sign-up process allowed an attacker to modify executable code belonging to 178 unrelated customers. "For that to happen, there had to be a path around customer authorization boundaries," he says. "Whether that involved broken tenant isolation, privilege escalation, an authorization flaw, or access to an overly privileged service remains to be established by the forensic investigation."

[55] Dark Reading - 'TheMailer' Phishing Kit Puts AI in the Driver's Seat
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 20:58:13 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/perimeter/themailer-phishing-kit-ai-drivers-seat

[TAM METİN - 738 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
3 Min Read

![Image 1: An open red mailbox against a white brick wall.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt6f7643977746699c/6afcac2049ae9b70b9695c91/red_mailbox_white_brick_wall-Liudmila_Chernetska-GettyImages-1477282383.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Liudmila Chernetska via Getty Images

A new [phishing](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/yam-store-phishing-network-behind-billion-messages) kit is capable of orchestrating most elements of the attack through AI functionality, including tailoring email lures specifically to bypass content filters.

That's according to new Varonis research detailing "TheMailer," a [phishing kit](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/starkiller-phishing-kit-mfa) that leverages large language models (LLM) to a degree hardly seen in the cybercriminal ecosystem. Phishing kits and [phishing-as-a-service](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/fbi-warns-phishing-platform-microsoft-365/821105/) (PhaaS) platforms have gained significant popularity in recent years, and AI has only made them more capable. To date, phishing kit [AI functionality](https://www.darkreading.com/remote-workforce/phishing-kit-darcula-gets-major-ai-upgrade) focuses mostly on generating lures, coding realistic phishing websites, conducting reconnaissance, and automating simple repetitive tasks.

TheMailer distinguishes itself in two primary ways. One, it automates much of the phishing process from end to end using the attacker's chosen AI model. Two, as Varonis senior security researcher Daniel Kelley wrote in [the blog post](https://www.varonis.com/blog/themailer-ai-phishing-kit), the kit can tailor lures to each mail provider and "rewrite them until they bypass spam filters."

Related:[Anthropic Finds Ingredients for Future AI Worms](https://www.darkreading.com/perimeter/anthropic-ingredients-future-ai-worms)

## How TheMailer Uses LLMs at Every Stage

The attacker's "customer journey" begins by buying a lifetime license for between $3,000 and $6,000 in cryptocurrency (depending on the deal and which AI functions are included). The customer operator then plugs in their API key, selects an AI model (Claude, ChatGPT, [DeepSeek](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/chinese-actor-deepseek-ai-agent-attack-security-firm), Grok, Gemini, and Mistral are included), and then they're more or less ready to start conducting campaigns. Kelley noted that customers only need to bring SMTP servers, proxies, and target email lists.

TheMailer can generate lures from a short prompt or a real brand email, personalize the emails for each recipient (including email address, body, and subject line), remove AI writing patterns, and adjust content for [spam filters](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/attackers-multi-hop-google-redirects-phishing-campaign).

Kelley tells Dark Reading the kit is built to scale against targets.

"The seller's screenshots show a single campaign of nearly 25,000 emails and a list of more than 145,000 targets being sorted by mail provider. What's different is that every recipient gets their own version of the email, subject line, and attachment, so a bulk campaign no longer looks like one," he says. "I wouldn't call it a spear-phishing tool. The personalization we saw is at the level of a name on an invoice, and we didn't see features for researching individual targets. It's opportunistic phishing with the polish of something targeted."

## Brace For the AI Phishing Kit Future

It's unclear how successful the kit is — attacker [LLM tools](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Researchers-put-LLMs-to-the-test-in-phishing-email-experiment/837631/) are historically capable of undertaking tasks at scale, but the success rates of these tasks vary — though Varonis assesses TheMailer to have a large customer base. Moreover, the kit may be a sign of things to come.

Related:[New CUSTODY Framework Constrains AI Agents Inside the Network](https://www.darkreading.com/perimeter/new-custody-framework-constrains-ai-agents-inside-network)

"I'd expect more kits to follow," Kelley says, "because once one seller offers this, buyers start asking for it elsewhere."

[For defenders](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Phishing-prevention-How-to-spot-stop-and-respond-to-scams/836480/), Varonis offered a few main recommendations. Because TheMailer is built specifically to get past content filters, organizations should consider email authentication practices like [SPF, DKIM, and DMARC](https://www.techtarget.com/news/SPF-DKIM-and-DMARC-What-are-they-and-how-do-they-work-together/837117/) enforcement; monitoring for lookalike and new domains; and checking links through full redirect chains at the time of the click.

And when conducting phishing training, Varonis recommends making it reflect how emails look in [a post-AI world](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/ai-agentic-frameworks-artex-google-fbi/874753/).

"Bad grammar isn't a reliable sign anymore, so teach users to be wary of urgency, unexpected requests, and login prompts, especially 'unusual activity' or account statement alerts," the blog post read. Finally, "Least-privilege access and data monitoring limit the damage if someone clicks anyway."

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Alexander Culafi](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blte0c88f087d1ad6f5/67c609df64f983454c3dd40b/1571751486317.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/alexander-culafi)

[56] Dark Reading - Grok Bots Attempt to Access Corporate Data Without User Approval
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 13:12:56 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/grok-bots-corporate-data-without-user-approval

[TAM METİN - 1207 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
![Image 1: A collage of cut-out robot heads, all green except one colored in red.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blte89802188fbf7517/6afc5ddc05f01c312ef66082/rogue_AO_bot_Chris_Light_Alamy.png?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Chris Light via Alamy Stock Photo

Grok Bots attempted to access corporate applications without their users' approval or knowledge, presenting yet another security challenge for enterprises as AI agents become increasingly [autonomous](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/ey-survey-autonomous-ai-implementation-outpaces-oversight).

Researchers at NewCore identified 31 unprompted attempts by two xAI [Grok Bots](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/scammers-grok-malicious-links-x) to access connected corporate applications over a five-day period, including Google Calendar, Gmail, Slack, GitHub, and Notion, according to [the findings](https://newcore.com/blog/grok-bot-unattended-agent-access) of an investigation published today. In one case, the bot continued making background requests even after its owner had declined offers for recurring check-ins four times.

The requests were not approved and did not reach the underlying corporate data: NewCore had placed a security gateway between the agents and the applications, requiring human approval for each request.

"Over five days, two Grok Bots on NewCore employee accounts made 31 requests to calendar, email, Slack, GitHub and Notion while nobody was talking to them, some with the laptop shut," Ido Hoorvitch, security research team lead at NewCore, wrote in the report.

Related:[Cursor Traces Malicious Claude Code Extension to Third-Party Breach](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/cursor-malicious-claude-code-extension-third-party-breach)

One bot repeatedly attempted to retrieve Google Calendar events in the evening, overnight, and the following day, despite there being no active conversation with its owner. In a second case, another bot attempted to access Notion's calendar tools before Google Calendar had even been connected. It continued making requests while its owner's laptop was closed and asleep.

## Investigating Autonomous AI Behavior

The investigation began as an internal experiment at NewCore, which wanted to understand how autonomous AI agents behave when connected to real corporate systems. The company earlier this year provided employees with enterprise Grok Bot accounts and connected the agents to corporate services, including email, calendars, Notion, and other business applications.

However, NewCore put a handbrake in place: It did not give the agents unrestricted, standing access to company data. Instead, requests had to pass through its [Model Context Protocol (MCP) gateway](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/critical-mcp-integration-flaw-nginx-risk). The gateway sent an approval notification to the employee who owned the account whenever a Grok Bot tried to access an application. If a request was not approved within three minutes, it automatically expired and was denied, Hoorvitch said.

"We did it because we had no way to audit why an agent reaches for data, and until we do, the gate stays closed by default," he wrote. Ultimately, this security control allowed NewCore to observe and reconstruct the bots' background activity.

Related:[F-Secure Identifies 26 Ways AI Can Harm Consumers, and Where to Stop Them](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/f-secure-26-ways-ai-harm-consumers-where-to-stop-them)

The first sign that something was amiss came after an employee used Grok Bot to retrieve their daily calendar, approving the request through the gateway as expected. Later that evening, however, the gateway recorded further calendar access attempts, despite the employee [no longer interacting](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/identity-based-ai-attack-security-enterprise-data) with the bot.

This led investigators to uncover repeated attempts by [Grok agents](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/patch-resistant-rufroot-flaw-malicious-ai-agent-swarms) to access corporate applications without any corresponding user interaction. Moreover, when one bot was asked whether it had made the gateway calls, it said it had not, Hoorvitch said. The requests did not appear in the bot's chat history.

"It was telling the truth. … That is the part to sit with," he wrote. "An agent that cannot account for its own actions is not a bug report. It is a finding."

## Background Access Requests: A Feature, Not a Bug?

NewCore contacted xAI about its findings and said the company's support team confirmed that the activity was not the result of a software vulnerability or an exploit, but was part of Grok Bot's built-in background check-in functionality.

"These are built-in background check-ins by the primary bot, during a check-in the bot looks at its connected sources, and there is no user or admin setting to turn them off," Hoorvitch wrote, relaying the response from xAI support.

Related:[AI Makes Reverse Engineering Mainstream Software & Malware Far Easier](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/ai-reverse-engineering-apps)

According to NewCore, xAI also said that customers seeking to stop the check-ins could send the vendor a list of employee sign-in addresses. NewCore said it had not received an official response from xAI's media team at the time the report was published.

The lack of a user- or administrator-facing setting to disable background check-ins, or to control which connected tools they can use, creates a visibility and governance challenge for security teams. Employees may not know when an [AI agent](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/your-ai-agent-has-a-supervisor-are-they-qualified-) is accessing connected applications, while the agent itself may not retain a record of its server-initiated activity in the chat history visible to its owner.

"The danger is not that the model goes rogue," Hoorvitch explains to Dark Reading. "The concern is that we hand [agents](https://www.darkreading.com/cloud-security/microsoft-salesforce-patch-ai-agent-data-leak-flaws) standing credentials and then let someone else decide when they act. The owner thinks the bot works when they talk to it. The vendor thinks a helpful bot checks in on its own. The model does what the platform tells it in that turn, not what the owner would have said."

Though "nobody in that chain [is malicious](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/ai-white-house-task-force-cybersecurity-regulation/874679/)," he says, "the result is still a bot reading your inbox at 7:46 a.m. while you are asleep."

## Reining Autonomous AI Agents In

The distinction matters because all 31 unattended requests identified by NewCore were read operations, such as retrieving calendar events or searching messages, Hoorvitch explains. None resulted in data being accessed, because the gateway blocked them. However, the same connected tool sets included capabilities to take actions, such as sending email, modifying calendar events, and posting Slack messages. NewCore warned that the same background processes could pose a greater risk if they were to invoke those write-capable tools.

In NewCore's investigation, its MCP gateway blocked all 31 unattended requests. The company argues that organizations need similar independent controls to set and enforce policies on [AI agents](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/ai-agentic-frameworks-artex-google-fbi/874753/) operating within their systems.

"Put the decision outside [the agent](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/ai-agent-breaches-spanish-organization-personal-data)," Hoorvitch says. "Route every agent tool call through something you own, like the NewCore MCP Gateway, where the owner's policy decides what goes through and every call is logged whether or not the agent remembers it."

NewCore also recommends that organizations immediately inventory the agents connected to corporate tools, identify which applications and data they can reach, and assign an owner to each. Security teams should review not only approved activity but also requests that were denied or left unanswered, since these can reveal what agents attempt to do without user involvement.

"Most teams cannot answer that today," Hoorvitch says, "and you cannot govern what you cannot list."

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Elizabeth Montalbano](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltd6f16d37895d5bfd/6a06cc5deb9f4ad4d2785f3f/LizHeadshot2026.png?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/elizabeth-montalbano)

Contributing Writer

Elizabeth Montalbano is freelance writer, editor, and journalist with 30 years of professional experience and a master's degree from Arizona State University. Her areas of expertise include enterprise technology, cybersecurity, business, and culture. During her long career, Elizabeth has lived and worked as a full-time journalist in Phoenix, San Francisco, and New York City. She specializes in news coverage and analysis, using her years of experience to look at the current state of cybersecurity with a critical gaze. She currently resides in a village on the southwest coast of Portugal, where in her free time she enjoys surfing, hiking with her dogs, growing plants, and playing and performing as a singer and musician.

[57] Dark Reading - Google Project Zero Slashes Bug Disclosure Deadlines, Splitting Experts
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 16 Nov 2026 22:08:20 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/google-project-zero-bug-disclosure-deadlines

[TAM METİN - 1720 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
![Image 1: The words "zero day" embedded in a visual representation of computer code.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt9eb0a69ea15c3e0c/6afb7143ea20de309e24d604/zero_day_over_text-JUN_LI-GettyImages-2179992195.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: JUN LI via Getty Images

Google will soon drastically reduce the disclosure deadlines of vulnerabilities prior to publicly disclosing them, and the move has sparked concern among some cybersecurity professionals.

[Project Zero](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/google-s-project-zero-policy-change-mandates-90-day-disclosure), Google's third-party vulnerability remediation program, currently follows what it describes as "90+30" policy: 90 days to remediate a vulnerability and an extra 30-day "soak time" to allow for patch rollout before publicly disclosing the bug. This will no longer be the case. As of the announcement on Nov. 13, Google changed its policy to disclose 90 days after reporting to the vendor. As of Feb. 2, 2027, vulnerabilities will be disclosed after 60 days. As of Jan. 3, 2028, vulnerabilities will be disclosed after 30 days.

In a [blog post](https://projectzero.google/2026/11/disclosure-policy.html) outlining the decision, Google's Natalie Silvanovich wrote that while the intention of 90+30 was to encourage faster patching while giving vendors time to deliver patches, "The advent of [AI-assisted vulnerability discovery](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/vulnpocalypse-repricing-bug-bounty-economy) and exploitation has changed the threat landscape such that the vulnerability remediation timeline encouraged by our disclosure policy is clearly insufficient."

Related:['Sleeper Agent' Tactic Hides Payloads in Open-Weight AI Models](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/sleeper-agent-tactic-payloads-open-weight-ai-models)

Specifically, Silvanovich noted that large language models have enabled threat actors to find and exploit vulnerabilities [much faster](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/proof-concept-15-minutes-ai-turbocharges-exploitation) in today's AI era.

"A vulnerability reported today presents a much higher immediate risk to users than one reported last year. In this new environment, it is crucial that vendors move towards remediating vulnerabilities faster," she wrote. "LLMs have also decreased the time it takes for attackers to reverse engineer and exploit a vulnerability based on a patch, to the extent that zero patch-to-exploitation time is a reasonable, though imperfect, model of attacker behavior."

Project Zero is not a conventional bug disclosure program. [Founded in 2014](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/google-forms-zero-day-hacking-team), the organization was established specifically so Google could help facilitate the safe reporting and disclosure of high-severity, high-impact vulnerabilities found in widely used software and hardware. That said, Project Zero's move reflects a broad inundation of AI-discovered vulnerabilities, and where Google goes, the rest of the industry may follow.

As for whether such a thing is good, bad, or necessary for the cybersecurity industry, experts appear to be split.

## Vulnerability Disclosure: An Imperfect Science

Disclosure timelines are a key part of the vulnerability ecosystem. When a researcher or firm discovers a vulnerability and submits it to the software developer responsible, a standard timeline is usually designated for the developer to fix the vulnerability; such deadlines are usually 90 or 120 days, but it varies depending on the bug, [disclosure policy](https://www.techtarget.com/news/JetBrains-Rapid7-clash-over-vulnerability-disclosure-policies/837321/), and circumstances.

Related:[November Patch Tuesday: An Overflowing Cornucopia of CVEs](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/november-patch-tuesday-cornucopia-cves)

If the software developer ignores the bug report, is slow to address it, or declines to communicate with the researcher, the researcher may choose to disclose the flaw at the end of the disclosure timeline to inform security teams and practitioners of the looming threat. These timelines hold software vendors accountable and encourage them to maintain secure software, but it's not perfect science.

Oftentimes, the researcher and software developer will work together to coordinate a disclosure date so relevant research, patches, and mitigations are ready at the same time. Sometimes, if the bug is especially dangerous or [requires more time to fix](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Lessons-learned-from-Meltdown-and-Spectre-disclosure-process/840656/), the timeline may change and the technical details and proof-of-concept exploits will be held until a later date.

It's worth noting there are no standard requirements between platforms. HackerOne keeps its default disclosure timeline to 30 days, though it's broadly set at the program level by each vendor customer, and many HackerOne programs set longer timelines. Bugcrowd researchers and vendors generally coordinate timelines; program rules again supersede standards.

Related:[OnlyFans Copyright Takedowns Expose Compromised Websites](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/onlyfans-copyright-takedowns-expose-compromised-websites)

TrendAI's Zero Day Initiative (which is not a platform but a vendor-agnostic vulnerability disclosure program) uses a standard disclosure timeline of 120 days for responsive vendors that release working patches, and shorter timelines for vulnerabilities that result from faulty patches.

It remains to be seen whether the larger industry will follow Project Zero's lead. Dustin Childs, ZDI's head of threat awareness, calls Google's decision "a bold move to be sure, especially considering the explosion of bug reports coming in thanks to AI assisted discovery."

However, he worries about how shorter timelines may impact remediation quality. "I fear it will lead to patches that don’t fully address the vulnerability. We see that happen at 120 days," Childs says. "We’ll be watching closely to see how this change impacts vendors and researchers alike."

Alex Rice, cofounder, CISO, and CTO at HackerOne, tells Dark Reading that he expects the industry to move in Google's direction, though not necessarily in lockstep. He argues that patch soak time no longer protects defenders, given how quickly attackers can exploit flaws now. And because Project Zero is a disclosure program for vulnerabilities with demonstrated impact and not a [bug bounty](https://www.techtarget.com/news/How-to-start-an-enterprise-bug-bounty-program-A-CISOs-guide/836279/) platform that addresses a broad range of flaws, Google is in a unique position to set aggressive expectations.

"The specifics will still vary by program, severity, and whether a fix is realistic, and platforms like ours will need to support that flexibility," Rice says. "But the direction isn’t in question. Organizations should plan for 30 days as the baseline expectation that will shrink even further."

## How Will Organizations Keep Up With Shorter Deadlines?

Between stories from this year about [AI models](https://www.darkreading.com/cloud-security/csa-cisos-prepare-post-mythos-exploit-storm) capable of discovering zero-day bugs off of human language prompts and vulnerability coordinators fighting [huge spikes in bug reporting volumes](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/ai-ending-era-hidden-vulnerabilities-are-vendors-ready) the past couple of years, Google's position seems defensible. The tech giant isn't alone in its move either: In January, [Anthropic's own coordinated disclosure policy](https://www.anthropic.com/research/coordinated-vulnerability-disclosure-timelines) will shorten from 90 days to 30 days for high- and critical-severity bugs, and 45 days for all other cases.

But not everyone agrees. Expel principal threat researcher [Marcus Hutchins](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/20-leaders-ciso-era-2-decades-change) took issue with the move [in a post to X](https://x.com/MalwareTechBlog/status/2121785862677786879), where he noted that 2026 has already seen far more CVEs than 2025. "Sure, there are some vendors who just sit on bugs forever and don't make any effort to patch them. But there's way more overworked & underpaid teams, non-profits, and solo projects who are patching bugs as fast as they physically can," he wrote.

[Katie Moussouris](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/agentic-ai-new-insider-threat-model), CEO of Luta Security and a pioneer in vulnerability disclosure, largely agrees. As she tells Dark Reading, even AI firms currently struggle to patch the issues they find, leaving volunteers and less resourced firms even further back against the wall. "A deadline doesn't magically manifest a fix."

"Researchers and AI companies are burying maintainers in bug reports and not doing enough to help fix them. This isn't even a matter of model access or token cost. The AI companies themselves can't even close the gap," she says. "[Anthropic reports](https://red.anthropic.com/2026/cvd/) that 629 of the 5,087 vulnerabilities it disclosed have been patched. OpenAI's Patch the Planet initiative, run with security engineers from Trail of Bits, [has written patches for a third of the 2,055 issues it found](https://trailofbits.com/patch-the-planet/), and 23% have been accepted upstream."

A [Google](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/ai-agentic-frameworks-artex-google-fbi/874753/) spokesperson tells Dark Reading that in creating the new policy, the company solicited feedback from the vendors Project Zero reports the most vulnerabilities to, including open source projects.

"The general consensus was that these timelines are achievable, and most open source projects are almost there," the spokesperson says. "Every vulnerability that Project Zero reported against a volunteer-maintained project in 2026 was fixed in 30 days, except for one, which was fixed in 31 days. Open source projects are among the vendors who will need to make the fewest changes to meet our new policy, as they are already doing so well."

## Shorter Disclosure Timelines May Be Inevitable

While one side of the argument disagrees with the rollout of shorter timelines, the other side sees it as inevitable or necessary to respond to how attackers now use AI to pursue their goals. HackerOne's Rice says that right now, teams are not ready to deal with a 30-day turnaround, but Google's move may offer an opportunity at the boardroom level to convince technology and executive partners that remediation is a shared responsibility.

"If you're a security team struggling to keep up, don't waste time playing the victim. Take this as a gift," he says. "Ask for dedicated engineering capacity for security fixes, pre-agreed remediation SLAs, automated validation and retesting, and the authority to ship mitigations while the full fix is built. The teams that turn this pressure into that conversation will be far ahead of the ones hoping for external relief."

Casey Ellis, founder of Bugcrowd and The disclose.io Project, says this is a necessary evolution, even if it feels aggressive. In the wake of proliferated vulnerability discovery, the timeline shortening "puts productive pressure on response and fix pipelines."

Ellis says he feels the typical targets of Project Zero researchers are in "decent shape," but community norms tend to follow Google's lead on CVE timelines, so the move could carry larger impact.

"My main concern would be companies that maintain safety-critical systems or those that are otherwise logistically difficult to update; a security fix doesn't always end with a patch, and more often than not, it doesn't," he says. "The Project Zero changes will ultimately reveal (and hopefully shrink or close) the readiness gap for many PSIRT teams, products, and hosted service providers, but unless product vendors become proactive about this aspect of vulnerability discovery and disclosure it is likely to be a bumpy ride for some."

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Alexander Culafi](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blte0c88f087d1ad6f5/67c609df64f983454c3dd40b/1571751486317.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/alexander-culafi)

Senior News Writer, Dark Reading

Alex is an award-winning writer, journalist, and podcast host based in Boston. After cutting his teeth writing for independent gaming publications as a teenager, he graduated from Emerson College in 2016 with a Bachelor of Science in journalism. He has previously been published on VentureFizz, Search Security, Nintendo World Report, and elsewhere.

At Dark Reading, he covers a variety of cybersecurity topics, including the cybercrime ecosystem, open source security, and the intersection between AI and threat actors. In his spare time, Alex hosts the weekly Nintendo podcast, "Talk Nintendo Podcast," and works on personal writing projects, including two previously self-published science fiction novels.

He has received numerous awards, including TechTarget's Writer of the Year in 2022 as well as more than 10 Azbee awards for his reporting between 2022 and today.

[58] Dark Reading - Ransomware Attacks Increase Behind AI, but Payments Decline
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 16 Nov 2026 19:56:05 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/ransomware-attacks-increase-ai-payments-decline

[TAM METİN - 1025 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
4 Min Read

![Image 1: Digital global map on black background with hand reaching out of the darkness.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt80dac13b14975660/6af7368e89398e7e289817aa/global-cyber-map-alestudio996-shutterstock.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: alestudio996 via Shutterstock

Ransomware attacks are expanding amid cybercriminals' increasing use of AI tools, but at the same time, fewer organizations are [paying ransoms](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/us-treasury-45b-ransom-payments-2013), according to multiple reports released this month.

Globally, organizations are seeing far more [ransomware incidents](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/ransomware-undisclosed-attacks-blackfog/819595/), while the number of attacks targeting US companies has remained fairly steady. In the third quarter of 2026, for example, ransomware groups claimed 2,766 breaches, an increase of nearly 40% over the previous quarter, according to threat data released by cybersecurity services firm Halcyon.

Threat groups adopting AI is likely a major reason for the increase in attacks. The ability of large language models (LLMs) to create convincing social-engineering messaging, find sensitive data quickly, and assist attackers has largely driven the surge in attacks in other nations, says Cynthia Kaiser, senior vice president of Halcyon's Ransomware Research Center and a former deputy assistant director at the FBI's Cyber Division.

Related:[Chinese AI Project Scouts Spies in Taiwan Chip Industry](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/chinese-ai-project-scouts-spies-taiwan-chip-industry)

"We think AI is a big part of [the growth] because it has removed the language barrier for phishing," she says. "We're now seeing much more convincing phishing in Japanese, for example. Countries like Japan are [treating this almost as a state of emergency](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/japan-emergency-funding-cybersecurity-ai-assisted-breaches)."

Overall, the jump in claimed ransomware cases — up 31% from the first quarter and 39% from the second, [according to Halcyon's report](https://www.halcyon.ai/ransomware-evolution-report/q3-2026) — has not necessarily resulted in more ransoms paid out.

Take the retail sector as an example: While 62% of attacks on companies succeeded in encrypting data — up from 48% in 2025 — less than a third (32%) of retailers actually paid to get the data back, nearly half of the 58% that paid in 2025, [according to Sophos's "The State of Ransomware in Retail 2026" report](https://www.sophos.com/en-us/blog/the-state-of-ransomware-in-retail-2026).

![Image 2: Line chart showing number of successful ransomware attacks in retail sector for 2026](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltb92e6dc68b011f27/6af735b92d0b181fe916a29e/retail-sector-ransomware-outcomes-Sophos-lemos.jpg?width=1400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Instead, a solid majority of retail firms (71%) recovered their data from backup, and most recovered within a week, Sophos found. Other sectors, such as local government and school districts, have seen declines in paying ransomware as well, says Chester Wisniewski, global field chief information security officer (CISO) at Sophos.

"None of them are paying the ransom," he says. "Whether there's extortion involved, whether data theft's involved, it kind of doesn't matter. They're largely not paying the ransom."

## New Ransomware Groups Coming Online

The number of ransomware groups claiming success took off in 2026, growing from 75 in the first quarter to 111 in the third, [according to Halcyon data](https://www.halcyon.ai/ransomware-evolution-report/q1-2026). While ransom amounts have continued to decline, the growth in the number of groups continues to pose a threat to companies, Sophos's Wisniewski says.

Related:[Shadowy AI Relay Disables OpenAI Codex, Claude Code Sandboxes](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/ai-relay-service-openai-codex-claude-code-sandboxes)

"There's been five or six new names I've seen in just the last few weeks, so it doesn't seem like ransomware's going away at all," he says. "But its efficacy has been declining as far as generating actual money for the crooks."

The median ransom demand has dropped to $700,000 in 2026, from $2 million in 2024, [according to Sophos data](https://www.sophos.com/en-us/content/state-of-ransomware).

That hasn't stopped new ransomware groups from continuing to appear. At the end of the quarter, about a third of all claimed ransomware successes came from groups that didn’t exist six months earlier, says Duy-Phuc Pham, a malware researcher at Trellix.

"At the top, The Gentlemen grew from a few dozen victims a year ago to [the most active group](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/gentlemen-rapidly-rise-ransomware), ahead of Qilin," he says. "That kind of success makes them the obvious next target for law enforcement, which spent this year going after the shared services these groups depend on, from criminal VPNs to laundering services."

## AI Brings Faster, More Effective Attacks

With attackers increasingly using AI, ransomware gangs will continue to broaden their campaigns against more industries in more regions — part of the reason that Europe has witnessed an increase in attacks, while US victims' share of incidents has remained the same or decreased, says Pham. AI allows attackers to create more convincing social-engineering messaging in a variety of languages, removing a significant historical barrier to targeting smaller populations.

Related:[Researchers Infiltrate Microsoft Tech Support Scam Operation](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/microsoft-tech-support-scam-operation)

Because of the greater scale that AI enables attackers to hit, ransomware threat actors are able to widen their coverage, he says.

"When a tool can scan, exploit, and move through networks with little human effort, a small crew can do the work of a large one, hit far more victims, and do it faster," Pham says. "That fits what we see on the leak sites: more groups and more newcomers."

The manufacturing sector continues to be the top target of ransomware gangs, because suppliers and partners often share the same platforms, so a single compromise can expose engineering data beyond the targeted company, says Halcyon's Kaiser.

The sector will likely continue to be the top targeted group in 2027, especially as international organizations continue to account for a greater share in the coming year, she says.

"I'd expect continued growth, especially outside the US," Kaiser says. "The big open question is whether more attacks means more money ... countries newly feeling the pain are paying much more attention, which should improve international cooperation."
