[59] Dark Reading - 'Sleeper Agent' Tactic Hides Payloads in Open-Weight AI Models
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 16 Nov 2026 19:10:03 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/sleeper-agent-tactic-payloads-open-weight-ai-models

[TAM METİN - 1115 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
5 Min Read

![Image 1: A cartoon spy in wide-brimmed black hat with a red band, dark sunglasses, and a dark trenchcoat ](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/bltc64dc86fdbfba025/6af7630521de23678b058bc7/Spy-IconicBestiary-Getty.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: IconicBestiary via Getty Images

AI models downloaded from the open Internet can be subtly poisoned with malicious payloads if attackers are careful enough.

[Open-weight AI models](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/open-weight-chinese-ai-models-drive-privacy-innovation-llm) have become seriously popular lately. Even early last year, [McKinsey found](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/tech-forward/open-source-in-the-age-of-ai) that 63% of organizations used them, and 76% of survey respondents said that they expected to use them more in the coming years. They're auditable, developers like using them, and, perhaps most of all, they're cheap.

Like any other software you can download off the Internet, however, open-weight and open source AI tools carry some degree of risk. Last week, researchers described a technique they call "[Lazarus](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/blog/lazare-veni-foras-reprogramming-dormant-neurons-to-backdoor-ai-agents/)," which could allow an attacker to poison a model using a near-imperceptible few of its neurons. Microsoft described it as turning a model into a "[sleeper agent](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/11/10/detecting-sleeper-agents-hiding-in-ai-model-neurons/)," capable of granting an attacker access to its user's environment.

"A model is a file almost nobody inspects, yet we download models from strangers and give them access to our code, credentials, and systems," explains Briland Hitaj, senior AI security research engineer at Confidencial, whose research into techniques like this preceded Microsoft's. "Malicious models have already turned up on Hugging Face, though so far they abuse [file formats like Python's Pickle](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/sleepy-pickle-exploit-subtly-poisons-ml-models) to run code when loaded. I'm not aware of anyone hiding payloads inside the weights themselves outside research, but that's where the research points."

Related:[Google Project Zero Slashes Bug Disclosure Deadlines, Splitting Experts](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/google-project-zero-bug-disclosure-deadlines)

## How to Poison Open-Weight AI Models

Since at least [2020](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3427228.3427268), researchers have been coming up with clever ways to store bad stuff inside of neural networks.

The premise behind this whole category of research is that the size and complexity of these networks provide cover for small, secret, but potentially powerful payloads. Just look at [Qwen 3.8-Max:](https://qwen.ai/blog?id=qwen3.8) It's totally open-weight, so you can inspect it to your heart's content. But it's made of 2.4 trillion parameters, and probably around 100 million [neurons](https://www.techtarget.com/news/artificial-neuron/847163/). If an attacker plants a functioning payload in some of those neurons or parameters, how exactly are you going to find it?

The trick, then, isn't finding a place to hide a payload. First, it's getting that payload to function reliably amid so many moving parts; second, it's making sure it doesn't noticeably impact the normal functioning of the model; and lastly, optionally making the payload robust to fine-tuning.

Different researchers have come up with different ways to do it. In 2022, Hitaj and his colleagues came up with a technique called "[MaleficNet](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/367585025_MaleficNet_Hiding_Malware_into_Deep_Neural_Networks_Using_Spread-Spectrum_Channel_Coding)," which repurposed an old military and, later, 2G and 3G mobile network technique called "code division multiple access" (CDMA).

Related:[November Patch Tuesday: An Overflowing Cornucopia of CVEs](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/november-patch-tuesday-cornucopia-cves)

Very simply, they split malware binaries into tiny pieces and spread the pieces across the many individual weights that make up a neural network. It wouldn't look like anything but noise to a passerby, yet with a trigger mechanism and a way to decode the weights, they could recover, recombine, and execute the binary.

"The vulnerability comes from how [neural networks](https://www.techtarget.com/news/What-is-a-neural-network/834260/) work, not from a bug," Hitaj explains. "Models have far more parameters than they strictly need, so you can nudge millions of weights slightly, or repurpose a few neurons that rarely fire, without visibly changing the model's behavior. And since nobody can read weights the way they read code, those changes go unnoticed."

Microsoft's new technique compromises on capacity for simplicity. Where Hitaj proposed encoding an entire binary across many model parameters, Microsoft researchers propose sneaking a brief command inside of fewer than 10 neurons. Specifically, one must target "dormant" neurons — those that prove to have little effect on the overall model's functioning — late in a model's processing cycle, and thus more directly influential over its output. One encodes a payload in these neurons, arranged to fire sequentially, and configures them to stay largely inactive unless they're fed a trigger. Eventually, you get a sleeper agent.

Related:[OnlyFans Copyright Takedowns Expose Compromised Websites](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/onlyfans-copyright-takedowns-expose-compromised-websites)

The target payload can be arbitrary, so long as it's small and formatted correctly. The trigger can be any particular user input to the model, or tool output, or something so simple as a date read in a user's terminal.

## How to Suss Out Poisoned AI

Luigi Vincenzo Mancini, a professor at Sapienza University of Rome and one of the co-authors of the MaleficNet paper, advises organizations to take a defense-in-depth approach to [open-weight AI security](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/ai-open-source-weights-tech-industry-promote/826240/), "rather than trying to find a single magical 'AI malware scanner.'"

Like any third-party software, users should download AI models from official sources. As an added measure, users can compare the models they download with a known, trusted baseline.

"Microsoft says that reliable detection of these kinds of modifications requires being able to diff the deployed weights against the original, unmodified weights," Mancini notes. "That's a powerful argument for treating model weights more like source code or binaries than like ordinary data files."

Next, users should scan their models in general for anomalies. Though as Hitaj points out, "No scan proves a model is clean, and ordinary testing rarely finds a trigger the attacker chose. So the key controls limit what a compromised model can do: give AI agents only the access they need, keep credentials and production systems out of reach, restrict network access, log what agents do, and require human approval for consequential actions. If a model turns malicious, you want it boxed in and visible."

Beyond restricting them from the outset, "Organizations should monitor for [unexpected agent behaviour](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/hundreds-agents-rogue-lead-up-hugging-face-breach/828963/) at the system boundary," Mancini says. Practically speaking, "a model emitting an unusual shell command, attempting an unexpected network connection, accessing credentials it normally doesn't need, or suddenly invoking a tool in an unusual context can be a much more actionable detection technique than directly inferring malicious intent from billions of model parameters."

[![Image 2: Nate Nelson](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt8edb70f1c73f8a5a/6978c3c26fdea754c9f2e2e9/Nate_Nelson_bio_pic_2-crop_2.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/nate-nelson)

Contributing Writer

Nate Nelson is a journalist and award-winning scriptwriter. In addition to Dark Reading he writes for Darknet Diaries, the most popular show in cybersecurity across all media.

He began his career as a freelancer, ghostwriting Forbes and CNBC op-eds for executives in tech and finance. Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. Throughout those years he co-created a cybersecurity podcast, Malicious Life, which in its day climbed into the Top 20 technology podcasts charts on Apple Podcasts and Spotify.

He holds degrees from New York University and Bard College. As a born and bred New Yorker, he enjoys a superiority complex, but is polite enough to keep it to himself.

[60] Dark Reading - China-Nexus Actors Breach 10 Organizations in AI-Driven Attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 16 Nov 2026 16:36:55 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/china-nexus-actors-breach-10-organizations-ai-driven-attacks

[TAM METİN - 1048 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
![Image 1: A Chinese flag on the right side with lines of computer code on the left side.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt98c5fdb26a9d6d01/669a8451220787d9a293b79f/China(1800)_Rokas_Tenys_Alamy.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Rokas Tenys via Alamy Stock Photo

[China-nexus](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/chinese-nexus-actors-shift-focus-qatar-iranian-conflict) threat actors unleashed another wave of attacks using autonomous AI tools to compromise microprocessor manufacturers, US defense contractors, and other organizations.

The malicious activity, tracked as CL-STA-1238 by researchers at Palo Alto Networks' Unit 42, is the [second documented attack](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/hackers-abuse-ai-pen-test-tool-cairn) in which China-linked actors used the open source AI penetration-testing framework called Cairn for automation, they revealed in [a blog post](https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cairn-framework-ai-autonomous-attacks/). In this latest campaign, attackers conducted 25 operations between August and September against at least 19 organizations, achieving "unauthorized access or data retrieval at 10 of the targeted organizations," according to the post.

In the successful attacks, the threat actors obtained dozens of valid credentials; achieved mass exposure of personal data, exceeding 1 million records at one organization; retrieved military and government identities; created persistent administrator accounts in a production system; and modified a live industry standards-ballot document.

Related:[Inside an AI Attack Against a US Retailer](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/inside-ai-attack-us-retailer)

Of the total organizations targeted, seven were in the semiconductor and advanced-manufacturing supply chain, and three organizations were in US defense and defense policy. The attackers also targeted various human rights and [civil society organizations](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/nation-state-paragon-spyware-infections).

According to Unit 42 researchers, the victims included an industry standards body, which suffered a [password-spraying attack](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Cisco-ASA-and-FTD-zero-day-used-in-password-spraying-attacks/836901/); a semiconductor company, where attackers brute-forced a production credential on a partner-integration application; a US teacher's union, which suffered an unauthenticated account-creation bypass on a content management system; and a defense policy think tank, where attackers targeted an open back-end registration endpoint on its identity platform.

The China-nexus actors also targeted content management systems through identity and configuration weaknesses at other organizations, including a semiconductor-lithography manufacturer, an industrial-automation manufacturer, and an education nonprofit.

## Shift in AI Offensive Maneuvers

[Cairn](https://github.com/oritera) played a significant hand in the campaign, and the use of it "represents a shift in how attackers are applying AI to targeted offensive operations," according to the post.

"The attackers supplied a target and a goal, then Cairn planned, executed, and recorded results on its own," Unit 42 researchers wrote.

Cairn is AI model agnostic, and in this case, attackers weaponized it using Chinese large language models (LLMs) Zhipu GLM 5.2 and [DeepSeek](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/chinese-actor-deepseek-ai-agent-attack-security-firm) V4 Flash and Pro. However, initial exposure to the organizations' networks came through many of the traditional security gaps found across industries, the researchers noted.

Related:[Artex: The AI Pen-Test Tool Hackers Turned Into a Weapon](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/artex-ai-pen-test-tool-hackers-weapon)

"It doesn't matter much to a victim whether a human or an AI agent is behind the keyboard," Tyler Oliver, CEO and founder of threat-hunting platform Huntbase, tells Dark Reading. "A human might hand the tool a mission and walk away, or direct it step by step. Either way, the [password spraying](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/stop-password-spraying-active-directory) looks like password spraying."

Indeed, the vulnerabilities the attackers exploited were known, according to Unit 42, including "authentication bypasses; insecure direct object references; mass-assignment privilege escalation; weak and default credentials; exposed cloud APIs; and dated public CVEs."

## AI Agents Target Set Objectives

As in the previous Cairn-driven attacks, the findings once again provide a look under the hood at how threat actors can weaponize the tool against organizations, a blueprint that may be used in future attacks.

Rather than manually directing every stage of an attack, operators supplied Cairn with a target and a high-level objective. The framework then developed its own plan, conducted reconnaissance, identified potential vulnerabilities, attempted exploitation, and recorded its findings.

Related:[CISA Announces Enhanced Reporting Form for Cyber Incidents](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/cisa-announces-enhanced-reporting-form-cyber-incidents)

"An [AI model drives](https://www.darkreading.com/ics-ot-security/worlds-first-ai-driven-cyberattack-couldnt-breach-ot-systems) each Cairn 'agent worker,'" according to the post. "Cairn coordinates its activities through a shared 'blackboard.' [Agents](https://www.darkreading.com/insider-threats/ai-agents-breaches-humans-liable) read the blackboard and write their findings back as a fact-intent graph, while human operators can inject 'hints' at any time to help guide the operations if needed."

"Cairn has a shared record of what it's already learned, so it can build on earlier findings without a person reviewing every result and deciding what happens next," Rickard Carlsson, CEO of AI security firm Detectify, tells Dark Reading. Meanwhile, this ability to work with limited supervision is already showing up across multiple tools, including another AI penetration-testing framework, [Artex](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/hackers-artex-github-repositories-exposed-secrets/874007/), which is also being weaponized by attackers, he says.

Individual CL-STA-1238 operations lasted anywhere from 20 minutes to more than 50 hours, according to Unit 42. Moreover, attackers could achieve simultaneous operations against multiple organizations, which reduced the dependence on human operators to manage each intrusion.

## Old and New Defense Tricks

Though AI is changing the game in terms of [speed](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/ai-attacks-security-strategies), volume, and expanse of attacks, it does not change the "complexity" of attacks, which, as mentioned, often rely on known security gaps, according to Unit 42.

That's a spot of good news for [defenders](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/ai-cyberattacks-automation-identity-data-microsoft-report/832020/), because "identity hygiene, attack-surface reduction, and software patching remain effective" against attacks that use Cairn or other AI tools, according to the post.

However, there are other safeguards that organizations can put in place to defend against AI-driven attacks, too, says John Strand, owner of security consulting firm Black Hills Information Security.

"There are two things I think every organization should be doing right now," he tells Dark Reading. "First, continuous attack surface management. Organizations need to identify all the different services and systems they have exposed to the internet and start restricting access wherever possible."

The second key defense move is to start using [cyber deception](https://www.darkreading.com/ics-ot-security/you-need-cyber-deception-ot) as much as possible by setting up "tripwires" that can detect attackers during reconnaissance and lateral movement "before they accomplish their objectives," Strand adds.

[61] Dark Reading - NIST Finalizes Post-Quantum Cryptography Guidance, GAO Offers a Reality Check
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Mon, 16 Nov 2026 14:22:15 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/nist-finalizes-post-quantum-cryptography-guidance-gao-offers-a-reality-check

[TAM METİN - 1004 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
4 Min Read

A nation-state actor breaches a network to steal medical records, classified government communications, or trade secrets, knowing full well it's encrypted and unreadable. But they don't consider inaccessibility a failure — that was actually the plan all along.

Attackers are banking on the advent of quantum computing to decode and exploit all that stolen data, now secured with traditional public- and private-key encryption. The federal government has set standards to help prepare now, but a new report warns that agencies are still not even close to implementing those defenses.

"Harvest now, decrypt later" (HNDL), a term coined over a decade ago, refers to attackers sitting on stolen data and waiting for quantum computing to catch up. Once they're equipped to decrypt it, they may sell it on the Dark Web, use it in extortion threats, or exploit it for espionage purposes. That [time is closer](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/google-2029-deadline-quantum-safe-cryptography) than it appears.

Related:[Nvidia Launches AI Agent Safety Platform to Prevent Rogue Activities](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/nvidia-launches-ai-agent-safety-platform-prevent-rogue-activities)

Threat actors are getting ready, but US federal agencies are lagging. The National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) laid out a technical road map to guide organizations through the process in its recent "[Transition to Post-Quantum Cryptography Standards](https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/ir/8547/final)" report. Technology providers, standards organizations, and infrastructure operations on which federal systems depend are also required to meet the report's deadlines.

The deadlines to implement measures to mitigate quantum risks are set for Dec. 31, 2030, but a report by the US Government Accountability Office (GAO) cautions that federal agencies are not prepared to meet them.

## Preventing Future Breaches

When quantum computing does take off, it will suddenly render the encryption organizations rely on today insecure. Its computing power will enable attackers to brute-force encryption keys commonly used to protect sensitive data across sectors. The technology is still out of reach for most organizations due to high costs and talent shortages, but progress toward mainstreaming it [is accelerating](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/hardware-makers-implement-post-quantum-cryptography).

NIST says preparations must begin now, even "before a cryptanalytically relevant quantum computer has been built," labeling it a "pressing threat" because of threat actors' ability to harvest data now and decrypt it later.

Some executive order and memorandum deadlines for federal agencies, such as completing cryptographic inventories and designating [a dedicated post-quantum cryptography](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/getting-past-post-quantum-cryptography-discovery) (PQC) migration lead, have already passed. But a big one remains: By December 2030, agencies are expected to implement migrations that mitigate as much quantum risk as possible.

Related:[Hardware Makers Implement Post-Quantum Cryptography as Security Threats Near](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/hardware-makers-implement-post-quantum-cryptography)

NIST urges organizations to "treat the migration to PQC as a current operational priority." But conducting cryptographic inventories, planning migration, and early deployments of PQC-enabled products and services may be difficult for a sector that already struggles with cybersecurity, largely due to legacy systems.

"Since sensitive data often retains its value for many years, starting the transition to post-quantum cryptography now is critical to preventing these future breaches," NIST states in the report.

## GAO Says Federal Agencies Not Ready for the Post-Quantum World

Any major migration requires a delicate balance between reducing risk and minimizing operational disruptions. This is another reason NIST wants organizations to act now, since data stolen today could become readable the moment attackers gain access to sufficiently powerful quantum computing.

However, despite best efforts, a [GAO report](https://www.gao.gov/products/gao-27-108740) documents real-world gaps between readiness and reality, suggesting that NIST's deadlines may be tough to meet. The US watchdog does not mince words, headlining a chapter: "Agencies Have Not Fully Implemented Preparatory Practices for Addressing Quantum Cyber Threats."

That was the conclusion reached after analysts evaluated cryptographic inventories, funding assessments, and other planning documentation for each of the 24 agencies covered by the 1990 Chief Financial Officers Act. That constitutes all cabinet departments and independent agencies, including NASA, the Nuclear Regulatory Commission, and the Social Security Administration.

Related:[New Tool Traces AI Videos Back to Their Source](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/new-tool-advances-ai-generated-video-detection)

And sufficient progress is unlikely. Only half of the 24 agencies fully agreed with GAO's recommendations. The rest pushed back, hedged, or did not respond, according to the report.

Still, GAO stands by its recommendations, which, like NIST, include establishing processes to inventory vulnerable cryptography and securing funding for the shift to post-quantum cryptography.

If legacy systems cannot be migrated to address the new concerns — which is highly likely — they must plan to replace them instead, a process that could take even longer as the threat grows more dire.

## Cybersecurity Shortcomings: A Case of Déjà Vu

GAO attributes the missed marks in part to a lack of cryptography expertise, noting that transitioning to post-quantum encryption requires specialized mathematical and cybersecurity skills that most agencies currently lack.

Whatever the reason, it's essential to act quickly, the auditor urges. Until the agencies address those weaknesses, their cryptography will not be able to withstand the threat quantum computers pose to their sensitive information, GAO's report concludes.

But forget quantum readiness — agencies aren't even fully prepared for today's threats, according to [other GAO reports](https://www.gao.gov/cybersecurity).

The agency has made over 4,400 cybersecurity recommendations, including 48 deemed a priority, since 2010. However, as of February 2026, more than 730 remain unimplemented.

"Until these shortcomings are addressed, federal and critical infrastructure IT systems will be increasingly susceptible to cyber threats," GAO states.

## About the Author

[![Image 1: Arielle Waldman](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blte685016783fc7faa/67ca10f9cf523d2a3ce48c04/arielle_waldman_headshot.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/arielle-waldman)

Features Writer, Dark Reading

Arielle spent the last decade working as a reporter, transitioning from human interest stories to covering all things cybersecurity related in 2020. Now, as a features writer for Dark Reading, she delves into the security problems enterprises face daily, providing context and actionable steps. She looks for stories that go past the initial news to understand where the industry is going. Her coverage areas include identity and access management, cyber risk and operations, industrial control systems, operational technology, and ransomware trends.

She previously lived in Florida where she wrote for the Tampa Bay Times before returning to Boston where her cybersecurity career took off at TechTarget SearchSecurity. When she's not writing about cybersecurity, she pursues personal projects that include a mystery novel and poetry collection.

[62] SecurityWeek - Oracle Patches 640 Vulnerabilities With November 2026 CSPU
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 08:47:18 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/oracle-patches-640-vulnerabilities-with-november-2026-cspu/

[TAM METİN - 2512 kelime]
Oracle Patches 640 Vulnerabilities With November 2026 CSPU

Over 300 patches address unauthenticated, remotely exploitable flaws, while 62 fix critical-severity flaws.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Oracle on Tuesday announced the release of 655 new security patches as part of its November 2026 Critical Security Patch Update (CSPU).
The patches address approximately 640 unique vulnerabilities across 21 products. Nearly half of the patches also fix additional CVEs and bugs that are not exploitable against Oracle products.



Just over 300 patches address security defects that can be exploited remotely without authentication. Sixty-two security patches resolve critical-severity flaws, including half a dozen max-severity bugs.



Hyperion received the largest set of fixes in the November 2026 CSPU, at 267, including 107 for vulnerabilities that can be exploited remotely without authentication.



The Supply Chain update contains 74 new security patches, including 36 for unauthenticated, remotely exploitable flaws. Fusion Middleware received 71 patches (53 unauthenticated, remotely exploitable), the same as E-Business Suite (19 exploitable remotely without authentication).



Oracle also pushed numerous patches for Communications (31 – 21 unauthenticated, remotely exploitable), Analytics (29 – 9), Siebel CRM (19 – 9), PeopleSoft (13 – 6), Java SE (11 – 11), Financial Services Applications (11 – 1), Virtualization (11 – 3), and MySQL (10 – 3).Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The updates for Fusion Middleware and Analytics resolve six max-severity flaws (five and one, respectively), while the Java SE security refresh fixes Java4Shell (CVE-2026-114938), a memory corruption issue leading to memory leaks and remote code execution.



Other Oracle products that received patches this month include Database Server, APEX, Autonomous Health Framework, REST Data Services, Application Testing Suite, Commerce, Construction and Engineering, Enterprise Manager, and JD Edwards.



While Oracle makes no mention of any of these vulnerabilities being exploited in the wild, organizations are advised to apply the patches as soon as possible, as security defects in the company’s products are an attractive target to threat actors.

Hyperion received the largest set of fixes in the November 2026 CSPU, at 267, including 107 for vulnerabilities that can be exploited remotely without authentication.



The Supply Chain update contains 74 new security patches, including 36 for unauthenticated, remotely exploitable flaws. Fusion Middleware received 71 patches (53 unauthenticated, remotely exploitable), the same as E-Business Suite (19 exploitable remotely without authentication).



Oracle also pushed numerous patches for Communications (31 – 21 unauthenticated, remotely exploitable), Analytics (29 – 9), Siebel CRM (19 – 9), PeopleSoft (13 – 6), Java SE (11 – 11), Financial Services Applications (11 – 1), Virtualization (11 – 3), and MySQL (10 – 3).Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The updates for Fusion Middleware and Analytics resolve six max-severity flaws (five and one, respectively), while the Java SE security refresh fixes Java4Shell (CVE-2026-114938), a memory corruption issue leading to memory leaks and remote code execution.



Other Oracle products that received patches this month include Database Server, APEX, Autonomous Health Framework, REST Data Services, Application Testing Suite, Commerce, Construction and Engineering, Enterprise Manager, and JD Edwards.



While Oracle makes no mention of any of these vulnerabilities being exploited in the wild, organizations are advised to apply the patches as soon as possible, as security defects in the company’s products are an attractive target to threat actors.

================================================================================

[63] SecurityWeek - Java4Shell Vulnerability Puts Many Enterprise Applications at Risk of Takeover
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 07:43:34 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/java4shell-vulnerability-puts-many-enterprise-applications-at-risk-of-takeover/

[TAM METİN - 10168 kelime]
Java4Shell Vulnerability Puts Many Enterprise Applications at Risk of Takeover

Calif demonstrated remote code execution in Jira, Jenkins and Keycloak, while VulnCheck says real-world RCE requires per-target work

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Oracle and the OpenJDK project have patched Java4Shell, a critical Java runtime vulnerability that researchers say can be exploited to hijack popular enterprise applications without authentication.
The issue, tracked as CVE-2026-114938, is a memory safety flaw in the way OpenJDK processes Base64-encoded data. Calif, whose researcher Quang Luong discovered the bug, made its findings public on November 17, after news of the flaw surfaced ahead of the coordinated disclosure.



Specifically, the bug was found in a routine that OpenJDK relies on to decode Base64 on x86-64 CPUs supporting AVX-512 VBMI. Calif’s analysis shows that invalid Base64 strings can cause the routine to write past the end of its output buffer, potentially corrupting nearby heap data before Java flags the input as invalid.



“With careful exploitation, the corruption can reveal sensitive memory, as Heartbleed did, or lead to remote code execution,” Calif explained.



The researchers call the memory leak capability JavaBleed. They warn that heap data exposed this way could contain PII, secret keys and tokens.



Default configurations exposed to Java4Shell attacks
In its initial tests, Calif achieved pre-authentication remote code execution (RCE) on Keycloak, Atlassian Jira and Confluence, JFrog Artifactory, Jenkins and PingFederate, all running with default settings. Authenticated attackers could also execute code on Apache Kafka Broker, Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit and Spinnaker, and the researchers say neither list is exhaustive.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Code execution was also achieved against sample apps based on Apache Struts and Spring Boot. Calif warns that the decoder can be reached through everyday functionality, including authentication and file uploads.



Client-side attacks are possible too. Based on an exploit shared with Calif by security researcher samczsun, a Java application can be compromised when it connects to an attacker-controlled server over TLS, even if it does not run any services.



“One thing to note about this vulnerability is that there is no universal exploit. Depending on how Base64 is used and how the application works, different steps may be required for exploitation,” Calif noted.



VulnCheck says crashing a vulnerable JVM is easy once an attacker finds a reachable entry point that relies on the affected decoding logic. In its tests, a quick series of crafted requests reliably took down the service.



The security firm says it has developed a full RCE exploit as well, but noted that achieving RCE is much harder, as the attacker must manipulate the heap layout and find a way (specific to each target) to leak addresses and bypass ASLR.



“In other words, we don’t anticipate that CVE-2026-114938 will be easily or widely exploitable, but as with PHP2Shell, adversaries are likely to find narrow but functional paths to RCE in specific applications,” VulnCheck said.



The proof-of-concept (PoC) code published by Calif demonstrates the memory corruption but does not achieve code execution. “The weaponized chains for individual products stay private until everyone has had a chance to upgrade. We will publish them later,” the company noted.



According to Calif, AI played a key role in both discovering and exploiting the vulnerability. 



“It took our own Quang Luong an hour to discover the vulnerability, and two more hours to develop the first working exploit. The total token cost was less than $200,” Calif said.



Affected versions and patches
According to Calif, OpenJDK versions starting with 17.0.6 are vulnerable, and so are the Java distributions based on it, including Amazon Corretto, Eclipse Temurin, Microsoft Build of OpenJDK, Azul Zulu, Red Hat OpenJDK and Oracle Java SE. The OpenJDK advisory lists Java 17, 21, 25 and 27 as affected by CVE-2026-114938.



Exploitation requires x86-64 hardware with AVX-512 VBMI support, such as Intel Xeon processors from the Ice Lake generation onward and AMD chips based on Zen 4 or newer architectures. Systems powered by ARM64 chips are not vulnerable.



Calif assigned the flaw a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2. Oracle’s advisory gives it a CVSS 3.1 score of 8.1 and lists it as remotely exploitable without authentication, but with high attack complexity.



Oracle released fixes in its November 2026 Critical Security Patch Update, which resolves 11 Java SE vulnerabilities.



Endor Labs pointed out that users of other Java distributions will need to wait for their vendors to roll out patched builds. It also noted that, as of November 17, the fix was not yet present in the public OpenJDK 17, 21 and 25 update repositories.



CISA has called for immediate patching, with internet-exposed systems at the top of the list. The agency also advises hunting for Java installations embedded in third-party software, containers and appliances, warning that these may not be listed separately in asset inventories.



Cisco Talos has yet to see the vulnerability exploited in the wild, but noted, “we anticipate probing attempts may pick up soon after public disclosure.” 



Related: Telegram Widgets Hit by RCE Demos, Malware Warning Days After Launch
Related: Nightmare Eclipse Drops Windows Zero-Day Exploit ‘SignDrop’
Related: N-able Kills Legacy APIs, Patches 17 N-central Zero-Days

Related: N-able Kills Legacy APIs, Patches 17 N-central Zero-Days

The researchers call the memory leak capability JavaBleed. They warn that heap data exposed this way could contain PII, secret keys and tokens.



Default configurations exposed to Java4Shell attacks
In its initial tests, Calif achieved pre-authentication remote code execution (RCE) on Keycloak, Atlassian Jira and Confluence, JFrog Artifactory, Jenkins and PingFederate, all running with default settings. Authenticated attackers could also execute code on Apache Kafka Broker, Solr, Elasticsearch, Gerrit and Spinnaker, and the researchers say neither list is exhaustive.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Code execution was also achieved against sample apps based on Apache Struts and Spring Boot. Calif warns that the decoder can be reached through everyday functionality, including authentication and file uploads.



Client-side attacks are possible too. Based on an exploit shared with Calif by security researcher samczsun, a Java application can be compromised when it connects to an attacker-controlled server over TLS, even if it does not run any services.



“One thing to note about this vulnerability is that there is no universal exploit. Depending on how Base64 is used and how the application works, different steps may be required for exploitation,” Calif noted.



VulnCheck says crashing a vulnerable JVM is easy once an attacker finds a reachable entry point that relies on the affected decoding logic. In its tests, a quick series of crafted requests reliably took down the service.



The security firm says it has developed a full RCE exploit as well, but noted that achieving RCE is much harder, as the attacker must manipulate the heap layout and find a way (specific to each target) to leak addresses and bypass ASLR.



“In other words, we don’t anticipate that CVE-2026-114938 will be easily or widely exploitable, but as with PHP2Shell, adversaries are likely to find narrow but functional paths to RCE in specific applications,” VulnCheck said.



The proof-of-concept (PoC) code published by Calif demonstrates the memory corruption but does not achieve code execution. “The weaponized chains for individual products stay private until everyone has had a chance to upgrade. We will publish them later,” the company noted.



According to Calif, AI played a key role in both discovering and exploiting the vulnerability. 



“It took our own Quang Luong an hour to discover the vulnerability, and two more hours to develop the first working exploit. The total token cost was less than $200,” Calif said.



Affected versions and patches
According to Calif, OpenJDK versions starting with 17.0.6 are vulnerable, and so are the Java distributions based on it, including Amazon Corretto, Eclipse Temurin, Microsoft Build of OpenJDK, Azul Zulu, Red Hat OpenJDK and Oracle Java SE. The OpenJDK advisory lists Java 17, 21, 25 and 27 as affected by CVE-2026-114938.



Exploitation requires x86-64 hardware with AVX-512 VBMI support, such as Intel Xeon processors from the Ice Lake generation onward and AMD chips based on Zen 4 or newer architectures. Systems powered by ARM64 chips are not vulnerable.



Calif assigned the flaw a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2. Oracle’s advisory gives it a CVSS 3.1 score of 8.1 and lists it as remotely exploitable without authentication, but with high attack complexity.



Oracle released fixes in its November 2026 Critical Security Patch Update, which resolves 11 Java SE vulnerabilities.



Endor Labs pointed out that users of other Java distributions will need to wait for their vendors to roll out patched builds. It also noted that, as of November 17, the fix was not yet present in the public OpenJDK 17, 21 and 25 update repositories.



CISA has called for immediate patching, with internet-exposed systems at the top of the list. The agency also advises hunting for Java installations embedded in third-party software, containers and appliances, warning that these may not be listed separately in asset inventories.



Cisco Talos has yet to see the vulnerability exploited in the wild, but noted, “we anticipate probing attempts may pick up soon after public disclosure.”

Calif assigned the flaw a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2. Oracle’s advisory gives it a CVSS 3.1 score of 8.1 and lists it as remotely exploitable without authentication, but with high attack complexity.



Oracle released fixes in its November 2026 Critical Security Patch Update, which resolves 11 Java SE vulnerabilities.



Endor Labs pointed out that users of other Java distributions will need to wait for their vendors to roll out patched builds. It also noted that, as of November 17, the fix was not yet present in the public OpenJDK 17, 21 and 25 update repositories.



CISA has called for immediate patching, with internet-exposed systems at the top of the list. The agency also advises hunting for Java installations embedded in third-party software, containers and appliances, warning that these may not be listed separately in asset inventories.



Cisco Talos has yet to see the vulnerability exploited in the wild, but noted, “we anticipate probing attempts may pick up soon after public disclosure.” 



Related: Telegram Widgets Hit by RCE Demos, Malware Warning Days After Launch
Related: Nightmare Eclipse Drops Windows Zero-Day Exploit ‘SignDrop’
Related: N-able Kills Legacy APIs, Patches 17 N-central Zero-Days

Exploitation requires x86-64 hardware with AVX-512 VBMI support, such as Intel Xeon processors from the Ice Lake generation onward and AMD chips based on Zen 4 or newer architectures. Systems powered by ARM64 chips are not vulnerable.



Calif assigned the flaw a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2. Oracle’s advisory gives it a CVSS 3.1 score of 8.1 and lists it as remotely exploitable without authentication, but with high attack complexity.



Oracle released fixes in its November 2026 Critical Security Patch Update, which resolves 11 Java SE vulnerabilities.



Endor Labs pointed out that users of other Java distributions will need to wait for their vendors to roll out patched builds. It also noted that, as of November 17, the fix was not yet present in the public OpenJDK 17, 21 and 25 update repositories.



CISA has called for immediate patching, with internet-exposed systems at the top of the list. The agency also advises hunting for Java installations embedded in third-party software, containers and appliances, warning that these may not be listed separately in asset inventories.



Cisco Talos has yet to see the vulnerability exploited in the wild, but noted, “we anticipate probing attempts may pick up soon after public disclosure.”

Is AI Making Us Worse at Cybersecurity?

Overreliance on AI could erode the creativity, clear communication, and critical thinking security teams need when there’s no playbook.
(Joshua Goldfarb)

The Agent Smith Dilemma: Governing Agentic Identities

Every autonomous AI agent deployed across your enterprise today is a new proxy identity operating inside your perimeter.
(Danelle Au)

Passkeys Are Better Than Passwords. But They Are Not Enough.

As organizations eliminate passwords, attackers will adapt. Instead of attacking the authenticator directly, they will look for opportunities around it.
(Torsten George)

When Risk Becomes Personal: What Security Leaders Can Learn From a Cancer Diagnosis

Risk managers are trained to spot weak signals, plan for disruption and make hard decisions under uncertainty. But the discipline looks different when the risk is not a system or an enterprise, but you.
(Steve Durbin)

Nobody Trusts the Security Questionnaire. We Rely on It Anyway.

The process that gates nearly every B2B deal is a self-assessment the sender distrusts and the receiver accepts. There is finally a better basis for trust between companies.
(Sravish Sridhar)

[64] SecurityWeek - Classified Communications Network at the National Security Agency Goes Down, Defense Official Says
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 02:15:07 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/classified-communications-network-at-the-national-security-agency-goes-down-defense-official-says/

[TAM METİN - 5481 kelime]
Classified Communications Network at the National Security Agency Goes Down, Defense Official Says

The problem disrupted the operations of NSANet, a classified internal network used by employees of the agency.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

An internal network the National Security Agency uses to communicate classified information was offline Tuesday after what the agency described as “routine maintenance.”
The problem disrupted the operations of NSANet, a classified internal network used by employees of the agency, according to a defense official who was not authorized to discuss the matter by name and spoke to The Associated Press on the condition of anonymity.



“The National Security Agency experienced temporary degradation during scheduled routine maintenance,” the NSA said in a statement. “While some internal systems were affected, mission-critical capabilities remain fully operational and our ability to defend national security continues. NSA’s infrastructure is designed with multiple layers of redundancy to maintain operations during planned maintenance or unexpected events.”



The intelligence agency did not provide any additional details on the cause of the outage or the scope of the disruption.



The outage was first reported by CNN.



The NSA episode is among a series of cyber-related disruptions to critical government and private sector functions in the last week, including a system overload Friday that resulted in a ground stop of air traffic in the United States, a technical issue at JPMorgan Chase on Monday that caused ripple effects in the financial system and an internal systems outage at United Airlines that delayed departing flights.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


On Tuesday , Apple acknowledged on its system status page that its Apple Music, App Store and AppleCare services suffered full outages after an internal update.



Top Trump administration national security officials said there was no indication that the events are related or attributable to a cyberattack or hostile activity.



“We have zero evidence or any reason to believe that any of these isolated or individual instances have anything to do with nefarious actors, with cyber hackers, with criminals, with another nation-state, with a malign actor of any kind,” Secretary of State Marco Rubio, who is also Trump’s national security adviser, told reporters while traveling with the president in Asia.



Rubio acknowledged that it was “unsettling to see these reports day after day of some technical failures” and that while the government is always vigilant, each of the recent events “are distinct, and they’re different in their causation — in some cases, highly technical.”



Jay Clayton, the Trump administration’s director of national intelligence, attributed the problem to government institutions and private companies pushing through upgrades of their computer systems around the same time, resulting in periodic technological hiccups.



“Many people are patching their systems,” Clayton said in a CNBC interview Tuesday about the spate of disruptions. “They know that their legacy systems have vulnerabilities. They’re outdated, they don’t work well with new systems. It is time for an upgrade across our digital infrastructure. And as you patch and upgrade your systems — taking them down and bringing them back up — there are things like this that happen.”



Sen. Mark Warner, the Democratic vice chairman of the Senate Intelligence Committee, echoed that assessment.



“AI is making it possible to identify vulnerabilities across enormous numbers of systems at unprecedented speed, creating enormous pressure on companies and government agencies to patch those vulnerabilities immediately, often all at once, putting tremendous strain on systems that weren’t built to handle that pace of change,” Warner said in a statement. “While we still need to get to the bottom of these incidents, the evidence so far points toward legitimate efforts to secure our systems, not malicious activity.”

Rubio acknowledged that it was “unsettling to see these reports day after day of some technical failures” and that while the government is always vigilant, each of the recent events “are distinct, and they’re different in their causation — in some cases, highly technical.”



Jay Clayton, the Trump administration’s director of national intelligence, attributed the problem to government institutions and private companies pushing through upgrades of their computer systems around the same time, resulting in periodic technological hiccups.



“Many people are patching their systems,” Clayton said in a CNBC interview Tuesday about the spate of disruptions. “They know that their legacy systems have vulnerabilities. They’re outdated, they don’t work well with new systems. It is time for an upgrade across our digital infrastructure. And as you patch and upgrade your systems — taking them down and bringing them back up — there are things like this that happen.”



Sen. Mark Warner, the Democratic vice chairman of the Senate Intelligence Committee, echoed that assessment.



“AI is making it possible to identify vulnerabilities across enormous numbers of systems at unprecedented speed, creating enormous pressure on companies and government agencies to patch those vulnerabilities immediately, often all at once, putting tremendous strain on systems that weren’t built to handle that pace of change,” Warner said in a statement. “While we still need to get to the bottom of these incidents, the evidence so far points toward legitimate efforts to secure our systems, not malicious activity.”



Learn More: Cyber AI & Automation Summit | December 8 & 9, 2026 | Virtual Event

Is AI Making Us Worse at Cybersecurity?

Overreliance on AI could erode the creativity, clear communication, and critical thinking security teams need when there’s no playbook.
(Joshua Goldfarb)

The Agent Smith Dilemma: Governing Agentic Identities

Every autonomous AI agent deployed across your enterprise today is a new proxy identity operating inside your perimeter.
(Danelle Au)

Passkeys Are Better Than Passwords. But They Are Not Enough.

As organizations eliminate passwords, attackers will adapt. Instead of attacking the authenticator directly, they will look for opportunities around it.
(Torsten George)

When Risk Becomes Personal: What Security Leaders Can Learn From a Cancer Diagnosis

Risk managers are trained to spot weak signals, plan for disruption and make hard decisions under uncertainty. But the discipline looks different when the risk is not a system or an enterprise, but you.
(Steve Durbin)

Nobody Trusts the Security Questionnaire. We Rely on It Anyway.

The process that gates nearly every B2B deal is a self-assessment the sender distrusts and the receiver accepts. There is finally a better basis for trust between companies.
(Sravish Sridhar)

================================================================================

[65] SecurityWeek - AI Framework Autonomously Weaponized Flaws in Real Attacks, Google Says
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 18:16:17 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/ai-framework-autonomously-weaponized-flaws-in-real-attacks-google-says/

[TAM METİN - 14276 kelime]
Artificial Intelligence

AI Framework Autonomously Weaponized Flaws in Real Attacks, Google Says

Threat actors are deploying multi-agent offensive frameworks to increase the velocity of multi-stage intrusions.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

State-sponsored and cybercrime groups are increasingly adopting generative AI, including multi-agent offensive frameworks, to increase the speed and scale of their operations, Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) reports.
For the first time, GTIG has identified threat actors using an AI framework to automate both vulnerability discovery and exploitation without human intervention.



The multi-agent framework, called exploit_agent, was discovered on an exposed staging server. It was designed to compare patched and unpatched web application code, identify vulnerabilities, develop exploits, and execute them automatically.



The server also contained data stolen from victims and scripts for post-compromise activities, including reconnaissance and webshell deployment. According to GTIG, evidence pointed to an interest in French media and entertainment websites, as well as the education sector.



Attackers are using frameworks such as Artex, Cairn, CyberStrikeAI, and PentAGI to increase the velocity of their multi-stage intrusions, and rely on LLMs and agentic frameworks to reverse engineer patches at machine speed and rapidly weaponize n-day vulnerabilities.



“Threat actor adoption of these frameworks represents a paradigm shift towards intrusions occurring at machine speed, with the automated chaining of complex attack paths scaling operations significantly,” GTIG notes in its latest AI threat tracker report.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Artex has emerged as the most used AI framework for threat actors, mainly Chinese-speaking groups, but Google and Mandiant also observed attackers using and experimenting with open source frameworks such as Cairn, PentAGI, CyberStrikeAI, and HexStrike AI.



GTIG observed Cairn being used in activity targeting the aerospace sector in the US, and PentAGI being used for systematic reconnaissance and vulnerability scanning across organizations in Asia.



Not only have threat actors experimented with these frameworks and harnesses, but GTIG also observed a Chinese group building its own automated attack framework, likely for use in espionage operations.



“These observations additionally extend to the use of other tools and services, such as ZeroHollow, an inference service used for exploit development and LLM routing, and Agent Zero, an open source agent framework which features autonomous code execution and dynamic tool generation,” GTIG notes.



According to Google, threat actors will continue to adopt these tools and their intrusions will become nearly fully agentic.



“As the sophistication of these tools and underlying LLMs increases, we anticipate not only reduced human involvement in these operations but also increased stealth and sophistication of the activity,” GTIG notes.



State-sponsored hackers expand AI use
State-sponsored groups known to have adopted LLMs include multiple Chinese APTs, such as Leaky Castle; Iran-linked APT42, also known as Calanque Ion; and North Korean hackers supporting IT worker activity and running attacks against cryptocurrency organizations, such as Midnight Neptune (UNC1069).



Phishing-as-a-Service (PhaaS) operators too are using LLMs. The Jieyou phishing service, active since March 2026, has a model-agnostic chatbot that allows clients to configure phishing panels and modify templates.



According to Google, threat actors such as state-sponsored groups, commercial Influence-as-a-Service (IaaS) vendors, and inauthentic political monetization (IPM) networks are adopting LLMs for content generation and infrastructure design, which improves the quality of long-running influence campaigns.



“This broad adoption has measurably improved the output quality of some long-running influence campaigns while enabling IPM networks to scale political clickbait that resembles the thematic focus, quality, and volume historically characteristic of state-backed IO campaigns. However, it is important to note that improvements in content quality do not necessarily equate to greater impact or success,” GTIG notes.



In August 2026, Google discovered an end-to-end IaaS platform that automated the entire IO campaign lifecycle, from persona creation to interactive posting across platforms. A separate commercial contractor was developing an AI-powered platform designed to manage over 100,000 accounts across multiple platforms.



Other threat actors are hunting for credentials that provide access to LLMs to monetize them as part of LLMJacking operations. This type of activity can generate significant usage and billing increases, with cumulative resource abuse reaching $1 million in one case.



The surge in LLMJacking has led to the proliferation of LLM Mixers, illicit marketplaces selling access to LLM services and underlying proxy infrastructure and routing traffic through pools of stolen credentials and compute instances. They offer access to multiple models, including frontier and jailbroken ones.



According to GTIG, the wide adoption of LLMs in malicious operations introduces forensic noise that degrades OPSEC and stealth. LLMs can reduce operational reliability, and stealth operators are likely to selectively employ noisy tools for speed and scale while looking to maintain long-term persistence.



“Conversely, we assess with high confidence that actors less concerned with operational stealth, including many cybercrime actors, will continue incorporating autonomous offensive tools into their operations, given that the increased reach of their campaigns will likely compensate for any decreases in success rates,” GTIG notes.



“For US critical infrastructure, the danger isn’t just more attacks, but attacks at machine speed. Water utilities, hospitals, and regional electric utilities that struggle to patch on a monthly cycle can’t win that race manually. AI is dramatically lowering the cost and expertise required to conduct sophisticated cyberattacks. Tasks that once required well-resourced teams, from discovering vulnerabilities to exploiting them and moving through networks, can increasingly be handed off to agents. That puts powerful capabilities within reach of smaller, less sophisticated groups,” said Merlin chief strategy officer Matt Hartman. 



“This report is an early warning of where things are headed. We should assume these capabilities will spread. Nation-state adversaries and criminal operators are watching closely to see what works. Defenders must do two things at once: nail the cybersecurity fundamentals today and accelerate the shift toward automated defenses that can detect and respond at machine speed,” Hartman added. 



Related: California Sues OpenAI, Seeking Court-Ordered Changes After the Hugging Face Hack
Related: Trump Administration Moves to Block China From Indirect Access to American AI Technology
Related: Is AI Making Us Worse at Cybersecurity?

“This broad adoption has measurably improved the output quality of some long-running influence campaigns while enabling IPM networks to scale political clickbait that resembles the thematic focus, quality, and volume historically characteristic of state-backed IO campaigns. However, it is important to note that improvements in content quality do not necessarily equate to greater impact or success,” GTIG notes.



In August 2026, Google discovered an end-to-end IaaS platform that automated the entire IO campaign lifecycle, from persona creation to interactive posting across platforms. A separate commercial contractor was developing an AI-powered platform designed to manage over 100,000 accounts across multiple platforms.



Other threat actors are hunting for credentials that provide access to LLMs to monetize them as part of LLMJacking operations. This type of activity can generate significant usage and billing increases, with cumulative resource abuse reaching $1 million in one case.



The surge in LLMJacking has led to the proliferation of LLM Mixers, illicit marketplaces selling access to LLM services and underlying proxy infrastructure and routing traffic through pools of stolen credentials and compute instances. They offer access to multiple models, including frontier and jailbroken ones.



According to GTIG, the wide adoption of LLMs in malicious operations introduces forensic noise that degrades OPSEC and stealth. LLMs can reduce operational reliability, and stealth operators are likely to selectively employ noisy tools for speed and scale while looking to maintain long-term persistence.



“Conversely, we assess with high confidence that actors less concerned with operational stealth, including many cybercrime actors, will continue incorporating autonomous offensive tools into their operations, given that the increased reach of their campaigns will likely compensate for any decreases in success rates,” GTIG notes.



“For US critical infrastructure, the danger isn’t just more attacks, but attacks at machine speed. Water utilities, hospitals, and regional electric utilities that struggle to patch on a monthly cycle can’t win that race manually. AI is dramatically lowering the cost and expertise required to conduct sophisticated cyberattacks. Tasks that once required well-resourced teams, from discovering vulnerabilities to exploiting them and moving through networks, can increasingly be handed off to agents. That puts powerful capabilities within reach of smaller, less sophisticated groups,” said Merlin chief strategy officer Matt Hartman. 



“This report is an early warning of where things are headed. We should assume these capabilities will spread. Nation-state adversaries and criminal operators are watching closely to see what works. Defenders must do two things at once: nail the cybersecurity fundamentals today and accelerate the shift toward automated defenses that can detect and respond at machine speed,” Hartman added.

Related: Trump Administration Moves to Block China From Indirect Access to American AI Technology
Related: Is AI Making Us Worse at Cybersecurity?

“Threat actor adoption of these frameworks represents a paradigm shift towards intrusions occurring at machine speed, with the automated chaining of complex attack paths scaling operations significantly,” GTIG notes in its latest AI threat tracker report.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Artex has emerged as the most used AI framework for threat actors, mainly Chinese-speaking groups, but Google and Mandiant also observed attackers using and experimenting with open source frameworks such as Cairn, PentAGI, CyberStrikeAI, and HexStrike AI.



GTIG observed Cairn being used in activity targeting the aerospace sector in the US, and PentAGI being used for systematic reconnaissance and vulnerability scanning across organizations in Asia.



Not only have threat actors experimented with these frameworks and harnesses, but GTIG also observed a Chinese group building its own automated attack framework, likely for use in espionage operations.



“These observations additionally extend to the use of other tools and services, such as ZeroHollow, an inference service used for exploit development and LLM routing, and Agent Zero, an open source agent framework which features autonomous code execution and dynamic tool generation,” GTIG notes.



According to Google, threat actors will continue to adopt these tools and their intrusions will become nearly fully agentic.



“As the sophistication of these tools and underlying LLMs increases, we anticipate not only reduced human involvement in these operations but also increased stealth and sophistication of the activity,” GTIG notes.



State-sponsored hackers expand AI use
State-sponsored groups known to have adopted LLMs include multiple Chinese APTs, such as Leaky Castle; Iran-linked APT42, also known as Calanque Ion; and North Korean hackers supporting IT worker activity and running attacks against cryptocurrency organizations, such as Midnight Neptune (UNC1069).

“This broad adoption has measurably improved the output quality of some long-running influence campaigns while enabling IPM networks to scale political clickbait that resembles the thematic focus, quality, and volume historically characteristic of state-backed IO campaigns. However, it is important to note that improvements in content quality do not necessarily equate to greater impact or success,” GTIG notes.



In August 2026, Google discovered an end-to-end IaaS platform that automated the entire IO campaign lifecycle, from persona creation to interactive posting across platforms. A separate commercial contractor was developing an AI-powered platform designed to manage over 100,000 accounts across multiple platforms.



Other threat actors are hunting for credentials that provide access to LLMs to monetize them as part of LLMJacking operations. This type of activity can generate significant usage and billing increases, with cumulative resource abuse reaching $1 million in one case.



The surge in LLMJacking has led to the proliferation of LLM Mixers, illicit marketplaces selling access to LLM services and underlying proxy infrastructure and routing traffic through pools of stolen credentials and compute instances. They offer access to multiple models, including frontier and jailbroken ones.



According to GTIG, the wide adoption of LLMs in malicious operations introduces forensic noise that degrades OPSEC and stealth. LLMs can reduce operational reliability, and stealth operators are likely to selectively employ noisy tools for speed and scale while looking to maintain long-term persistence.



“Conversely, we assess with high confidence that actors less concerned with operational stealth, including many cybercrime actors, will continue incorporating autonomous offensive tools into their operations, given that the increased reach of their campaigns will likely compensate for any decreases in success rates,” GTIG notes.



“For US critical infrastructure, the danger isn’t just more attacks, but attacks at machine speed. Water utilities, hospitals, and regional electric utilities that struggle to patch on a monthly cycle can’t win that race manually. AI is dramatically lowering the cost and expertise required to conduct sophisticated cyberattacks. Tasks that once required well-resourced teams, from discovering vulnerabilities to exploiting them and moving through networks, can increasingly be handed off to agents. That puts powerful capabilities within reach of smaller, less sophisticated groups,” said Merlin chief strategy officer Matt Hartman. 



“This report is an early warning of where things are headed. We should assume these capabilities will spread. Nation-state adversaries and criminal operators are watching closely to see what works. Defenders must do two things at once: nail the cybersecurity fundamentals today and accelerate the shift toward automated defenses that can detect and respond at machine speed,” Hartman added. 



Related: California Sues OpenAI, Seeking Court-Ordered Changes After the Hugging Face Hack
Related: Trump Administration Moves to Block China From Indirect Access to American AI Technology
Related: Is AI Making Us Worse at Cybersecurity?
Related: 3 Charged in Scheme to Use AI to Impersonate CEOs and Attorneys to Steal $129 Million

Phishing-as-a-Service (PhaaS) operators too are using LLMs. The Jieyou phishing service, active since March 2026, has a model-agnostic chatbot that allows clients to configure phishing panels and modify templates.



According to Google, threat actors such as state-sponsored groups, commercial Influence-as-a-Service (IaaS) vendors, and inauthentic political monetization (IPM) networks are adopting LLMs for content generation and infrastructure design, which improves the quality of long-running influence campaigns.

[66] SecurityWeek - Market Research Giant Ipsos Investigating Breach of Internal System
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:55:30 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/market-research-giant-ipsos-investigating-breach-of-internal-system/

[TAM METİN - 2526 kelime]
Market Research Giant Ipsos Investigating Breach of Internal System

The company made the incident public on Monday, November 16, five days after filing a complaint with authorities.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

French market research company Ipsos is investigating a breach that hit one of its internal information systems.
The company made the incident public on Monday, November 16, five days after filing a complaint with authorities.



According to Ipsos, the breach has not disrupted its business. “This incident had no impact on our operations or our ability to deliver for our clients,” the company said.



After detecting the incident, Ipsos launched its response process and worked to contain it, bringing in outside security experts to assist with the probe.



“The investigation is ongoing to determine the nature and scope of the incident,” Ipsos said.



The company has not said whether any data was accessed or stolen.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“We take protection of the information entrusted to us very seriously. Should the investigation establish that information relating to clients, projects or business partners has been affected, we will contact those concerned directly,” Ipsos said.



The market research giant has not revealed when it detected the breach, which system was affected, or how the intrusion occurred. Ipsos said it could not provide further comment while the probe is underway.



No known cybercrime group appears to have taken credit for an attack on Ipsos. 



Established in France in 1975, Ipsos ranks among the biggest market research firms globally. It has nearly 20,000 employees across 90 markets and works with 5,000 clients. Its services rely on data the company collects itself, including from surveys and social media monitoring.

No known cybercrime group appears to have taken credit for an attack on Ipsos. 



Established in France in 1975, Ipsos ranks among the biggest market research firms globally. It has nearly 20,000 employees across 90 markets and works with 5,000 clients. Its services rely on data the company collects itself, including from surveys and social media monitoring.

“The investigation is ongoing to determine the nature and scope of the incident,” Ipsos said.

[67] SecurityWeek - Fandango Warns Ticket Buyers of Payment Card Data Exposure
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:14:24 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/fandango-warns-ticket-buyers-of-payment-card-data-exposure/

[TAM METİN - 2484 kelime]
Fandango Warns Ticket Buyers of Payment Card Data Exposure

Card numbers, CVVs, expiration dates, and email addresses used at Fandango’s ticketing checkout over four days in October may have been exposed.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

US movie ticketing company Fandango is warning customers that payment card information from some purchases made through its checkout page between October 8 and 11 may have been accessed by an unauthorized party.
Fandango says it launched an investigation, assisted by external cybersecurity experts, after learning of a problem affecting the page.



“Our investigation identified evidence that an unauthorized party could access payment card information associated with certain transactions on our checkout page from October 8-11, 2026,” the company says in its notice to customers.



Fandango recently determined which customers’ data was potentially at risk. It has not disclosed how the incident occurred, but says the problem has been fixed and its security processes have since been strengthened.



Potentially exposed data includes the card number, CVV code, expiration date, and billing ZIP code, as well as the customer’s email address. According to Fandango, customer names, Social Security numbers, government-issued identification details, and other account data were not affected.



“Fandango is not currently aware of any fraudulent use of your information as a result of this incident, but nonetheless is informing customers to take appropriate steps to protect their information,” the notice reads.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Affected individuals can sign up for two years of free IDX services, including credit monitoring, dark web scanning, identity theft insurance, and managed identity restoration.



Fandango has not disclosed the total number of impacted customers. Filings with state regulators list 913 affected residents in Oregon and 18 in Vermont.



A notice was also submitted to the California Attorney General’s office, which requires a copy whenever a breach notification is sent to more than 500 California residents.



Fandango, a Versant Media Group brand, handles online ticket sales for more than 31,000 movie screens in the US and also runs the Rotten Tomatoes review site.

Related: Uber Data Breach Impacts 150,000 People
Related: Apify Confirms Customer Data Accessed in Cyberattack

[68] SecurityWeek - Pretxt Emerges Stealth With $5M Seed and an AI Supervision Tool
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/pretxt-emerges-stealth-with-5m-seed-and-an-ai-supervision-tool/

[TAM METİN - 5964 kelime]
Artificial Intelligence

Pretxt Emerges Stealth With $5M Seed and an AI Supervision Tool

Pretxt provides technology to prevent AI agents escaping their guardrails and causing unintended harm.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Pretxt, with offices in Aspen, CO, and Dublin, Ireland, has emerged from stealth with $5 million seed funding raised from Deciens Capital, Howdy Partners and Service Provider Capital. Pretxt enters the emerging category of firms providing technology to prevent AI agents escaping their guardrails and causing unintended harm.
AI is more like a person than like traditional computer code: it doesn’t simply run; it receives, interprets, misinterprets and responds to ambiguous instructions. It is the combination of misinterpretation and wide agency of response that results in AI agents going rogue and potentially causing harm. 



Since it is impossible to categorically exclude ambiguity in language (which is how we instruct the agents), our only alternative method of controlling them is to limit their agency. But too stringent a limitation of agency could become an unacceptable restriction on a potentially huge business benefit.



So, without a new category of supervision control, it is likely that we cannot have beneficial AI agents without also having potentially dangerous rogue agents. This requirement for a new control is leading to the emergence of a type of cybersecurity product that attempts to recognize a potentially harmful agentic action at runtime and control it before it can cause harm. 



Pretxt is announcing just such a product which it describes as AI Supervision, “a purpose-built runtime technology to supervise AI agents as they work, guiding them back on course before mistakes become business consequences.”



Rather than neutering the capability of an agent, explains Aaron Costello, Pretxt co-founder and CTO, “We say, ‘Okay, you give an AI agent the same level of access as an authorized employee, and we will continuously monitor the manner in which it interacts with systems and other people. We will validate and ensure that all such interaction conforms with your business standards and operates in the same way that you expect your employees to operate.”Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


If the agent exceeds its expected authority or deviates from its authorized purpose, Pretxt doesn’t simply shut it down – it ‘steers’ the offender back on track. It’s a bit like having an experienced supervisor permanently on the shoulder of a trainee, who just steps in when necessary, with, “Don’t do it like that, do it like this instead.”



‘Steering’ is governed by context, continues Costello. “We ask new customers to onboard and connect their AI agent to our product, so we can immediately see the agent’s instructions and understand its purpose. As we watch it in operation, we learn how it speaks. We see what tools it can access, and we learn what abilities it has been given – such as ‘send’ an email, ‘post’ to LinkedIn, or ‘update’ a database.”



The customer then explains to Pretxt, I never want my AI agent to do x, or I only want my agent to do y, or see this kind of data. “It could be anything,” adds Costello. “The information is imported into Pretxt’s own AI. It becomes the context for acceptable behavior and is hooked into anywhere within the agent that could potentially violate the agent’s intended purpose and constraints.”



He gives the example of a customer service agent used by a medical practice to help patients book an appointment. Pretxt may have been told by the medical practice, ‘I never want this agent to give medical advice.’ “What we do,” he continues, “is hook into every single occasion the AI agent interacts with the user. Our own agent will look at this interaction and check whether it contains medical advice. This is done in real time. If it does contain medical advice (which we know should not be offered), we prevent it. But not by shutting down the agent, because that would leave patients unable to book appointments. We use a different approach that we call ‘steering’. If we detect medical advice, we invisibly talk back to the agent and effectively tell it, ‘You tried to give medical advice. Please continue to help the user with their booking request, but do not attempt to give medical advice again’.”



This is the purpose of Pretxt’s AI supervision app – not to shut down wanton AI agents, but to keep them safe, and prevent them straying from their predefined constraints.



Related: US Agencies Detail Incident Response Practices for Rogue AI Agents
Related: Outerlimit Raises $16 Million to Stop Rogue AI Agents From Causing Harm
Related: Capsule Security Launches ‘AI Circuit Breaker’ to Stop Rogue Agents
Related: Wikimedia Says Rogue OpenAI Agents Tried to Turn Its Tools Into Proxies

AI is more like a person than like traditional computer code: it doesn’t simply run; it receives, interprets, misinterprets and responds to ambiguous instructions. It is the combination of misinterpretation and wide agency of response that results in AI agents going rogue and potentially causing harm. 



Since it is impossible to categorically exclude ambiguity in language (which is how we instruct the agents), our only alternative method of controlling them is to limit their agency. But too stringent a limitation of agency could become an unacceptable restriction on a potentially huge business benefit.

Pretxt is announcing just such a product which it describes as AI Supervision, “a purpose-built runtime technology to supervise AI agents as they work, guiding them back on course before mistakes become business consequences.”
