We will validate and ensure that all such interaction conforms with your business standards and operates in the same way that you expect your employees to operate.”Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


If the agent exceeds its expected authority or deviates from its authorized purpose, Pretxt doesn’t simply shut it down – it ‘steers’ the offender back on track. It’s a bit like having an experienced supervisor permanently on the shoulder of a trainee, who just steps in when necessary, with, “Don’t do it like that, do it like this instead.”



‘Steering’ is governed by context, continues Costello. “We ask new customers to onboard and connect their AI agent to our product, so we can immediately see the agent’s instructions and understand its purpose. As we watch it in operation, we learn how it speaks. We see what tools it can access, and we learn what abilities it has been given – such as ‘send’ an email, ‘post’ to LinkedIn, or ‘update’ a database.”



The customer then explains to Pretxt, I never want my AI agent to do x, or I only want my agent to do y, or see this kind of data. “It could be anything,” adds Costello. “The information is imported into Pretxt’s own AI. It becomes the context for acceptable behavior and is hooked into anywhere within the agent that could potentially violate the agent’s intended purpose and constraints.”



He gives the example of a customer service agent used by a medical practice to help patients book an appointment. Pretxt may have been told by the medical practice, ‘I never want this agent to give medical advice.’ “What we do,” he continues, “is hook into every single occasion the AI agent interacts with the user. Our own agent will look at this interaction and check whether it contains medical advice. This is done in real time. If it does contain medical advice (which we know should not be offered), we prevent it. But not by shutting down the agent, because that would leave patients unable to book appointments. We use a different approach that we call ‘steering’. If we detect medical advice, we invisibly talk back to the agent and effectively tell it, ‘You tried to give medical advice. Please continue to help the user with their booking request, but do not attempt to give medical advice again’.”



This is the purpose of Pretxt’s AI supervision app – not to shut down wanton AI agents, but to keep them safe, and prevent them straying from their predefined constraints.



Related: US Agencies Detail Incident Response Practices for Rogue AI Agents
Related: Outerlimit Raises $16 Million to Stop Rogue AI Agents From Causing Harm
Related: Capsule Security Launches ‘AI Circuit Breaker’ to Stop Rogue Agents
Related: Wikimedia Says Rogue OpenAI Agents Tried to Turn Its Tools Into Proxies

Since it is impossible to categorically exclude ambiguity in language (which is how we instruct the agents), our only alternative method of controlling them is to limit their agency. But too stringent a limitation of agency could become an unacceptable restriction on a potentially huge business benefit.



So, without a new category of supervision control, it is likely that we cannot have beneficial AI agents without also having potentially dangerous rogue agents. This requirement for a new control is leading to the emergence of a type of cybersecurity product that attempts to recognize a potentially harmful agentic action at runtime and control it before it can cause harm. 



Pretxt is announcing just such a product which it describes as AI Supervision, “a purpose-built runtime technology to supervise AI agents as they work, guiding them back on course before mistakes become business consequences.”



Rather than neutering the capability of an agent, explains Aaron Costello, Pretxt co-founder and CTO, “We say, ‘Okay, you give an AI agent the same level of access as an authorized employee, and we will continuously monitor the manner in which it interacts with systems and other people. We will validate and ensure that all such interaction conforms with your business standards and operates in the same way that you expect your employees to operate.”Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


If the agent exceeds its expected authority or deviates from its authorized purpose, Pretxt doesn’t simply shut it down – it ‘steers’ the offender back on track. [69] SecurityWeek - MSP ScreenConnect Servers Targeted in Month-Long Campaign
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 13:24:43 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/msp-screenconnect-servers-targeted-in-month-long-campaign/

[TAM METİN - 3998 kelime]
MSP ScreenConnect Servers Targeted in Month-Long Campaign

More than 40 servers received over 688,000 requests exploiting CVE-2026-84869 to execute guest-pushed files.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Dozens of MSP ScreenConnect servers have been targeted in a month-long campaign exploiting a vulnerability patched in September, cybersecurity firm Huntress reports.
The attacks, ongoing since at least October 20, aim to exploit CVE-2026-84869 (CVSS score of 9.9), a critical missing authorization and improper privilege management bug that allows for files to be transmitted and executed during a connection without authorization or confirmation.



ConnectWise patched the security defect on September 8, and CISA added it to the KEV catalog on September 11. Huntress warned at the time that exploitation started on August 20.



Now, the cybersecurity firm says a new wave of attacks is targeting ScreenConnect instances, with a focus on managed service provider (MSP) servers.



The hackers exploit CVE-2026-84869 via requests to run a guest-pushed batch file that connects to a Telegram bot and installs a rogue ScreenConnect client.



“Attackers appear to be spraying servers blindly: Since October 20, we’ve seen at least 688,000 guest ‘run file’ requests for update*.bat hit 42 MSP ScreenConnect servers from 29 IP addresses, waiting for any technician to join,” Huntress says.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


According to the cybersecurity firm, the activity is different from the recently observed campaigns targeting ScreenConnect instances with the SCKit malware toolkit and with unauthorized sessions from machines posing as crypto exchanges.



The exploitation of CVE-2026-84869, it explains, allows attackers to push malicious files to technicians’ machines and get their own clients to execute them when the technician joins the session. The technician sees no prompt when the files are executed.



“An MSP technician’s workstation typically holds RMM consoles, saved credentials, and access to every customer, making it an exceptionally attractive target. When a technician joins a remote session, their ScreenConnect host client and the guest machine can exchange files,” Huntress notes.



In one attack, a rogue ScreenConnect client disguised as Windows Update was installed and spawned within nine seconds of the host client running the guest-pushed bat file.



As part of another, the guest machine pushed a text file two seconds after the technician joined the session, and the technician’s client ran the file three seconds later.



In October, a targeted attack hit an MSP server: the attacker joined as a guest in three real sessions the technician had created and asked the client to execute a file designed to drop a script into the technician’s computer’s Startup folder.



Organizations should update technicians’ ScreenConnect host clients to version 26.6.5 or later, along with all the Access agents ConnectWise lists.



Related: N-able Kills Legacy APIs, Patches 17 N-central Zero-Days
Related: Optimizely Vulnerability Exploited to Serve Malware on Major Websites
Related: Google Project Zero Sets Course for 30-Day Disclosure Deadline
Related: Recent UnrealIRCd Vulnerability Exploited in Attacks

“Attackers appear to be spraying servers blindly: Since October 20, we’ve seen at least 688,000 guest ‘run file’ requests for update*.bat hit 42 MSP ScreenConnect servers from 29 IP addresses, waiting for any technician to join,” Huntress says.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


According to the cybersecurity firm, the activity is different from the recently observed campaigns targeting ScreenConnect instances with the SCKit malware toolkit and with unauthorized sessions from machines posing as crypto exchanges.



The exploitation of CVE-2026-84869, it explains, allows attackers to push malicious files to technicians’ machines and get their own clients to execute them when the technician joins the session. The technician sees no prompt when the files are executed.



“An MSP technician’s workstation typically holds RMM consoles, saved credentials, and access to every customer, making it an exceptionally attractive target. When a technician joins a remote session, their ScreenConnect host client and the guest machine can exchange files,” Huntress notes.



In one attack, a rogue ScreenConnect client disguised as Windows Update was installed and spawned within nine seconds of the host client running the guest-pushed bat file.



As part of another, the guest machine pushed a text file two seconds after the technician joined the session, and the technician’s client ran the file three seconds later.



In October, a targeted attack hit an MSP server: the attacker joined as a guest in three real sessions the technician had created and asked the client to execute a file designed to drop a script into the technician’s computer’s Startup folder.



Organizations should update technicians’ ScreenConnect host clients to version 26.6.5 or later, along with all the Access agents ConnectWise lists.



Related: N-able Kills Legacy APIs, Patches 17 N-central Zero-Days
Related: Optimizely Vulnerability Exploited to Serve Malware on Major Websites
Related: Google Project Zero Sets Course for 30-Day Disclosure Deadline
Related: Recent UnrealIRCd Vulnerability Exploited in Attacks

ConnectWise patched the security defect on September 8, and CISA added it to the KEV catalog on September 11. Huntress warned at the time that exploitation started on August 20.



Now, the cybersecurity firm says a new wave of attacks is targeting ScreenConnect instances, with a focus on managed service provider (MSP) servers.



The hackers exploit CVE-2026-84869 via requests to run a guest-pushed batch file that connects to a Telegram bot and installs a rogue ScreenConnect client.



“Attackers appear to be spraying servers blindly: Since October 20, we’ve seen at least 688,000 guest ‘run file’ requests for update*.bat hit 42 MSP ScreenConnect servers from 29 IP addresses, waiting for any technician to join,” Huntress says.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.

[70] SecurityWeek - Access Dental Management Data Breach Impacts Over 340,000
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 12:59:04 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/access-dental-management-data-breach-impacts-over-340000/

[TAM METİN - 2394 kelime]
Access Dental Management Data Breach Impacts Over 340,000

Hackers breached Access Dental’s network and stole personal, health, and insurance information.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Texas-based dental services provider Access Dental Management (ADM) has disclosed a data breach impacting more than 340,000 individuals.
Providing dental care across Texas and in Illinois, Indiana, and New Mexico, ADM says it discovered the incident on May 5 and immediately began an investigation with help from third-party forensic specialists.



The investigation determined that, between May 1 and May 4, hackers had accessed its network and viewed or copied certain data, including personal information.



On October 21, ADM’s review of the compromised information determined that the attacker accessed names, addresses, dates of birth, Social Security numbers, medical information, and health insurance information.



“Upon learning of this incident, ADM moved quickly to investigate and respond to the incident. As part of its ongoing commitment to the security of information, ADM is reviewing and enhancing its existing policies and procedures related to data privacy to reduce the likelihood of a similar future incident,” ADM said in a notice (PDF).



Last week, the organization notified the Texas and Vermont Attorney General’s Offices that 341,049 individuals were affected by the data breach (341,039 in Texas and 10 in Vermont), but the total number could reportedly surpass 455,000.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


ADM is sending written notification letters to the potentially affected individuals, encouraging them “to remain vigilant against incidents of identity theft and fraud by reviewing your account statements, explanation of benefits forms, and monitoring your free credit reports for suspicious activity and to detect errors.”



While the organization did not share further details on the cyberattack, the WorldLeaks ransomware group claimed responsibility for the intrusion on June 5.



SecurityWeek has emailed ADM’s parent platform Surge Private Equity for additional information on the incident and will update this article if it responds.

Related: Cursor Supply Chain Attack Started at Fireworks AI
Related: Apify Confirms Customer Data Accessed in Cyberattack
Related: Notion Customer Data Compromised in Fireworks AI Hack

[71] SecurityWeek - Corma Receives $125 Million to Train Foundation Models for Defensive Cybersecurity
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 12:51:54 +0000

Cybersecurity Funding

Corma Receives $125 Million to Train Foundation Models for Defensive Cybersecurity

Led by NEA and supported by existing investors, the funding brings the company’s valuation to $1 billion.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Cybersecurity startup Corma has raised $125 million in a Series A funding round, bringing the total raised to $185 million and its valuation to $1 billion.
Led by NEA, the investment round saw additional support from previous backers Sequoia Capital, Khosla Ventures, and Coatue.



Founded in 2025 and headquartered in Tel Aviv and San Francisco, Corma emerged from stealth mode in August with a frontier foundation model specifically designed for defensive cybersecurity.



Corma says AI’s offensive capabilities have grown exponentially over the past several months, while its defensive capabilities have not, and it aims to fill that gap.



The startup trains frontier foundation models and deploys them as AI agents alongside existing security tools to audit security data and correlate signals across environments to improve defenses and help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyberattacks.



“Cybersecurity is now a battle of intelligence against intelligence, and Black October made that impossible to ignore,” said Corma co-founder and CEO Alon Pluda.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“Since we came out of stealth, demand from the world’s largest organizations has been overwhelming, and one of our hardest problems has been securing capacity fast enough to keep up with the demand,” Pluda added.

[72] Help Net Security - Ubuntu AI security patches are here, if you’re willing to test them yourself
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 08:47:50 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/ubuntu-ai-security-patches-edge-archive/

[TAM METİN - 486 kelime]
Ubuntu AI security patches are here, if you’re willing to test them yourself

Canonical is letting Ubuntu Pro customers install security patches written by AI agents before its engineers finish reviewing and testing them. The patches come from Surge, a system Canonical built to fix known vulnerabilities in Ubuntu’s userspace software, meaning everything outside the operating system’s kernel. They ship through a new opt-in channel called the Edge archive.

If you leave Edge switched off, nothing changes in how your updates are checked: Ubuntu’s regular security updates still get human validation before release. Switch it on and you get fixes sooner, but you also pick up a job Ubuntu’s own testing normally handles, which is deciding whether a patch is safe to run in production.

Twice the fixes, still too slow

Ubuntu’s security teams have already shipped twice as many fixes as they did last year. The company says AI agents are behind a wave of vulnerability reports and attacks across open source. Human review and soak testing, where a fix runs for an extended stretch to catch problems before release, take longer than some customers are willing to sit exposed. Its answer is that “we need to fight AI with AI.”

Machines check machines, then call a human

Surge passes the work down a line of AI agents, each with one job. The first reads vulnerability feeds, matches each report to Ubuntu packages, and drops anything that doesn’t affect Ubuntu. The next grabs the upstream fix and the code around it. Then several competing models each write their own backport, which means adapting a fix made for the current version of a program to the older version a stable Ubuntu release ships.

A panel of separate judge models looks over the candidates and has to reach a consensus. If the judges deadlock, aren’t confident, or run into an unusual case, the patch goes to a human on the Ubuntu security team. Anything that makes it through gets built and run through the same deterministic tests as any other Ubuntu package. The company says this setup protects against regressions and against supply-chain attacks, where someone slips malicious code into software through its build or update path.

The kernel gets its own track

Kernel fixes stay out of Surge’s hands. The kernel needs a different backporting setup, the company says, and just shipping the newest version would be a big stability risk for enterprise systems. So kernels now run on a two-week Stable Release Update cycle, with the cycles overlapping so a new proposed kernel lands every week.

Surge is aimed at the backlog of vulnerabilities that are already known. New flaws, including the ones Canonical’s automated research turns up, still go through coordinated disclosure: the company reports them privately and works with upstream maintainers under embargo before anything goes public.

“We strongly disagree with those who have abandoned coordinated disclosure practices,” the company wrote.

vulnerability management

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/ubuntu-ai-security-patches-edge-archive/, helpnetsecurity.com, 18.11.2026)

[73] Help Net Security - Deutsche Telekom IoT’s CEO on securing devices that run for 20 years
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 07:30:25 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/christian-schwalbe-deutsche-telekom-iot-security/

[TAM METİN - 1376 kelime]
Deutsche Telekom IoT’s CEO on securing devices that run for 20 years

In this Help Net Security interview, Christian Schwalbe, CEO of Deutsche Telekom IoT, talks about the forgotten devices that create the most risk in IoT deployments. He explains what the EU Cyber Resilience Act means for manufacturers whose products stay in the field for 15 or 20 years, and how threats can be spotted through communication patterns without inspecting payloads.

Schwalbe also discusses post-quantum cryptography for constrained devices, including research on splitting secrets across terrestrial and satellite paths, and why digital sovereignty depends on understanding and controlling your dependencies.

That’s a good question.It’s probably the device nobody thinks about anymore. If you have a critical industrial controller, you usually know it’s important. You know what happens if it fails. You have people responsible for it, and hopefully you have security measures in place.

But now think about a small sensor somewhere in a factory or a warehouse. It was installed years ago, it’s still working, and nobody really pays attention to it. As long as it sends data, everybody is happy. That is where the risk begins.

Attackers are not concerned with the price of a device. They look for weaknesses and opportunities. Even a simple device can create exposure for a wider system.

This is one of the biggest challenges in IoT security. We’re connecting more and more devices, but we also need to make sure we don’t lose visibility and control over them. IoT security cannot end at deployment. It has to be managed throughout the device lifecycle.

I think the Cyber Resilience Act is an important step because it makes cybersecurity and vulnerability handling a responsibility for manufacturers throughout a product’s support period. At the same time, implementing these requirements raises practical questions, especially for long-lived IoT products. The support period needs to reflect how long a product is expected to be used, and manufacturers need workable ways to manage updates and vulnerabilities across devices already in the field.

We’ve been talking about IoT security for years. But if we’re honest, security hasn’t always received the same attention as functionality, costs or time to market. That’s changing now.

I wouldn’t claim that every company was surprised, or that everyone was fully prepared. The reality is more complex. Some manufacturers have been investing in security for a long time. Others are now taking a much closer look at what it means to secure a connected product throughout its lifetime. And that’s the important point.

We’re not talking about securing a device on the day it leaves the factory. We’re talking about products that might operate for ten, fifteen or even twenty years.

What happens when a vulnerability is discovered? Can the software be updated? Who takes responsibility? And how do you manage all of this when thousands of devices are already deployed around the world?

These are real challenges for manufacturers. This is also why we value partnerships like the one we have with Exein. We strongly believe in the importance of security approaches that can help manufacturers protect devices during operation. I believe the CRA will accelerate a change that should have happened anyway: Security needs to become a fundamental part of how connected products are designed, deployed and managed. Not something we add at the end.

For me, security and privacy belong together. You cannot build trust in connected technology if customers feel they have to sacrifice one for the other.

And I think it’s important to understand that effective security doesn’t automatically mean inspecting everything.

There are different ways to identify potential threats. For example, unusual communication patterns can tell you that something might be wrong with a device. Is it suddenly communicating with an unexpected destination? Is its behaviour different from what we normally see?

Communication patterns can sometimes reveal anomalies without requiring inspection of the payload. Of course, even metadata needs to be handled responsibly. We have to be clear about what information is necessary, how it’s processed and who has access to it.

But there’s another aspect. Not every threat is visible in network traffic. Especially with encrypted communications, you need additional security capabilities.

That is why layered security matters. Depending on the architecture, network-level measures can be complemented by capabilities on the device. That’s particularly relevant for IoT, where devices are often deployed in environments that are difficult to access.

In the end, our job is to help customers operate their connected solutions securely while keeping control over their data. That’s what good security should deliver.

Absolutely. I think there’s one important point we shouldn’t overlook: Post-quantum security is not just a topic for the future. It’s already relevant today.

There’s a concept called “Harvest Now, Decrypt Later”. The idea is quite simple. Attackers collect encrypted data today, store it, and potentially decrypt it in the future once sufficiently powerful quantum computers become available.

So even if the encryption is secure today, the information itself might not be secure in ten or fifteen years.

Now, does that mean every connected sensor needs post-quantum cryptography immediately? Of course not.

For many IoT applications, data simply doesn’t need to remain confidential for decades. But think about critical infrastructure, sensitive industrial information or applications where long-term confidentiality really matters. For those use cases, waiting is not a good strategy.

We’re already seeing the industry move. Post-quantum cryptography is no longer just a research topic. It’s being implemented in real-world applications, and we’re seeing developments that make it increasingly relevant for IoT devices as well.

Of course, constrained devices bring their own challenges. Limited processing power, memory, energy consumption and low data rates all have to be considered. But that’s exactly where innovation comes in.

Together with our colleagues at T-Labs and research partners, we’re exploring how post-quantum security can be made practical for resource-constrained IoT devices. Our research focuses on end-to-end, threshold-based trust establishment, combining post-quantum cryptography with secret sharing across multiple terrestrial and satellite communication paths. By distributing security-critical information across different connectivity paths, this approach aims to enhance security and resilience while reducing dependence on any single path.

We’ve already developed a proof of concept and presented our research at European Wireless 2026. Our results demonstrate the technical feasibility of this approach, while further optimization and validation are needed before broader deployment.

We need to distinguish between applications that require quantum-resistant protection today and those that can follow a gradual migration path.

But in both cases, we should build devices and systems that can adapt. Because with IoT devices staying in the field for fifteen or twenty years, waiting until quantum computing becomes a practical threat is simply too late.

Yes, but digital sovereignty involves much more than the location of a data centre. And I think we sometimes make this discussion too simple.

Of course, where your data is stored matters. Who operates the infrastructure matters. The legal framework matters. But let’s look at a typical IoT solution.

You have a device, a communication module, firmware, software, connectivity and a cloud platform. Often, these components come from different companies in different countries.

So, even if your data is hosted in Europe, you still need to ask some important questions.

Do you know what’s running on your devices? Do you understand your dependencies? Can you identify vulnerabilities? Most importantly, can you do something about them?

To me, digital sovereignty means understanding your dependencies and retaining meaningful control over the technology you rely on.
It doesn’t mean that everything has to be developed and manufactured in one country. That wouldn’t reflect the reality of global IoT deployments.

Our customers operate internationally. They manufacture products in one market and deploy them in many others. They need connectivity and security solutions that work across borders.

So I believe we need both: global technology ecosystems and standards for security, transparency and responsibility. This is where Europe has an opportunity.

We can help establish a framework where security is built into connected products from the beginning, where responsibilities are clear and where companies have the tools to manage their devices throughout their lifetime.

Because ultimately, digital sovereignty is not just about where technology comes from.

It is about whether you can understand it, trust it and remain in control of it.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/christian-schwalbe-deutsche-telekom-iot-security/, helpnetsecurity.com, 18.11.2026)

[74] Help Net Security - Once a scam ends the victim blaming begins
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 07:00:33 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/nicola-fox-hamilton-victim-blaming-scams-phishing/

[TAM METİN - 822 kelime]
Once a scam ends the victim blaming begins

Blaming people who fall for scams deepens the harm and teaches them to stay silent the next time, cyberpsychologist Dr. Nicola Fox Hamilton told the audience at IRISSCON 2026 in Dublin.

Dr. Nicola Fox Hamilton, cyberpsychology researcher and lecturer in IADT, talked about the psychology behind phishing and scams

Fox Hamilton, a cyberpsychology researcher and lecturer at IADT, talked about the lasting impact scams have on victims and made a point to note that nobody in the room was safe. “We are all potential victims of cybercrime at some point. It’s almost inevitable.”

What happens after the click matters just as much, she argued, whether the victim is someone who lost a pension pot to an investment scammer or an employee who typed a password into the wrong page, because shame keeps both of them quiet.

She admitted she learned this firsthand. While finishing her PhD during marking season at IADT, she got an email in her rarely checked university account telling her to update an old password. “I was like, grand, that makes sense,” she recalled. She clicked and entered her credentials, and “as soon as I hit the button, I knew what I’d done.”

The IT team was kind about it. Her own reaction was harsher. “I was like, I’m so stupid. But it’s not stupidity, it’s catching you in a moment of vulnerability, which everybody has at some point.”

Bad things happen to other people

Part of the problem lies in how people judge their own risk. Most of us carry an optimism bias, which she summed up as: “Bad things are much more likely to happen to other people than me. I’m not gonna get targeted. I’m not gonna fall for it. Other people will.” People who hold that belief strongly are much more likely to victim blame other people, she noted.

Overconfidence works the same way: assuming you’re safe leaves you more exposed. Her own click cured her of it.

A scammer who listens

Standard advice about romance scams tells people never to send money to someone they haven’t met. She argued that this misses how the scams work. Scammers spend months building a relationship before they ask for anything, and investment fraudsters do the same through a friendly “advisor.”

By that stage, the victim believes they are in a relationship. “And when somebody you’re in a relationship with asks you for money, that’s very socially normal,” she pointed out.

Scammers also cut victims off from the people who might warn them. When relatives question the relationship, the scammer tells the victim that the family doesn’t want them to be happy and is jealous. They let the victim do most of the talking, partly to gather information they can use and partly so they don’t have to keep track of their own lies. “Being listened to is very powerful,” she noted.

When the scam falls apart, victims lose their money and the relationship they believed in, and the second loss often cuts deeper.

Victims feel deceived and powerless and turn the blame on themselves, which is why outside judgment does so much harm. “They feel powerless and engage in a lot of self blame, so they don’t need extra people victim blaming on top of that, which happens a lot,” she noted. Shame, fear, anxiety and depression are common, and in extreme cases romance and sextortion scams have led victims to suicidal thoughts or suicide.

The effects reach beyond individuals, she added, and cited a study from the Philippines that linked widespread phishing and cybercrime to lower trust in institutions, with people pulling away from one another and growing afraid of social media.

Organizations deal with the same dynamic on a smaller scale. Employees often assume phishing tests are there to catch them out, and Fox Hamilton urged security teams to explain that the tests look for gaps in training, and to respond to failures with support and education.

Fear of blame pushes people to hide mistakes, because we all wanna look good in front of other people, especially in front of those higher up the hierarchy. That instinct is dangerous when the mistake is a click on a phishing link, since every month of silence gives an attacker another month inside the network.

Blame and accountability are different things, she added. “If someone comes to you for the 50th time in a month and says I clicked on the thing, you’ve got another issue. But most people won’t be doing that.”

Her advice was to talk openly about incidents and near misses, starting with the people in charge. Admitting her own click in front of a room of security professionals was part of that. “That’s really helpful if you have done that to be able to be open about the fact that it is possible for anybody to do this. It doesn’t mean you’re stupid or not aware.”

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/nicola-fox-hamilton-victim-blaming-scams-phishing/, helpnetsecurity.com, 18.11.2026)

[75] Help Net Security - TapMap: Free, open-source network map
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 06:30:24 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/tapmap-network-map-open-source/

[TAM METİN - 478 kelime]
TapMap: Free, open-source network map

TapMap is a free, open-source desktop app that plots a computer’s active network connections on an interactive world map. It scans open sockets, pulls out the remote IP addresses and looks each one up in a local GeoIP database. Then it draws the result in the browser, with the application behind each connection a click away.

“Locations are based on IP geolocation data and are approximate. TapMap does not independently determine or verify the physical location of the network endpoint,” Ola Lie, the developer of TapMap, told Help Net Security. “For some network infrastructure, including anycast services, CDNs, proxies, and VPNs, the displayed location may differ from the physical location of the endpoint handling the connection.”

The labels need a similar caveat. TapMap groups connections by ASN (autonomous system number), the number that identifies a network on the internet. “ASN and ASN organization identify the network operator, not necessarily the service owner,” Lie said.

Some GeoIP databases attach a radius to each result, which estimates how far off the pin might be. “I’ve been looking into possible improvements, including GeoIP accuracy-radius data and independent anycast measurements. One finding is that a small reported accuracy radius does not necessarily mean a location is reliable, particularly for anycast addresses,” Lie said.

“I’m considering making geolocation uncertainty more visible in the interface and possibly identifying known anycast addresses,” he said. “However, reliable classification and incomplete coverage remain challenges, so I wouldn’t want to introduce indicators that could be misleading themselves.”

If you use TapMap, the application name is the most reliable part of each entry, because it comes from your own operating system. Treat the location as a starting point.

Alerts wait out a learning period

TapMap keeps a running log of up to 500 recent events it calls Significant Connections. “TapMap already supports desktop and MQTT notifications for Significant Connections, such as new applications, countries, network providers, ports, and failed application verification,” Lie said. Desktop alerts are on by default on Windows, Linux and macOS. The second channel publishes events to a message broker so another system can collect them.

“Significant Connections are recorded from the beginning, but notifications are delayed until a learning period has passed (7 active Insights days by default). This helps avoid excessive notifications during initial use,” Lie said. The count is in active days, so a machine that is often switched off will take longer to finish the learning period.

“TapMap continues monitoring in the background even when the browser tab is closed, so users can receive notifications without keeping the interface open. On desktop installations, automatic startup at login is also available and can be enabled or disabled,” he said.

TapMap is available for free on GitHub.

20 open-source cybersecurity tools to keep your team ready for anything

GitHub CISO on security strategy and collaborating with the open-source community

[76] Help Net Security - BubbleLoader malware turns copies of WinRAR and Windows Media Player into carriers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 06:00:03 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/winrar-bubbleloader-malware/

[TAM METİN - 629 kelime]
BubbleLoader malware turns copies of WinRAR and Windows Media Player into carriers

ReversingLabs has taken apart BubbleLoader, a loader that turns ordinary Windows programs into carriers for other malware. The attackers rewrite a handful of instructions at a program’s entry point, the spot where Windows begins running it, and add two PNG images that hold the hidden code. Known hosts include WinRAR, the x64dbg debugger, Windows Media Player, the Firefox launcher and virtiofs.

The earliest BubbleLoader samples appeared in February 2026, and ReversingLabs tracked two clusters of activity in mid-September and early October, so the tool is in use now. It also scrambles its payload’s fingerprint every time it runs, which undercuts one of the first checks an investigator reaches for.

Obfuscated prologue of BubbleLoader entry point patch (Source: ReversingLabs)

Two images, one of them fake

The patch at the entry point is small. It reserves a block of memory, copies both images into it and jumps into the second one. Only the first image is a real picture, a gradient that runs from red to blue. The second opens with the correct PNG signature, then breaks off where the image’s width should be and continues as encrypted code.

A short piece of code hidden in that fake image uses the real image’s bytes as a rolling XOR key, combining each byte of hidden data with a byte of the key, to decrypt the next stage. That stage decrypts the final payload and loads it directly into memory.

The gradient makes a poor key. The image file contains long runs of zeros, and XOR with zero changes nothing, so stretches of the loader sit in the file unencrypted, readable strings included. The fake image gives itself away too. The first byte of a PNG’s width field should be 0x00 for any image of normal size, but in the fake image that’s where the encrypted data begins. In the sample ReversingLabs shows, that byte is 0x55, which would mean an unusually wide picture for one stored inside a program.

The hash changes on every run

Investigators often track malware by its hash, a short fingerprint computed from a file’s bytes. While decrypting its payload, BubbleLoader finds the 0xCC filler bytes that compilers leave between functions and overwrites them, all but the first in each block, with values read from the processor’s timestamp counter. It takes a fresh reading for every byte. The payload’s in-memory hash comes out different on every execution, and analysts recover the original by patching that loop out of the loader.

The loader also hooks RtlPcToFileHeader, a routine in Windows’ ntdll library, and registers the payload’s exception table. That gives the payload working error handling, which malware often uses to throw off analysts. Other details point to one code base built for many hosts and payloads. The patch tries two string formats for the same Windows call, and the loader guards against being run twice, a step DLL payloads need, even in a sample carrying an EXE.

Start by making sure the WinRAR on your network is the copy its publisher shipped. ReversingLabs recommends strict application allowlisting and code-signing verification to catch altered binaries. The company also advises alerts on memory allocated as readable, writable and executable at once, alerts on protection changes aimed at ntdll’s exception routines, and static rules that flag PNGs with truncated headers. Because payload hashes shift from run to run, it points responders toward behavior in memory and checks on each program’s entry point.

ReversingLabs does not say how the tampered programs reach victims, and the final payload varies from sample to sample. The company listed 24 SHA-256 hashes for BubbleLoader samples. Those hashes describe the patched programs on disk. The payload those programs unpack changes its fingerprint every time it runs.

Product showcase: iVerify Basic helps users check device security and improve privacy

iVerify Basic is a mobile security app for individuals who want to check their devices for signs of compromise and improve their security settings. It is available for iOS and Android, including tablets.

Device security checks

The Device tab puts scan controls and security status on one screen. Users can select Run Scan to check device integrity. After a check, the app displays its findings and records when the device was last checked.

Device Health lists whether Face ID and screen lock are enabled, alongside the status of iOS and iVerify updates. These checks give users a starting point for reviewing their configuration.

Security and privacy guides

The Guides tab groups recommendations into categories covering theft protection, software exploits, signs of compromise, wireless data, communications, and advertising data leakage. Each category shows a completion count.

The theft protection section includes instructions for disabling Control Center while locked, using Emergency SOS and Lockdown Mode, enabling Stolen Device Protection, and configuring automatic updates.

Individual guides explain the feature and any required setup, with an option to flag an item for later.

The Online tab adds guidance on security software, account authentication, and network security.

I found the guides very useful for people who need understandable instructions on improving their iPhone’s security and personal privacy. The topic groups make it easy to choose an area to work on, and marking each reviewed guide Done provides a record of progress.

[78] Help Net Security - Fraudsters feed the right face into identity checks through virtual cameras
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 05:00:34 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/18/entrust-document-verification-fraud-report/

[TAM METİN - 579 kelime]
Fraudsters feed the right face into identity checks through virtual cameras

Document fraud remains the largest attack category in Entrust’s The Professionalization of Fraud: 2027 Identity Fraud Report, with criminals combining physical counterfeits, digital manipulation and impersonation to target online services.

The document fraud rate fell 19% year over year. Attacks classified as medium sophistication accounted for nearly 39% of document fraud, involving alterations and counterfeits that require closer inspection to detect. Physical counterfeits made up 61.1% of fraudulent document submissions.

Attack sophistication (Source: Entrust)

“Fraudsters are investing in better templates, stolen credentials, and specialized skills to make attacks more convincing. AI is accelerating this shift, helping fraudsters scale operations and target multiple points across the identity lifecycle,” said Simon Horswell, Senior Fraud Specialist Manager at Entrust.

Deepfakes target identity checks

Deepfake detections rose 165% year over year. Criminals use AI-generated IDs, photos and videos to impersonate customers during verification. These attacks can be personalized, automated and repeated across services, increasing the reach of an operation.

Creating the image or video is a stage in the attack. The content must enter the verification process and pass security checks before criminals can use the resulting access to commit fraud.

Financial services attract these attempts because verified accounts provide access to funds and can support money laundering, money mule activity and subsequent fraud. An account opened using a fraudulent identity can become a resource criminals reuse.

Injection attacks deliver the impersonation

Injection attacks feed images or video into verification software, bypassing the device’s camera. A virtual camera can present fabricated footage as a live recording, while device emulation software imitates the phone or computer submitting it.

Stolen photographs and videos can enter the same process. Genuine media gives criminals material they can replay without creating a synthetic identity. A photograph may be authentic even when the person submitting it has no right to use it.

Device intelligence examines the camera, device and session involved in a submission. The fraud rate measured through device intelligence rose 42% year over year.

Social engineering turns victims into participants

Social engineering exploits trust. Attackers manipulate customers or employees into approving transactions, disclosing information or completing identity checks.

A customer following a scammer’s instructions can complete verification with genuine documents and biometric information. An employee can authorize actions that expose an organization to fraud.

Criminal groups specialize in different stages of an operation, coordinating the people, tools and information needed to carry it out. This division of labor helps them repeat attacks and target several points in a customer relationship.

Europe recorded the largest regional share of deepfake attacks in the company’s dataset, with detections growing 462% year over year. Physical counterfeits accounted for 64.2% of document fraud, and digital forgeries represented 26.4%. Digital forgeries led in Italy, physical counterfeits dominated in Spain, and medium-sophistication attacks outnumbered easy attempts in the UK.

The Americas recorded the highest document fraud rate and the fastest growth in deepfake attacks. Driver’s licenses were the most targeted credentials across the region, including licenses from Texas, California and Florida. National identity cards dominated fraudulent submissions in Latin America, where Chile’s national ID was the most frequently targeted individual credential.

Asia-Pacific had the highest proportion of digital forgeries and the second-largest share of deepfake activity. Criminals often altered document images submitted through online and mobile registration processes. Most document fraud attempts were classified as easy, with medium-sophistication attacks increasing. Social engineering had the lowest recorded rate of the regions studied.

identity verification

[79] Help Net Security - Envoy Visitors Critical turns visitor policies into approval workflows
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:28:49 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/17/envoy-visitors-critical/

[TAM METİN - 943 kelime]
Envoy Visitors Critical turns visitor policies into approval workflows

Envoy today launched Visitors Critical, an infrastructure-grade visitor security offering that brings identity verification, screening and approval workflows together on a shared security architecture that keeps the record protected and audit-ready.

It’s built for high-consequence environments, where the impact of a security event is unacceptable and one-size-fits-all visitor workflows can leave gaps in security posture. Visitors Critical closes those gaps with the highest level of control, assurance and governance across every visitor scenario.

It is the only solution in the market that does all of this in one governed system that replaces the patchwork of tools, spreadsheets and manual checks most sites run today. Visitors Critical proves each visitor’s identity with identity assurance, screens that identity against visitor risk, routes the decision through the right approvers, informs access at the door or zone, and evidences all of it afterward.

When controls fail, the cost is rarely recoverable. For compliance-driven organizations, a wrong entry is a reportable violation that can put penalties, debarment or a facility clearance at risk. Liability can fall on the person who signed the authorization, and ITAR settlements alone have reached $51 million. For security-driven organizations, it’s a loss that can’t be undone.

Cargo theft costs an estimated $15 billion to $35 billion a year in the U.S. alone, according to Homeland Security Investigations, and insider risks cost organizations an average of $19.5 million a year, according to the Ponemon Institute.

Most organizations try to close those gaps by adding friction: more forms, more handoffs, more people between a visitor and the door. Visitors Critical takes the opposite approach. It gives these sites the highest level of control without making the process harder for anyone to follow.

“We’ve spent the last 13 years building Envoy to deliver the highest levels of security, safety and compliance in a way that feels natural to the people using it,” said Larry Gadea, CEO of Envoy. “Visitors Critical brings that to regulated and high-consequence industries, where every step carries more weight, without compromising on simplicity. Security only works when everybody takes part, and Critical’s advanced capabilities make that possible at a level these facilities have never had before.”

Critical is a visitor approval engine

Visitors Critical runs the full decision chain end to end. Envoy scans each visitor’s government-issued ID, matches the name to the invitation and the face to the ID photo with a liveness check, and checks both name and document number against the organization’s blocklist.

At the most sensitive sites, supported hardware readers can also authenticate the document itself, catching forged or altered IDs. Visitors are screened against denied-party and export-control lists through Visual Compliance and Descartes MK, and contractor qualifications are confirmed through ISN. Critical also strengthens and consolidates with IT and security teams’ existing tools and tech stack, bringing higher security to their visitor approval workflows and access control.

Critical turns an organization’s written visitor policies into workflows that enforce it. Visits route by visitor type, nationality or destination; sequential chains require compliance to sign off before security; only designated approvers can make the decision; and the approver’s reasoning is recorded alongside it.

Because the authorization, the screening result, and the approver’s identity and reasoning are all captured in the course of the work itself, an audit becomes an export rather than three weeks of assembling calendars and vendor portals.

Built for secure and regulated environments

Visitors Critical is built for organizations where a single wrong entry can cause irreversible harm, from defense contractors and semiconductor fabs to the data centers powering AI. Some adopt it to meet a specific obligation, such as ITAR, CMMC or export-control requirements. Others are CISOs extending the identity standards they already hold for their networks to the people entering their lobbies.

Attalon, an aerospace and defense supplier, built Envoy into its baseline security program. Every U.S. site, from its largest campuses to its smallest offices, follows the same visitor process and stays audit-ready at any time.

“In aerospace and defense, the lobby is the face of the company and the front line of the security perimeter. If you can’t secure the lobby, you can’t secure the rest of the site,” said Tim Carr, Director of Security & EHS at Attalon.

“Our lasers, optics and coatings support some of the industry’s most sensitive programs, so every visitor is a security decision. That’s why visitor management is one of the five pillars of our security standard, alongside access control, intrusion detection, CCTV and badging, and Envoy is how we deliver it. At all 12 of our sites, Envoy scans every visitor’s ID, helps us verify identity and citizenship status, and screens each visitor against denied-party and block lists. Because Envoy gives us one consistent visitor process everywhere, an auditor who inspects one site has effectively inspected them all, and we can pull visitor records in seconds, not days. Visitors Critical builds on that foundation by turning visitor policy into approval workflows, so the right people sign off before anyone arrives, and we can prove it afterward.”

The launch comes as new Envoy research of 1,000 security leaders finds that the organizations most exposed to a wrong entry are often the least able to prove who got in. Among enterprise leaders, 62% are very confident they’d pass a physical-security audit today without preparation, yet 63% of that group had a security incident in the past year.

At sensitive workplaces, 44% of leaders can’t confidently say who was in their building yesterday. Most sites still run visitor security across email, spreadsheets and multiple systems, which makes proof hard to produce. Visitors Critical is built to close that gap.

[80] Help Net Security - Linx Security extends identity governance to AI agent actions
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:23:26 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/17/linx-security-agent-identity-control/

[TAM METİN - 655 kelime]
Linx Security extends identity governance to AI agent actions

Linx Security has announced four new capabilities that bring every agent under identity control, managed or unmanaged, in any gateway. Shadow AI Agent Detection finds the agents no one sanctioned. Agent Runtime Control decides every agent action in the gateway organizations already run.

And for identity teams, Autopilots for any use case put governed agents to work, a first among major identity vendors, while Prompt-to-Workflow turns a plain-English description into a tested, agentic workflow. These capabilities extend identity governance beyond managing access to controlling the actions AI agents take across the enterprise.

Agent security starts with identity

Agents are created, get access and act at machine speed, while governance still runs on human time, through tickets, approvals and periodic reviews. Agents also break the controls built for people: they are ephemeral, spawned, renamed, modified and reactivated with little trace; they run without a script, so what they do next is hard to predict; and they act autonomously across company tools and data.

Many are shadow AI, running on personal accounts, in pipelines and in AI apps outside sanctioned platforms, with no owner and no review. Gartner predicts that through 2028, over 50% of enterprise AI initiatives will halt due to unresolved agentic identity challenges.

Rather than treating AI agents as a separate security problem, Linx extends its existing identity governance architecture to every human, NHI and AI agent identity. Closing this gap takes more than discovering agents or monitoring their activity. It requires understanding each agentʼs identity, permissions, relationships and actions in context, and Linx applies that context in three steps.

Graph it: put every agent, sanctioned or not, on one Identity Graph alongside every human and NHI, with who owns it and whose access it carries. Decide with context: use that graph to weigh risk and set policy for every agent tool call and every access change. And control the action: enforce those decisions in whatever gateway agents run through, and give identity teams agents and agentic workflows of their own to carry out the work. Linx customers already govern more than 25,000 AI agent identities in production, and governed agent identities grew 450x in six months.

Shadow AI Agent Detection. Linx already inventories agents on the platforms where they’re built. Shadow AI Agent Detection extends that to AI running outside them, from endpoints and CI/CD pipelines to SaaS and cloud. Each finding lands on the Linx Identity Graph with its owner, how it could be exploited and how to fix it, so security teams can govern shadow agents like any other identity.

Agent Runtime Control. Organizations can now bring their own gateway: Linx integrates with the AI gateway they already run and acts as its decision point, through the AuthZEN open standard. Organizations without one can use the Linx MCP Gateway. Every agent tool call is allowed or blocked based on who is behind it, its risk and what it is trying to do, without replacing existing infrastructure.

Autopilots for any use case. Identity teams can now add mission-specific team members. They give an agent a mission in plain language, set its scope, and choose which actions it takes on its own, which need human approval and which it never takes. Each Autopilot works from everything Linx knows about the organization’s identities and access, and teams review its plan and dry-run it before turning it on.

Prompt-to-Workflow. Teams describe a workflow in plain English, such as deactivating an agent when its owner leaves. Linx builds each step, shows the code behind it and dry-runs it before it goes live.

“AI agents are fundamentally changing the identity security problem,” said Israel Duanis, CEO of Linx Security. “Enterprises need to know not just who has access, but what every identity is connected to, what it’s doing and why. That context is the foundation we’ve built at Linx, and we’re now extending it to every AI agent.”

[81] Help Net Security - Detectify launches Agentic Tooling to verify vulnerabilities in live applications
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 14:13:55 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/17/detectify-agentic-tooling/

[TAM METİN - 689 kelime]
Detectify launches Agentic Tooling to verify vulnerabilities in live applications

Detectify has launched Agentic Tooling. It lets organizations’ own AI agents, running in their own environments, proactively investigate suspected vulnerabilities and test whether they can be exploited in running applications. A new Security Ledger lets security teams verify agents’ findings against objective evidence, ensuring informed decisions rather than relying solely on probabilistic conclusions.

The tools address a growing gap in DevSecOps. Engineering teams have built software factories that move code from commit to production in minutes. AI now writes a growing share of that code, and vulnerabilities move through the same pipelines at the same speed, which turns those software factories into vulnerability factories.

Detectify customers report that roughly 80% of the findings from their AI-driven static application security testing (SAST) tools are noise, showing that handing work to AI agents helps only if you can trust their output. Agents reason well, but they cut corners to deliver an answer and can produce a convincing report about a test they never ran.

Built for DevSecOps teams, Agentic Tooling gives agents access to the testing engines Detectify has built over more than a decade, including payload assessments sourced from its ethical hacker community, Alfred AI, and its Surface Monitoring data. The tools run in customers’ own environments through MCP and a CLI, use customers’ own agents and models, and operate only on the data customers provide.

Agentic Tooling is built for loop engineering, the emerging practice of running AI agents in automated, repeating workflows instead of prompting them by hand at every step. A CI run or scheduled job starts the loop. Findings from code scans, bug bounty reports, red teams, and Detectify’s attack surface data each become a ticket.

An agent picks up the ticket, reads the relevant code, and uses Detectify’s tools to test the live application from the outside, as an attacker would, to confirm whether the issue can be exploited. Each pass takes one finding from trigger to proof, then moves on to the next. Run continuously, the loop becomes what Detectify calls a verification factory: the counterpart to the software factory, able to keep pace with the code it ships.

Detectify’s Security Ledger makes all of that work verifiable. Each time an agent uses Detectify’s tools, the ledger records the testing session, including the requests sent, payloads used, and results returned, and links the evidence back to the original ticket. Engineers can see what the agent did, what it concluded, and the proof supporting its assessment in a single timeline.

Because the ledger is written by Detectify’s tools rather than the agent, and is append-only, teams can distinguish findings backed by actual tests from unsupported AI-generated conclusions. This allows organizations to scale security investigations without losing visibility into how decisions were reached.

“Ask an agent to write your code, then ask the same agent whether it’s secure. If it says yes, you don’t actually know. There are plenty of examples of agents fabricating proof because they want to succeed and please the person asking,” said Rickard Carlsson, CEO of Detectify. “Code review used to mean a colleague reading a page or two. Now it’s 50 pages that nobody can get through, and it goes to production anyway. We’re building proof that an agent can’t fabricate, so teams can ship at that speed and know what was actually tested.”

The launch comes as security teams are already struggling to fix known vulnerabilities. Detectify’s H2 2026 Cyber Hygiene Index found that nine in ten open critical and high-severity vulnerabilities had stayed exposed for more than 90 days. Meanwhile, security teams face a growing volume of alerts that demand investigation. A 2026 study by researchers at Fudan University and Tencent found that 76% of the static-analysis alerts examined were false positives, and developers spent 10 to 20 minutes reviewing each.

Agentic Tooling builds on the Detectify MCP Server, released in May 2026, which gave AI agents access to Detectify’s security findings and scanning capabilities. Detectify plans to feed the loop with data from across its platform. For example, a newly opened port could automatically trigger an agent investigation.

[82] The Record - Website management platform Optimizely confirms hackers put malicious code into customer sites
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 21:20:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/optimizely-malicious-code-customer-sites

[TAM METİN - 577 kelime]
Website management platform Optimizely confirms hackers put malicious code into customer sites

A breach at a popular website management company exposed customers’ visitors to malicious code that stole cryptocurrency and attempted to skim payment cards or install malware.

Researchers at cybersecurity firm Report URI said they found malicious JavaScript code on 178 different widely visited websites run through the Optimizely Web experimentation platform. Optimizely allows companies to personalize and manage their websites and apps.

Optimizely confirmed the incident in a brief statement on Monday evening, writing that it “identified and contained a security incident affecting some Optimizely Web Experimentation customers.”

“The vulnerable path is blocked, the injected code was removed, and customers have been contacted directly,” the company said. It did not respond to requests for comment about the specifics of Report URI’s study.

In a technical advisory, Optimizely confirmed Report URI’s findings that a vulnerability in its Experimentation platform allowed threat actors to inject malicious JavaScript into some customers’ websites.

Report URI listed dozens of prominent companies impacted by the breach and used blockchain evidence to track at least $7,000 stolen through the malicious code.

Most of the malicious code has now been removed, but Helme urged companies that use Optimizely to check their websites. Helme said the infected Optimizely files have all been cleaned since they reported the incident.

The malicious code was installed on 32 finance or banking websites and several platforms for technology, news, airline and retail companies.

Helme noted that they first saw the injected code on Turkish websites in September before the threat actor conducted its latest campaign on Monday.

Experts at cybersecurity firm Hudson Rock confirmed Report URI’s findings. Last week, threat hunting firm iVerify warned that cybercriminals are increasingly infecting devices on a large scale with spyware — with about 75% of detections involving criminal actors deploying Coruna or DarkSword exploit kits.

DarkSword allows users to be infected without clicking on a link. Simply visiting a compromised website through a web browser can trigger an attack. Coruna is also delivered via web-based attacks, where people visiting malicious sites trigger WebKit and kernel vulnerabilities.

Research from InfoStealers also found that poisoned third-party JavaScript has been actively routing iOS visitors into Coruna and DarkSword exploit-kit infrastructure.

Collin Hogue-Spears, a cybersecurity expert at Black Duck, said companies typically use the affected Optimizely tool so that teams can change a live website without a code release. The attacker's loader went into Project JavaScript, the field where a customer adds custom code to Optimizely.

“The Optimizely skimmer reads forms the affected sites built themselves; a payment processor's hosted iframe sits outside its reach, so security teams at affected companies must reconstruct which pages loaded the infected Optimizely snippet, for how long, and which card payments those pages took in that time period,” he said.

“If the Optimizely skimmer ran unblocked on a page with your own card form, count as exposed every card entered there while the skimmer was live.”

The incident resembles a recent incident impacting movie ticket site Fandango, which notified state regulators that payment card numbers, CVV numbers, expiration dates and more were stolen when hackers put malicious code into the sites' ticketing checkout page.

[83] The Record - Kazakh ransomware hacking suspect extradited from Italy
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 19:09:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/kazakh-suspected-extradited-charges-8-digits-team-inc-lynx

[TAM METİN - 401 kelime]
Kazakh ransomware hacking suspect extradited from Italy

An affiliate of the INC and Lynx ransomware strains appeared in a Tennessee court on Monday after being extradited from Italy.

Roman Khlynovskiy, a 43-year-old from Kazakhstan, is accused of launching ransomware attacks against U.S. schools, hospitals and companies.

Khlynovskiy is facing a maximum penalty of 27 years in prison on computer fraud, money laundering, wire fraud, extortion and identity theft charges.

Prosecutors accused him of being a member of a cybercriminal group called the “8-Digits Team” that launched ransomware attacks on behalf of the larger INC and Lynx ransomware gangs.

The team’s cyber intrusions led to $500 million in ransom demands and they claimed to have stolen 50 terabytes of data consisting of more than 70 million confidential health records.

FBI director Kash Patel said U.S. agencies worked with Italian law enforcement to extradite Khlynovskiy.

“The defendant is charged with participating in one of the largest healthcare data breaches in decades,” said Assistant Attorney General A. Tysen Duva. “The indictment alleges a conspiracy that targeted hospitals and healthcare providers, stole vast quantities of sensitive patient information, and then sought to profit by threatening some of the most vulnerable members of our society. These alleged crimes exploited the deeply personal medical information of millions of people.”

Khlynovskiy made his first federal court appearance in Knoxville, Tennessee, on Monday.

Members of the 8-Digits Team attacked companies in at least six states, often targeting schools and hospitals. The hackers breached networks, stole data and extorted victims, demanding payments in exchange for not publishing the data.

The INC ransomware gang took credit for a 2024 ransomware attack on prominent Michigan healthcare system McLaren Health Care — which was forced to cancel dozens of surgeries and operate without critical digital systems for days.

The group also caused chaos last year with an attack on the Dutch conglomerate behind several major U.S. supermarket brands.

The INC gang emerged in 2023 and attacked the State Bar of Texas, Yamaha Motor and printer manufacturer Xerox.

According to Palo Alto Networks and Group-IB, INC either rebranded to Lynx or Lynx actors purchased the group’s source code.

is a Breaking News Reporter at Recorded Future News. Jonathan has worked across the globe as a journalist since 2014. Before moving back to New York City, he worked for news outlets in South Africa, Jordan and Cambodia. He previously covered cybersecurity at ZDNet and TechRepublic.

[84] The Record - Chinese state group developing automated attack tool for cyber espionage, Google says
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 18:50:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/chinese-state-group-developing-automated-attack-tool-espionage

[TAM METİN - 815 kelime]
Chinese state group developing automated attack tool for cyber espionage, Google says

Both criminal and state-backed groups in China are leveraging AI tools for cyber espionage, vulnerability research and social media influence operations.

Google released its latest AI Threat Tracker report on Monday, writing that threat actors are rapidly evolving their use of artificial intelligence in attacks — now leaning more heavily on autonomous multi-agent offensive frameworks like Artex, CAIRN, CyberStrikeAI and PentAGI to execute multi-stage intrusions at greater operational velocity.

While multiple countries — including Russia, Iran and North Korea — are using AI for nefarious purposes, few countries have matched China.

Google researchers said they saw Chinese state-backed and financially motivated actors using AI tools for “reconnaissance, front-end scanning, researching targets across various sectors and geographies, and weaponizing exploits.”

Incident responders saw Chinese contractors using large language models to translate and comb through tranches of stolen data that were eventually organized and sold to customers. Other Chinese actors are using AI platforms’ file-hosting infrastructure to stage archives of phishing lures and exfiltrate stolen data.

Several different Chinese actors are also experimenting heavily with Artex — which was spotlighted on Friday by the FBI in an alert that drew alarm among cybersecurity experts. The tool was created by a Chinese researcher and allows any user to turn a large language model into a powerful hacking tool that can “automate multiple phases of cyber intrusions at machine speed, from reconnaissance through data exfiltration,” according to the FBI.

Google said it found evidence that Chinese government groups are now developing their own version of Artex for “likely use in cyber espionage operations.”

While Artex has garnered the most media coverage, Google said they have seen similar tools used in real attacks or tested by threat actors in private — including CAIRN, PentAGI, CyberStrikeAI and HexStrike AI.

CAIRN allows AI agents to collaborate on breaching targets, often starting with a target’s IP address and ending with an operational objective. Google saw CAIRN used in intrusions of companies in the U.S. aerospace sector.

Some threat actors used PentAGI to conduct “systematic reconnaissance and vulnerability scanning against a wide range of organizations in Asia,” the researchers added.

Vulnerability research and disinformation

Google said multiple countries and vendors now routinely employ large language models to research new bugs, create fake content, manipulate SEO and generate influence content.

The report cites one instance of Iranian government-backed actor APT42 using several large language models, including Google’s Gemini, to conduct targeted reconnaissance and social engineering attacks.

North Korea has continued to deploy AI to support its IT worker campaign, using it to create resumes and supplement knowledge during job interviews.

The researchers added that recent intrusions into cryptocurrency organizations in North America and Europe saw North Korean hackers use AI to create fake recruiter profiles on professional networking profiles.

The fictitious personas then conducted ClickFix attacks — where hackers force victims to take actions on their device to fix problems that are not real. The tactic has been used widely by North Korean actors in multiple attacks on cryptocurrency companies and was recently spotlighted as part of a long-running campaign recently disrupted by the FBI.

Google found another version of the scheme, with North Korean actors conducting ClickFix attacks and breaching a victim’s device, allowing them to pivot into internal code repositories to install code directly into the large language models the companies use.

The hackers modified code so that when users started up Claude Code, it would automatically trigger the download of a backdoor researchers call SOMBERMEME.

Threat actors are also using LLMs to automate labor- and time-intensive phases of vulnerability analysis, allowing them to quickly analyze patches to find bugs — dramatically reducing the window between patch disclosure and exploitation.

Google said zero-day exploits rose significantly this year, going from eight per month in 2025 to 22 in the month of August.

As an example, Google cited two vulnerabilities that emerged this summer affecting PaperCut print management servers. Researchers were able to generate working exploits for the bug within a day of their disclosure using LLMs.

Researchers noted in their report that while AI tools have compressed attack timelines and allowed some threat actors to move at “machine speed,” they do generate significant forensic traffic and rely on recognizable identifiers, leaving behind distinct code-level traces.

“While these artifacts increase the likelihood of detection and mitigation, these tools remain highly capable of achieving operational objectives,” Google said. “As the sophistication of these tools and underlying LLMs increases, we anticipate not only reduced human involvement in these operations but also increased stealth and sophistication of the activity.”

[85] The Record - California fines Hertz $1.5 million for breaking state data privacy law
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 17:13:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/california-fines-hertz-state-data-privacy-law

[TAM METİN - 334 kelime]
California fines Hertz $1.5 million for breaking state data privacy law

California’s data privacy regulator on Tuesday fined rental car giant Hertz $1.55 million for making it difficult for customers to protect their data and failing to honor consumers’ requests to opt out of having their information shared.

Hertz agreed to the fine and said it would change its business practices, according to a court filing shared by the regulator, the California Privacy Protection Agency (CPPA).

The car rental company allegedly broke state law by not processing opt-out preference signals, which are automated digital signals consumers send from their devices or browsers to notify websites not to sell or share their data.

Hertz, which also owns the Dollar and Thrifty brands, allegedly did not tell consumers how it processed the signals and added “friction” to the opt-out process, according to a CPPA press release.

A spokesperson for Hertz did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Hertz has agreed to quarterly testing and monitoring practices to ensure they are submitting the correct information to CPPA, reviewing all new tracking technologies and testing opt-out compliance, the press release said.

“Businesses have been on notice for years that we expect them to listen when Californians

say no to trackers,” Lara Kehoe Hoffman, Acting Deputy Director of Enforcement for

CalPrivacy, said in a statement.

CPPA also announced a separate settlement with outdoor clothing and accessories manufacturer CC Filson Co. on Tuesday. The company must pay a $218,000 fine and agree to stop all device-based sales and sharing of consumers’ personal information to advertising technology companies within 30 days.

A spokesperson for CC Filson Co. did not immediately respond to a request for comment.
