[86] The Record - NSA Net, agency’s classified internal network, faces unspecified disruptions
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 16:21:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/nsa-net-disruptions

[TAM METİN - 526 kelime]
NSA Net, agency’s classified internal network, faces unspecified disruptions

Editor's Note: Story updated 2:30 Eastern U.S. time with statement from the NSA.

The National Security Agency’s classified internal communications network is experiencing what is believed to be a technical issue, resulting in a delayed opening and staggered employee arrivals.

The difficulties with the system, known as NSA Net, are not believed to be the result of a cyberattack, according to two people familiar with the ongoing situation. CNN first reported the development.

NSA “experienced temporary degradation during scheduled routine maintenance. While some internal systems were affected, mission-critical capabilities remain fully operational and our ability to defend national security continues,” an agency spokesman said in a statement.

The organization’s “infrastructure is designed with multiple layers of redundancy to maintain operations during planned maintenance or unexpected events."

There was a staggered arrival at agency headquarters, located at Fort Meade, Maryland, specifically because officials want to reduce the load of tens of thousands of personnel signing in the morning at the same time on the same network, one source told Recorded Future News.

This same person noted that the system is not completely shut down, but is only providing intermittent service.

Another person said NSA Net has been unstable for weeks, prompting frustration among employees.

NSA backup centers located around the country are expected to pick up the slack caused by the delay and conduct the agency’s chief mission of collecting signals intelligence from around the globe.

The disturbance at the secretive spy agency is the latest in a series of high-profile technical disruptions that have impacted the United States in recent days — though there is no evidence that they are linked.

Last week there was a day-long, global outage that crippled software produced by tech giant Microsoft, including Outlook. On Monday, a senior company official revealed a months-old bug was responsible for the blackout, which lasted for 24 hours and lingered through the weekend.

On Friday, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) issued a nationwide ground stop that halted all U.S. departing flights for more than four hours. The agency has since traced the issue to a single router in Arkansas.

Still, the FBI on Tuesday said its cybersecurity teams are working with the FAA, the Transportation Department and contractor L3Harris “providing technical assistance following Friday’s air traffic communications outage.”

The NSA has experienced glitches in the past, including in 2013 when its public website went dark. Though some hackers claimed they had crashed the site with a denial of service attack, officials said it was the result of a scheduled update.

In 2000, the agency’s computers went dark for three days. The issues were eventually attributed to a software anomaly and infrastructure problems. Recovery required thousands of man-hours and the agency reportedly spent $1.5 million to restore its IT infrastructure.

He previously was a reporter at The Hill, National Journal Group and Inside Washington Publishers.

[87] NCSC UK - Why threat hunting should matter to every board
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 12:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.ncsc.gov.uk/blogs/why-threat-hunting-should-matter-to-every-board

[TAM METİN - 1463 kelime]
Why threat hunting should matter to every board

Monitoring alone cannot detect every threat. Proactive threat hunting can expose gaps in defences and help organisations learn from others facing common threats.

J Studios via Getty Images

Boards should have confidence that their organisation can detect and investigate emerging cyber threats, including activity that existing security alerts may miss.

Organisations use a range of cyber security controls. They use security products, receive threat intelligence and monitor alerts from trusted providers. Many have well-developed processes for responding to known threats. But these measures can’t tell you everything about your ability to detect an attack.

When a new attacker technique is identified, ask: could we detect this technique if it was used against us?

If your organisation can’t answer that question, it may have gaps in its visibility or detection capability.

As attackers use AI to operate at greater speed and scale, identifying and addressing these gaps becomes increasingly important. Organisations need to be able to identify signs of malicious activity early, rather than relying solely on detecting an attack once its effects become apparent.

Threat hunting can help organisations test their ability to detect relevant attacker activity and expose gaps in their defences.

This blog is for boards of organisations that monitor their systems for cyber threats, either themselves or through a third-party provider. It explains why threat hunting matters as threats evolve, and how boards can support their organisation to learn from what it finds and turn those insights into stronger cyber defences.

Monitoring cannot detect every threat

Security monitoring is fundamental to effective cyber defence. It helps organisations identify potentially malicious activity using established detections and alerts. But not every threat will trigger an existing alert. This is where threat hunting can help.

Threat hunting is the proactive search for signs of cyber threats or intrusion that existing security rules and alerts may have missed. A hunt starts with a question or hypothesis, based on factors such as attacker behaviour or threat intelligence, and uses available security data to look for evidence. Threat hunting complements security monitoring; it doesn’t replace it.

Focus on attacker behaviour

Cyber defenders have traditionally made extensive use of indicators of compromise (IoCs), such as malicious IP addresses, domains, file hashes, or malware signatures. These are useful but they can change quickly.

Attacker behaviours can be more enduring. Security professionals describe these behaviours as tactics, techniques and procedures (TTPs). Put simply, TTPs describe how an attacker operates, rather than what specific tools or infrastructure they use.

An IP address may change overnight. A technique for moving through a network may remain relevant for much longer. Focusing on attacker behaviour can therefore provide a more durable basis for detection.

Threat hunting allows defenders to use knowledge of these behaviours to ask whether they can see evidence of them in their own environment.

For more detail on how threat hunting works in practice, including the use of attacker TTPs, read our blog on observability and threat hunting.

You can’t hunt what you can’t see

Threat hunting depends on observability: having a clear and detailed view of what is happening across your organisation’s networks, systems and services.

To investigate whether a particular attacker technique could be present, defenders need the right security data. Systems need to record relevant activity, and defenders need access to that data. Without it, they may be unable to see evidence that a technique has been used.

This means a threat hunt can reveal more than malicious activity. It can also identify gaps in an organisation's visibility and detection capability. For example, a hunt may show that defenders don’t have the data they need to determine whether a particular attacker technique has been used.

For boards, that is valuable information. Even when a hunt finds no evidence of malicious activity, it can reveal where the organisation needs better visibility or stronger detection capabilities.

Those findings should feed back into the organisation’s defences, for example by improving the security data available to defenders, strengthening detections, or addressing other weaknesses identified by the hunt.

What should boards be asking?

Responsibility for cyber defence is often spread across different teams, including security operations, infrastructure, technology, risk and governance functions. Boards need assurance that those teams can turn information about emerging threats into action, and that gaps in visibility or detection are identified, owned and addressed.

Boards don't need to understand the technical details of threat hunting or conduct hunts themselves. They should ask questions that test whether the organisation has an effective capability:

Which attacker techniques are most relevant to our organisation?

Which attacker techniques are most relevant to our organisation?

Can our defenders detect or investigate those techniques in our environment?

Can our defenders detect or investigate those techniques in our environment?

Do our defenders have the security data and visibility they need to investigate them?

Do our defenders have the security data and visibility they need to investigate them?

When they identify gaps in visibility or detection, who is responsible for addressing them?

When they identify gaps in visibility or detection, who is responsible for addressing them?

How do we know whether those gaps are being addressed and our ability to detect threats is improving?

How do we know whether those gaps are being addressed and our ability to detect threats is improving?

Are our defenders learning from and collaborating with other organisations facing similar threats?

Are our defenders learning from and collaborating with other organisations facing similar threats?

Boards should look for evidence, rather than relying on reassurance alone. This could include:

examples of relevant attacker techniques the organisation has tested its visibility and detection capability against

examples of relevant attacker techniques the organisation has tested its visibility and detection capability against

gaps those investigations have identified

gaps those investigations have identified

what has been done to address them

what has been done to address them

Over time, boards should be able to see whether those gaps are being closed and the organisation’s ability to detect relevant threats is improving.

Build on the capability you already have

Effective threat hunting doesn’t necessarily require a new threat-hunting team. Organisations may already have people, data and security functions that can provide much of the capability they need.

A useful starting point is to understand what already exists and where the gaps are. For example:

do defenders have the security data they need?

can they use that data to investigate attacker techniques across the organisation's systems?

is there a clear process for acting on gaps that hunts identify?

Organisations should also consider how they use the threat intelligence they receive. Threat intelligence is most valuable when defenders can act on it, for example by turning knowledge of relevant attacker behaviour into questions they can test against their own systems.

Where skills or capability are limited, organisations should consider how existing security providers and trusted practitioner communities can support them, rather than assuming they need to build a new team.

Learn from other defenders

Organisations often face common adversaries, vulnerabilities and technologies. It therefore makes little sense for every organisation to solve every detection problem independently.

Sharing knowledge of attacker behaviour, relevant threat intelligence and approaches to threat hunting can help defenders build a broader picture of common threats, learn from each other and tackle security problems they have in common.

The NCSC encourages organisations to engage with defender communities, where they can exchange insights, discuss emerging threats and learn from real-world incidents.

What one organisation learns from investigating an attacker technique may also help others facing the same threat. Sharing these insights can improve the ability of individual organisations and the wider community to detect and respond to malicious activity.

Organisations can also learn from other defenders through sector-based Information Sharing and Analysis Centres (ISACs) and industry forums. Commercial threat intelligence providers can be another source of information about relevant threats.

Focus on capability, not the solution

For boards, the question is not whether to commission a particular threat-hunting product or create a new team. It is whether the organisation can investigate relevant attacker behaviour, identify gaps in its ability to detect threats, and act on what it learns.

Ask your defenders how they know they could detect the attacker techniques most relevant to your organisation, what gaps they have found, and what they are doing about them.

Cyber security professionals

[88] Microsoft Security - Security for the agentic era, powered by Microsoft Perception
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 18:14:00 +0000
Link: https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/11/17/security-for-the-agentic-era-powered-by-microsoft-perception/

[TAM METİN - 2365 kelime]
A new cyber stack, built to fight AI with AI

Delivering agentic security at Microsoft Ignite

Secure your foundations

Secure agentic insiders

Changing how security work gets done

A year ago, finding a serious, exploitable vulnerability was slow and expensive work done by elite security researchers. That’s no longer true. Over the past 12 months, frontier AI models have become remarkably good at discovering and exploiting software vulnerabilities, and similar levels of capability are quickly becoming available in open weights models with limited cyber guardrails. Attacks that once required specialized resources are now within reach of almost anyone.

We saw what that looks like in a single week this fall when PHP disclosed two critical remote code execution (RCE) vulnerabilities, flaws that let an attacker take control of a server with a single request over the internet.1 At the time of disclosure, tens of millions of internet-facing PHP services were running affected versions. Nearly two weeks later, about 29 million services remained vulnerable. Behind those servers are banks, healthcare providers, energy companies, and much of the infrastructure the internet runs on. And that same day brought five other major disclosures across the industry.

Turning a flaw like the PHP one into a working attack once took a specialist team months. Within roughly a day of PHP2Shell’s disclosure, security researchers had developed a working, weaponized exploit. By the following week, that research had expanded to cover additional PHP versions and configurations. The time from discovery to exploit is shrinking from months to minutes.

The physics of cybersecurity are changing, and fast.

AI is amplifying risk. Attackers can now find and exploit gaps faster than defenders can close them.

Attacks are becoming fully automated. AI has moved from assisting attackers, to executing their tasks, to planning and running entire campaigns on its own.

Agents are the new insiders. While not inherently malicious, they perform tasks and business workflows at machine speed, around the clock, and have broad access to data, apps, tools, and other agents. Like any insider, they can make mistakes, be manipulated, or be compromised.

What we built for humans does not scale for AI. Frontier Transformation starts with business outcomes and a simple idea: technology was invented to serve humanity, not the other way around. To help organizations bring their ideas to life and innovate safely in the agentic era, we need a new cyber stack, built for agentic security.

A new cyber stack, built to fight AI with AI

The Microsoft cyber stack has three layers:

Signals, sensors, and actuators. This foundational layer is powered by sensors and actuators in Microsoft Defender, Microsoft Entra, Microsoft Intune, Microsoft Purview, Microsoft Sentinel, the network, the operating system, the browser, and our hyperscale cloud. Sensors tell you something is wrong. Actuators let you do something about it: contain a device on the network, revoke an identity’s access, or block an agent at the authorization layer. And because we know we’re not the only security solution provider in your environment, we connect to the wider ecosystem through more than 500 connectors. Together, Microsoft and partner sources produce more than 165 trillion signals a day,2 which, enriched by Microsoft and customer threat intelligence, fuel the context layer.

Context. This layer organizes those signals, together with each customer’s own environment, into agent-ready, real-time understanding of the digital estate that is optimized for precision, recall, and token efficiency.

Agents, harness, and models. On top are specialized security agents that work together as teams through playbooks, directed by a harness, and a multi-model approach that uses the right model for each task, designed to ensure effective and efficient security outcomes, at scale.

Delivering agentic security at Microsoft Ignite

At Microsoft Ignite 2026, we’re delivering innovation across every layer of the stack. It starts with Microsoft Perception, now generally available.

Perception brings the top of the stack together. It orchestrates red, blue, and green team agents through playbooks, working together in a closed loop to deliver security outcomes. Red team agents simulate attacks from an attacker’s perspective before a breach occurs. Blue team agents detect, triage alerts, and investigate attacks, freeing analysts to focus on the critical decisions that matter most. Green team agents fix and remediate.

Perception is built to help security teams move at machine speed, drive outcomes rather than tasks, and automate while staying in control. Your team sets the guardrails and signs off on high-impact actions, and automated triggers launch playbooks when an incident meets the criteria you set. New cost management dashboards and a cost estimator help you track AI usage, forecast spending, and connect costs to measurable security outcomes.

Agents are only as good as what they can see and what they can do. That’s why we are investing as much in the foundation as in the layers above it.

Integrated Security Operations Center (ISOC) in Microsoft Defender, generally available for core capabilities, is part of that foundation. ISOC brings security information and event management (SIEM) capabilities, user and entity behavior analytics (UEBA), threat intelligence, and threat protection and disruption together so you can start your journey to the agentic security operations center (SOC). Eligible Defender Suite, Microsoft 365 E5, and Microsoft 365 E7 customers receive all Microsoft Sentinel SIEM features, 90-day default data retention for Defender data, more than 500 connectors, reduced ingestion rates, and a built-in data lake. The complete breadth of SIEM capabilities is now in preview.

Read on to learn how Microsoft Perception, ISOC in Defender, and new capabilities across the stack work together to help customers secure their foundations, secure agentic insiders, and defend with agents.

Secure your foundations

Defending against AI-speed attacks starts with closing the doors attackers walk through first, and the scale of that work is increasing. In 2026, more than 96,000 vulnerabilities3 have been published to the CVE (Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures) Program, more than double 2024’s roughly 40,000 and more than 20 times the level of 2010,4 driven in part by AI-assisted vulnerability discovery tools.

Yet only 26% of vulnerabilities in the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog were remediated in 2025, as remediation typically extends into weeks and months. AI finds those gaps faster than teams can close them. Today we’re announcing new capabilities to help.

Strengthen your security foundation with Perception. In preview in January 2027, new AI Readiness Initiatives in Perception turn Secure Now guidance into actionable plans with shared progress tracking, helping organizations close critical security gaps, and prepare for AI adoption. Initiatives focus on scenarios that help reduce AI-amplified risk such as modernizing authentication and deploying monthly updates.

Prioritize and reduce exposure with Perception agents and playbooks. Focus on the risks that matter most and coordinate remediation using threat intelligence and business context. Two new playbooks bring together green team agents, including the Posture Prioritization Agent, now generally available, and the Risk Reduction Agent, now in public preview. Coverage spans Microsoft, Amazon Web Services (AWS), and Google Cloud.

Vulnerability shielding, available in preview, helps protect vulnerable assets from exploitation by applying targeted, temporary protection to buy defenders time before they can apply a permanent patch. During routine operations the Risk Reduction Agent proactively suggests shielding when patches aren’t immediately available. During an active attack, vulnerability shielding helps reduce exposure to certain vulnerabilities by applying targeted, temporary protections while organizations prepare immediate remediation. In supported scenarios, these protections can include adjustments to network accessibility and permissions to help limit exposure.

Secure data across humans and agents with Microsoft Purview. Enhanced data classification drives precise protection with less manual effort, generally available in January 2027. Also entering preview in January: grounding protection for Copilot based on sensitive information types, which lets customers adopt Copilot without labeling every file first, and expanded data loss prevention (DLP) controls for prompts to Copilot and local agents running on Windows, which help protect sensitive data from leaking while customers use AI. Learn how Microsoft Purview accelerates data protection for the AI era.

A SOC where people work through a queue of alerts by hand can’t keep up with increasingly autonomous attacks. Perception brings specialized red, blue, and green agents, and playbooks that automate common SecOps workflows, so defenders can get ahead.

Stop threats at machine speed with new Perception playbooks and agents. Accelerate threat detection, investigation, and response with new playbooks and four blue team agents, now generally available: Alert Triage Agent, Attack Investigation Agent, Detection Authoring Agent, and Threat Intelligence Agent. Helping teams move from detection to response faster, with less manual effort.

Stay ahead of attackers with new Perception playbooks and agents. Proactively find and validate weaknesses with playbooks that coordinate red team agents, including the Recon Agent, now generally available, and the Web App Pen Test Agent, now in preview, helping security teams identify and address vulnerabilities before attackers can exploit them.

Use Perception in your flow of work. Teams can now invoke Perception playbooks in Defender, connect through Model Context Protocol (MCP) and APIs to SIEM, security orchestration, automation, and response (SOAR), and IT service management tools, and bring in signals from AWS, Okta, and Salesforce. These integrations are in preview.

We are also expanding the preview of Codename MDASH, our agentic code scanner, which helps customers find and fix vulnerabilities. MDASH can now analyze repositories up to 5 GB, run in regulated clouds, and MDASH code scans can be used by the Web App Pen Test Agent to prioritize and fix vulnerabilities. MDASH adds more of the MAI Cyber family, including MAI Cyber 1.1 Flash and MAI-Cyber-2 in preview,5 improving efficiency while maintaining the quality of security findings. Learn more about MAI-Cyber-2.

Secure agentic insiders

Agents are the new insiders. They operate continuously, collaborate with other agents, change shape, and pursue goals in ways that can surprise the people who deployed them. To mitigate this risk we must discover agents, onboard them to management, secure and contain them, and do it continuously—even as their behavior and context change. Building on our October 2026 announcement, at Ignite we are introducing new capabilities to help customers secure agentic insiders, around the clock.

Continuously contain agentic insiders with new Perception playbooks and agents. Investigate and remediate AI posture risks and anomalous agent behavior with new playbooks and green team agents. Microsoft Entra ID Governance dynamic permissions grant task-scoped, time-bound access, while Conditional Access dynamic controls adjust protections as risk changes. These capabilities remain in limited preview.

Discover, govern, and secure agents with Microsoft Agent 365 and Microsoft Entra Suite capabilities for agentic insiders. Discovery expands by 35 local agent types, to 60 in total, including Claude Code, OpenAI Codex, and GitHub Copilot, plus third-party cloud agents on AWS and Google Cloud. Discovered agents receive a Microsoft Entra Agent ID and can be governed with Conditional Access. Intune policies contain supported local agents on the device. Additionally, Microsoft Autopilot agents get their own Agent user account and ID, so admins can enable Conditional Access, ID Protection, and access governance to the work an Autopilot does under its own identity. Defender and Microsoft Purview Data Loss Prevention protect runtime and sensitive data access, and Dynamic Agent Risk assesses each agent’s exposure, blast radius, and runtime behavior. Available in preview.

While these capabilities can enable customers to secure and contain agents, they will not give them insight into how the model behind an agent behaves. Without that visibility it is hard for customers to answer three basic questions about consequential AI activity:

What was the AI asked and authorized to do?

What happened between the request and the outcome?

Can we independently verify it?

That’s why Microsoft is announcing the next phase of our commitment to responsible AI: the AI Transparency Initiative. We believe every organization should be able to see what its AI accesses and does, independently verify those actions, and control what it is permitted to do, whatever model or provider it runs on. To that end, we are expanding our participation in the National Institute of Standards and Technology’s (NIST) SI Agent Standards Initiative,6 plus drawing on our own research to develop open, industry-wide standards for AI transparency, security, and governance. We’re shaping this approach with more than 40 organizations including Accenture, Adobe, KPMG, Mastercard, and Volvo Cars. And we will implement this work across Microsoft AI models and products, including Microsoft Foundry and Copilot.

The more agency we give AI, the more transparency we should expect in return.

Changing how security work gets done

AI is changing how attacks happen, and how security work gets done. Developers, IT, and security teams now share responsibility for building, managing, and securing AI, so Microsoft Foundry, Microsoft Agent 365, and Perception share the same signals and context. Inside the SOC, analysts need agents to defend at machine speed, which is why we’re building agentic security into the tools they already use.

Technology alone won’t get us there. Securing our own environment through the Secure Future Initiative has taught us a great deal, and our Frontier Security Engineers, services teams, and partners are working side by side with customers to put those lessons to work.

Join us at Microsoft Ignite, online or in person, to learn how Microsoft Perception, ISOC in Defender and new capabilities across the stack can help secure your organization at machine speed. We’d love to see you there.

[89] Microsoft Security - The risks of device code authentication for AI coding agents
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 15:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/11/17/the-risks-of-device-code-authentication-for-ai-coding-agents/

[TAM METİN - 2317 kelime]
From a familiar phishing technique to an AI coding agent

One approval, four ways the impact can spread

Turning a phishing attempt into a Defender signal

Mitigation and protection guidance

Device code phishing has become a recurring route to account takeover. Instead of stealing a password, an attacker persuades someone to approve a sign-in on a legitimate site simply by entering a code. The resulting access often can reach email, files, or other cloud services, depending on the account and the permissions granted. Notable earlier campaigns such as EvilTokens show how readily this technique can be scaled to do real damage. Microsoft worked hard with partners in industry and law enforcement to disrupt EvilTokens in September 2026.

That led us to a different question: what else can an attacker do with a phished device code authorization? What happens when the same trick targets an AI agent rather than a mailbox?

We used GitHub Copilot CLI as an illustrative example. Depending on its configuration, it can work with code, run commands on a user’s machine, retain context across sessions, and allow an active session to be steered remotely. Like many command-line tools, it supports the OAuth device flow for sign-in. The phishing technique itself is not specific to GitHub or Copilot CLI: any public client that supports the device flow can be abused the same way, and the attacker chooses the requested scopes when starting the flow.

We explored this scenario in a controlled lab simulation. A victim test account approved a GitHub device-code request initiated by an attacker client we controlled. With that approval, we were able to analyze four possible outcomes:

Steer an active Copilot CLI session remotely, if the user has enabled remote control and organization policy allows it, and, subject to local permissions and approval settings, have the agent run commands on the user’s machine.

Add misleading entries to the agent’s memory that could influence later interactions.

Read Copilot CLI sessions that were synchronized to the cloud.

Access private repositories available to the affected account.

These are proof-of-concept results showing how existing features, working as designed, could be abused after a phished authorization; they are not attacks we have observed in the wild. These outcomes require the victim user to approve a device code request initiated by the attacker; each outcome also depends on the account permissions and enabled features described below.

Later in this post, we show how Microsoft Defender helps detect these attacks by connecting phishing attempts with GitHub authentication activity, and how organizations can disrupt suspicious access to protect affected identities.

From a familiar phishing technique to an AI coding agent

The OAuth device flow helps clients such as command-line tools sign in without displaying a full browser. A client requests a short code; the user enters it on the service’s verification page; the requesting client receives access after approval. In device code phishing, the attacker starts the request and persuades someone else to approve it.

Microsoft has reported device code phishing against accounts in several campaigns, including Storm-2372, an AI-enabled campaign in 2026, and EvilTokens. Those cases motivated us to examine a different target: a coding agent that can work with repositories and, when remote control is enabled, expose an active session to remote interaction.

The remainder of this post describes a controlled GitHub Copilot CLI simulation; we have not observed the four outcomes below in those campaigns or in the wild.

Initiate: The attacker starts a device-code sign-in request. In our simulation, we used the Copilot CLI client, but any device-flow client could be used.

Lure: The attacker sends the short code and GitHub verification link to the user, for example under the pretext of an IT request or a Copilot license check. The verification page is genuinely hosted by GitHub; the legitimate domain alone does not establish who initiated the request.

Approve: The user opens the GitHub page, signs in if needed, and approves the request. This is the social engineering step.

Sign in: After approval, the attacker-controlled client completes the sign-in automatically.

Operate: The attacker can act within the permissions granted to the application and the resources available to the affected account.

The request behind the code

The following example shows the initial HTTP request to GitHub’s device authorization endpoint. The Copilot CLI client identifier is public; it is not a client secret. The attacker sends the short code returned by GitHub to the user. Once the user approves it, the requesting client signs in without another manual step.

The user signs in on the real GitHub site, not a lookalike page. A familiar domain therefore cannot tell the user whether the request was theirs. An authorization also appears under a legitimate application name; the name alone cannot distinguish the attacker-controlled client from an expected sign-in.

One approval, four ways the impact can spread

The device code approval was only the starting point. What an attacker could do next depended on the account’s permissions, enabled features, memory settings, and whether an active session could be controlled remotely.

1. An attacker can steer an enabled remote session

Copilot CLI supports remote steering of an active interactive session when the user has enabled remote control for that session and organization policy allows it. In our lab, the attacker-controlled client used the test account’s authorization to interact with a remotely controllable session. The agent ran a command on the test machine, subject to the session’s approval prompts and local operating-system permissions. This demonstrates misuse of an authorized remote-session capability working as designed; it is not unauthenticated remote code execution and does not apply when remote control is disabled, the session is not running, or the machine is offline.

2. Memory can carry the attack forward

Copilot Memory can retain repository facts and user preferences. In the simulation, we added misleading entries using the agent’s /memory command. If those entries are used in later interactions, the effects can outlast the initial sign-in. The behavior depends on which memory features and policies are enabled.

3. Synced sessions reveal earlier context

Where cloud session storage is enabled, we could read the test account’s synced Copilot CLI sessions, including sessions from before the simulation. They may contain code snippets, file paths, architecture discussions, and other work context. For Copilot Business and Enterprise, availability depends on the organization’s session-storage policy; syncing a session does not itself enable remote control.

4. Repository access follows the account

In the lab, the authorization let us access private repositories available to the test account. The request included the repo scope along with read:user, read:org, gist, and codespace. These scopes are chosen by the client that starts the flow; the resulting repository access reflects the requested scopes and the account’s permissions, not a property of Copilot CLI itself. The potential exposure is bounded by the account’s repository access, which can include application code and infrastructure definitions.

A phishing message, a click, and a GitHub authorization can each look unremarkable on their own. The challenge for defenders is recognizing when they belong to the same attempted takeover. The legitimate verification page is part of the story, not evidence that the request was safe.

Turning a phishing attempt into a Defender signal

Microsoft Defender helps organizations detect and respond to device code phishing targeting GitHub accounts. With the GitHub Enterprise Cloud connector enabled in Microsoft Defender for Cloud Apps, organizations gain visibility into GitHub activity that can support investigations of suspicious authorization patterns.

As part of our research, we developed two detections for suspected GitHub Copilot CLI device code phishing, covering attacks delivered through email and Microsoft Teams.

These detections correlate suspicious messages and clicks with subsequent GitHub OAuth token creation and regeneration events, helping identify potentially unauthorized Copilot CLI authorizations that might otherwise appear legitimate. Microsoft Defender XDR customers can refer to the list of applicable detections below.

Beyond detection, we also developed a disruption action for GitHub Enterprise Managed Users (EMU). This action suspends the affected managed identity through the organization’s identity provider, interrupting the attacker’s access. We validated this capability in our research environment.

Responders should also review and revoke unexpected OAuth authorizations, verify that access is blocked, and investigate potentially affected sessions, repositories, and saved memory.

Together, these capabilities help security teams move from identifying a potential phishing attempt to containing the affected identity. Detection and disruption availability depend on the supported configuration, required telemetry, identity integration, and deployment prerequisites.

Mitigation and protection guidance

Make the approval rule clear to everyone who uses developer and AI tools: enter a device code only for a sign-in you initiated yourself. An unexpected request to “verify” GitHub or activate Copilot should be treated as a phishing attempt, even if the page is hosted on github.com. GitHub’s own verification page warns users never to use a code sent by someone else.

Configure Safe Links policies in Microsoft Defender for Office 365 for email and verify Safe Links protection for Microsoft Teams. These protections can help with malicious links and provide investigation context; a legitimate GitHub verification URL still requires the user to recognize an unsolicited code request.

Review GitHub Copilot CLI session settings. For organization-provided seats, GitHub’s “Store local sessions in the Cloud” policy determines whether sessions can be stored and remotely controlled. Choose the least permissive setting that supports your workflows. Remote control also requires an active, online interactive session.

Review authorized OAuth applications in GitHub account settings and revoke authorizations that are unexpected or no longer needed. Limit repository access with appropriate organization roles so an affected account cannot reach more code than its work requires.

If Copilot Memory is enabled, use GitHub’s memory-management controls to review and remove suspicious repository facts or user preferences. Disabling memory does not automatically delete entries already stored, so review the saved content during incident response.

Connect GitHub Enterprise Cloud to Defender for Cloud Apps and validate the activity available in your environment before relying on it for investigations.

Remember: A legitimate sign-in can still be the wrong one

The most striking part of this simulation was how little needed to look suspicious. The user visited the real GitHub site and approved a normal-looking request. Yet the approval belonged to a client the user had not started. From there, the consequences depended on what that identity could access and which Copilot CLI capabilities were enabled.

That is why this is more than a story about a code. As AI agents gain access to repositories, session history, and local tools, the impact of a phished authorization increasingly depends on what those agents can reach.

A legitimate verification page does not mean the request is safe. Users should approve only device-code requests they initiated. Organizations should regularly review and revoke unexpected GitHub OAuth authorizations, restrict Copilot session and memory settings to what their workflows require, and validate GitHub activity visibility in Microsoft Defender for Cloud Apps to support investigations.

Ultimately, protecting against device code phishing requires both user awareness and the ability to connect suspicious authorization activity to the phishing attempt that preceded it.

MITRE ATT&CK techniques observed

These mappings describe behaviors demonstrated in the proof-of-concept simulation, not an attributed campaign.

T1566.002 – Phishing: Spearphishing Link (Initial Access). In the tested email delivery, a link led the user to the device authorization page and prompted approval.

T1528 – Steal Application Access Token (Credential Access). After the test account approved the request, the attacker-controlled client obtained an OAuth token.

Authorizing OAuth apps. GitHub Docs. Explains the OAuth device authorization flow, including how users approve requests and how applications obtain access tokens.

Authenticating GitHub Copilot CLI. GitHub Docs. Describes the supported authentication methods for GitHub Copilot CLI.

Remote control of GitHub Copilot CLI sessions. GitHub Docs. Explains how active Copilot CLI sessions can be accessed and controlled remotely, including relevant prerequisites.

GitHub Copilot session data. GitHub Docs. Provides information about session history and cloud synchronization in GitHub Copilot CLI.

Managing Copilot Memory for an enterprise, organization, or repository. GitHub Docs. Describes how administrators can manage memory settings and stored information.

Reviewing your authorized OAuth apps. GitHub Docs. Explains how users can review and revoke OAuth application authorizations.

Repository roles for an organization. GitHub Docs. Describes repository permission levels and how organizations can limit access according to user responsibilities.

Protect your GitHub Enterprise environment. Microsoft Learn. Explains how to connect GitHub Enterprise Cloud to Microsoft Defender for Cloud Apps to gain visibility into GitHub activity and support security investigations.

Configure Safe Links policies in Microsoft Defender for Office 365. Microsoft Learn. Provides configuration guidance for Safe Links protection against malicious URLs.

To get notified about new publications and to join discussions on social media, follow us on LinkedIn, X (formerly Twitter), and Bluesky.

Review our documentation to learn more about our real-time protection capabilities and see how to enable them within your organization.

Evaluate your AI readiness with our latest Zero Trust for AI workshop.

[90] Securelist (Kaspersky) - The forced audit: how AuditTeam’s OpsAgent framework masks ransomware as consulting
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 07:00:07 +0000
Link: https://securelist.com/auditteam-opsagent-forced-audit/122031/

[TAM METİN - 2087 kelime]
AuditTeam is an active threat group that uses the OpsAgent framework to compromise organizations across at least seven countries and territories. The campaign began in January 2026 and remained active through October 2026, at a minimum. In its ransom note, AuditTeam demands an “Audit & Consulting fee” in exchange for vulnerability reports. During this period, the group has infected dozens of organizations in Russia, Italy, the Philippines, Hong Kong, Senegal, Finland and China. The group’s leak site also claims victims in seven additional countries.

OpsAgent provides file system management, process injection, privilege escalation, keylogging, screenshot capture, and network pivoting. During the campaign OpsAgent was transitioned from C++ to Rust while adding new capabilities including interactive shells and WebSocket upgrades. Its command-and-control domains use Amazon CloudFront subdomains, which OpsAgent discovers via a public GitHub repository. Two specialized modules extend its functionality. An antivirus/endpoint detection and response (AV/EDR) killer module installs a digitally signed Beijing Jiangmin Antivirus driver to terminate security products. A cryptor module encrypts files using AES-CBC or ChaCha20 with X25519 key exchange and targets database processes for termination prior to doing so.

On March 10, 2026, we discovered suspicious activity targeting an organization in Hong Kong. The tool used was OpsAgent, a framework designed to resemble a penetration testing utility. Further investigation revealed attacks in additional countries, prompting an active research phase. This implant offers extensive operator capabilities, including host reconnaissance, shellcode injection via named pipes, and lateral movement through agent networks based on compromised hosts.

Analysis indicates OpsAgent was used by AuditTeam. The group was first mentioned publicly by dbdigest.com in March 2026. On June 25, 2026, DeXpose reported that AuditTeam had claimed responsibility for an attack on a Russian organization and listed it on its leak site. AuditTeam leaves a ransom note threatening to release stolen data unless a fee is paid. They call this fee the “Audit & Consulting fee.” According to the note, payment results in a vulnerability report being delivered to the victim instead of leaking the stolen data archive to the public internet. While the note does not mention file encryption, it is left by the cryptor module. Therefore, if a victim sees the note, their files are already encrypted.

GitHub commit history shows the campaign started on January 5, 2026. The leak site lists its first victim on February 21, 2026. Dozens of machines across at least seven countries were infected. On August 19, 2026, a C2 domain for the new OpsAgent version was added to the public repository and subsequently used in attacks.

AuditTeam activity timeline

OpsAgent uses multiple loader variants during its campaign. All discovered loaders unpack OpsAgent by parsing the MZ header and transferring execution to the entry point of the unpacked payload.

The C loader was discovered in April 2026. It injects shellcode into msiexec.exe using standard API calls: CreateProcessA, VirtualAllocEx, WriteProcessMemory, and CreateRemoteThread.

The .NET loader was found in June 2026. It uses NtQueueApcThread instead of direct process creation for injection. It contains strings such as DApc, DProtect, DResume, and msiexec.exe. Both the C and .NET loaders inject shellcode into a separate target process. They are also protected by OLLVM obfuscation with control-flow flattening enabled.

In September 2026, we discovered the Rust loader. It retrieves an encrypted payload from the C2 server, decrypts it, parses the PE header, and runs the Execute export function within its own process rather than injecting into another process. This in-process execution model eliminates the need for remote process manipulation.

OpsAgent main implant

The implant (version 5.2.8) rivals Cobalt Strike in functionality. It uses a worker-thread architecture: the main thread monitors connection health, while a worker thread processes commands from a queue. Three concepts structure its behavior:

the worker thread (handles nearly all C2 commands)

jobs (background tasks run in separate processes)

links (connections to child agents used for lateral movement)

Exceptions include info, jobs, jobkill, worker_status, worker_restart, and exit, which execute immediately in the main thread.

OpsAgent can optionally obtain its C2 address by reading a public GitHub repository: hxxps://github[.]com/RonaldCMalcolm1983/staticPage. The address is stored in an encrypted form using AES‑CBC with the SHA‑256 hash of f4bb8c7e15d8b98d535eb20b09176b3d as the key and a zero initialization vector. The encrypted payload is wrapped with the markers GetConfig and EndConfig and then base64‑encoded.

Several commands — mimikatz_spawn, execute_assembly, pth_spawn, runpe, monitor start, and screenshot_service start — run background jobs in a separate process. To run a job, each creates a process and a named pipe, and receives shellcode from the C2 server. No command contains embedded mimikatz or PTH binaries. All payloads arrive remotely through the pipe, encrypted with XOR using key 604902af7b70b271. By default these commands use msiexec.exe as a host process, but the path can be changed. The exception is the monitor command, which uses notepad.exe for shellcode injection. This design keeps the implant small and lets operators deliver custom payloads remotely.

Injection commands support two modes: Legacy and DbgInjector. Legacy mode uses CreateProcessA and NtCreateThreadEx. DbgInjector creates a process in DEBUG state, allocates shared memory, sets a hardware breakpoint on FileTimeToSystemTime, then redirects RIP to the shellcode without creating a new thread.

Several code patterns suggest LLM-assisted development. For example, OpsAgent resolves WinAPI functions dynamically by hash: it calculates the hash of a function name, then searches for the matching address. Sometimes the plaintext name remains in memory before hashing. Additionally, all injection commands share near‑identical code structures, differing only in argument count and target process selection. This uniform approach matches LLM‑generated scaffolding.

The OpsAgent command set covers file system operations, process management, privilege escalation, shellcode injection, monitoring, and network pivoting. The C2 protocol runs over WebSocket with TLS and exchanges data in units called frames. Protocol frame types function as an extra command set enabling SOCKS proxy establishment, chunked file uploads, and inter-agent data forwarding. These frames are handled by a dedicated frame-handler function rather than the standard command decoder. The following combined table lists all text-based commands and protocol frame types together.

Several commands deserve closer attention. The monitor command manages keylogging through subcommands that start, stop, and configure logging threads. Screenshot features follow a similar design with session management subcommands. In contrast, the pivot subsystem uses a flatter command structure where each pivot command (pivot_list, pivot_start, pivot_stop, pivot_add, pivot_remove, pivot_auto) operates independently without nested subcommand hierarchies.

Some frame type numbers such as 0x20 appear unused in the current implementation. These may be traces of previous versions.

This module installs a driver disguised as Beijing Jiangmin Antivirus (KV series), utilizing the \KvCorePortX0 filter port for communication. Upon receiving the 0x1D command followed by a target filename, the driver terminates the corresponding process. Its target list covers nearly every major security product on the market.

Additionally, the driver carries a valid digital signature, which facilitates its initial execution.

The process kill list contains approximately 220 entries, including:

Security products: MsMpEng, SecurityHealthService, avp, ekrn, ccSvcHst, fcag, eadrline
Remote access tools: TeamViewer_Desktop, TeamViewer_Service, tv_w32, tv_x64
Monitoring/logging: Sysmon, Sysmon64, splunkd, wazuh-agent
Backup services: VeeamNFSSvc, AcronisAgent, MBAMService
Database processes: sqlservr, mysqld, mongod, redis-server

Security products: MsMpEng, SecurityHealthService, avp, ekrn, ccSvcHst, fcag, eadrline

Remote access tools: TeamViewer_Desktop, TeamViewer_Service, tv_w32, tv_x64

Monitoring/logging: Sysmon, Sysmon64, splunkd, wazuh-agent

Backup services: VeeamNFSSvc, AcronisAgent, MBAMService

Database processes: sqlservr, mysqld, mongod, redis-server

This module encrypts files using either AES-CBC or ChaCha20 based on a command-line flag. Each file receives a unique encryption key produced by RtlGenRandom. A 12-byte array serves as the ChaCha20 nonce or expands to a 16-byte AES initialization vector. Key exchange relies on X25519 Diffie-Hellman with a static public key embedded in the binary. The module generates an ephemeral key pair per session and encrypts the file key with ChaCha20 using the shared secret.

Beyond file encryption, the module actively disrupts infrastructure by terminating database processes including SQL Server, Oracle, MySQL, PostgreSQL, MongoDB, and Redis. It also stops backup services such as Veeam. The module removes shadow copies, empties the recycle bin, and wipes temporary folders. Furthermore, any file deletion overwrites content with random data before removing the entry, preventing forensic recovery.

After encryption, the module drops a ransom note. It formats the message as an audit log demanding payment through a Tor onion address. The note offers two options: pay the “Audit & Consulting fee” for a vulnerability report or face public data disclosure.

Finally, the module’s configuration parameters include file extensions to encrypt, directories and files to whitelist, processes and services to terminate, and shadow copy deletion flags.

OpsAgent-rs (Rust version)

The Rust rewrite maintains functional parity with the C++ version while introducing new capabilities. OpsAgent-rs initially communicates with the C2 via HTTP POST requests to retrieve commands. There are special commands that change this behavior. After receiving an upgrade command, the implant establishes a persistent WebSocket connection over TLS for ongoing C2 communication instead of continuing to send POST requests. Additionally, command identifiers shifted from text names to numeric constants. New commands include interactive shells, directory creation, and sleep-interval changes.

Command mapping between versions shows most legacy commands have Rust equivalents. However, screenshot and monitor commands now consolidate into a unified identifier rather than relying on an extensive set of subcommands. The Rust version removes the entire pivot command set, eliminating pivoting task management functionality.

C2 acquisition in the Rust version changed significantly. The configuration contains a flag controlling whether the implant checks GitHub for new C2 addresses. It also stores markers indicating the beginning and end of the encrypted C2 string. The GitHub README page holds both the legacy C2 format and an additional base64-encoded blob specifically for OpsAgent-rs. The encryption scheme was reworked to use AES-GCM instead of AES-CBC. To derive the GCM key, the decryption flow computes HMAC-SHA256 of the secret key c2-framework-psk-salt-v1 against the configuration data blob, then applies a second HMAC with the salt c2-encryption-key\x01. The nonce is the first 12 bytes of the decrypted payload and the tag occupies the last 16 bytes.

The implant uses AWS-generated CloudFront CDN subdomains as C2 endpoints. Because these domains operate on shared infrastructure, their underlying IP addresses cannot serve as reliable standalone IOCs.

The GitHub repository stayed active throughout the campaign until November 2026. Commit history shows ongoing updates until September 29, 2026, when the actor deleted the legacy C2 domain from the repository. This suggests the actor maintains the infrastructure actively and rotates domains as needed.

In November 2026, the GitHub repository was removed. However, this does not mean the campaign is over, as the latest Rust implants found contained a configuration with the GitHub feature disabled.

AuditTeam targets organizations from different countries. We observed victims in China, the Philippines, Senegal, Italy, Finland, Hong Kong and Russia. And according to the group’s leak site, there are victims in Turkey, Thailand, Ukraine, Argentina, South Korea, Japan, and Spain. All identified victims belong to different industries.

We attribute the OpsAgent campaign to AuditTeam with high confidence. The following evidence supports this assessment:

Ransom note format and “Audit & Consulting fee” terminology unique to this group

Tor-based leak site listing targeted organizations

Operational philosophy distinguishing them from conventional ransomware groups through remediation reports

Open sources first identified AuditTeam in March 2026 via dbdigest.com. In June, DeXpose reported that AuditTeam claimed responsibility for attacking a Russian organization and listed it on its leak site. The group claims a mission to educate organizations about cybersecurity through exploitation and remediation reports. This tactical approach separates them from traditional ransomware operators.

AuditTeam frames extortion as a paid security audit — a distinctive model among ransomware groups. The C++ to Rust transition signals continued toolchain investment. New Rust commands cover interactive shells, WebSocket upgrades, and directory management.

The specialized modules reveal thorough operational preparation: the AV/EDR killer uses a signed Jiangmin driver to disable security products, and the cryptor module terminates database processes, encrypts files, and removes shadow copies. These tools let the group neutralize defenses and optionally destroy data.

CloudFront CDN subdomains complicate takedown and make IP-based blocking impractical because the IP space is shared with legitimate traffic. While the actor rotates infrastructure, the public GitHub repository enables C2 discovery and its removal disrupts the attack chain.

Custom malware frameworks are typically developed and maintained by APT groups with dedicated engineering resources. AuditTeam shows that a ransomware crew can build comparable tooling. This shift may be enabled by AI-assisted development tools lowering the barrier to creating functional, multi-module implant architectures.

Indicators of compromise

[93] Schneier on Security - Formal Verification of the Signal Protocol Using AI
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: 2026-11-17T12:05:43Z
Link: https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/11/formal-verification-of-the-signal-protocol-using-ai.html

[TAM METİN - 293 kelime]
Formal Verification of the Signal Protocol Using AI

This seems like a good thing:

Google Deepmind researchers used agentic AI to mathematically prove, or “formally verify,” a number of the Signal protocol’s key cryptographic guarantees­a landmark development for the widely used cryptography and AI itself.

Conducted as a sort of proof-of-concept project in just four weeks, the work is significant in two ways. It serves as further independent validation of the cryptography that protects massive quantities of the world’s communications on platforms like the Signal app, Google Messages, and WhatsApp. And it demonstrates Google DeepMind’s formal verification capabilities using a new AI framework called AlphaProof Code.

The DeepMind analysis did not find any new vulnerabilities in the portions of the Signal protocol it looked at, but when the researchers used AlphaProof Code on older versions of Signal that contain known and long-fixed vulnerabilities, it correctly identified them and showed that the protocol could not be formally verified. The researchers are releasing the specification and proof they developed for the Signal protocol analysis to aid other formal verification work.

First, our researchers collaborated with a mix of models to translate Signal’s security goals into formal mathematical specifications, with human experts reviewing every one. From there, agents powered by Gemini Carbon did the proofs, generating over six million lines of Lean, a computer language that mathematically proves the accuracy of code.

Next, the agents audited the proof and highlighted areas that needed a second look by a human ­ just one percent of the codebase, a massive time savings. Because Lean automatically checks the rest, our experts could focus their review where human judgment matters most.

Tags: academic papers, AI, protocols, security analysis, Signal

[94] The Cyber Express - Optimizely Cyberattack: Malicious Project JavaScript Hits 178 Major Websites
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 08:16:57 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/optimizely-cyberattack-project-javascript/

[TAM METİN - 618 kelime]
Optimizely Cyberattack: Malicious Project JavaScript Hits 178 Major Websites

A cyberattack on Optimizely, a provider of website-testing software, placed malicious code on websites run by 178 organizations, including the BBC, CNN, Zoom, HSBC, Salesforce and Equifax. Researchers say the tampered project JavaScript could expose visitors’ payment details, cryptocurrency transfers and logged-in sessions.

Report URI, the security firm that identified the attack on Monday, examined 1,611 Optimizely scripts and found 188 infected projects. Founder Scott Helme estimated the code was served during about 34 million page visits, with a possible range of 6 million to 150 million.

That counts visits that loaded the code, not people who lost money or data. The actual harm is far smaller,” Helme told Newsweek on Tuesday.

How the Optimizely Project JavaScript Was Compromised

Instead of breaching each organization separately, the attackers exploited a vulnerability in Optimizely’s Experimentation platform, which businesses use to test different versions of their web pages. Because the injected code sat inside scripts Optimizely hosted, simply opening an affected site could load it.

“This is a supply-chain attack: Compromise one trusted vendor, and its code runs on every customer’s website at once,” Helme said, adding that researchers saw “no evidence of AI involvement.”

The operator is not new. “The same crypto wallet has been receiving and cashing out funds since March 2026,” Helme said, noting that the same infrastructure was injected through at least three other third-party script vendors in the days before Optimizely.

Affected Companies Respond to the Cyberattack

In a Tuesday status update, Optimizely said “an unauthorized party exploited a vulnerability” and that it had “fixed the vulnerability and removed the injected code from affected projects.” It also blocked the vulnerable path, ended active sessions, revoked tokens, and reset access for affected user accounts.

Salesforce said in a security advisory that the modified scripts “could load malicious code in visitors’ browsers.” It disabled them across its preview and production instances and pledged to notify affected customers “without undue delay” if it finds unauthorized access to their data. Equifax said it had “immediately investigated this issue and ceased our use of Optimizely,” adding that it found no evidence of compromise to its systems or data. IKEA said it was investigating the potential impact on its digital services and customers.

John Walsh, Field CTO at IGEL, urged caution in reading the list. “The 178 organizations represent potentially affected websites, not 178 confirmed corporate breaches or a known number of victims,” he said. He put potential exposure in the tens of millions but stressed separating exposure, code execution, and confirmed compromise.

What Visitors Should Do After the Optimizely Cyberattack

Opening an affected site does not by itself mean data was stolen, but anyone who visited one on November 16, especially to enter card details or send cryptocurrency, should take precautions. “Anyone logged in to an affected site: Log out of all sessions and change your password,” Helme said.

It is advised to update iPhones, iPads and other devices; check bank accounts for unfamiliar charges; confirm cryptocurrency went to the correct wallet; watch for unexpected login alerts; and report unauthorized payments to the bank immediately. Walsh added transaction alerts, multifactor authentication, and following each affected organization’s guidance, noting that avoiding unfamiliar links is not enough when malicious code arrives through a trusted website.

[95] Industrial Cyber - OTCC urges US policymakers to bolster OT cybersecurity, fund resilience, govern AI agents as threats accelerate
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 07:24:28 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/ai/otcc-urges-us-policymakers-to-bolster-ot-cybersecurity-fund-resilience-govern-ai-agents-as-threats-accelerate/

[TAM METİN - 1838 kelime]
OTCC urges US policymakers to bolster OT cybersecurity, fund resilience, govern AI agents as threats accelerate

The Operational Technology Cybersecurity Coalition (OTCC) urges U.S. policymakers to make critical infrastructure resilience a central pillar of national AI security policy. In a new report, the coalition sets out 18 recommendations to help essential services withstand, contain and recover from AI-enabled cyberattacks. Developed through four summer roundtables involving nearly 70 representatives from critical infrastructure sectors, government, and the cybersecurity community, these recommendations focus on strengthening baseline cybersecurity, enabling AI-supported defenses in OT (operational technology) environments, and improving resilience during disruptions.

Titled ‘The Missing Pillar of AI Security: Critical Infrastructure Resilience,’ the report calls for closing regulatory gaps without duplicating existing requirements, directing funding to under-resourced entities, establishing an AI governance framework for OT environments and ensuring long-term access to AI-enabled cybersecurity tools. It also recommends funding the Cyber Response and Recovery Fund and reconstituting the Cyber Safety Review Board.

OTCC grounded its recommendations in three core principles covering prioritizing resilience, assuming compromise, and involving AI developers in OT defense. The coalition cited an August warning from CISA and federal partners that attackers are using AI-generated scripts to target industrial controllers in the energy, water, and manufacturing sectors, allowing adversaries to identify and exploit longstanding vulnerabilities faster and at greater scale.

“Congress and the Administration don’t need to start from scratch,” Michael Garcia, policy director of OTCC, said in a media statement. “Every recommendation in this report builds on authorities, programs, and institutions that already exist. What’s missing is a decision to treat operational resilience as part of AI security policy, and to fund it that way.”

While emerging AI capabilities introduce novel threats, operational technology (OT) defense still relies on operational resilience grounded in fundamental cybersecurity practices like access management, network segmentation, and zero trust. Because AI tools alone cannot compensate for missing baseline security, national AI security policy must measure success by whether critical infrastructure operators can sustain and rapidly restore essential services during an incident, a priority echoed in U.S. strategies like NSM-22 and the 2026 National Resilience Strategy, as well as recent U.K. guidance.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, critical infrastructure operators must adopt an ‘assume compromise’ mindset rather than relying solely on patching. Roughly 70% of OT vulnerabilities reside in legacy or deeply embedded systems that cannot be patched or rebooted without disrupting continuous physical operations, and a shortage of specialized engineering talent creates severe bottlenecks in testing the over 12,000 ICS vulnerabilities tracked by CISA. Assuming compromise reframes OT defense around building resilience so infrastructure can withstand inevitable breaches.

Securing critical infrastructure in the AI era requires proactive collaboration among the federal government, AI labs, cybersecurity vendors, and OT owners and operators. AI companies must partner with OT and federal stakeholders to safely train models on sensitive infrastructure data, such as keeping protected electric utility data within owner environments or controlled test beds, and include OT partners in industry policy initiatives to encourage active defensive experimentation.

The OTCC report follows last week’s nationwide FAA ground stop and Microsoft outage, which caused nationwide chaos and briefly sparked fears of a coordinated cyberattack. “Although neither event was a cyberattack, both demonstrated the exact scale of disruption our adversaries are working to engineer, now with the added speed and sophistication of artificial intelligence. A software glitch is an inconvenience, but a deliberate, AI-driven strike on the operational technology powering our essential services would cause untold harm to Americans around the country and impact our national security.”

OTCC outlines 18 recommendations that build on existing authorities, programs, and institutions. By integrating AI into OT cybersecurity with appropriate safeguards, Congress and the Biden administration can take practical steps to protect the systems underpinning U.S. critical infrastructure.

To strengthen baseline cybersecurity against AI-enabled threats, the report calls for closing cross-sector regulatory gaps without duplicating requirements, establishing secure-by-design mandates and a cyber duty of care, funding under-resourced entities, enacting whistleblower protections, and introducing compensating controls for legacy, unpatchable systems.

For years, malicious cyber actors have compromised entities and OT systems through well-known security gaps. They exploited weak passwords, the absence of multi-factor authentication, overprivileged access, and inadequate network segmentation. AI will only exacerbate this challenge. Recent attacks show that bad actors, whether using AI-enabled tools or not, compromise systems through these same gaps. While cyber defenders could use AI to identify these flaws and address them using established practices, there is no guarantee they will. In other words, too few incentives or requirements exist to compel organizations to act, despite increased AI-enabled cyberattacks.

Meanwhile, organizations are simultaneously deploying their own AI agents and other non-human identities (NHIs) to fight back against AI-enabled attacks. Yet, they are adopting them at a rate that far exceeds their ability to govern them. In this new landscape, where NHIs can outnumber human employees by more than 100 to 1, the very definition of identity has changed. This creates a security concern in its own right by potentially having an exponential increase in insider threats within an organization. Therefore, any organizational cybersecurity framework must account for securing AI agents by extending common security controls such as zero trust and identity access management protocols.

The report identified that Congress and the Executive Branch must establish a national framework that aligns enforceable cybersecurity requirements, incentives, accountability, and financial assistance to increase the adoption of baseline cybersecurity and identity security controls across critical infrastructure. This includes mandatory, harmonized cybersecurity requirements across critical infrastructure; secure-by-design requirements for hardware and software producers to increase resilience at scale; liability protections for those that fall outside critical infrastructure sectors or hardware and software providers; and financial assistance to under-resourced entities.

The framework should establish a common cybersecurity floor based on CISA’s Cross-Sector Cybersecurity Performance Goals (CPGs), while allowing sector-specific regulators to impose additional, risk-based requirements. These requirements must also account for AI agents and other NHIs operating with privileged access to sensitive systems. This framework is not meant to add another layer of cybersecurity compliance, but to ensure every critical infrastructure sector meets a minimum level of security commensurate with the risks it poses to national security, public safety, and essential services.

Forging connective tissue between AI labs, the federal government, state and local governments, and OT partners will be critical to strengthening resilience against AI-enabled cyberattacks. AI developers possess unique knowledge of emerging model capabilities and potential misuse, while OT manufacturers, cybersecurity providers, and critical infrastructure operators understand the operational realities of securing industrial systems.

Yet, AI adoption rates within OT have been slow. Nozomi Networks found that fewer than 8% of organizations they surveyed have deployed AI across multiple OT cybersecurity functions. Congress, the Executive Branch, and private-sector entities should establish sustainable partnerships that bring these communities together to identify high-value applications of AI, test defensive capabilities in realistic OT environments, and accelerate the adoption of proven technologies across critical infrastructure.

These partnerships should include OT owners and operators, industrial automation providers, and cybersecurity service providers alongside AI developers, so that pilots address real-world deployment barriers, including legacy infrastructure, workforce constraints, interoperability, and the need to maintain operational continuity. Federal agencies should use pilot findings to develop practical, risk-based guidance that helps operators evaluate and procure effective AI-enabled defensive capabilities.

To enable AI-based defenses in OT environments, the coalition recommends establishing an OT-specific AI governance framework, scaling successful state and regional models, ensuring long-term access to AI-enabled capabilities, speeding the adoption of certified commercial off-the-shelf solutions, and testing autonomous AI deployments. It also urges renewed support for information sharing and analysis centers (ISACs), a public-private forum for AI developers and OT organizations, and stronger international coordination.

OTCC recognizes that malicious actors will increasingly use AI to identify vulnerabilities, compromise systems, and execute attacks with improved speed and scale. Even organizations that implement strong cybersecurity controls cannot eliminate every vulnerability. While implementing basic cybersecurity controls will reduce the overall risk landscape, it will not eliminate risk. Federal cybersecurity policy should emphasize the ability to withstand, contain, and recover from a successful attack.

OT owners and operators and the broader U.S. government should adopt zero trust principles for AI models and agents, embrace CISA’s CI Fortify approach, and assume compromise in either their system or in a system they rely upon. Congress and the Executive Branch should strengthen national cyber response and recovery capabilities, improve coordination between cybersecurity professionals and emergency responders, and support investments in operational resilience.

Federal resilience initiatives should encourage critical infrastructure owners and operators to regularly assess recovery readiness, including whether their security teams can detect incidents across operational assets, coordinate OT-specific response, and restore priority functions with minimal disruption. Policymakers should consider technical assistance and voluntary exercises that evaluate these capabilities in realistic operating conditions.

To enhance resilience during disruptions, the report advises funding the Cyber Response and Recovery Fund, reconstituting the Cyber Safety Review Board, improving incident management processes, and adopting Critical Infrastructure Fortify controls. In the coming months, OTCC plans to convene policymakers, AI developers, and the OT community to test and refine these proposals, while engaging Congress, federal, state, and local governments, and AI laboratories to drive implementation.

In conclusion, the OTCC report observed that the past several months have made clear that AI-enabled threats to OT environments are no longer hypothetical. Attackers are leveraging AI to discover vulnerabilities, compromise devices, and target industrial controllers at a speed and scale that traditional defenses were not designed to meet.

The recommendations rest on a simple premise that operational resilience, grounded in fundamental cybersecurity practices and enhanced with AI-enabled defenses, forms the foundation of OT defense in this new era. Many of these actions build on existing authorities, programs, and institutions, requiring prioritization, sustained funding, and the will to act before the next crisis strikes. While CISA is responsible for implementing several of these measures, the agency faces low staffing levels and insufficient resources. Rebuilding and supporting CISA and the SRMAs is imperative for national cybersecurity, because as AI continues to lower the barrier to sophisticated attacks, the cost of delay will only grow.

The coalition is firm in its commitment to advancing these recommendations beyond this report. In the coming months, the OTCC will publish follow-up papers examining pressing issues in greater depth, convene policymakers, AI developers, and the OT community to test and refine these proposals, and engage directly with the Executive Branch, Congress, state and local governments, and AI labs to drive implementation. While the window to build resilience before the next major AI-enabled cyberattack is narrowing, it remains open, and the OTCC looks forward to working with public and private sector partners to seize this opportunity.

Earlier this month, the OTCC, along with 34 critical infrastructure organizations, urged U.S. congressional leaders to include a ten-year reauthorization of the Cybersecurity Information Sharing Act of 2015 (CISA 2015) in the final Fiscal Year 2027 National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA). The law enables voluntary sharing of cyber threat indicators and defensive measures between private-sector organizations and the federal government, providing liability protections and legal certainty for participating organizations.
