[96] Industrial Cyber - Health Management Academy details hospital cyberattacks, leading to care disruption and prolonged system outages
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 07:15:44 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/medical/health-management-academy-details-hospital-cyberattacks-leading-to-care-disruption-and-prolonged-system-outages/

[TAM METİN - 1434 kelime]
Health Management Academy details hospital cyberattacks, leading to care disruption and prolonged system outages

At least 65 cyberattacks disrupted patient care at U.S. hospitals and health systems between January 2022 and September 2026, affecting at least 305 hospitals across 36 states, the District of Columbia, Guam and the U.S. Virgin Islands, according to an analysis by The Health Management Academy. These attacks forced ambulance diversions in 26 incidents, canceled or postponed surgeries, treatments or appointments in 28, and prompted staff to switch to paper records in 58. Clinical systems remained offline for a median of 15 days among the 23 attacks with reported restoration dates, with five outages lasting at least a month.

The analysis, based on publicly reported incidents, highlights gaps in federal tracking of cyberattacks that disrupt hospital operations. The U.S. Department of Health and Human Services’ breach registry records incidents involving exposed patient data affecting at least 500 people, but does not capture operational consequences such as diverted ambulances, canceled procedures or closed clinics. The log counts 11 such attacks in 2022, 21 in 2023, 13 in 2024, 12 in 2025, and eight in 2026 through the end of September.

Of 55 attacks in the analysis that began at least a year earlier, 12, or 22%, had no related registry filing. Meanwhile, healthcare organizations reported a record 776 data breaches to federal regulators in 2025, with hacking accounting for 81% of reported breaches between 2022 and 2025.

“On February 19, 2026, the University of Mississippi Medical Center was hit by a cyberattack that took its Epic electronic health records system offline. Staff switched to paper charts, and all 35 of the center’s statewide clinics were forced to close until March 2, according to CNN and the Mississippi Free Press,” Andrew Reed, associate director at The Health Management Academy, wrote in a recent blog post. “The Mississippi attack was one of at least 65 cyberattacks that disrupted patient care at US hospitals and health systems between January 2022 and September 2026, according to a log compiled by The Health Management Academy from news coverage, trade publications, and hospital statements.”

Reed added that the attacks reached at least 305 hospitals in 36 states, the District of Columbia, Guam, and the US Virgin Islands.

The federal registry lists breaches of patient data affecting 500 or more people and has no field for diverted ambulances, canceled treatments, or closed clinics. Of the disruptive attacks that began more than a year ago, one in five has no entry in it.

“Ambulances were diverted to other hospitals in 26 of the 65 attacks, or 40%. Surgeries, treatments, or appointments were canceled or postponed in 28, or 43%, and clinics or hospital departments closed in 10. In 58 attacks, or 89%, electronic records or other clinical systems went offline and staff worked on paper,” Reed detailed. “Twenty-one of the attacks hit two or more hospitals at once. The largest, a ransomware attack on a national hospital system in May 2024, took electronic health records offline across roughly 140 hospitals.”

The count includes only attacks whose effects on patient care were reported publicly, so the full number is higher. Meanwhile, cybersecurity firm Emsisoft counted 46 hospital systems hit by ransomware in 2023 and 85 in 2024.

Reed said that care-disrupting attacks touched hospitals across 39 states and territories, with an attack on a multi-state health system counted in every state where it affected hospitals. Texas stood alone at the top with seven attacks, followed by California, Idaho, Illinois, and New York with five each. Florida, Georgia, Maryland, and Massachusetts each saw four, while Kentucky, Maine, Michigan, New Jersey, Oklahoma, and Wisconsin recorded three. Most of the remaining jurisdictions, including Alabama, Arizona, Kansas, Mississippi, Missouri, South Carolina, Tennessee, and the US Virgin Islands, had two or one.

The multi-state picture is just as telling. Texas had the most attacks on health systems spanning state lines, with three of its seven, while Florida, Illinois, Kansas, Oklahoma, Tennessee, and Wisconsin each had two. In Kansas and Tennessee, every attack involved a multi-state system. The same held for ten jurisdictions that recorded a single attack: Connecticut, the District of Columbia, Indiana, Iowa, Nebraska, New Hampshire, New Mexico, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, and Washington. In those places, the one disruption was felt through a system that reached beyond their borders.

Some states looked very different. New York recorded five attacks, none of them on a multi-state system, and Idaho had five with only one crossing state lines, suggesting that locally contained systems carried much of the burden there. Arizona, Colorado, Guam, Louisiana, Mississippi, Missouri, Ohio, the US Virgin Islands, West Virginia, and Wyoming also saw no multi-state involvement at all.

In another incident, on April 6, 2026, the emergency room at Brockton Hospital in Massachusetts stopped accepting ambulances, which were routed to other hospitals instead. Among other disruptions, chemotherapy infusions were canceled the following day. Three days after the attack, on April 9, the Anubis ransomware group claimed it had stolen two terabytes of patient data. Brockton lifted its code black emergency status on April 15. The disruption made headlines with NBC Boston and HIPAA Journal.

For the 23 attacks in which hospitals reported a restoration date, clinical systems stayed down for a median of 15 days. Fourteen of those outages lasted at least two weeks, five lasted at least a month, and the longest ran 41 days. Patients admitted before an attack begins are also more likely to die in the hospital. University of Minnesota researchers found that in-hospital mortality rose by 34 to 38% for those patients, and that hospital volume fell by 17 to 24% in the first week.

This comes as healthcare organizations reported a record 776 data breaches to federal regulators in 2025, the third straight annual increase. Hacking accounted for 81% of the 2,878 breaches reported from 2022 through 2025.

Reed called on health systems to plan for outages that last weeks, not days. Staff fell back on paper records in 58 of the 65 attacks, and where hospitals reported a restoration date, clinical systems stayed down for a median of 15 days. Downtime plans built for that length of outage need to cover order sets, medication records, and lab and imaging requests that work without the electronic record, plus enough printed supplies and trained staff to sustain them.

Some hospitals asked patients to bring printed medication lists, insurance cards, prescriptions, and physician orders to appointments, and those instructions can be drafted in advance as part of a downtime plan. Because systems stayed down for two weeks or longer in 14 of the 23 attacks with a reported restoration date, and for a month or more in five, recovery plans should also account for staffing paper workflows, manual billing and claims, and the backlog of postponed procedures once systems return.

Ambulance diversion, seen in 26 of the 65 attacks, pushes the strain onto neighboring hospitals. Researchers at the University of California San Diego, writing in Critical Care Explorations, found that survival with good neurological function after out-of-hospital cardiac arrest fell from 40.0 percent to 4.5 percent at two emergency departments while a neighboring four-hospital system was down after a 2021 ransomware attack.

An earlier study of the same hospitals in JAMA Network Open found that ambulance arrivals rose from 1,741 to 2,354 and stroke code activations from 59 to 102 compared with the period before the attack. Regional agreements that spell out how diversion works during a cyberattack, including where stroke, trauma, and cardiac patients should go, give EMS agencies and receiving hospitals time to prepare for that surge.

The deeper problem is that nobody is counting. The federal registry tallies exposed patient records but has no field for diverted ambulances, canceled procedures, or days spent on paper, so no federal dataset shows how often cyberattacks interrupt care or for how long. Health systems and the companies that support them each hold a piece of that picture. A shared set of measures, such as days of clinical downtime, hours on diversion, and procedures postponed, would let organizations benchmark their readiness and recovery against peers.

Reed pointed to the Health Management Academy’s Catalyst and Convening programs, which bring health system and industry leaders together to share data and practices, as one way for leaders to compare their downtime and diversion plans with those who have already lived through an attack. Health systems and their technology partners can also agree on what to track after an incident, so that the true cost is measured in days of downtime and hours on diversion rather than simply in the number of records exposed.

[97] Industrial Cyber - Fortress.ai launches AI-native platform to manage cyber supply chain risks across critical infrastructure
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 07:13:06 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/news/fortress-ai-launches-ai-native-platform-to-manage-cyber-supply-chain-risks-across-critical-infrastructure/

[TAM METİN - 711 kelime]
Fortress.ai launches AI-native platform to manage cyber supply chain risks across critical infrastructure

Fortress.ai, an AI-powered risk management company serving critical infrastructure and national security, launched the Fortress Command Platform to help utilities, federal agencies and defense organizations manage cyber supply chain risk. The Fortress Command Platform ingests, analyzes and enriches data across critical supply chains and the systems they support, then deploys AI agents to address operational exposures. Fortress analysts oversee remediation, track issues through resolution and document the results.

Critical infrastructure risk is being turned upside down. Supply chains are being redrawn by geopolitics and energy dislocation, a historic data center build-out, the reshoring of manufacturing and the arrival of AI itself. Infrastructure and the supply chains behind it are now a front line of geopolitical competition, attacked three ways: kinetically, economically, and through cyber-attacks and espionage.

The result is far more risk than human teams can find, prioritize and fix alone. AI is the answer, and adversaries already know it. But AI is new, and on its own it cannot yet be trusted to act where a mistake carries operational consequences. The Fortress Command Platform closes that gap with responsible AI: AI does the volume, Fortress experts validate and act, and organizations keep up with the scale of these new risks without trusting AI to act alone.

The Fortress Command Platform manages cyber risk across an organization’s supply chain and the assets it delivers. Fortress combines AI, the customer’s environment data, and its proprietary vendor, product, and vulnerability intelligence to prioritize what matters, then puts Fortress analysts to work driving remediation with vendors to closure.

“Critical infrastructure operators and federal agencies need clear answers about what puts their operations at risk and what to do next. They have enough alerts. They need outcomes,” said Alex Santos, co-founder and CEO of Fortress. “AI brings speed and scale, but delivering results also requires people who understand the customer’s environment and can help get the work done. Success means customers know what needs attention, have the support to address it, and can demonstrate what has been resolved.”

For organizations responsible for essential services and national security, identifying risk is only the beginning. Findings scattered across systems and teams make it hard to determine what needs immediate attention, who owns it, and whether the work is complete. Resolving an exposure often requires coordination across security teams, operational staff, and outside suppliers, with each competing for limited time and resources.

The Fortress Command Platform connects findings with the operational context and hands-on support needed to move that work forward. Fortress rebuilt its platform with AI at the core enabling continuous analysis across vendors, assets, and compliance requirements, while Fortress analysts validate critical findings, assess their implications, and work alongside customer teams to act.

The Fortress Command Platform helps customers address three connected challenges. The first is knowing what needs attention. The platform connects information across vendors, products and cyber assets. It then prioritizes exposures based on their potential impact on operations and mission.

The second is moving remediation forward. The platform combines analyst validation with practical support. This helps coordinate action across vendors and customer teams, with clear responsibilities and tracked progress. The third is demonstrating results. The platform documents validated findings, remediation actions and remaining risks. Security teams, leadership and boards can use this to assess progress. The evidence also supports audits and regulatory reviews.

The Fortress Command Platform unifies disciplines that have historically lived in separate tools and teams: governance, risk and compliance (GRC), third-party risk management (TPRM), cyber supply chain risk management (C-SCRM), software supply chain security, vulnerability management and AI governance. Connecting these disciplines lets organizations assess how supplier, technology and compliance risk affect one another and determine where action is needed.

AI agents continuously ingest, analyze and enrich data across vendors, products and the IT and operational technology (OT) assets they deliver, then take action on what they find. Fortress analysts validate critical findings and work with vendors and customer teams to address them.

This approach supports decisions throughout the technology lifecycle, from evaluating suppliers and purchasing products to deploying technology securely and managing emerging exposures. Customers gain a clearer view of what puts their operations at risk, support to address it, and evidence of the work completed.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 06:57:01 +0000

[TAM METİN - 540 kelime]
Barry-Wehmiller, Kasm Technologies partner to secure remote access across manufacturing OT environments

Barry-Wehmiller Design Group (BWDG) and Kasm Technologies today announced a partnership that integrates Kasm for OT into DG360, BWDG’s customizable OT security managed services platform. The combined solution gives manufacturers precise control and auditability over who connects to production systems, which assets they can reach, and how long that access lasts.

Manufacturing is the most cyberattacked industry, with an average cost of $4.88M per incident. Remote access sits at the center of that exposure. OEM contracts often require it, skilled labor is scarce, and sites are spread across regions. VPNs lack the granularity and audit trails these workflows demand, and many alternatives require endpoint agents, leave legacy applications outside the strategy, or take months to deploy.

“Kasm’s roots in demanding defense environments, including work with DOW, show in how the platform handles access control and auditability,” Dino Busalachi, director at Barry-Wehmiller Design Group, said in a statement. “It also gives our customers room to grow. The customer owns the access layer and the architecture is modular, so we can design DG360 around each facility’s needs today and adapt as their tools, sites, and strategy evolve.”

“BWDG has earned its place as a top-tier service provider by understanding how plants actually run and by supporting customers long after go-live,” according to Justin Carlson, chief strategy officer at Kasm Technologies. “Their customers want a next generation platform for remote operations, and BWDG is committed to delivering it. We are proud that Kasm for OT will sit at the center of that offering and look forward to bringing manufacturers a simpler path to modernization together.”

Kasm for OT provides a single browser-based platform for managing access to tools across multiple vendors, eliminating the need for separate access products for each vendor environment. Each session runs in an isolated, containerized workspace, preventing compromised credentials or endpoints from gaining a direct path to the plant floor. Built-in data loss prevention (DLP), keystroke logging, file transfer and screen capture controls, watermarking and kiosk mode further restrict user activity during remote sessions.

The platform also provides a protective layer for legacy and end-of-life operating systems and applications, allowing organizations to keep older systems operational without directly exposing their vulnerabilities. Its multi-tenant, software-defined architecture supports deployment across multiple sites without requiring additional physical hardware at each location. By retaining ownership of the access layer, customers can replace underlying tools and technologies over time without having to redesign their access model, supporting a more flexible approach to OT modernization.

DG360 is built around the Five ICS Cybersecurity Critical Controls and configured to each facility’s environment, risk profile, and operational priorities. Powered by a Manufacturer’s Mindset, BWDG pairs more than 450 system integration professionals with 24/7 managed services that monitor, tune, and evolve each customer’s security posture. Secure remote access is one of the five controls, and Kasm now serves as the access layer within it, working alongside DG360 technology partners including Claroty, TXOne, Salvador Technologies, Elastic, and Xiid.

BWDG brings OT context, integration depth, and managed operations. Kasm brings a platform that simplifies the path to modernization while securing uptime and availability. Manufacturers get remote access that fits real plant conditions, with every session logged and auditable.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 06:56:43 +0000

Arcova, Young Management & Consulting partner to integrate cybersecurity into critical infrastructure engineering and delivery

Arcova, a global cybersecurity and AI consulting firm, and Young Management & Consulting (YMC), an engineering and consulting firm serving utility and critical infrastructure markets, announced a partnership to combine engineering, construction, cybersecurity, regulatory compliance, AI and owner-side program expertise in an integrated delivery model. The partnership aims to embed security-by-design requirements across the engineering, procurement and construction lifecycle, incorporating cybersecurity and compliance considerations before systems reach commissioning.

The arrangement will give clients flexible access to specialized expertise and delivery capacity on a seat, workstream or full-program basis, supporting projects across utilities, power infrastructure and post-acquisition data centers.

The partnership addresses a growing challenge across utility and data center infrastructure: the requirements that determine whether an asset can be energized, commissioned and operated successfully increasingly cross traditional engineering, cybersecurity and regulatory boundaries. YMC will lead engineering, procurement, construction and commissioning support, while Arcova will provide cybersecurity, regulatory compliance, AI and owner-side program integration across the project lifecycle.

For data center developers, the partnership is designed primarily for the work that begins after a site and preliminary power path have been identified. The combined capabilities span power and interconnection, permitting and entitlement, design and construction, commissioning and Day-2 operations, adding delivery capacity within the teams, standards and processes clients already have in place.

“Powered land is not energized land,” said Jerome Farquharson, managing director for critical infrastructure at Arcova. “Once the site is acquired, the interconnection path, long-lead equipment, entitlement record, security architecture and commissioning evidence still have to be carried through. This partnership brings Arcova’s regulatory, cyber, AI and program capabilities together with YMC’s engineering and construction execution so clients can address those workstreams as one coordinated program, while keeping the teams and processes that already work for them.”

The partnership also brings cybersecurity into the engineering process earlier through a security-by-design approach. Rather than treating security and compliance as requirements to validate after construction, Arcova and YMC can integrate them into planning, conceptual and detailed design, procurement, factory testing, commissioning and ongoing operations.

That integration is becoming increasingly important as reliability requirements for large computational loads rapidly take shape. In September, NERC reached a key milestone when preliminary results indicated its foundational Computational Loads Reliability Standards passed the initial ballot, advancing efforts to support the reliable integration of data centers, AI computing clusters and other large computational loads into the bulk power system. The development underscores why reliability obligations and security considerations need to be addressed while facilities are still being designed.

“YMC is committed to delivering reliable results and strengthening the communities we serve.  Our true differentiator is our people and the innovation we bring in helping clients navigate the evolving energy landscape,” said Brandon Young, president and CEO at Young Management & Consulting.

Arcova and YMC will initially focus their partnership on three areas. The first covers power, interconnection and infrastructure delivery, combining YMC’s expertise in substation, transmission and distribution engineering, interconnection, construction management, procurement and project controls with Arcova’s regulatory and tariff strategy, load modeling, study acceleration, owner-side advisory and program integration capabilities, including POWERBRIDGE and GRID-AI.

The second area focuses on embedding cybersecurity across the engineering, procurement and construction (EPC) lifecycle. Arcova’s cybersecurity practitioners will work alongside YMC engineers from early design through construction and commissioning, incorporating OT and IT architecture, asset and network documentation, supply-chain requirements, security controls, Integrated Factory Acceptance Testing and compliance evidence through SECURE NEXUS.

The third area provides flexible program capacity extending into Day-2 operations. Clients can access specialized expertise through individual roles, defined workstreams or broader programs, depending on their requirements. Following commissioning, Arcova and YMC can extend their support to operations, lifecycle engineering, continuous OT security and ongoing compliance through offerings including ARCOVA OPERATE and AEGIS.

The partnership is designed to scale across engagements, from targeted assessments and embedded workstreams to multi-year programs, giving clients access to complementary engineering, cybersecurity, compliance and program delivery expertise as project requirements evolve. The joint offering is available now.

[TAM METİN - 547 kelime]
Cohesity expands cyber resilience across Microsoft Azure, Microsoft 365 and DevOps environments

Cohesity, vendor of AI and data security solutions, announced that it is advancing data protection and resilience across the Microsoft ecosystem while creating new opportunities for organizations to securely derive value from their enterprise data. Recent innovations span cloud, productivity and DevOps environments. That progress also extends to AI and analytics through the Cohesity Gaia Catalog integration with Azure Databricks.

“Organizations depend on more applications and data than ever to keep their businesses running, making resilience across those environments increasingly critical,” said Vasu Murthy, chief product officer, Cohesity. “Our work with Microsoft is focused on giving customers greater confidence in the systems and data they rely on, while creating new opportunities to put that data to work as AI becomes more central to the business.”

Cohesity has expanded its data protection and recovery capabilities across Microsoft Azure and Microsoft 365, adding support for critical Azure services, public sector cloud deployments and DevOps environments. Enhancements include extended protection for Azure Blob Storage, Azure Data Lake Storage and Azure Kubernetes Service, with application-consistent backups and granular recovery of Kubernetes resources. These additions complement existing coverage of Azure virtual machines and database-as-a-service offerings. For public sector customers, Cohesity has extended cloud-native protection in Azure Government to include Azure SQL Database, SQL Managed Instance, PostgreSQL, MySQL, Table Storage and Cosmos DB.

The company has also introduced preview capabilities for protecting GitHub and Azure DevOps environments, covering repositories and complete Azure DevOps organizations, including pull requests, boards, wikis and pipelines. Recovery options include maintaining an isolated, immutable copy of development assets. Microsoft 365 enhancements include threat scanning across Exchange, SharePoint and OneDrive, protection of Preservation Hold Libraries in OneDrive and SharePoint Online, and metadata preservation during recovery across OneDrive, SharePoint, Teams and Groups. Additional updates include expanded use of Microsoft Graph APIs and multi-geo support.

Cohesity has extended recovery capabilities for Microsoft Entra ID to cover permanently deleted objects synchronized with on-premises Active Directory, supporting identity restoration across hybrid environments. Administrators can also assign new passwords when restoring Entra ID user accounts to prevent continued access through compromised credentials. Protection now also extends to Microsoft Intune configuration objects as part of Entra ID backups.

The updates also address Microsoft 365 data optimization and governance. Cohesity’s data security posture management (DSPM) capabilities identify redundant, obsolete and trivial (ROT) data, while Cohesity Cloud Services can automatically tier identified data to archive storage based on policy. The platform can also stub archived data, helping organizations reduce unnecessary data in active Microsoft 365 environments while retaining access to relevant information for applications such as Microsoft Copilot.

Cohesity’s Microsoft momentum also extends to AI and analytics. The integration between Cohesity Gaia Catalog and Azure Databricks allows organizations to find, curate and publish enterprise data directly into Azure Databricks for AI and analytics. Curated datasets appear in Azure Databricks as an external data source while the underlying data remains in Cohesity Data Cloud, eliminating the need for new extract, transform and load pipelines to move or duplicate the data.

Gaia Catalog builds on Cohesity Gaia’s existing integration with Microsoft 365 Copilot, giving employees the ability to query enterprise data using natural language without leaving Copilot. Role-based and file-level access controls ensure responses reflect existing user permissions.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

Nozomi Networks, Lockheed Martin develop automated cybersecurity assessment capabilities for defense environments

Nozomi Networks, a vendor of operational technology (OT), Internet of Things (IoT) solutions, has secured a co-development project with Lockheed Martin to advance cybersecurity and automated security configuration assessment for government and defense environments. Lockheed Martin will provide offset funding and technical expertise to support the development, beginning with capabilities for assessing compliance with the Defense Information Systems Agency’s Security Technical Implementation Guides (DISA STIGs).

The collaboration will focus on helping government and defense organizations secure distributed environments, assess security configurations and demonstrate compliance across defense networks. The project combines Lockheed Martin’s defense expertise with Nozomi Networks’ capabilities in OT, IoT and cyber-physical systems (CPS) security.

“This work with Lockheed Martin is a testament to our joint commitment to bringing the power of AI to improve how our public-sector customers secure their systems,” Andrea Carcano, Nozomi Networks CEO, said in a Monday company media statement. “With automated DISA STIG assessments, defense organizations can now validate configuration compliance across diverse environments safely and at scale, without the spreadsheets and manual processes that have held them back. We are proud to take this important, industry-leading step with Lockheed Martin to deploy the most advanced AI tools to support government agencies while adhering to the highest standards of cybersecurity governance.”

As an offset project tied to the F-35 sale to Switzerland, Nozomi Networks developers, supported by Lockheed Martin cyber experts, are building significant new capabilities within the Nozomi Networks platform. These enhancements will enable the safe automation of security configuration assessments (SCAs) across diverse, distributed environments while simplifying configuration compliance and reporting tasks.

The collaboration will enable government and defense industrial base organizations globally to automate configuration compliance assessments and reporting against established benchmarks such as Defense Information Systems Agency (DISA) Security Technical Implementation Guides (STIGs). It will also safely extend automated configuration compliance checks to OT and cyber-physical systems that cannot tolerate traditional, IT-centric assessment techniques and merge configuration compliance reporting tasks with advanced asset identification, threat detection, and risk analysis capabilities.

Furthermore, the deal will produce repeatable, auditable evidence of configuration compliance to support DoD Risk Management Framework (RMF) and authorization activities.

The first of these capabilities is automated SCA, initially mapped to DISA STIGs. Existing STIG assessment workflows were designed for laptops, servers, and enterprise infrastructure and are less effective for engineering workstations, facility systems, and other operational assets that may not tolerate traditional assessment techniques. As a result, many defense organizations still rely on spreadsheets and manual validation to demonstrate compliance, an approach that cannot scale as frameworks such as the DoD Control Systems Security Requirements Guide (SRG) extend cybersecurity requirements to industrial control and cyber-physical systems.

‍Using Nozomi Arc sensors, organizations can safely collect host-level configuration data to evaluate endpoints against DISA STIG benchmarks, also supported by passive monitoring of network communications. Assessment results are consolidated and centrally managed, either on-premises in the Nozomi Networks Central Management Console (CMC) or in the cloud through Vantage or Vantage for Government. Security teams can track compliance posture and changes over time, review pass/fail findings and supporting evidence, investigate rule-level findings and remediation guidance, and view configuration posture alongside asset inventory, vulnerability, and risk data.

‍DISA STIGs are only the beginning. Nozomi Networks plans to add safe active polling to collect configuration data from assets where endpoint sensors can’t be deployed and to extend the platform to additional security frameworks and benchmarks across OT, IoT, and IT environments.

[103] Industrial Cyber - Continuous process manufacturing: Why OT cybersecurity demands different approach when plants cannot stop
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 17 Nov 2026 13:14:31 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/features/continuous-process-manufacturing-why-ot-cybersecurity-demands-different-approach-when-plants-cannot-stop/

[TAM METİN - 2993 kelime]
Continuous process manufacturing: Why OT cybersecurity demands different approach when plants cannot stop

Continuous process environments have inherent constraints that make applying standard OT (operational technology) cybersecurity practices difficult and potentially hazardous. A discrete manufacturer can most often stop a line and restart it at will, while continuous process sites rarely have that option. Typically across refineries, chemical manufacturing facilities, LNG plants, and pulp and paper mills, these continuous processes may run for months between a shutdown and an unplanned interruption, which can impact process stability, plant equipment and on-site people safety.

This is exactly why continuous process manufacturing warrants special treatment. Safety Instrumented Systems (SIS) help bring the operational process to a safe state in the event of a hazardous condition, and thus cannot be treated as normal endpoints. Clearly, any modification or change that affects their availability or communications has to undergo appropriate engineering and safety assessment prior to organizational implementation.

Furthermore, patch management in this scenario becomes a risk-based decision rather than a simple routine, particularly when maintenance windows can be as long as months. CISA’s recommended practices call for separate ICS patch processes and validation, while NIST SP 800-82 suggests testing and compensating controls if the patching is going to be delayed. Such measures usually include passive monitoring, tighter remote access, application allowlisting, backups, and engineered network zones.

Known vulnerabilities that require the next rotation are certainly not ignored. They are managed by mitigating the paths of the attacks. Network segmentation is done on the basis of this same concept, where the process dependencies must first be known by the engineers before allowing any topology changes. Similarly, incident management is restricted since organizations cannot easily isolate a system responsible for managing hazardous processes.

The resilience challenge is absolutely there in the numbers. The SANS 2026 survey found that 34% of the 696 respondents had an ICS/OT incident in the last year and 72% of those incidents resulted in downtime. Only 15% of respondents said they could sustain critical operational processes and safe production in manual mode, without centralized SCADA or distributed control system (DCS) capabilities, for more than 48 hours, while 40% were unsure whether they could do so.

Visibility does appear to make a real difference. Honeywell’s 2026 OT Cybersecurity Benchmark Report disclosed that organizations with stronger asset visibility demonstrated better incident recovery, with 42% reporting downtime of six hours or less compared to 25% among those with weaker asset visibility.

Across continuous process manufacturing sites, containment is never a purely technical decision, but emerges more as a call shared among cybersecurity, control engineering and operations teams. Compromised controllers that are still holding a reaction stable or keeping pressure within limits cannot simply be switched off the way an infected laptop can be unplugged. Moreover, responders have to weigh the cost of letting an attacker linger against the cost of destabilizing operational processes, and oftentimes that trade-off is rarely clear in the first few minutes. Getting it right depends on conversations and relationships that have been built long before the cyber threat incident begins.

Why continuous process industries need different cyber controls

Industrial Cyber asked experts to examine which cybersecurity controls commonly deployed in IT and discrete manufacturing are impractical or potentially unsafe in continuous-process environments. They also looked into what alternative approaches operators are adopting to protect OT without disrupting production or compromising safety.

“Many core cybersecurity controls still apply in continuous process environments. What changes is how, when, and where they are applied to account for process dependencies, safety, and operational constraints,” Andrew McPhee, principal architect for industrial security at Cisco, told Industrial Cyber. “Some conventional IT practices assume that an individual device can be changed or isolated as soon as a threat is identified. Automatic patching, reboots, or immediate quarantine can create problems if applied to a controller, drive, or other asset without understanding its role in the process. A change intended to protect one device can interrupt communications or affect the process it helps control.”

He added that securing these environments requires careful planning; actions cannot be purely reactive. Controls and response actions should be built around consequence boundaries designed to preserve safety.

“While a fix is deferred, operators can reduce exposure by disabling an unnecessary service or restricting which devices can reach it,” McPhee added. “Network controls should be based on observed communications, tested before enforcement, and designed around process zones. If containment is needed, the response and cutoff point should already be understood so a zone can be isolated while its internal control and safety functions remain available.”

Chris McLaughlin, principal advisor at Trusted OT Advisors, LLC, told Industrial Cyber that continuous process environments may operate for years without shutting down, making software patching and technology refreshes difficult to schedule. “Some discrete manufacturing operations also run for extended periods between maintenance windows. This includes industries such as steel or glass production that have assets that run for over a decade or longer without going down. These long operating cycles require a different approach to patching and upgrade planning, rather than relying on frequent shutdowns.”

“Controls that require a restart or interrupt communications can be impractical and potentially unsafe in continuous process manufacturing. Where those interventions cannot be performed safely, alternatives focus on continuous oversight and tightly controlled access,” Christina Hoefer, vice president of OT and IoT strategy at Forescout, told Industrial Cyber. “Continuous network monitoring and anomaly detection help operators identify changes and compare observed activity with expected behavior and known process flows.”

She added that tight controls on both local and remote network access complement that visibility, keeping process networks as isolated as practical. “Where connectivity is necessary, it should be temporary, explicitly approved and limited to the specific systems required, rather than leaving standing access in place.”

Asad Naeem, manager for instrument, control systems and OT cybersecurity at Engro Fertilizers, mentioned that immediate deployment of manufacturer updates and security patches, as commonly practiced in IT, is not feasible in continuous process plants.

“Untested patches can disrupt communications, cause loss of operator visibility, or affect process control. Even representative testing cannot eliminate all risks, as differences between test and production environments may lead to unexpected issues,” Naeem told Industrial Cyber. “A safer approach is to deploy OEM-recommended and validated patches during planned shutdowns. System architecture should also incorporate redundant operator and engineering workstations, allowing patches to be tested on one station while maintaining operational availability through the other.”

Rethinking patching in continuous process operations

The executives address how operators approach patching when the next maintenance window may be months away, particularly if a known vulnerability is being actively exploited elsewhere.

“Operators should not treat patching as a simple yes-or-no decision. A vulnerability creates a potential route for an attacker and applying a patch addresses that known flaw,” McPhee said. “If the firmware cannot be upgraded, a patch is unavailable, or it cannot be applied safely before the next maintenance window, operators can still reduce exposure and lower the risk of exploitation.”

He added that they should first check whether the exploit’s conditions apply to the device, then consider ways to reduce exposure. “For example, if a vulnerable PLC web service is not needed for operations, disabling it may remove that path to exploitation. If a vulnerability only applies when a PLC is out of RUN mode, strict controls over who can change its mode—and when—may reduce the risk until patching is possible. Where the vulnerable service must remain available, network segmentation can limit which devices can reach it. These measures do not remove the vulnerability, but they can substantially reduce the risk of exploitation until the next patch window.”

Operators often use redundancy, clustering, and virtualization to reduce disruption during patching, McLaughlin identified. “Patches are first approved by the industrial control system (ICS) manufacturer and tested in a separate environment. They are then applied in stages: patch the secondary system, switch operations to it, and monitor performance before patching the primary system. When patching must be deferred, organizations use other mitigating controls such as application allowlisting, segmentation and system hardening.”

“In continuous process environments, patching is challenging because many OT systems must remain available around the clock, and updates may require extensive testing, vendor approval, or a planned shutdown to avoid disrupting production,” Hoefer said. “Patching decisions must account for asset criticality, exposure, communication paths, and operational dependencies, rather than vulnerability severity alone.”

She noted that when immediate patching isn’t operationally safe, operators should prioritize systems that are externally exposed, reachable through remote access, or essential to production, using segmentation, restricted access, and continuous monitoring as compensating controls until a maintenance window is available.

Naeem said that when patching must be deferred, operators can adopt virtual patching and other compensating controls to reduce exposure. “These include restricting network access, tightening firewall rules, disabling unnecessary services, and strengthening endpoint protection. If a vulnerable system has unnecessary internet connectivity, it should be disconnected immediately.”

He added that operators should also understand how the vulnerability is being exploited in the wild and implement layered countermeasures to break the attack path, following the defense-in-depth principle. “The objective is to manage exposure and reduce the likelihood of exploitation until a properly tested and validated patch can be deployed during a suitable maintenance window.”

Balancing network segmentation and continuous process safety

The executives examine considerations that shape network segmentation in continuous process environments, where cutting communication between control system components could disrupt production or compromise process safety.

For years, McPhee noted that OT cybersecurity efforts have focused on visibility. “That work now needs to translate into protection. Network segmentation is a key part of that step, but it cannot be implemented blindly. Operators need to understand which assets communicate, how they behave, which services they use, and how those relationships support plant operations.”

He noted that in continuous process environments, routine communication patterns may be stable, giving operators a useful baseline. Operators can use this visibility to build segmentation policies and, with the right tools, simulate their effects before enforcing them in production. This approach helps accelerate segmentation projects by suggesting rules based on observed baseline and using simulation to avoid unintended impacts when actively controlling access.

“Network segmentation must limit the spread of an attack without disrupting essential communications. Placing several production lines in one security zone increases the potential impact: malware introduced on one line could affect the others,” McLaughlin said. “Operators should map dependencies and communications between lines, then assess how losing each connection would affect production and process safety. That analysis should guide zone boundaries and permitted communications.”

Hoefer recognizes that segmentation must reflect how the industrial process actually operates, including which devices communicate, the protocols they use, and the dependencies that support production and safety.

“Segmentation and access control can be more stringent at the perimeter and between supporting services and OT networks, assuming there is no direct process dependency, such as on time servers or user management systems,” she observed. “Then within the control system networks, segmentation will need to be more dynamic and OT-aware with fallback options to allow safety system to run without interruptions.”

Network segmentation must be based on process dependencies, not just cybersecurity requirements, Naeem said. “Consider communication between a Distributed Control System (DCS) and a Safety Instrumented System (SIS).”

He added that while ISA/IEC 62443 requires separate security zones, introducing a firewall between these systems may create additional failure modes or a single point of failure. “Where technically feasible, dedicated point-to-point links can provide an alternative, keeping communication physically isolated from the wider OT network. Any segmentation approach must undergo engineering and safety assessments to ensure reliable communication without compromising process safety.”

Containing cyber incidents without disrupting operations

The executives assess how operators make containment decisions when isolating a compromised system could jeopardize process safety, and who has the authority to approve or override those decisions.

Highlighting that safety should never be compromised, McPhee said that safety procedures work because the boundary and response are understood in advance. “If a person steps across a laser tripwire, the operator knows what will happen and why. Cybersecurity needs the same clarity. For every zone, the plant should know where the cutoff point is, what will be isolated if an incident crosses that boundary, and what that means for the process. If isolation affects production, that consequence should already be understood and accepted. When something goes wrong, people can follow the agreed response rather than improvise under pressure.”

He added that the ISA/IEC 62443 zones and conduits can help define these boundaries. Segmentation should reflect how the process works.

“The cyber ‘big red button’ should cut east-west communications between process zones while preserving the control and safety functions within the isolated zone,” according to McPhee. “Because a PLC or drive may depend on other assets in its process zone, the planned response to a compromised asset may be to isolate the whole zone, not to contain the single asset. Everyone should know which zone-to-zone connections will be cut and what will happen to production. The cutoff point, response, and authority to act should be clear in plant procedures. There should be no surprises when a zone must be isolated.”

“Organizations should identify and test cyber incident scenarios in advance, mapping system dependencies and the safety consequences of isolation. Pre-agreed decisions help teams respond quickly,” McLaughlin pointed out. “For situations not anticipated, the Cyber Incident Response Plan (CIRP) should define how the appropriate operational technology (OT) and IT personnel assess the trade-offs and who has decision-making authority.”

Hoefer observed that an abrupt disconnection could cause greater harm than allowing the compromise to continue while a safer response is coordinated. “An IT/SOC analyst who detects an attack, such as ransomware, therefore needs to consult production staff or the OT security representative before initiating isolation that could affect the process.”

She added that the assessment is joint – IT/SOC explains the attack and the consequences of leaving it uncontained, while OT assesses the affected systems, their process dependencies and what a sudden interruption would mean for production, equipment and people. IT/SOC advises on the cyber threat, but the final decision on actions affecting the operating process rests with the OT team.

“Incident containment decisions require collaboration between Control Systems, Cybersecurity, and Operations teams. Cybersecurity assesses the threat and recommends containment measures, while Operations evaluates process and safety implications,” Naeem said. “The Control Systems team develops a technically safe response based on system architecture, functionality, and recovery procedures.”

For example, he added operator HMIs may be isolated while the control system continues operating autonomously, with field operators maintaining process oversight where feasible. HMIs and servers can then be restored from validated backups. Remote programming access to safety-critical systems should remain disabled to minimize potential cyber risks.

Managing cyber exposure without disrupting production

The executives explain how continuous process operators assess residual cyber risk, and brief leadership on it when patching and other standard fixes are off the table or must wait.

McPhee identified that there is no such thing as zero risk in a running plant. “Risk is about more than whether an asset has a vulnerability. Does it use insecure protocols? Can it accept unauthenticated commands? Does its web server have default credentials—or none at all? The key question is: if the device were compromised, what is the worst credible outcome, and how likely is it? Risk assessment considers both the potential impact and the likelihood of that outcome.”

He added that risk tolerance is the level of risk the organization is prepared to carry, taking into account safety, environmental impact, production loss, and financial cost. Safety must have the highest priority; financial and operational impacts also need to be clear. Risk acceptance is the explicit decision to carry a specific residual risk, with its controls and limits understood.

“Operators should frame the decision in business terms. If a line went down, how quickly could it return to service? Tested backups and recovery plans can reduce the impact and cost of downtime, though they do not remove the vulnerability,” according to McPhee. “What would the likely downtime cost? Is leadership willing to accept that potential cost until remediation is possible, or does the risk justify action sooner? Framing residual risk in terms of business impact gives leadership the information it needs to make an informed decision.”

McLaughlin called for communicating residual cyber risk in terms of likelihood and business impact, explaining how people, process, and technology controls reduce it. “When patching or other conventional remediation must be deferred, explain which alternatives are in place and what risk remains. These may include application allowlisting, which permits only approved applications to run, and other system-hardening measures.”

“When remediation must be deferred, the assessment needs to go beyond the presence of a vulnerability to examine how it could be exploited in the operating environment. That means evaluating network connectivity, local access and remote access by employees or third parties,” Hoefer detailed. “The assessment must also consider what successful exploitation would mean for production uptime, process integrity and safety. The next step is to assess how compensating controls reduce that likelihood and limit the potential impact.”

She also noted that leadership should receive a clear explanation of the exposure, the operational consequences, and the next opportunity for remediation. This makes the decision explicit: whether the residual risk is acceptable until that opportunity.

“When vulnerabilities cannot be immediately remediated due to operational or technical constraints, the residual cyber risk should be formally documented and communicated through the organization’s enterprise risk register,” Naeem said. “This provides leadership with visibility of the exposure, its potential operational and safety consequences, and the compensating controls in place.”

He concluded that where permanent remediation requires additional investment, the requirement should be prioritized and incorporated into upcoming capital expenditure (CAPEX) or operating expenditure (OPEX) planning cycles. “This approach ensures that deferred remediation remains a managed business risk rather than an overlooked technical issue, with clear ownership, visibility, and a planned path toward resolution.”
